# OT1997 chunk 12 entering-law reading slice

Derived copies only. Current state trackers and dated current-term Records control. Independent June 25 matters share law effective before June 25; independent June 26 peers share pre-June-26 law. Later events use all earlier effective public law. No same-day processing order supplies a precedent. Every selected passage below is mechanically copied from saved authority, not independently authored.

## Source: state/holdings/administrative-law.md — John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86

#### John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1074.

**Decided:** 1993-12-13.

For the other questions in John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 1993-12-13, No. 92-1074, see Labor and Employment — The guaranteed-benefit exclusion operates by component; Labor and Employment — Insurance regulation does not displace ERISA's express direction.

##### An agency interpretation cannot erase the qualified exclusion

**Holding:** The Labor Department's reading excluding general-account assets categorically cannot control because it exceeds the permissible statutory range by eliminating §401(b)(2)'s component limitation. The Court does not hold that inconsistency or presentation in litigation invariably defeats deference, and leaves the general degree of deference due agency litigation positions unresolved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, and Souter. These six adopt the statutory-limit ground; the dissenters separately reject the categorical agency position without adopting the Court's entire investment-risk analysis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. NRDC: applied at the limit imposed by the statute; no categorical exception for inconsistent interpretations or litigation positions is adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The contract contains guarantees and a residual floor; those do not guarantee the disputed free-funds benefits. A guaranteed reasonable return and fixed conversion protection are indicators, not an exhaustive mandatory formula. No breach, loss or remedy follows from status alone, and no categorical anti-deference rule for inconsistency or litigation positions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Second Circuit's relevant judgment is affirmed, 6–3. Asset status and applicable fiduciary functions remain distinct from breach, causation, loss and relief, which must be addressed in further proceedings; no monetary or equitable award is made.

## Source: state/holdings/bankruptcy.md — BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp., 511 U.S. 531

#### BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp., 511 U.S. 531

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1370.

**Decided:** 1994-05-23.

##### Regular foreclosure procedure does not conclusively establish federal value

**Holding:** A noncollusive mortgage foreclosure of real property that complies with state law does not conclusively establish reasonably equivalent value under 11 U.S.C. §548(a)(2)(A); when the statutory avoidance claim is timely and otherwise properly presented, the bankruptcy court must assess the value exchanged independently under that federal requirement. Procedural compliance remains relevant evidence, and rejection of conclusiveness establishes neither insolvency nor any other avoidance predicate, entitlement to recovery, or a defect in the debtor's property interest.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Ginsburg; five Justices directly adopt the proposition and explanation. No fractured-decision synthesis is needed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Butner v. United States, 440 U.S. 48 (1979): applies its distinction between state-defined property interests and countervailing federal bankruptcy consequences.
- Owen v. Owen, 500 U.S. 305 (1991): applies the independent operation of an enacted federal avoidance power.
- Dewsnup v. Timm, January 15, 1992: distinguishes its limited §506(d) Chapter 7 lien-survival rule; express avoidance powers remain separate.
- Nobelman v. American Savings Bank, June 1, 1993: distinguishes the §1322(b)(2) principal-residence protection; its rights and qualified cure rules remain intact.

##### Value is compared under realistically comparable forced-sale conditions

**Holding:** The §548(a)(2)(A) comparison concerns the value transferred and the total legally relevant consideration in realistically comparable forced-sale conditions, with competent evidence of property condition, enforceable interests and liens, realistic sale constraints, available purchasers, actual marketing and notice, and reliable comparable distressed transactions where pertinent; these are sources of valuation evidence, not mandatory elements or new foreclosure procedures. The avoidance claimant retains its statutory burden, and neither an unsupported appraisal above the price, procedural regularity alone, a fixed percentage, nor hindsight about a better hypothetical sale supplies the required conclusion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Ginsburg; five Justices directly adopt the proposition and explanation. No fractured-decision synthesis is needed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Gelfert v. National City Bank, 313 U.S. 221 (1941): uses its recognition of forced-sale conditions without converting price into a conclusive federal measure.
- In re Bundles, 856 F.2d 815 (7th Cir. 1988): supports attention to the transaction's circumstances; no wholesale adoption of a formal presumption or every part of its method.
- Durrett v. Washington National Insurance Co., 621 F.2d 201 (5th Cir. 1980): does not supply a controlling minimum percentage.
- In re Madrid, 21 B.R. 424 (BAP9 1982), and In re Winshall Settler's Trust, 758 F.2d 1136 (6th Cir. 1985): their conclusive regular-foreclosure approach cannot govern the federal value issue after this decision.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The inquiry concerns timely mortgage foreclosure avoidance of real property, not every forced sale. All statutory predicates and transferee protections remain necessary. The allegation of a value above $725,000 against a $433,000 sale price is not a finding. No Durrett percentage or Bundles presumption is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both the district-court/private-buyer and Bankruptcy Appellate Panel/Imperial routes are reversed on the categorical value ground and remanded, 5–4. The rejected no-property-interest alternative is not revived. No avoidance, title transfer, refund or fixed recovery is automatic; proved avoidance and recovery requirements and transferee protections remain necessary, without reopening titles outside this timely proceeding.

## Source: state/holdings/business-and-commercial-law.md — Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264

#### Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1175.

**Decided:** January 17, 1996.

##### Judicial enforcement of the distinct interbank liability regime

**Holding:** A depository institution may bring an action in federal district court or another court of competent jurisdiction to enforce interbank liability established by the Federal Reserve Board within 12 U.S.C. §4010(f)'s delegation; §4010(d) supplies the judicial forum for that action. Section 4010(a)'s exclusion of another depository institution continues to withhold that subsection's separate customer remedy, but does not exclude the subsection (f) action from subsection (d).

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Board's authority under §4010(f) concerns receipt, payment, collection or clearing of checks and related check-payment-system functions. The ordinary liability ceiling is the check's amount; other damages require bad faith and proximate consequence. Section 4010(d) permits an action within one year of violation, without deciding a timeliness dispute. Subsection (a)'s additional individual/class damages and successful-enforcement costs and reasonable attorney fees do not follow merely from access under (d) and (f). Subsection (c) separately protects an institution proving by a preponderance that a violation was unintentional and resulted from bona fide error despite reasonably adapted procedures, excluding errors of legal judgment. Subsection (e) separately protects good-faith conformity with a Board rule, regulation or interpretation despite later amendment, rescission or invalidation. No application of either defense, bad faith, amount or fee entitlement is decided. Section 4009(c)(1) does not create exclusive Board adjudication; absence of an administrative mechanism itself creates no jurisdiction. No independent §1331 basis, general jurisdictional-deference rule, state action or rule that every Board regulation creates a private action is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Touche Ross's authorization requirement is applied and its missing-liability setting distinguished; Transamerica's recognition of textually supported, limited relief is applied; Things Remembered's coexistence principle is applied by analogy without importing its removal-review rules.

##### Removing the jurisdictional bar leaves regulatory liability for the remaining appeal

**Holding:** Because the Act permits this interbank action, the Seventh Circuit's judgment requiring dismissal for want of jurisdiction is reversed, and the case is remanded for consideration of properly remaining challenges to the district court's judgment under the governing Federal Reserve rules. The recognition of that action does not establish Bank One's entitlement to prevail on liability or damages and does not unconditionally restore the district court's award.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the jurisdictional dismissal and remand for properly remaining appellate challenges under the Federal Reserve rules. The $43,912.06 district award is not unconditionally restored. The district court's omitted-reason breach finding is not adopted; no negligence, causation, recoverable loss, bad-faith, timeliness or defense issue is resolved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Town & Country's distinction between legal coverage and individual relief is applied by analogy; Zicherman's respect for a governing remedial scheme is applied without importing its maritime damages rule.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Hudson v. McMillian, 503 U.S. 1 (1992)

#### Hudson v. McMillian, 503 U.S. 1 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-6531.
**Decided:** February 25, 1992.

##### Whether limited injury defeats the established nontrivial malicious beating of a restrained prisoner

**Holding:** A nontrivial beating inflicted maliciously and without disciplinary justification on a restrained convicted prisoner violates the Eighth Amendment even when the resulting physical injuries are limited, where the established conduct satisfies even *Whitley*’s demanding malicious-and-sadistic inquiry. Injury remains relevant evidence and bears on compensation; accidental harm or a merely technical battery is not enough.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part A of O’Connor’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter; six Justices adopt this rule and its application. Blackmun’s concurrence in the judgment is not counted as a join. O’Connor’s Part B, joined by White, Kennedy and Souter, has only four votes for extending *Whitley* to every prison excessive-force claim. No *Marks* aggregation supplies that universal rule.

**Material application:** Hudson was handcuffed and shackled; the undisturbed findings established unnecessary repeated blows and supervisory condonation. Dental and facial injuries confirmed that the force was not trivial. The findings satisfied even *Whitley*’s heightened culpability inquiry.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No comprehensive mental-state standard for every force setting, psychological-injury-only rule, immunity decision, or exhaustive rule for unusual forms of cruelty is adopted. Medical-care, confinement-conditions, legitimate-discipline and compensation rules remain distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand to the Fifth Circuit for further disposition consistent with the undisturbed findings and without its significant-injury bar. No increase above the existing $800 award, immunity ruling or prison-management injunction is ordered.

**Material judgment:** Reversal and remand, 7–2: Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter; Scalia and Thomas dissent.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Whitley v. Albers*, 475 U.S. 312, is applied as a sufficient ground on these findings; its disturbance rule is not extended universally. *Wilson v. Seiter*, 501 U.S. 294, is distinguished: its objective-deprivation requirement does not become a significant-injury floor for this beating. *Estelle v. Gamble*, 429 U.S. 97, supplies the prohibition of unnecessary and wanton pain while retaining the distinct serious-medical-need and deliberate-indifference requirements.

##### Whether an assumed isolated, unauthorized assault sustains affirmance on this record

**Holding:** The undisturbed finding of supervisory condonation and the limits of the granted question prevent affirmance on an assumed characterization of this beating as an isolated, unauthorized assault.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part C of O’Connor’s opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter; six-Justice case-specific rejection of the alternative ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court announces no general definition of punishment or general rule governing isolated, unauthorized conduct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The alternative characterization supplies no ground for preserving the Fifth Circuit’s judgment; the remand must respect the intact supervisory-condonation finding.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Hunter v. Bryant, 502 U.S. 224 (1991)

#### Hunter v. Bryant, 502 U.S. 224 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1440.
**Decided:** December 16, 1991.

##### Who decides qualified immunity from an arrest-without-probable-cause claim and what record governs at summary judgment

**Holding:** Qualified immunity from a claim of arrest without probable cause is a legal question for the judge under ordinary summary-judgment constraints. The court must determine whether a reasonable officer could believe the arrest lawful on the supported nonmovant version of the complete contemporaneous record; choosing the “more reasonable” interpretation is not the test, and a generalized security concern alone does not establish a reasonable belief that the offense's elements were satisfied.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion, joined by Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, and Souter; these five Justices supply the controlling majority of the eight participants.

**Material application:** The agents could consider assassination references, a reported gesture, oral statements, the confusing interview, and refusal to elaborate. They also had the letter's repeated attribution of the supposed plot to an outside group, bearing on whether Bryant communicated a serious threat under §871 or reported an imagined danger. The Ninth Circuit's comparison of the relative reasonableness of interpretations and its general assignment of legal immunity to a jury were erroneous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court finds neither guilt, innocence, bad faith, nor a constitutional violation. It neither directs immunity nor orders an automatic trial. The separate warrant ruling remains intact.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is vacated and the case remanded. The remand court must specify any supported dispute over material historical facts and decide immunity promptly under the correct standard.

**Material judgment:** Vacatur and remand are supported by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, and Souter, 6–2. White would direct immunity; Stevens would affirm, and Scalia would reverse and direct immunity. These differing remedial positions do not enlarge the five-Justice controlling rationale.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Harlow, Anderson v. Creighton, and Malley supply protection for reasonable mistakes and early judicial resolution when supported facts permit it. Watts supplies the contextual distinction between a true threat and protected expression. Mitchell, Beck, and Liberty Lobby are applied and preserved. No qualified-immunity rule is revised.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Richardson v. McKnight

#### Richardson v. McKnight

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-318.

**Decided:** 1997-06-23.

##### I. These commercial prison employees are not eligible for Harlow immunity

**Holding:** Guards employed full time by a private, for-profit firm that independently undertakes substantial continuing prison management under limited direct governmental supervision and potential market competition are not entitled to Harlow qualified immunity merely because they exercise delegated governmental custody. Neither the historical showing nor the functional justification establishes that immunity in this institutional setting; brief private assistance, adjunct service, close official supervision and integration into government remain open.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Six Justices directly adopt the eligibility rule, its limits and the bounded affirmance/remand.

**Material application:** The historical showing did not establish modern immunity from suit for these employees. Potential competition, contractual accountability and private employment arrangements inform functional need without findings of perfect competition, particular insurance coverage or indemnification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** State action is assumed only for the immunity appeal. Neither a constitutional violation, causation, damages, corporate immunity or liability, nor the existence, elements or burdens of an independent private good-faith defense is decided. Eligibility precedes application of Harlow; no replacement notice or conduct test is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Denial of qualified immunity is affirmed, 6–3, and the case is remanded for ordinary proceedings on unresolved predicates and independently available defenses. There is no automatic trial entitlement or damages award.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Wyatt's separation of attribution, immunity and a potential merits defense is applied in the commercial custodial setting. Harlow remains for eligible officials. Fletcher requires function-specific justification, while Behrens and Johnson v. Jones distinguish legal immunity review from factual-sufficiency appeals. Elder and Crawford-El preserve the actual governing law and constitutional proof requirements without creating eligibility or an additional evidentiary burden.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, 508 U.S. 602 (1993)

#### Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, 508 U.S. 602 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-904.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, June 14, 1993, No. 91-904, see Property and Economic Rights — Pension withdrawal liability and takings.

##### The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication

**Holding:** The interested plan sponsor’s withdrawal-liability assessment and demand are enforcement acts, not an adjudication. Due process permits that preliminary demand because the dispute first receives adjudication before a neutral arbitrator, under the lawful arbitral burdens stated separately below.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Tumey, 273 U.S. 510 — interested adjudication forbidden; the sponsor is not the adjudicator here.
- Ward, 409 U.S. 57 — appellate review does not automatically cure initial biased adjudication; that premise is absent here.
- Withrow, 421 U.S. 35 — preliminary and adjudicative functions distinguished.

##### The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion

**Holding:** In neutral arbitration, §1401(a)(3)(A) places on the employer the burden to disprove the challenged factual determination by a preponderance. It does not add appellate deference to the sponsor’s factual determinations, a clear-and-convincing requirement or a conclusive presumption.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Crowell v. Benson, 285 U.S. 22 — constitutionally sound construction used within the interpretive choice the text permits.
- Section 1401(c) — judicial review presumes arbitral findings correct unless rebutted by a clear preponderance; no transfer of that standard into arbitration.

##### Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes

**Holding:** Under §1401(a)(3)(B), a contesting employer may rebut the actuarial calculation by a preponderance showing either that assumptions and methods were unreasonable in the aggregate, considering plan experience and reasonable expectations, or that the actuary made a significant error in applying them. The professional and statutory constraints make this presumption constitutionally permissible here; fraud need not be proved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Section 1393 — statutory constraints on actuarial assumptions and methods applied.
- Section 1401(a)(3)(B) — aggregate unreasonableness OR significant application error preserved as separate routes.
- Connolly, 475 U.S. 211 — pooled-plan structure provides context, not an irrebuttable actuarial presumption.

##### This withdrawal allocation is rational

**Holding:** Allocating a proportionate share of unfunded vested-benefit costs to this withdrawing employer is rational in light of its participation in the pooled pension undertaking. Neither private contribution limits nor the absence of a one-to-one match between its contributions and benefits to its own employees defeats this substantive-due-process application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, This withdrawal allocation is rational portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Arbitral factfinding and actuarial judgments remain distinct. Under the applicable §1393(a)(1) route, assumptions and methods collectively must be reasonable and represent the actuary’s best estimate of anticipated plan experience; another reasonable estimate alone proves no defect. Judicial review under §1401(c) separately presumes arbitral facts correct unless rebutted by a clear preponderance. Defined-benefit classification is outside review.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm enforcement of this award under independent arbitration and the stated statutory construction; no second arbitration is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Usery v. Turner Elkhorn, 428 U.S. 1 — rational retrospective economic allocation applied.
- PBGC v. R. A. Gray, 467 U.S. 717 — pension-protection allocation sustained within actual statutory setting.
- Connolly, 475 U.S. 211 — pooling and contractual limits treated consistently with its constitutional holding.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Fletcher v. Kalina

#### Fletcher v. Kalina

**Docket or dockets:** Ninth Circuit No. 95-36129; no Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** June 16, 1997.

##### Protected prosecutorial advocacy remains distinct from personal evidentiary acts

**Holding:** A prosecutor has absolute immunity under §1983 for preparing and filing an information or warrant motion, evaluating evidence for those submissions, and presenting the State's legal case when performing an advocate's function. An allegation of bad purpose or false evidence does not remove that protection from the advocacy function, but the protection does not extend to a distinct personal evidentiary certification merely because the same prosecutor performs both acts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part I, joined in full by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: eight Justices. Stone-Zsela joins protection of legitimate advocacy and separation of the personal evidentiary act, but not refusal of an additional dishonesty exception.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The function, rather than a categorical pre-charge or post-charge boundary, determines immunity. No general dishonesty exception to otherwise protected advocacy is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Imbler preserves charging and courtroom advocacy; Burns separates legal presentation from other acts. Buckley preserves genuine preparation while excluding ordinary investigation; Antoine requires historical and functional justification rather than importance to adjudication.

##### A prosecutor personally certifying arrest-warrant facts acts as a complaining witness

**Holding:** A prosecutor who personally swears to factual assertions offered to establish probable cause for an arrest warrant performs the evidentiary function of a complaining witness and has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for that certification. This classification depends on the personal evidentiary undertaking, not on proof of falsity, and does not withdraw immunity from ordinary courtroom witness testimony.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, unanimous.

**Material application:** Kalina personally guaranteed the facts offered to obtain Fletcher's arrest warrant; that evidentiary undertaking is separate from preparing and presenting the charging papers.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither actual falsity nor the availability of another truthful affiant determines the functional classification. Ordinary courtroom testimony immunity remains intact.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the denial of absolute immunity for the personal certification and remand, while preserving immunity for protected legal preparation, filing and presentation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Malley supplies the warrant-affiant comparison; official prosecutorial title does not immunize the same evidentiary function. Buckley and Antoine extend their functional distinction to the personal certification, without changing their other boundaries.

##### The function-specific immunity denial leaves constitutional liability and personal defenses unresolved

**Holding:** Denial of absolute immunity for the personal oath leaves Fletcher to establish an actionable constitutional deprivation, the required culpability, materiality, causation and compensable injury; Kalina retains qualified immunity and other available personal defenses. The existing qualified-immunity framework governs the remand, using the law and information relevant to the conduct at issue, without a new defense-burden rule or a finding that any element or defense has been established.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stevens's opinion, adopted in full by all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela joins the unresolved-liability and bounded-remand propositions and accepts the existing qualified-immunity framework, without joining the source-specific causation and notice explanations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A judge's warrant does not categorically break causation when material deception procured it, but a corrected truthful account may still establish probable cause. Neither question is resolved here. Disputed historical facts follow ordinary procedural rules; legal immunity is for the judge. No damages judgment or mandatory trial follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hafer preserves personal-capacity liability only on proof of the officer’s own violation and preserves personal defenses. Harlow and Anderson retain objective, particularized clearly-established-law protection. Elder requires consideration of all relevant precedent; Lanier rejects categorical source and factual-twin restrictions, without supplying retrospective notice.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

#### Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-757.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

For the other questions in Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 1994-04-26, No. 92-757, see Federal Courts — Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences.

##### Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct

**Holding:** Section 102’s new compensatory and punitive liability does not attach to discriminatory conduct completed before November 21, 1991, because it adds legal consequences without sufficient congressional direction for that reach. Its Title VII compensation supplements, rather than replaces, §706(g) relief and excludes backpay, interest on backpay and other relief authorized by that provision; punitive damages have the distinct statutory predicates of malice or reckless indifference to federally protected rights and exclude government, government-agency and political-subdivision respondents. No such damages award or predicate finding is made here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s §102 application, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas independently reach the same unavailability of new damages through their concurrence. Blackmun disagrees about compensation and reserves punitive damages.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bennett and Bowen: applied to the new monetary burden attached to completed conduct.
- Bradley: distinguished; the collateral fee award did not create this new compensation or punishment for the underlying private conduct.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, 503 U.S. 60 (1992): distinguished; appropriate relief in an implied Title IX action does not enlarge Title VII’s earlier express remedial scheme.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, 503 U.S. 429 (1992): distinguished; its express substitution of law governing identified pending disputes does not supply the temporal direction missing here.

##### The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim

**Holding:** Under §102(c), if the complaining party seeks compensatory or punitive damages available under that section, any party may demand a jury trial, and the court must not inform the jury of the statutory monetary limits. Because §102 damages are unavailable for this preenactment conduct, its conditional jury provision supplies no independent right to a new trial; this conclusion does not prohibit applying a genuinely procedural change to an appropriate future procedural act in another case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s jury portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, supplies the controlling five-Justice explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reach the same conditional conclusion in their concurrence; Blackmun would permit a jury election because he would make compensation available.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kaiser: applied to the enacted condition linking the jury option to §102 damages.
- Bradley: distinguished; current-law treatment of collateral matters does not erase an express condition on a new remedy or automatically reopen a completed trial.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is provision-specific, not a declaration that the whole 1991 Act is inapplicable in pending cases. Section 402(a)'s enactment-date clause, §109(c)'s later-conduct limitation for extraterritorial amendments, and §402(b)'s exception for disparate-impact complaints filed before March 1, 1975 with initial decisions after October 30, 1983 remain distinct. Judicial retroactivity under Harper does not control legislative reach. An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. The present case concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 8–1. No new damages trial, award or remand is ordered, and the existing equitable-remedy judgment and unreviewed constructive-discharge findings remain intact.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510

#### Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-8579.

**Decided:** 1994-02-23.

##### Appellate review must consider all relevant law on the preserved immunity issue

**Holding:** Whether the asserted federal right was clearly established when the officers acted is a legal question reviewed independently on appeal using all relevant precedent, including decisions neither cited to nor discovered by the district court. That duty does not admit a new claim or factual record, make every considered precedent controlling, or permit later decisions to supply notice retroactively; here the notice inquiry remains fixed at April 27, 1987.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. All nine adopt the legal-authority rule and the limited reversal and remand.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court neither grants nor denies ultimate immunity, decides the factual or legal sufficiency of exigency, awards damages, orders new factfinding or an automatic trial, nor imposes a mandatory merits-first sequence. United States v. Al-Azzawy, 784 F.2d 890, is relevant circuit law predating the arrest; its rule concerning a compelled exit and its ultimate exigency disposition must both be evaluated. Depositions about access to guns and five days of advance arrest planning bear in different directions and are not resolved here. Florida warrants and the absence of an Idaho warrant are distinct facts; the Court does not decide that either alone disposes of the home-arrest question. The lower courts must distinguish a coerced exit from a genuinely voluntary appearance under the governing home-arrest doctrine. Ordinary preservation of claims and evidence remains separate from consideration of legal authority on this preserved claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800: applies objective clearly-established-law protection; the defense is preserved, not replaced by a new reliance-only rule.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511: applies the legal character of the immunity inquiry.
- Pierce v. Underwood, 487 U.S. 552: applies independent appellate review of legal questions; this later decision does not itself supply officers with notice in April 1987.
- Davis v. Scherer, 468 U.S. 183: distinguishes a requirement that the relevant federal right be clearly established from a rule excluding uncited authority; unrelated duties do not defeat immunity.
- Hunter v. Bryant, December 16, 1991: preserves judicial determination under ordinary summary-judgment constraints using the supported nonmovant version of the complete contemporaneous record; its later decision date supplies no retroactive notice.
- Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635: preserves the particularized objective inquiry used in current review; because it was decided after April 27, 1987, it is not itself pre-arrest notice authority.
- Payton v. New York, 445 U.S. 573: retains its warrant protection against a nonconsensual home arrest absent exigent circumstances for the lower court's application; this decision does not resolve that application.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for independent immunity review using all relevant law and the proper summary-judgment record. Ultimate immunity, exigency, Fourth Amendment liability and damages remain undecided; no automatic trial or new factfinding is ordered.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825

#### Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-7247.

**Decided:** 1994-06-06.

##### Actual awareness and unreasonable disregard of substantial danger

**Holding:** A prison official violates the Eighth Amendment by disregarding a substantial risk of serious harm only if the official knows facts indicating that risk, actually appreciates the risk, and fails to respond reasonably. A danger that an official merely should have discovered does not establish deliberate indifference; an official who responds reasonably is not liable under this rule even if harm occurs.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Eight Justices adopt this rule; Thomas joins only the judgment.

**Material application:** The official need not foresee the precise attacker, timing or mechanism when the known danger encompasses the prisoner's situation. No defendant is found to have the required knowledge, responsibility or causal role; reasonable but unsuccessful protection remains outside deliberate indifference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Estelle v. Gamble, 429 U.S. 97: applies its distinction between deliberate indifference and negligence without reopening the accepted conditions framework.
- Wilson v. Seiter, 501 U.S. 294: applies its separate objective and subjective requirements and specifies actual-awareness recklessness for this failure-to-protect claim.
- Hudson v. Palmer, 468 U.S. 517: applies its recognition of custodial protection duties; it supplies no automatic liability from an assault.
- Hudson v. McMillian, February 25, 1992: preserves the nontrivial malicious-beating holding and leaves its unresolved broader force standards untouched; no universal malicious-and-sadistic requirement enters this conditions claim.

##### Circumstantial proof and reconsideration of summary judgment

**Holding:** Actual awareness may be proved by ordinary circumstantial evidence, including an obvious risk, and does not require an express warning by the prisoner. Obviousness permits but does not compel an inference: a genuine failure to appreciate the risk defeats the knowledge element, whereas deliberately refusing to confirm strongly suspected facts or their obvious inference may establish awareness; summary judgment here must be reconsidered without treating Farmer's silence as dispositive.

**Proposition-level authority:** The proof-and-remand portion of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Eight adopt its reasoning; all nine support vacatur and remand.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The transfer officials may contest their knowledge, responsibility, causation and reasonable response. The Court does not combine different defendants' innocent information into collective knowledge, find the alleged assault proved, or decide qualified immunity. The event-date rule is Rule 56(f).

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Wilson v. Seiter, 501 U.S. 294: applies its actual culpability requirement without making a prisoner warning an additional element.
- Estelle v. Gamble, 429 U.S. 97: preserves the negligence boundary while allowing ordinary evidence of deliberate indifference.

##### Current danger governs preventive relief

**Holding:** Prospective protection requires an established current substantial danger and knowing unreasonable nonresponse that persists through the litigation and is likely to continue. The court may consider later developments bearing on present danger and knowledge; litigation notice cannot create past damages liability, and officials may defeat prospective relief by showing that the danger or culpable response has ceased and will not recur.

**Proposition-level authority:** The prospective-relief portion of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt this rule and the bounded remand.

**Current-force modification:** The constitutional liability rule is unchanged. PLRA §802 now separately conditions prospective relief in a federal-law civil prison-conditions action, excluding habeas challenges to the fact or duration of confinement: the court must find the relief narrowly drawn, no broader than necessary to correct the particular plaintiff's federal violation and the least intrusive necessary means, giving substantial weight to adverse public-safety or criminal-justice effects. Relief requiring or permitting officials to exceed state or local authority, or otherwise violating state or local law, additionally requires that federal law permit that contrary relief, that it be necessary to correct the federal violation and that no other remedy correct the violation; the statute authorizes neither prison construction nor tax increases. Section 802(b)(1) applies amended §3626 to prospective relief granted before, on or after enactment. No existing order is terminated by admission, and the original constitutional holding does not alone establish these additional statutory findings.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Terre Haute warden and Bureau Director were sued only in official capacities; the Oxford warden, case manager and two regional officials faced both-capacity claims. Equitable discretion and appropriate internal grievance procedures are not converted into a new mandatory exhaustion holding. No later statutory exhaustion regime is applied.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Helling v. McKinney, June 18, 1993: applies preventive protection against serious future harm and its direction to examine current conditions.
- Wilson v. Seiter, 501 U.S. 294: retains the separate culpability requirement for the threatened conditions violation.
- Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents, 403 U.S. 388: preserves the presented individual-officer damages channel without creating a Treasury claim.
- Carlson v. Green, 446 U.S. 14: preserves the individual-officer Eighth Amendment damages source; damages entitlement remains unproved.
- FDIC v. Meyer, February 23, 1994: applies the distinction between an individual claim and an unavailable direct agency constitutional damages action.
- Elder v. Holloway, February 23, 1994: preserves review of immunity using all relevant law existing when the conduct occurred; this decision supplies no retroactive notice in 1989.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Seventh Circuit's judgment is vacated and the case remanded for reconsideration of the Rule 56(f) request and summary judgment without an express-warning prerequisite. Discovery may be allowed or denied on a proper basis, and renewed summary judgment remains available. Knowledge, responsibility, causation, reasonable response, capacities and immunity remain individualized. No trial, damages or placement injunction is ordered; prospective relief must fit any established continuing danger.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Prison Litigation Reform Act of 1995, Pub. L. 104-134, Title VIII, enacted April 26, 1996: §802, additional §3626(a)(1) findings and express prospective-relief transition. This is a noncase-law change, not an overruling or new judicial holding.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois

#### Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-783.
**Decided:** May 22, 1995.

##### Program-wide participation opportunity

**Holding:** Title IX does not guarantee preservation of a particular varsity team or sport-for-sport counterparts. Lawful separate teams remain subject to equal opportunity; effective accommodation may be established through substantial proportionality, a history and continuing responsive expansion for the underrepresented sex, or full and effective accommodation of that sex's interests and abilities, with any one route sufficient and distinct regulatory requirements independently binding.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, Part I; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Separate teams are permitted for competitive-skill selection or contact sports while equal opportunity remains required. When a noncontact team is offered only to one sex, the excluded sex must receive tryouts if its athletic opportunities previously were limited. Section 1681(b) rejects required preference merely because of imbalance while permitting relevant statistics. Proportionality is an alternative safe harbor, not a compulsory quota. Men's participation remained above substantial proportionality after the cut; complete compliance for women and independently regulated facilities, coaching, equipment, scheduling and benefits are not decided or excused.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837: applies lawful delegated interpretation; authorizes no quota contrary to statute.
- Martin v. Occupational Safety & Health Review Commission, 499 U.S. 144: applies reasonable regulatory interpretation within its terms.

##### Independent constitutional scrutiny

**Holding:** A public university may consider sex during genuine fiscal or programmatic restructuring to preserve opportunities against demonstrated inequality only when the choice serves an actual important objective and is substantially related to it. Ratios, budget pressure or attempted federal compliance alone are insufficient; stereotypes, pretext and measures materially broader than the demonstrated need are forbidden.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, Part II; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The record includes demonstrated earlier denied opportunities, persistent inequality and preservation of actual women's swimming places during genuine restructuring, not percentages alone. The rule operates symmetrically for a demonstrably disadvantaged sex. Sexual orientation, gender identity, transgender status and individual team eligibility are not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Craig v. Boren, 429 U.S. 190: applies intermediate scrutiny to sex-conscious treatment.
- Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan, 458 U.S. 718: applies genuine-objective and connected-disadvantage requirements.
- Metro Broadcasting, Inc. v. FCC, 497 U.S. 547: its racial-treatment analysis supplies no changed sex standard or federal-compliance immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm summary judgment for the University on the distinct Title IX and equal-protection claims. No reinstatement, damages or remand is ordered, and no broad section 1983 preclusion rule is adopted.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Crawford-El v. Britton

#### Crawford-El v. Britton

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 94-7203; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

**Decided:** 1997-06-16.

##### Ordinary proof governs unconstitutional motive in this retaliation claim

**Holding:** The ordinary preponderance standard governs proof of unconstitutional motive in this §1983 retaliation claim; neither qualified immunity nor litigation-burden concerns authorize a special clear-and-convincing requirement. Legally competent direct and circumstantial evidence remain available, and all actual constitutional elements and independent defenses must still be established or resolved under their applicable burdens.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, unanimous on all three propositions and their limits. His separate proposed immunity revision is not adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides the quantum Kimberlin reserved; it neither abolishes every specificity practice nor resolves Leatherman’s individual-officer pleading reservation beyond the barriers decided here. A motive allegation alone defeats no constitutional element, ordinary procedure or conduct-date immunity defense.

##### Necessary discovery cannot be barred by the rejected heightened proof requirement

**Holding:** A court may resolve a genuinely dispositive legal defense first and require specific, nonconclusory support, but it may not categorically demand proof satisfying the rejected heightened quantum before allowing discovery necessary to obtain that proof. Rule-authorized clarification, replies, relevant and proportionate discovery limits, sequencing, protective orders and ordinary summary-judgment duties remain available; no unrestricted discovery or particular deposition is required.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, unanimous on all three propositions and their limits. His separate proposed immunity revision is not adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The court may clarify allegations, resolve actual dispositive legal defenses and limit discovery to material issues. An adequately supported summary-judgment motion requires responsive evidence; a general credibility attack is insufficient. No fixed discovery plan, particular deposition, unrestricted inquiry, mandatory trial or new interlocutory appeal follows.

##### An actual same-decision showing remains distinct from a conceivable lawful explanation

**Holding:** A merely possible lawful explanation for an official's conduct does not itself establish a same-decision defense or make competent proof of unconstitutional motive inadmissible. Under Mt. Healthy, the claimant must establish protected conduct and its substantial or motivating causal role; the defendant may then prove by a preponderance that the same action would have occurred without that conduct. The separate clearly-established-law defense remains available on the law governing the conduct when it occurred.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, unanimous on all three propositions and their limits. His separate proposed immunity revision is not adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Protected communication, personal responsibility, actual motive, causation, injury and conduct-date immunity remain unresolved. Later Kimberlin and Settle doctrine supplies no notice in 1989. Prison-access limits remain and no unrestricted press-conference entitlement is recognized.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate only the heightened motive burden and dependent directions; preserve the existing remand otherwise. Resolved access-to-courts and due-process claims are not revived. The municipal issue retains its separate panel posture; the reinstated pendent conversion claim remains subject to ordinary jurisdiction and merits. No liability, immunity, damages or trial entitlement is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kimberlin v. Quinlan: applies the distinction between motive as a constitutional element and objective immunity, competent circumstantial proof and lawful case management; decides here the proof quantum Kimberlin left open.
- Harlow v. Fitzgerald: retains the objective clearly-established-law defense and early legal screening; it supplies no independent heightened evidentiary code.
- Anderson v. Creighton: retains particularized conduct-date notice and appropriately tailored discovery; no ultimate immunity determination is made.
- Leatherman v. Tarrant County: uses its source-based municipal pleading reasoning by analogy while preserving its express individual-officer reservation outside the specific categorical barriers resolved here.
- Siegert v. Gilley: preserves early examination of a cognizable constitutional deprivation without converting its threshold holding into a special motive burden.
- Anderson v. Liberty Lobby: applies a valid governing substantive burden at summary judgment, without authorizing courts to invent a new one.
- New York Times Co. v. Sullivan: distinguishes the constitutional speech basis of its defamation burden from this retaliation action against an official.
- Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle: applies the claimant's protected-conduct and motivating-factor burden and the defendant's separate preponderance same-decision defense.
- Settle v. Dickson County School Board: applies its actual-causation distinction, not school-specific substantive rules; a possible lawful reason is insufficient by itself.
- Pell v. Procunier and Turner v. Safley: preserve prison-access limitations and supply no unrestricted press-conference right.
- Johnson v. Jones and Behrens v. Pelletier: preserve the distinction between legal immunity review and disputed factual sufficiency; no new interlocutory-review right is created.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

#### Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 16, 1995.

##### Knowing, causally effective supervisory acquiescence in sexual abuse through school authority

**Holding:** A school supervisor violates a pupil's bodily-integrity right by actually knowing of sexual abuse through school authority or a substantial risk of it, deliberately and unreasonably disregarding the known danger despite authority and a reasonable opportunity to protect, and thereby causing injury or its continuation. A reasonable response is not actionable merely because injury occurs; awareness may be proved circumstantially, but warning signs do not compel it and what the official merely should have known is insufficient. The properly assumed Lankford facts describe this personal knowing acquiescence, for which preconduct law gave fair notice, so immunity does not bar this claim at this stage.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct votes.

**Material application:** The teacher's exploitation of official authority and access supplies the governmental connection although some acts occurred away from school. The alleged warnings and culpable nonresponse are assumed for legal review; earlier contact, later intercourse and continuing abuse remain distinct for causation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Negligent failure to discover misconduct, a reporting-law breach alone, supervisory office or vicarious liability does not suffice. No communicated approval, actual liability, damages, district liability, separate Title IX claim or additional equal-protection merits ruling is established. May 1987 authorities cannot give retroactive notice for earlier omissions.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm Lankford's immunity denial, 5–4, and return the surviving claim for proof of actual awareness, culpable inaction and attributable injury.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Ingraham supplies bodily security; Monell and Rizzo preserve personal attribution and causal linkage. Hafer confirms individual responsibility; Elder requires all relevant conduct-date law. Collins's voluntary-employment omissions do not decide knowing participation in deliberate bodily abuse. Farmer is an actual-awareness analogy, not retroactive notice. Albright supplies no controlling general rule displacing this claim. The earlier supervisory cases inform the specific notice inquiry without becoming interchangeable general duties; Jefferson and Lopez's later dates and distinct direct-action/supervisory settings limit their use.

##### Caplinger's immunity on the distinct particularized notice question

**Holding:** Caplinger receives qualified immunity because conduct-time law did not clearly establish that his particular supervisory conduct, on his own assumed information and responses, violated Doe's federal right. The Court neither decides that conduct's constitutional merits nor imputes Lankford's fuller knowledge to him.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III; all eight Associates join. Stone-Zsela concurs only in the judgment on a separate merits ground; that ground is not controlling.

**Material application:** Caplinger had limited later information and undertook inquiries, an interview at which Doe denied sex, and warnings; a promised wider meeting did not occur. These circumstances are distinct from Lankford's alleged knowing acquiescence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No constitutional approval of Caplinger's response, broad school-official immunity, mandatory merits-first sequence, or resolution of the conflicting January/February notice descriptions follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm Caplinger's immunity, unanimous in judgment. The legal/factual appellate boundary is separately recorded under Federal Courts, Doe, October 16, 1995, Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Elder and Hafer require individualized conduct-date examination. The May 1987 Jefferson and Lopez decisions neither supply earlier notice nor equate the superintendent's circumstances with the principal's.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Hodge v. Jones

#### Hodge v. Jones

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 93-1182; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 23, 1995.

##### Damages immunity for the preserved confidential-record retention claim

**Holding:** The individual officials have qualified immunity for the challenged 1989–1990 retention of the cleared, confidential child-protection investigation report because the asserted federal disclosure, expunction and procedural-treatment duties were not then clearly established. Whether the preserved conduct violated a constitutional or state-created substantive right remains unresolved.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I; all eight Associates join. Stone-Zsela agrees in the judgment on a noncontrolling merits ground.

**Material application:** The retained report recorded clearance, not parental dangerousness. The preserved claim establishes no removal, interference with a particular parental decision, public publication, benefit or employment loss, or revoked clearance. Statutory access by specified recipients was limited, not absolute secrecy.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal tangible-loss requirement, general authorization of confidential records, inaccurate entries or disclosure is adopted. The distinct erroneous AMF sexual-abuse code was not pleaded. The lower court's characterization of AMF as the statutory central registry is neither an enacted command nor resolved here. Constitutional informational privacy, any disputed substantive statutory entitlement, broader disclosure and fees remain open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm judgment for the individual defendants, unanimous in judgment. Prospective mootness is a distinct holding under Federal Courts, Hodge, October 23, 1995, Fourth Circuit No. 93-1182.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Elder governs particularized historical notice; Whalen concerns personal information and confidentiality; Paul separates reputation from protected deprivation; Roth and Olim separate substantive interests from procedure alone. Collins preserves the distinction between state duty and federal injury. Albright's fractured views impose no controlling confinement-or-monetary-loss rule. Doe's physical-abuse holding neither decides informational retention nor supplies retrospective notice.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — McMillian v. Monroe County

#### McMillian v. Monroe County

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-542.
**Decided:** June 2, 1997.

##### Final policymaking requires identification of the particular entity and function

**Holding:** Under Monell and Jett, the court identifies through state and local law, including custom or usage with legal force, the official who speaks with final policymaking authority for the particular governmental entity concerning the challenged action. Final operational discretion does not alone identify that entity; office labels, election, territory, funding and absence of commission control are each insufficient standing alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Ginsburg), Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Final operational discretion and the identity of the represented government are separate; County government need not consist solely of its commission.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Monell requires the institution's own policy. Pembaur preserves a single authorized final decision without treating four-Justice Part II-B as the whole Court's rationale. Praprotnik's plurality preserves legally effective custom, genuine delegation and ratification; Jett Part IV supplies the majority identification rule. Collins and Kinman separate deprivation and institutional attribution. Swint decided appellate jurisdiction; the later permitted §1292(b) route lawfully presents the merits.

##### The Alabama sheriff supplies County policy in this criminal-investigation function

**Holding:** An Alabama sheriff represents the County when setting and executing the law-enforcement policy involved in these criminal-investigation acts. The independently constituted final local office supplies that County authority without a delegation from, or power of review in, the county commission; the holding does not classify every sheriff function or every act as County policy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Material application:** The locally elected, independently final law-enforcement office, County salary and necessary equipment obligations together establish the local institution. State executive placement, immunity classifications, duties to prosecutors and courts, and §36-22-5 written-investigation directions do not displace that autonomous local authority here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No actual State instruction is assumed, and financing is not treated as investigative control. Other functions, including jails, other States, State damages liability and immunity require separate analysis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hereford and Parker v. Amerson inform Alabama structure but do not decide federal attribution. No all-functions rule follows from separate analogies to Parker v. Williams or Hess.

##### Reinstatement leaves violation, attributable policy and moving-force causation to proof

**Holding:** The County-attribution dismissal is reversed and the claim based on the sheriff's final law-enforcement policymaking is reinstated for further proceedings. McMillian must still establish a federal violation and an attributable County policy or final decision that was the moving force behind his injury; the sheriff's institutional status establishes neither the allegations' truth nor liability for every act.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Parts II–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the County-attribution dismissal and reinstate the policy claim without a liability finding, damages or compulsory trial. Personal claims and immunity and unreviewed evidentiary rulings remain in their own posture.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Collins retains the separate deprivation and institutional inquiries; Kinman's single-final-decision route makes the claim legally possible without guaranteeing recovery.

## Source: state/holdings/civil-rights.md — Gilbert v. Homar

#### Gilbert v. Homar

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-651.

**Decided:** 1997-06-09.

##### Independent felony arrest can justify this brief initial unpaid suspension

**Holding:** Assuming the protected property interest for this case, due process permits the initial brief unpaid suspension of a police officer from sensitive duties following an independent felony arrest and formal charges when the Mathews balance supports immediate provisional action and prompt further process remains required. The Constitution does not impose an invariably required advance hearing, continued pay, or a separate fiscal-emergency showing for that limited action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's initial-suspension portion, joined by all Justices except Stone-Zsela; eight direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The protected property interest is assumed. The holding depends on the contextual balance, not guilt, a finding that state law compelled suspension, or permission for indefinite punishment. It approves neither every unpaid suspension nor the entire 23-day period.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the advance-process requirement on this record, 8–1; no blanket restoration of summary judgment for the officials.

##### Continued nonpayment requires separately justified, prompt and meaningful process

**Holding:** Justification for immediate suspension does not independently justify its continued duration: the employee must receive a prompt, informed opportunity to contest the employment consequence, assessed in light of the continuing loss, the reasons for delay, and the reliability of the remaining grounds. When criminal charges are dismissed, continued deprivation must be evaluated on the employer's actual employment grounds rather than indefinite reliance on the original arrest; no fixed numerical deadline or automatic reinstatement rule is adopted.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's continuing-process portion, unanimous.

**Material application:** Dismissal of the charges changed the original basis for nonpayment without resolving employment misconduct. Later receipt of a report could supply a different basis, but disputed attributed admissions and withheld information require an assessment of meaningful response and justified delay.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A meeting does not itself establish adequate process; eventual access to the report does not validate earlier delay. Backpay may affect relief but cannot retroactively supply process. No fixed deadline, automatic reinstatement, invariably required evidentiary trial, final-demotion ruling, immunity ruling, damages award or substantive-due-process or stigma determination follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** All nine require remand to assess the grounds for continued deprivation, reasons for delay, and adequacy of the response opportunity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Mathews v. Eldridge:** Applied to the initial wage loss and, separately, to the continuing deprivation; neither wages nor government interests are excluded from the balance.
- **Cleveland Board of Education v. Loudermill:** Its ordinary response-opportunity requirement remains effective. Its paid-suspension option is not treated as an absolute command governing every provisional suspension.
- **FDIC v. Mallen:** Applied for independently supported urgent provisional action and separate scrutiny of subsequent delay; its banking context and timetable are not an employment safe harbor.
- **Barry v. Barchi:** Applied to require prompt and effective review after justified initial action.
- **Young v. Harper:** Applied for the distinction between provisional action and the continuing deprivation; parole-specific procedures are not transferred to employment.

## Source: state/holdings/constitutional-structure.md — Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., 511 U.S. 658

#### Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., 511 U.S. 658

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-609.

**Decided:** 1994-05-23.

##### Bounded statutory reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals

**Holding:** Article III permits §27A(b)'s expressly retrospective requirement to reinstate a private §10(b) action commenced on or before June 19, 1991, dismissed as time barred after June 19, 1991, and timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as that law existed on June 19, 1991, upon a motion filed within sixty days after the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment. The federal court must independently determine whether those cumulative conditions are satisfied and, if they are, must reinstate the action without a discretionary balancing test, retaining independent responsibility for the claims, defenses and remedies; this bounded entitlement to adjudication neither directs a merits judgment nor establishes an unrestricted power to reopen completed federal judgments.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. These five direct joins control the rule, explanation, qualifications and remedy; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Material application:** Pacific Mutual's January 31, 1992 motion met the sixty-day deadline; that fact is not a finding of fraud or entitlement to a merits judgment.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: extends its distinction between changing governing law and prescribing adjudication to this bounded reinstatement procedure; resolves the final-judgment question left open there only within the present limits.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128 (1872): applies its protection of independent adjudication; a new eligibility rule requiring renewed adjudication does not supply a legislative finding or dictate securities liability.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102 (1898): preserves protection of completed judicial determinations while distinguishing this new statutory entitlement to litigate claims dismissed on limitations grounds from legislative contradiction of adjudicated facts or liability.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421 (1856): distinguishes a change in the continuing operation of prospective relief; its setting does not itself resolve reinstatement of this completed dismissal.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371 (1980): distinguishes waiver of the Government's own defense; retained judicial adjudication is relevant, but no governmental waiver of a private defendant's defense is assumed here.
- Lampf, Pleva, Lipkind, Prupis & Petigrow v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S. 350 (1991): identifies the intervening limitations rule to which Congress responded; §27A(b) supplies the bounded statutory remedy without deciding the underlying fraud claims.
- James B. Beam Distilling Co. v. Georgia, 501 U.S. 529 (1991), and Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, June 18, 1993: retain the distinction between judicial retroactivity in still-open litigation and final judgments; the present entitlement arises from an express statute, not judicial retroactivity alone.
- Landgraf v. USI Film Products and Rivers v. Roadway Express, April 26, 1994: preserve the separation between express legislative temporal direction and constitutional limits; no ordinary presumption against retroactivity defeats §27A(b)'s explicit reach.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304 (1945): preserves its due-process treatment of revived limitations claims without treating that decision as dispositive of Article III.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may not declare a completed judicial factual finding erroneous and compel the contrary finding, dictate a merits judgment, or disguise an individualized award or assessment of liability as a procedural reopening statute. This holding does not decide compelled reopening of merits judgments or individualized legislative commands to adjudicate liability. The Court announces no independent due-process holding and does not decide §27A(a), general severability, fraud, liability, damages or entitlement to judgment on the securities merits. Pending litigation, prospective relief and independently authorized judicial reopening remain distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit's judgment sustaining the reinstatement procedure is affirmed. Proceedings may continue under §27A(b). The federal court must determine the statutory prerequisites and reinstate the action when they are proved; it has no discretion to deny reinstatement through a balancing test. The securities claims, defenses and remedies remain for ordinary judicial adjudication. No fraud finding, liability judgment or damages award is entered.

**Material judgment:** Affirmed, 5–3. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg support affirmance; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent. O'Connor does not participate.

## Source: state/holdings/constitutional-structure.md — Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.

#### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1121.
**Decided:** April 18, 1995.

##### Section 27A(b) permits mandatory reinstatement of this completed limitations dismissal

**Holding:** Under Morgan Stanley, Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement under §27A(b) of a qualifying private §10(b) action even though its limitations dismissal became final without appeal before the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment: the action must have commenced on or before June 19, 1991, been dismissed as time barred after that date, and been timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991, with a reinstatement motion filed within sixty days after enactment. The court independently determines those cumulative conditions and, when they are satisfied, must reinstate without discretionary balancing, retaining independent responsibility for all claims, defenses, and remedies; this expressly retrospective entitlement does not authorize legislative findings, dictated liability, or unrestricted reopening of completed merits judgments.

**Proposition-level authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Those five direct rationale joins control every proposition, qualification, and remedy stated here. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas dissent; no combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Material application:** A narrowly defined, exclusively retrospective class does not by itself turn this procedure into a legislative judgment: the statute covers every action meeting its legal criteria and selects no parties for legislatively determined liability. The statutory conditions are satisfied, including the timely February 11, 1992 motion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994: applies its five-Justice Article III holding sustaining §27A(b)'s bounded mandatory reinstatement procedure to this qualifying completed private limitations dismissal; preserves independent eligibility and merits adjudication and all constitutional limits.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: applies its distinction between genuine legislative change and prescribed judicial adjudication; Morgan Stanley supplies the completed-dismissal rule that Robertson did not decide.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128: applies its protection of the independent judicial function; a class-defined entitlement to renewed adjudication does not dictate findings or securities liability.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102: preserves protection of completed judicial determinations while distinguishing the statutory entitlement to litigate a limitations-dismissed claim from a legislative declaration of facts or liability contrary to an adjudication.
- James B. Beam Distilling Co. v. Georgia, 501 U.S. 529: preserves finality against reopening by later judicial doctrine alone; the entitlement here comes from an express statute whose Article III validity Morgan Stanley establishes.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421: distinguishes the continuing operation of prospective equitable relief; that setting does not itself decide this completed limitations dismissal.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371: distinguishes the Government's waiver of its own defense; no governmental waiver of a private defendant's defense supplies the holding here.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304: leaves its due-process revival rule intact without treating it as a controlling answer to Article III.
- Lampf, Pleva, Lipkind, Prupis & Petigrow v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S. 350: identifies the limitations rule that produced the dismissal and the statutory response; §27A(b) supplies reinstatement without establishing the securities merits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may not command findings contrary to the record, impose liability on identified defendants by legislative declaration, or disguise an individualized award as a procedural reopening law. The Court decides no general power to set aside completed judgments adjudicating the underlying merits, no constitutionality of every retrospective statute, and no independent due-process question; applicable due-process protections and other constitutional restrictions remain.
