Section 27A(a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, federal waiver, continuing prospective relief, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The decision neither adjudicates other parties' completed cases nor creates criminal or innocence remedies. Fraud, reliance, causation, liability, damages, and ordinary merits defenses remain for the courts; reinstatement establishes none of them.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment of the Sixth Circuit is reversed. The case is remanded for reinstatement under the satisfied conditions of §27A(b) and further judicial proceedings on the underlying securities claims. Reinstatement is required, not a discretionary remedy. The courts retain responsibility for the merits, defenses, and any relief; no merits outcome is prescribed.

**Material judgment:** Reversed and remanded, 5–4. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer support reversal; O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent.

## Source: state/holdings/constitutional-structure.md — Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger

#### Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-851.
**Decided:** June 5, 1995.

##### Congressional agents may not control the legal effectiveness of airport decisions

**Holding:** Congress may advise, oversee, obtain reports and enact a fixed waiting period, but may not vest a congressional agent with selective, legally operative control over execution outside the constitutional allocation of power. The revised Board is such an agent and its ability to trigger an additional restraint on covered Authority actions makes the challenged control provisions unconstitutional; mere influence, nominations considered alone, consultation, reporting and nonvoting attendance are not independently invalidated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; nine Justices adopt the congressional-agency-plus-operative-control ground. Stone's independent Article I ground is not an additional Court holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Appointments Clause, incompatibility and ineligibility grounds remain undecided. The holding requires congressional agency plus selective legally operative control; it does not invalidate mere nominations, consultation, reporting or nonvoting attendance.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Citizens for the Abatement of Aircraft Noise, 501 U.S. 252, is applied to the revised agency and retained operative control. Bowsher v. Synar is applied to congressional execution. Buckley v. Valeo supplies constitutional accountability for governmental power without independently deciding appointments here. INS v. Chadha preserves bicameralism and presentment for responsive congressional prohibition; no broader independent Article I holding is adopted.

##### The enacted contingency limits the remedy

**Holding:** When a judicial order prevents the Board from carrying out its statutory functions, 49 U.S.C.App. §2456(h) withdraws the Authority’s power thereafter to perform actions required to be submitted to that Board. The restraint is affirmed subject to that enacted consequence: unaffected functions, the lease and compact survive, past Board actions are not automatically invalidated, and severance cannot authorize prohibited covered actions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; all nine adopt the precise statutory remedial limit.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The enacted covered-action categories and timing, selection and vacancy conditions set out below define the restraint's scope. Particular retrospective reliance and finality disputes are not resolved; no entire-compact invalidation or authorization of prohibited covered actions follows.

Members must have aviation and airport-user experience, frequently use the metropolitan airports and be registered voters outside Virginia, Maryland and the District of Columbia. The Authority appoints from mandatory Speaker and President-pro-tempore lists, may request additional names but cannot select outside them, and may remove a member for cause by a two-thirds directors’ vote. Covered actions include adoption or amendment of an annual budget; bond authorization and an annual bond plan or its amendments; adoption, amendment or repeal of a regulation; adoption or revision of a master plan; appointment of the chief executive; award of a directors-approved contract; approval or modification of terminal design or airport layout; and authorization to acquire or dispose of land or grant a long-term easement. The recommendation period is the longer of thirty calendar days or ten legislative days. Silence through that period permits immediate effectiveness on expiration; earlier notice that no recommendation will be made permits immediate effectiveness then. Acceptance permits the proposed action with the recommended changes; rejection triggers sixty legislative days of review. Legislative days exclude Saturdays, Sundays, holidays, and days neither House sits because of sine-die adjournment or recess or adjournment longer than three days. Congressional prohibition must proceed by constitutional lawmaking. No general urgent-and-compelling-circumstances override exists. More than four vacancies after congressional lists have been supplied independently disables covered actions under §2456(f)(2)(D). Those statutory branches do not eliminate the selective control or the §2456(h) remedial consequence.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Citizens for the Abatement of Aircraft Noise is applied to the constitutional defect, not extended into permission to ignore §2456(h). The earlier FEC v. NRA jurisdictional disposition supplies no merits or severability rule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the restraint on the challenged control with section 2456(h)'s consequence for covered actions, preserving unaffected functions, the lease and compact and no automatic invalidation of past acts. The October 24, 1994 stay operates through the applicable mandate machinery: the June 5 final Supreme Court disposition satisfies its applicable branch; March 31, 1995 was not an earlier automatic expiry while that branch remained pending. The sixty-day-after-certiorari-denial branch does not govern this merits disposition. No new transition stay is imposed.

## Source: state/holdings/constitutional-structure.md — Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748

#### Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1966.
**Decided:** June 3, 1996.

##### Did Congress authorize the challenged presidential limits on military capital murder punishment?

**Holding:** UCMJ Articles 18 and 56 authorize the President to restrict the availability of the death punishment Congress selected for Article 118(1) and (4); Article 36 supplies additional statutory confirmation and procedural authority. Together they authorize the challenged RCM 1004 restrictions, but not new capital offenses, death under Article 118(2) or (3), or punishment exceeding or contradicting Congress's choice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Holding I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (eight Justices). Thomas concurs in the judgment only and supplies no join to this or the following three holdings.

**Material application:** Withholding death from part of an already capital class is a punishment limit under Articles 18 and 56 even though it is not a numerical maximum. Congress defined the capital murders and death-or-life punishment; these factors neither add an offense nor enlarge its penalty. Article 36 supports implementing procedures: generally recognized federal criminal-trial principles apply so far as the President considers practicable; regulations may neither contradict nor be inconsistent with the Code, must be uniform insofar as practicable, and must be reported to Congress. Calling a factor procedural does not independently establish substantive authority.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Whether Article 36 alone would suffice remains undecided. The assignment permits neither creation of civilian crimes nor vague or otherwise unconstitutional capital factors. The decision uses the governing pre-1990 Article 36 and pre-1991 RCM 1004; it determines no actual transmission or reporting violation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Neal v. United States*, 516 U.S. 284, retains the boundary between statutory punishment and valid delegated sentencing rules: these restrictions implement rather than replace Congress's punishment choice.

##### Must Congress additionally name capital aggravating factors expressly to authorize these restrictions?

**Holding:** The enacted punishment-limiting authority suffices without an additional formula expressly naming capital aggravating factors. Article 106a's later express espionage provisions neither withdraw that authority nor make it inapplicable to these murder offenses.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Holding II; the same eight Justices directly join.

**Material application:** Articles 18 and 56 expressly assign punishment limitations. More explicit treatment of a distinct offense in Article 106a, including its additional presidential factor under Article 36, does not repeal that assignment. Existing authorization also did not expire when additional narrowing became constitutionally necessary on the assumption used in this case; an otherwise authorized use remains within the grant when a later constitutional requirement makes it necessary.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Actual enacted authority remains essential. Executive practice, congressional silence and usefulness cannot substitute for it. The Court resolves no general criminal-law clear-statement question beyond the challenged assignment.

##### Is this military capital-sentencing assignment constitutionally nondelegable or inadequately bounded?

**Holding:** Congress may assign the President identification of narrowing factors for military capital sentencing when Congress defines the capital offenses and punishment, supplies intelligible legal boundaries, and confines implementation to the President's assigned military-administration role. Congress's offense selection, death-or-life alternatives, express punishment-limiting assignment and consistency restraints adequately bound this delegation even assuming that criminal and capital delegations require greater specificity than the ordinary standard.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Holding III; the same eight Justices directly join.

**Material application:** For this authority question, the Court assumes that these military murders require additional eligibility narrowing and that the three factors would adequately narrow if Congress enacted them. Neither Article I's military rulemaking clause nor the assumed capital requirement categorically requires Congress personally to prescribe each limiting factor. The President's executive and Commander-in-Chief responsibilities reinforce the assigned military role without enlarging statutory authority. The enacted limits and that actual role suffice without an additional express factor-selection principle; executive safeguards or panel unanimity could not cure absent congressional authority.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide whether criminal delegations generally require enhanced specificity, whether additional eligibility narrowing is indispensable throughout military capital proceedings, whether Article 118 alone supplies all necessary narrowing, or whether independent Article II authority could support factors without congressional delegation. No rule for newly created actors, unrelated executive functions, wholesale civilian capital-doctrine incorporation, or military immunity from structural review follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Mistretta v. United States*, 488 U.S. 361, supplies guided sentencing implementation with Congress retaining legislative responsibility; its conditional capital discussion did not decide these factors. *Touby v. United States*, 500 U.S. 160, supplies review of an actual criminal delegation without resolving its open general specificity question; its drug-scheduling criteria are not a universal checklist. *Weiss v. United States*, 510 U.S. 163, and *Ryder v. United States*, 515 U.S. 177, retain constitutional review of military adjudicator arrangements and their respective appointment, tenure and defect-specific remedial limits.

##### What relief follows from the rejection of the presented authority challenges?

**Holding:** The statutory-authorization, additional-clear-authorization, categorical-nondelegability and inadequate-guidance objections establish no error and furnish no ground to disturb the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces' judgment. The affirmance rests on that absence of error, without harmless-error review or adjudication of unpresented individual-factor and collateral claims.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Holding IV; the same eight Justices directly join. Thomas separately supports the judgment alone.

**Material application:** The panel found premeditated murder during robbery, another Article 118 murder in the same case, and actual perpetration of the felony-murder killing under the authorized rule. Sustaining the rule's source does not independently adjudicate every factor's validity, every sentencing safeguard, or every underlying conviction claim. Congress's offense boundaries constrain delegation without necessarily satisfying the separate assumed eligibility-narrowing requirement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The constitutional need for a service connection in capital military offenses remains open; no new prerequisite is imposed. Survival of an aggravator does not replace correction of a separately established injury.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The convictions and sentence remain undisturbed against these authority challenges; no resentencing, replacement life sentence, new guilt trial, acquittal, release or execution is ordered. Article 71 independently requires a final judgment as to legality through applicable military and Supreme Court review routes where review has not been waived or withdrawn. Death cannot be executed without presidential approval; the President may commute or remit it but may not suspend it. Article 71(c)(2)'s separate waiver-or-withdrawal route concerns dismissal or punitive discharge, and subsection (d)'s suspension power excludes death. The Court supplies no presidential approval, execution date or determination that every execution prerequisite is complete.

**Material judgment:** Affirmed, 9–0; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer support the judgment. The common rationale has eight direct supporters; Thomas's judgment vote is not a ninth rationale join.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Arave v. Creech* retains genuine eligibility narrowing and the distinction between factor formulation and application. *Tuilaepa and Proctor* retain bounded individualized selection after unchallenged lawful eligibility, without enlarging partial joins. *Graham v. Collins* and *Johnson v. Texas* retain effective consideration of supported independent culpability mitigation. *Stringer v. Black*, *Sochor v. Florida* and *Espinosa v. Florida* retain their distinct established-weighing-error and adequate-cure requirements. *Tuggle v. Netherland* retains the independent-assistance-claim rule and unresolved error-effect standard. None establishes a delegation defect or harmlessness shortcut here. *Solorio v. United States*, 483 U.S. 435, remains a noncapital jurisdiction decision and does not resolve capital service connection.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Soldal v. Cook County, 506 U.S. 56 (1992)

#### Soldal v. Cook County, 506 U.S. 56 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-6516.

**Decided:** December 8, 1992.

##### A seizure of a home protects possession independently of privacy

**Holding:** Meaningful government-attributable interference with possession of a home is a seizure under the Fourth Amendment even without a search, invasion of privacy or liberty, or criminal investigation. The family’s alleged forcible dispossession meets that threshold, but coverage alone does not establish unreasonableness or every element of §1983 liability.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; nine Justices adopt the same rule.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Jacobsen, 466 U.S. 109 — applied; possession and privacy remain independently protected interests.
- United States v. Place, 462 U.S. 696 — applied; seizure scrutiny does not require an accompanying search.
- Arizona v. Hicks, 480 U.S. 321 — applied; plain-view seizure remains subject to the Fourth Amendment.
- Adickes v. S. H. Kress & Co., 398 U.S. 144 — applied to the summary-judgment joint-action inference, without a final conspiracy finding.
- Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658 — preserved; seizure coverage does not establish municipal liability.
- Wyatt v. Cole, May 18, 1992 — preserved; attribution does not settle immunity or a private merits defense.

##### A due-process characterization does not displace the specific seizure claim

**Holding:** A court may not reject an otherwise applicable Fourth Amendment claim merely because the same conduct also concerns a deprivation of property under the Due Process Clause. Each applicable constitutional protection must be examined on its own terms; this disposition does not adjudicate the family’s separate substantive-due-process claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Graham v. Connor, 490 U.S. 386 — applied; the specific Fourth Amendment source governs its covered conduct and is not displaced by a dominant-character theory.
- Parratt v. Taylor, 451 U.S. 527 — distinguished; postdeprivation procedural remedies do not answer the distinct seizure claim.
- Hudson v. Palmer, 468 U.S. 517 — confined to its prison setting for the relevant coverage proposition; no rule excluding possession of a home follows.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Private action alone is insufficient. Ultimate reasonableness, municipal policy, causation, immunity, damages and the separate substantive-due-process claim remain undecided. The holding addresses a home, without deciding coverage of every property category.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse adverse summary judgment and remand the seizure claim; no final finding of unreasonableness or award of damages.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — United States v. Dixon, 509 U.S. 688 (1993)

#### United States v. Dixon, 509 U.S. 688 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1231.
**Decided:** June 28, 1993.

##### Nonsummary punitive contempt triggers double-jeopardy protection

**Holding:** A completed nonsummary criminal-contempt prosecution places the accused in criminal jeopardy; private initiation and an order’s protective purpose do not categorically remove that protection. Whether a later prosecution is for the same offense remains a separate inquiry; coercive civil sanctions, summary courtroom contempt and separate sovereigns are not decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bloom v. Illinois, 391 U.S. 194: criminal character of punitive contempt applied.
- Gompers v. Bucks Stove & Range Co., 221 U.S. 418: punitive/coercive distinction applied.
- Young v. United States ex rel. Vuitton, 481 U.S. 787: independent judicial enforcement authority retained, not a categorical exception from criminal protection.

##### Previously prosecuted necessary offenses cannot be tried again

**Holding:** When a criminal-contempt adjudication necessarily prosecutes the substantive offense forbidden by the order, the Government cannot prosecute that same substantive offense again. Dixon’s drug offense and Foster I’s November assault were necessarily prosecuted in the respective contempts and are barred; this shared proposition does not supply a common analysis of Foster II–V.

**Proposition-level authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brown v. Ohio, 432 U.S. 161: successive greater/included-offense protection applied.
- Harris v. Oklahoma, 433 U.S. 682: necessary substantive offense cannot be erased by an abstract description of the first charge.
- Blockburger v. United States, 284 U.S. 299: elements inquiry retained; disagreement over its broader application separately recorded.

##### Grady remains governing successive-prosecution law

**Holding:** Grady is not overruled: beyond Blockburger’s elements protection, a later prosecution is barred when establishing an essential element requires proof of conduct constituting an offense for which the defendant has already been prosecuted. Mere reuse of evidence or a common episode does not suffice, and Felix’s independently punishable conspiracy holding remains; no five-Justice rationale determines Grady’s application to Foster II–V in this fractured decision.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Grady survives; evidentiary overlap and a common episode do not alone establish a bar. Summary contempt, coercive civil sanctions and separate sovereigns are not decided. Foster II–V have no controlling rationale, as stated separately below.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the bar for Dixon’s drug charge and Foster I (6–3), leaving the contempt judgments intact. Reverse the bar for Foster II–V and remand (5–4); threat charges remain subject to a properly established Ashe bar from actual acquittals and other defenses. Foster V may proceed without authorizing a barred simple-assault conviction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Grady v. Corbin, 495 U.S. 508: expressly retained; no overruling by this decision.
- United States v. Felix, March 25, 1992: conspiracy and evidentiary-overlap distinctions preserved, not recast as an overruling.
- Illinois v. Vitale, 447 U.S. 410: successive-prosecution concern beyond formal elements supports retention.
- Brown v. Ohio, 432 U.S. 161: finality and greater/included-offense protection retained.
- Ashe v. Swenson, 397 U.S. 436: independent issue-preclusion rule preserved for the remanded threat charges.

##### Whether Foster II–V may proceed under a common double-jeopardy rationale

**Authority of the judgment:** Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reverse the bars to Foster II–V and remand, 5–4. No controlling rationale exists under Marks: the four-Justice elements-only rationale would overrule Grady, while Blackmun retains Grady but treats judicial-order enforcement differently. Those incompatible grounds are not logical subsets. The judgment permits these counts without creating a general contempt exception or displacing the separately majority-supported retention of Grady.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Foster II–IV remain subject to any actual-acquittal issue preclusion established under Ashe and other preserved defenses. Foster V’s distinct assault-with-intent-to-kill charge may proceed; the judgment does not authorize a barred simple-assault conviction or disturb the contempt dispositions.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Caspari v. Bohlen, 510 U.S. 383

#### Caspari v. Bohlen, 510 U.S. 383

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1500.

**Decided:** 1994-02-23.

##### The preserved nonretroactivity defense is fairly included here

**Holding:** In this habeas case, Missouri's consistently asserted Teague defense is fairly included as a subsidiary limit on applying the very constitutional rule and granting the writ challenged by the petition, although the stated question names the double-jeopardy issue rather than Teague. The Court therefore considers that nonjurisdictional defense; preservation, disclosure in the petition's body or logical priority alone does not make every independent omitted question reviewable.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices. Stevens disagrees with fair inclusion and would decline the defense under Rule 14.1(a).

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Yee v. City of Escondido: applied to distinguish subsidiary legal arguments from distinct questions; preservation and fair inclusion remain separate requirements.
- Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp.: preserved; an omitted independent question does not become reviewable merely through notice, logical priority or ordinary economy. This confined same-rule habeas limitation is distinguished from its separate intervention determination.
- Schiro v. Farley: distinguished on presentation; the defense there first appeared at merits briefing after earlier omissions. Its discretionary treatment of a nonjurisdictional defense remains intact.

##### The requested extension was a new rule at finality

**Holding:** Bohlen's conviction and sentence became final on January 2, 1986, and the rule forbidding renewed proof of persistent-offender status in this noncapital proceeding was not dictated by the precedent then in force. Federal habeas relief therefore depends on a recognized Teague exception; the Court does not decide whether the proposed double-jeopardy protection should govern a case eligible for a decision on the constitutional merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt the finality-specific new-rule determination, not a prospective rule permitting every noncapital sentencing rehearing.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Teague v. Lane: applied to the date at which the state judgment became final and the distinction between dictated applications and new rules.
- Graham v. Collins: applied in its controlling form; a dictated application does not become new merely because the facts differ or courts disagree.
- Gilmore v. Taylor: applied to preserve the possibility of a genuinely new noncapital extension after substantive comparison.
- Bullington v. Missouri and Arizona v. Rumsey: preserved for acquittal-like capital adjudications; their extension to this noncapital status proceeding was not dictated at finality, and their ultimate reach is not decided.
- United States v. DiFrancesco: applied as prefinality support for distinguishing sentencing determinations from offense acquittals.
- Griffith v. Kentucky: used for the finality convention; direct review was exhausted and the certiorari period expired on January 2, with January 1 a legal holiday.
- United States v. Dixon: its retention of Grady remains in force but does not answer whether this sentencing-status proceeding has the necessary jeopardy character.

##### Neither Teague exception permits this writ

**Holding:** The proposed rule falls within neither the exception for rules excluding conduct or a class of persons or punishments from the State's substantive power, nor the exception for watershed procedures essential to accuracy and fundamental fairness. Its protection against renewed litigation of offender status does not remove the robberies or these authorized sentences from lawful punishment and does not supply a watershed accuracy procedure; the habeas grant on this new rule must therefore be reversed.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices apply both exceptions as they exist at this decision's date. Neither exception is eliminated or prospectively narrowed out of existence.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Teague v. Lane: applies both contemporary exceptions after finding a new rule; neither is abolished.
- Penry v. Lynaugh: applies the substantive exception's protection against punishment beyond the State's constitutional power; this claim does not satisfy it.
- Sawyer v. Smith: applies the distinction between an important procedural protection and the exceptional accuracy-and-fairness requirement.
- Bullington v. Missouri: preserved; refusing retroactive extension does not overrule its established capital-acquittal protection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Both contemporary Teague exceptions remain. The State's consistently presented defense is subsidiary here; preservation, mention in the petition body and logical priority do not automatically make an omitted independent question reviewable. The ultimate constitutional reach of double jeopardy to the noncapital status hearing is not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Eighth Circuit's conditional habeas grant on this claim is reversed, 8–1, under Teague. No new State proof opportunity, replacement sentence or release deadline is prescribed, and unrelated properly preserved claims are not disturbed.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Witte v. United States

#### Witte v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-6187.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

For the other questions in Witte v. United States, decided June 14, 1995, No. 94-6187. see Criminal Procedure — Double jeopardy and relevant-conduct sentencing (Relevant conduct is not a prior cocaine prosecution or punishment here).

##### Future sentence coordination remains conditional and separate

**Holding:** The possibility of an overlapping later sentence does not itself bar this prosecution, and any sentence following conviction must satisfy the then-applicable coordination provisions and independent constitutional limits. Under received § 5G1.3, subsection (a) first requires the instant sentence to run consecutively to the undischarged term if the instant offense occurred during service of that term, including work release, furlough or escape, or after sentencing for that term but before service began; only if (a) does not apply does subsection (b) require concurrency when the earlier offenses were fully taken into account in the instant offense level; in all remaining cases, policy statement (c) requires consecutiveness to the extent necessary for reasonable incremental punishment, with the commentary's approximation task, not an automatic total or repeated departure.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, conditional coordination discussion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six support this statutory and remedial proposition. Stevens still dissents from allowing the prosecution, and Kennedy does not join the detailed coordination discussion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Coordination requires an actual conviction, actual undischarged term, relevant-conduct calculation and then-effective Guidelines. Lawful departure authority may address unusual circumstances, including substantial-assistance effects, but permits rather than commands repetition of the earlier departure. Coordination cannot cure a valid successive-prosecution bar.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Harris — distinguished from successive prosecution; its one-prosecution authorization rule remains unchanged.
- Felix, Dixon and Grady — preserved; statutory sentence coordination cannot cure a valid prior-prosecution bar.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Fifth Circuit reversal of pretrial dismissal, permitting the cocaine indictment to proceed without finding guilt or fixing a sentence. The prior marijuana-attempt judgment remains intact. No 292-month later sentence, time-served finding, credit award, repeated substantial-assistance departure or 144-month aggregate cap is imposed.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Witte v. United States

#### Witte v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-6187.
**Decided:** June 14, 1995.

For the other questions in Witte v. United States, decided June 14, 1995, No. 94-6187. see Criminal Procedure — Coordination of sentences for related conduct (Future sentence coordination remains conditional and separate).

##### Relevant conduct is not a prior cocaine prosecution or punishment here

**Holding:** Consideration of relevant uncharged conduct in selecting a sentence within the authorized statutory range for the offense of conviction does not itself constitute prosecution or independent punishment for the uncharged offense. Witte's marijuana-attempt sentence therefore does not bar the distinct cocaine indictment under the retained Grady rule; this holding does not approve a separate disguised penalty, punishment beyond statutory authority or disregard of independent sentencing safeguards.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, constitutional-characterization discussion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; six support this rationale. Scalia and Thomas affirm only in the judgment on the narrower distinct-prosecution ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The prior judgment imposed 144 months after substantial assistance within the five-to-forty-year statutory range; 292–365 months was the Guidelines calculation. Actual cocaine aggravation does not make that judgment an independent cocaine penalty. No acquitted-conduct rule, statutory-maximum increase rule, complete sentencing-jury test or same-transaction bar is adopted. The plea-agreement contention remains outside review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Williams v. Oklahoma, 358 U.S. 576 — applied to consideration of other conduct when fixing lawful offense-of-conviction punishment.
- McMillan v. Pennsylvania, 477 U.S. 79 — applied within statutory authority; independent due-process and jury limits remain.
- United States v. Felix — applied by analogy for the difference between use of conduct and prior prosecution; no same-evidence bar is created.
- United States v. Dixon and Grady v. Corbin — expressly preserved; retained essential-conduct protection has no prior-cocaine-prosecution predicate here.
- Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch — distinguished as a distinct punitive exaction under its combined-operation analysis, not overruled.
- United States v. R.L.C. — preserved: statutory sentencing authority is not replaced by the Guidelines range.
- United States v. Harris — confined to authorized cumulative punishment in one prosecution; it does not cure a valid successive-prosecution bar.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Fifth Circuit reversal of pretrial dismissal, permitting the cocaine indictment to proceed without finding guilt or fixing a sentence. The prior marijuana-attempt judgment remains intact. No 292-month later sentence, time-served finding, credit award, repeated substantial-assistance departure or 144-month aggregate cap is imposed.

**Later-authority backlinks:** United States v. Watts / United States v. Putra, January 6, 1997, No. 95-1906: resolves the reserved acquitted-conduct question within conviction-authorized sentencing, preserving independent sentencing limits and actual successive-prosecution protection.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690

#### Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690

**Docket or dockets:** 95-5257; Seventh Circuit Nos. 94-3349 and 94-3350
**Decided:** May 28, 1996

##### Which suppression questions remain preserved for appellate review through these conditional pleas?

**Holding:** Review encompasses both ultimate reasonable suspicion for the initial stop and ultimate probable cause for the warrantless search, as preserved in these conditional pleas. Agreement between the parties about a proposed review standard neither determines that legal standard nor expands review to unpreserved discovery or suppression-merits issues.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Part I, joined by all eight other Justices; unanimous controlling rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Rule 11(a)(2) permits a written reservation of specified adverse pretrial determinations with the court's approval and the Government's consent. The selected orders establish preservation of both thresholds here; incompleteness of other documents does not establish litigant default. The Court reviews the allocation of appellate responsibility, not a newly presented database-discovery question or the ultimate suppression outcome.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Success in obtaining the proper review standard does not itself establish a right to withdraw the pleas. The ordinary requirement of prevailing on the reserved issue remains.

##### How must appellate courts review reasonable suspicion and probable cause for a warrantless search?

**Holding:** Appellate courts independently determine the ultimate constitutional sufficiency of reasonable suspicion for a stop and probable cause for a warrantless search under the objective totality of the circumstances. Historical facts, credibility determinations and genuinely factual subsidiary inferences receive clear-error protection, while grounded contextual inferences drawn by resident judges and experienced officers receive genuine due weight without deference to the ultimate constitutional conclusion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Parts II-III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight-Justice majority. Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** Independent review develops workable constitutional standards and permits comparison of recurring circumstances. The reviewing court must nevertheless respect what occurred, the trial court's credibility role, local context and grounded police experience. Miller v. Fenton and Thompson v. Keohane support allocating mixed questions by function rather than a single label; First Options preserves separate legal and factual review. Brinegar, Gates, Sokolow and White supply the substantive totality inquiry but do not decide who finally applies it on appeal. Gates's substantial-basis review of a magistrate's warrant decision concerns a different institutional task. Mere acknowledgment of an officer's inference is insufficient due weight, but experience cannot manufacture an unproved fact or entitle the officer's ultimate conclusion to a presumption of correctness.

**Limits and questions not reached:** There is no numerical weighting formula, intermediate standard, new evidentiary fact or police-correctness presumption. Pierce and Cooter & Gell retain deference for their task-specific discretionary determinations; Salve Regina's treatment of legal review remains. The Court imports neither habeas burdens from Thompson nor a general solution to every mixed question; Markman's reserved appellate issue remains open.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**
- Miller v. Fenton and Thompson v. Keohane supply functional allocation of legal and factual review; Thompson's habeas setting does not transfer its burdens here.
- First Options preserves independent legal review and protection of historical findings.
- Brinegar, Gates, Sokolow and White retain objective totality-of-circumstances substantive standards.
- Gates's warrant-issuance substantial-basis rule is distinguished from independent review of a warrantless search.
- Pierce and Cooter & Gell retain their task-specific deference; Salve Regina supports independent legal review.
- Markman does not decide the reserved appellate-review question by analogy alone.

##### What follows for these two appeals and the district court's suppression findings?

**Holding:** Both appellate judgments must be vacated and reconsidered under this allocation, without automatically ordering suppression or reopening the evidentiary record. Under section 636(b)(1)(B), the district judge independently determines specifically objected-to portions of the magistrate's recommendation and may receive evidence or recommit; that duty does not invariably require a new hearing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (O'Connor), Part III, joined by the same eight-Justice majority.

**Controlling explanation:** The district court accepted the credited loose-panel finding; rust was rejected, scratches were inconclusive, and actual NADDIS identification was unproved. Independent legal review cannot improve those facts by invoking experience. Raddatz permits the district judge's specified independent determination without an invariably repeated hearing; its concern about rejecting credibility findings is not triggered by the acceptance here. Section 636(b)(1)(A) does not set the ultimate suppression standard or the appellate allocation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes the circuit's limited-consent premise: consent did not authorize dismantling the panel. It adopts no general consent rule and does not revive waived inevitable discovery. The allocation also governs authorized Government suppression appeals, but section 3731's actual conditions remain: the statutory restriction after jeopardy attaches and before verdict, required certification of no delay and substantial material proof, the thirty-day and diligence requirements, and double-jeopardy limits. No independent Government appeal is authorized here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both Seventh Circuit judgments, reported at 16 F.3d 714 and 52 F.3d 328, are vacated and remanded, 8-1. The Court orders reconsideration of both constitutional thresholds, not suppression, plea withdrawal, release, a new evidentiary hearing or a particular ultimate result.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Raddatz preserves the distinction between a required independent judicial determination and an invariably new hearing; its reserved credibility-rejection question remains untriggered.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806

#### Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-5841.
**Decided:** June 10, 1996.

##### Investigative motive and customary enforcement in an ordinary traffic stop

**Holding:** An ordinary initial traffic stop supported by objective probable cause of a traffic violation does not become unreasonable under the Fourth Amendment because officers also investigate an unrelated offense. Validity does not additionally depend on whether reasonable officers ordinarily would enforce the violation absent that other aim. Independent constitutional limits on manner, scope, and duration remain; detention or search materially exceeding what the traffic violation reasonably justifies requires independent grounds.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous, including the application and qualifications.

**Material application:** The accepted observations of waiting unusually long at a stop sign, failing to signal, and unreasonable speed supply traffic probable cause. Petitioners' proposed objective “would-have” test receives the same rejection as a subjective-motive prerequisite: variable local priorities, uniforms, assignments, markings, and enforcement customs do not change constitutional authority for the known violation. A separate balance of ordinary traffic-safety gains against plainclothes-intervention anxiety supplies no additional requirement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Inventory and administrative-inspection authority retain their genuine-purpose and standardized or other source-specific constraints; motive is not irrelevant to every constitutional question. General Order 303.1 compliance, its immediate-safety-threat exception for otherwise unauthorized plainclothes or unmarked enforcement, and its extreme-circumstances oral-warning exception are not adjudicated. Neither speed nor missing execution details establish an exception or constitutional execution finding. The later search is not independently presented.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Scott* applies objective assessment of justified action; *Robinson*, *Gustafson*, and *Villamonte-Marquez* retain their distinct lawful-arrest, search, and customs-inspection predicates without creating new power. *Prouse* distinguishes suspicionless discretionary stops; *Martinez-Fuerte* and *Brignoni-Ponce* do not require a further balance for this ordinary probable-cause stop. *Bertine* and *Wells* preserve inventory constraints and forbid investigatory ruses; *Burger* preserves purpose-limited administrative inspection. *Abel* and *Bannister*'s regular-procedure and no-pretext observations do not establish the proposed customary-enforcement condition. *Wilson* preserves reasonable-execution review with its supported danger, futility, escape, and evidence-destruction qualifications, leaving suspicion quantum and automatic suppression open. *Garner*, *Welsh*, and *Winston* retain their distinct deadly-force, home-entry, and bodily-intrusion limits.

##### Review of the accepted predicate and separation of discriminatory selection

**Holding:** The accepted traffic observations permit resolution under *Ornelas*: historical facts, credibility, and genuinely factual subsidiary findings or inferences receive clear-error review, with genuine due weight to grounded contextual inferences; the legal standard and ultimate constitutional sufficiency properly at issue receive independent judgment. No new factual inquiry is required to decide this legal-rule challenge.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same unanimous Court opinion.

**Material application:** Due weight means substantive consideration of supported trial and local-officer context, not a numerical weight, officer-credibility presumption, or intermediate standard insulating constitutional conclusions. Direct review imports no habeas presumption or clear-and-convincing burden and supplies no supposed antecedent drug probable cause.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Purposeful racial selection remains independently forbidden by equal protection; traffic probable cause does not establish nondiscrimination. No discrimination finding, police-stop discovery entitlement, immunity rule, suppression consequence, or damages remedy is decided, and assumed adequacy of another remedy is not a ground for judgment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm rejection of suppression on the initial-stop ground, 9–0. The completed vacatur of lesser-included §841 convictions and §860 resentencing, including nonreimposition of Whren's fine, remain undisturbed. Later sentencing appeals are not resolved. No new trial, hearing, release, sentencing proceeding, or determination of every admissibility ground follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Ornelas*'s review allocation is applied unchanged. *Kimberlin* permits circumstantial proof when motive is an underlying element without adding one here. *Armstrong* remains confined to federal charging and discovery; its requirements and unresolved discrimination remedy do not become police-stop rules.

## Source: state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md — United States v. Lanier

#### United States v. Lanier

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1717.
**Decided:** March 31, 1997.

##### What law must supply fair warning under 18 U.S.C. §242

**Holding:** Section 242, read with authoritative constructions, must have made the particular constitutional unlawfulness reasonably apparent when the defendant acted. Neither a later decision nor willful bad purpose can supply missing conduct-time notice, and novel or unforeseeable criminal enlargement is forbidden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Moral reprehensibility and state criminality do not replace a definite federal right and notice. Vagueness, lenity, and the bar on unexpected retrospective enlargement retain related but distinct functions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* supplies definite-right and separate willfulness requirements; courts construe the criminal statute rather than create common-law offenses.

##### Whether notice requires a Supreme Court decision on fundamentally similar facts

**Holding:** Fair warning may come from relevant pre-existing lower-court authority and from an obviously covered application of an established rule without a factual twin. Neither a Supreme-Court-only requirement nor a fundamentally-similar-facts prerequisite is lawful; conversely, an abstract liberty or bodily-security proposition does not settle an uncertain application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** One lower-court decision or a fixed numerical consensus is not automatically sufficient. Actual conflicts, authority, particular context, and express reservations matter. No count's constitutional basis, notice, personal responsibility, or official nexus is resolved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Elder* supplies examination of all relevant existing authority. *Anderson* requires particularized apparent unlawfulness, not repeated factual identity. *Pinder* and *Uhlrig* preserve unsettled-application and attribution limits; later holdings cannot supply earlier notice.

##### Whether the civil clarity standard substitutes for criminal elements

**Holding:** Criminal fair warning under §242 and civil clearly-established-law analysis use the same legal-clarity threshold: pre-existing law must make the constitutional unlawfulness apparent. The common threshold does not displace proof of deprivation, action under color of law, criminal willfulness, the criminal burden of proof, or any other statutory element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Civil immunity analysis cannot itself convict, and judicial office alone does not prove that every act occurred under color of law. No categorically stricter criminal clarity standard is adopted.

##### Whether due-process rights or direct official assaults outside custody are categorically excluded

**Holding:** Appellate review may not exclude due-process rights from §242 categorically or treat the victim's lack of custody as automatic immunity for an official's own assault. Where a specific constitutional provision covers the claim, that provision governs; the Fourth and Eighth Amendments do not exhaust every potentially covered abusive official act.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the en banc judgment and remand for count-specific consideration under the proper conduct-time notice rule and all remaining properly presented issues. The mandate does not reinstate convictions or sentence, order acquittal or retrial, or require a new evidentiary hearing.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal constitutional-assault formula or factual assault finding is made. Constitutional wrongdoing, personal participation, omitted protection, and supervisory attribution remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* and *Price* preserve §242's due-process reach. *DeShaney*'s private-violence rule does not immunize direct official wrongdoing. *Graham* supplies the specific-provision rule. *Collins*, *Doe v. Taylor*, *Pinder*, *Uhlrig*, and *Nabozny* retain their distinct deprivation and attribution boundaries, without supplying notice retrospectively.

## Source: state/holdings/election-law.md — Akins v. FEC

#### Akins v. FEC

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 94-5088; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

**Decided:** 1997-06-25.

For informational standing and §437g(a)(8) review, see Akins v. FEC, D.C. Circuit No. 94-5088, decided 1997-06-25, Federal Courts — Electoral informational injury and statutory review.

##### III. Major purpose is not a categorical exemption for otherwise covered candidate financing

**Holding:** Buckley and MCFL do not establish an organization-wide major-purpose exemption from political-committee treatment for genuinely covered candidate financing under §431(4)(A). That provision reaches a committee, club, association, or other group of persons receiving contributions aggregating in excess of $1,000 in a calendar year or making expenditures aggregating in excess of $1,000 in a calendar year, subject to the actual definitions, exclusions and constitutional limits; it does not literally make every group that makes a contribution a political committee.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III, including III-A for actual candidate cash transfers and genuine conduits and III-B for other genuinely covered candidate-connected financing, is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: five direct votes with Souter. Kennedy supplies a sixth vote only for III-A. Stone-Zsela reserves both subparts. No combination of separate rationales is necessary to make the five-Justice rule controlling.

**Material application:** Covered purpose, amounts and exclusions must be found for actual transactions. Where contribution treatment depends on candidate connection, cooperation, consultation, concert, request or suggestion must actually exist; ideological alignment, policy cooperation, lobbying or partisan identity alone is insufficient.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Genuine independent expression remains protected. The Court does not conclusively classify AIPAC or uphold every organizational or donor-disclosure duty in every application; supported particular-duty challenges remain possible.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Buckley and MCFL's independent-expression narrowing does not create the claimed categorical exemption for covered candidate financing. Colorado Republican preserves independent expression and requires real candidate connection rather than organizational identity.

##### IV. Membership exclusions and their distinct reporting proviso govern the remand

**Holding:** The Commission must determine membership, covered transactions, candidate connection, calendar-year thresholds and applicable exclusions before imposing political-committee duties. Section 431(9)(B)(iii)'s membership-communication exclusion retains its separate reporting proviso; §441b(b)(2)'s exclusions and the §431 cross-references retain their different terms.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Souter's Court opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven direct votes. Part III's five-Justice construction governs the remand notwithstanding Stone-Zsela's and Kennedy's narrower positions.

**Material application:** Section 431(9)(B)(iii) excludes communications by a membership organization or corporation to its members, stockholders, or executive or administrative personnel if it is not organized primarily to influence nomination or election to federal office. The proviso requires a membership organization, including a labor organization, or a corporation to report costs directly attributable to communications expressly advocating election or defeat of a clearly identified candidate when those costs exceed $2,000 per election, except a communication primarily devoted to other subjects. Reporting follows §434(a)(4)(A)(i) and, for a general election, §434(a)(4)(A)(ii). This is neither the $1,000 calendar-year committee threshold nor an exception transplanted into §441b. Section 441b(b)(2) separately excludes corporate communications on any subject to stockholders and executive or administrative personnel and their families, and labor-organization communications to members and their families. Its nonpartisan registration and get-out-the-vote exclusion covers the same respective recipients. Its separate-fund exclusion covers establishment, administration and solicitation of contributions to a separate segregated fund used for political purposes by a corporation, labor organization, membership organization, cooperative or corporation without capital stock. The loan exception concerns a national or State bank's ordinary-course loan made under applicable banking law and regulations. Sections 431(8)(B)(vi) and (9)(B)(v) cross-reference activity allowed by §441b without importing the $2,000 proviso. The agency must apply each provision to the actual record. No final membership finding, coordination finding, threshold calculation, later membership rule, donor list, liability or penalty is supplied by this decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The five-Justice coverage construction governs remand notwithstanding the narrower separate preferences. Membership, actual covered transactions and each distinct exclusion require lawful Commission determination; no registration, donor list, disclosure award, enforcement order, liability or penalty is supplied.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

#### Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1014.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

For the Establishment Clause grounds in *Lee v. Weisman*, June 24, 1992, No. 90-1014, see First Amendment — Official school prayer.

##### Whether continued exposure at a forthcoming graduation preserves a live claim for prospective relief

**Holding:** A student who remains in the same public-school system and faces its challenged official prayer policy at her forthcoming high-school graduation has a live claim for prospective protection although her earlier graduation is over. Deborah Weisman's continued exposure permits review and preservation of the injunction without deciding her father's taxpayer standing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six Justices adopt the threshold proposition.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for the student's continuing prospective exposure.

**Material application:** Deborah remains enrolled at Classical High School, and the stipulated record makes clergy-led prayer at her later graduation likely. Her relationship to that forthcoming policy application is concrete; formal optional attendance does not remove it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no taxpayer-standing rule and creates no general advisory-jurisdiction exception for completed school controversies that might recur.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Preserve review and the injunction against the challenged official practice. The injunction's substantive scope follows the First Amendment holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203, supply the substantive school-prayer interest without supplying a new taxpayer-standing rule.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992)

#### Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1424.
**Decided:** June 12, 1992.

##### What evidence of Article III injury, causation and redressability is required at summary judgment

**Holding:** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury, a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant rather than independent third parties, and a likelihood that the requested relief will redress the injury. At summary judgment, the plaintiff must supply specific facts by affidavit or other evidence on the challenged elements; favorable reasonable inferences remain available, but allegations cannot substitute for the missing evidence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the elements and litigation-stage evidentiary requirements. Stone concurs in the judgment without joining this section.

**Material application:** Earlier survival of the pleading stage does not establish the specific facts needed at summary judgment. The Court resolves this action on insufficient threatened personal injury.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The general redressability element controls. Its application to absent funding agencies and uncertain foreign-project responses appears only in the three-Justice Part III-B and is not controlling. Favorable reasonable inferences remain available; the decision does not replace Rule 56 or the separate APA programmatic-review holding of National Wildlife Federation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The merits declaration, mandatory rulemaking relief and dependent §1540(g)(4) fee award are vacated, and the action is remanded for jurisdictional dismissal. Six Justices support dismissal for lack of standing; seven support reversal, with Stevens relying on a noncontrolling statutory ground. No independent fee-entitlement rule or substitute award is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975): applied for the plaintiff's personal stake and burden to establish standing; its statutory-rights discussion remains subject to concrete injury.
- Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied for the constitutional elements and the limits on generalized judicial supervision.
- Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization, 426 U.S. 26 (1976): applied for fair traceability and likely redress; no new majority application concerning these absent agencies follows.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied for the evidentiary distinction between pleading and summary judgment; its separate APA programmatic-review holding is not enlarged.
- Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317 (1986), and Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242 (1986): applied to require evidence of challenged essential elements while preserving genuine material disputes and reasonable inferences.

##### Whether past visits, indefinite return intentions and general wildlife interests establish imminent personal injury

**Holding:** Aesthetic, recreational and professional interests in observing wildlife can support standing, but these plaintiffs must show threatened interference with their own concrete use. Prior visits and an indefinite intention to return, without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure, do not establish standing for these prospective claims; an ecosystem, animal or professional connection that supplies only general interest in distant species does not cure that defect.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III-A of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone's separate concurrence supplies a sixth vote for the record-specific personal-injury conclusion without a formal opinion join.

**Material application:** The affidavits describe visits to Egyptian and Sri Lankan project areas but no sufficiently definite return undertaking or continuing activity showing imminent exposure. General ecosystem, animal and professional connections supply no missing evidence of these members' own threatened use.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No purchased-ticket or particular-document prerequisite is imposed. Actual use of an affected ecosystem and demonstrated injury transmitted across geographic distance remain undecided. Aesthetic, recreational and professional harm remains cognizable when it affects the claimant's own concrete activity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Jurisdictional dismissal leaves the statute's geographic reach unresolved and does not preclude a later action supported by sufficient standing facts.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): applied; aesthetic injury is cognizable, but an organization's special interest cannot replace injury to a member.
- Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, 432 U.S. 333 (1977): applied to require an injured member for this associational claim; no other associational requirement is changed.
- Los Angeles v. Lyons, 461 U.S. 95 (1983): applied for the distinction between past exposure and a present or imminent injury supporting prospective relief.
- Whitmore v. Arkansas, 495 U.S. 149 (1990): applied for actual or imminent rather than conjectural injury.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied to specific affected use; no categorical bar to geographically transmitted injury is established.
- Japan Whaling Association v. American Cetacean Society, 478 U.S. 221 (1986): distinguished because its whale-watching and studying members alleged an adverse effect on their own activities; concrete professional or ecological injury remains possible.

##### Whether ESA citizen-suit permission and an asserted procedural right replace threatened concrete injury

**Holding:** ESA §11(g)'s authorization for any person to sue does not give Article III standing to a plaintiff asserting only the public's common interest in lawful administration. A procedural right may protect a plaintiff's threatened concrete interest without requiring proof that the procedure will certainly change the ultimate agency decision, but the procedural label cannot replace that personal interest; these respondents have not shown the required connection.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the citizen-suit and concrete-interest rule. Stone does not join this section.

**Material application:** Section 11(g), 16 U.S.C. §1540(g), permits enforcement but identifies no concrete injury to these members independent of the unsupported future use. The public's common interest in lawful administration cannot supply the missing personal connection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may recognize concrete factual injuries previously inadequate in law; the outer reach of that power is not settled. Procedural protections tied to a threatened concrete interest remain cognizable without certainty of a changed ultimate decision. Neither §7(a)(2)'s geographic reach nor the binding effect of a revised rule on absent agencies is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The action is dismissed for want of Article III standing; no new consultation rule or cancellation of any foreign project is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Fairchild v. Hughes, 258 U.S. 126 (1922): applied to reject a general right to compel lawful administration as a sufficient personal injury.
- Massachusetts v. Mellon, 262 U.S. 447 (1923), and Ex parte Levitt, 302 U.S. 633 (1937): applied for the distinction between direct injury and an undifferentiated public grievance.
- United States v. Richardson, 418 U.S. 166 (1974), and Schlesinger v. Reservists Committee to Stop the War, 418 U.S. 208 (1974): applied to generally shared governmental grievances without distinctive concrete harm.
- Valley Forge Christian College v. Americans United for Separation of Church and State, Inc., 454 U.S. 464 (1982), and Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied; a claimed right to lawful government conduct alone does not satisfy Article III.
- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975), and Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): preserved for Congress's power to recognize injuries and broaden protected interests while retaining the plaintiff's own concrete injury.
- Trafficante v. Metropolitan Life Insurance Co., 409 U.S. 205 (1972), and Hardin v. Kentucky Utilities Co., 390 U.S. 1 (1968): distinguished as statutory recognition of concrete personal interests, not authorization of a wholly abstract grievance.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Johnson v. Jones

#### Johnson v. Jones

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-455.
**Decided:** June 12, 1995.

##### Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal

**Holding:** A denial of qualified immunity is not immediately appealable under §1291 insofar as it determines only whether the pretrial evidence presents a genuine factual dispute about the official's participation in the alleged conduct. Mitchell's immediate appeal remains available for the separable legal question whether the conduct, on the relevant assumed facts, violated clearly established law; the present excessive-force participation dispute does not meet that condition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this bounded rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A proper legal immunity appeal uses the district court's assumed facts. If unstated, the appellate court may identify likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant without deciding truth or reweighing sufficiency. An abstract legal contention does not bootstrap factual review. Genuine necessary connected review and future mixed-order questions remain at their reserved scope.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541 (1949): applies separateness as a cumulative collateral-order condition.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985): retains immediate legal immunity review on assumed facts, bounded against ordinary factual-sufficiency appeals.
- Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct (June 6, 1994): applies category and cumulative-condition discipline; public immunity importance does not erase separateness.
- Swint v. Chambers County Commission (March 1, 1995): applies rejection of convenience-based appellate expansion; genuine necessary connected review remains at its reserved scope.
- Elder v. Holloway (February 23, 1994): preserves review of all relevant date-eligible legal authority on an authorized immunity issue; supplies no fact-reweighing jurisdiction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal of the reviewed immediate excessive-force factual-sufficiency appeal. The circuit's separate false-arrest immunity reversal remains undisturbed. No participation, beating, evidence-sufficiency, liability, state-law claim or other defendant's liability is adjudicated.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Leatherman v. Tarrant County Narcotics Intelligence and Coordination Unit, 507 U.S. 163 (1993)

#### Leatherman v. Tarrant County Narcotics Intelligence and Coordination Unit, 507 U.S. 163 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1657.

**Decided:** March 3, 1993.

##### The Federal Rules supply the pleading standard for municipal § 1983 claims

**Holding:** A federal court may not impose a judge-created heightened factual-particularity requirement on a § 1983 complaint against a municipality beyond the then-operative Federal Rules. Rule 8 governs the complaint, Rule 9(b)'s fraud-or-mistake categories do not create a municipal-liability category, and Monell and Canton retain their separate substantive requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling authority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S. 41 (1957): applied for fair-notice pleading; no subsequent plausibility rule is imported.
- Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658 (1978): preserved; municipal policy/custom and causation remain necessary, while freedom from employer-based liability supplies no immunity from suit.
- Owen v. City of Independence, 445 U.S. 622 (1980): applied to reject municipal qualified immunity and the pleading argument built on it.
- City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378 (1989): preserved as substantive failure-to-train attribution law, not authority for greater pleading particularity.
- Elliott v. Perez, 751 F.2d 1472 (5th Cir.1985), and Palmer v. City of San Antonio, 810 F.2d 514 (5th Cir.1987): rejected insofar as their rule demands heightened particularity for municipal § 1983 complaints; individual-immunity pleading remains unresolved.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary Rule 8 and Rule 11 duties, discovery control and Rule 56 remain. Monell/Canton attribution and the underlying constitutional wrong are not relaxed. Individual-officer immunity pleading, collateral estoppel and unreviewed alternative summary-judgment grounds remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Fifth Circuit and remand under the ordinary pleading rules; no municipal liability finding or new trial is ordered.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, 509 U.S. 579 (1993)

#### Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, 509 U.S. 579 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-102.
**Decided:** June 28, 1993.

##### The enacted Rules displace mandatory general acceptance

**Holding:** Frye’s mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite does not govern admission of scientific expert evidence in federal trials under the Federal Rules of Evidence. Acceptance remains potentially relevant under Rule 702, but its absence does not alone require exclusion and its presence does not dispense with the Rules’ other requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Frye v. United States, 293 F.1013: displaced as a mandatory prerequisite in federal trials; not a Supreme Court Rule 702 holding being overruled.
- Beech Aircraft v. Rainey, 488 U.S. 153: statutory interpretation of enacted Rules applied.
- United States v. Abel, 469 U.S. 45: compatible common-law guidance retained, incompatible prerequisite rejected.
- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S. 171: Rules supersede incompatible common law.

##### Reliable scientific foundation and fit require flexible preliminary screening

**Holding:** Under original Rules 104(a) and 702, the proponent must establish by a preponderance the preliminary foundation for scientifically reliable knowledge that fits the disputed factual question and will assist the factfinder. The judge examines principles, methodology and valid application using relevant nonexclusive considerations—testability and testing, peer review/publication, meaningful error information and operational standards, and acceptance—without making any one consideration an invariant prerequisite; admissibility does not determine ultimate causation or sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original Rules govern. No later Rule 702 text, universal technical-specialty rule, appellate-review standard or substantive causation finding is adopted. Cross-examination supplements screening for admissible evidence; Rules 403, 703, 706 and ordinary sufficiency rules remain available.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the Frye-based summary judgment and remand for evaluation of material proffers and reconsideration of summary judgment. Further fair foundation submissions may be received; no direction to admit every expert.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S. 171, 175–176: preponderance for preliminary admissibility applied, not a substantive causation burden.
- Rock v. Arkansas, 483 U.S. 44: adversarial testing remains appropriate for admissible evidence; no excuse for unsupported foundation.
- United States v. Downing, 753 F.2d 1224: helpful fit and flexible reliability discussion informs interpretation, not a binding Supreme Court multifactor checklist.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

#### Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-757.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

For the other questions in Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 1994-04-26, No. 92-757, see Civil Rights — Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct; Civil Rights — The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim.

##### Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences

**Holding:** When Congress has prescribed a civil statute’s temporal reach, courts apply that direction subject to constitutional limits; absent sufficient direction, they ask whether applying the provision would impair rights possessed when a party acted, increase liability for past conduct, or impose new duties concerning completed transactions. If it would, the provision does not govern those events without clear congressional intent; a pending case or reference to earlier facts alone does not establish such an effect, and prospective relief, jurisdiction, procedure and collateral matters require examination of what the particular provision actually changes rather than an automatic reopening of completed acts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s general-framework portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this organizing rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur on a distinct relevant-activity approach, not as additional joins at this level of generality.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kaiser Aluminum & Chemical Corp. v. Bonjorno, 494 U.S. 827 (1990): applied to make statutory temporal direction the starting inquiry.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S. 204 (1988): applied to the presumption against retrospective legal burdens; no automatic civil constitutional prohibition is inferred.
- Bennett v. New Jersey, 470 U.S. 632 (1985): applied to protect substantive obligations fixed under earlier law.
