- Bradley v. School Board of Richmond, 416 U.S. 696 (1974): retained in its collateral litigation-fee setting and reconciled with the new-consequence inquiry, not overruled.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): distinguished as judicial retroactivity; its equal-application rule remains unchanged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is provision-specific, not a declaration that the whole 1991 Act is inapplicable in pending cases. Section 402(a)'s enactment-date clause, §109(c)'s later-conduct limitation for extraterritorial amendments, and §402(b)'s exception for disparate-impact complaints filed before March 1, 1975 with initial decisions after October 30, 1983 remain distinct. Judicial retroactivity under Harper does not control legislative reach. An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. The present case concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 8–1. No new damages trial, award or remand is ordered, and the existing equitable-remedy judgment and unreviewed constructive-discharge findings remain intact.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

#### United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1170.
**Decided:** February 22, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see First Amendment — Public-employee honoraria restrictions (A broad prospective honoraria ban requires justification for its actual speech burden).

##### The existing ban is unenforceable throughout the certified class

**Holding:** Relief against the existing §501(b) prohibition extends throughout the certified class of affected Executive Branch employees below GS–16. The Court declines to create a general employment-nexus qualification by moving the series-specific parenthesis in §505(3) into the entire honorarium definition; this class remedy does not establish that every class payment is unrelated or that a law separately and validly restricting connected payments would fail.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Pickering remains the merits framework rather than authority to rewrite statutory coverage. Simon & Schuster and Turner retain their stated financial-burden and evidentiary principles; neither compels a judicially created nexus statute. The remedy preserves the independent force of valid ethics laws and decides no new constitutional rule for congressional or judicial employees.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 505(3)'s series-specific nexus qualification and the travel and charitable-payment conditions are set out with this decision's First Amendment holding. The class remedy does not rewrite those conditions or extend to §501(a)'s distinct outside-income ceiling.

##### The injunction does not adjudicate the claims of unrepresented senior employees

**Holding:** The injunction must be confined to the represented class and the individually represented claim retained here; it may not extend this adjudication to unrepresented Executive employees at GS–16 or above. Removing that nonparty protection does not hold their claims meritless or decide materially different applications of the honoraria ban.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-A, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen v. Women's Health Center, June 30, 1994, supplies attention to actual persons, claims and the lawful scope of remedial adjustment; it is applied without importing its different clinic facts or an automatic bar on all broader relief in other cases.

##### Crane retains his individual relief without a grade-only remand

**Holding:** Peter G. Crane's existing individual relief is retained; his GS–16 status and resulting exclusion from the certified class do not alone require its removal or a separate remand. This disposition concerns a named respondent under the Government's alternative respondents-plus-class request, not a general constitutional exemption for senior employees or a substantive concession of Crane's entire merits case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-B, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices; Stone-Zsela adds a judgment-only sixth vote for Crane's relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen's bounded remedial treatment is applied to distinguish the necessary removal of nonparty protection from an unsupported grade-only reopening of an individual judgment. No new universal appellate-waiver rule or general senior-employee First Amendment exemption is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. Nonenforcement of the existing ban throughout the certified below-GS–16 class is preserved by five votes; Crane retains individual relief by six; relief for unrepresented senior employees is removed. Congressional and judicial coverage, §501(a)'s outside-income ceiling and valid independent ethics restrictions are not invalidated. No penalty or compensation award is entered.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette

#### Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-780.
**Decided:** June 29, 1995.

For the other questions in Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, decided June 29, 1995, No. 94-780. see First Amendment — Private religious displays in public forums (Ohio’s categorical Establishment Clause defense cannot sustain this exclusion).

##### The expired permit dispute remains reviewable

**Holding:** A dispute over a short seasonal permit remains live when its duration prevents full review and there is a reasonable expectation that the same claimant will again face the challenged restriction. These applicants meet both requirements; recurrence affecting other speakers alone would not suffice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone, Part I, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: nine votes for this threshold holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A broader prayer alone does not establish renewed exposure. Reviewability neither revives the expired permit nor extends its relief to every future application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Weinstein v. Bradford: applies both short duration and same-claimant recurrence; the exception does not extend to every recurring public issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Sixth Circuit at the original scope of the Count One merits judgment: the December 21–24, 1993 permit period remains expired. No new permit, extension, sign condition, removal order, damages or remand is entered. Unappealed rally, damages and other matters remain outside review.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

#### Doe v. Taylor Independent School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 16, 1995.

##### Immediate review of legal immunity questions and factual sufficiency

**Holding:** An immediate qualified-immunity appeal may decide a separable legal question on the plaintiff's properly supported assumed facts, but may not decide only whether the evidence is sufficient to establish those facts. A factual-sufficiency portion must be dismissed; an appellate court cannot resolve disputed knowledge or causation by choosing whose account is true and recasting that choice as law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No particular additional factual-only appeal is found to exist. The Court resolves neither credibility nor the inconsistent January/February descriptions of Caplinger's first notice.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the separable legal immunity judgment and remand; dismiss any purely factual-sufficiency portion. The distinct supervisory-right and individual-immunity holdings appear under Civil Rights, Doe, October 16, 1995, Fifth Circuit No. 90-8431.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson v. Jones supplies the legal-question/factual-sufficiency boundary. Elder v. Holloway requires examination of all relevant law, not reconstruction of a more favorable factual record for the officer. Neither rule expands collateral appellate jurisdiction.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299

#### Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1244.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### A prior appeal does not categorically exhaust legal-immunity review

**Holding:** An earlier pleading-stage qualified-immunity appeal does not, merely because it occurred, bar a later summary-judgment appeal presenting a separable legal immunity question that otherwise satisfies the collateral-order requirements. The later appeal may address legal immunity on properly assumed facts; this rule authorizes neither factual-sufficiency review nor repeated appeals of an unchanged legal ruling or matters resolved by the first mandate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Cohen's cumulative conditions and Mitchell's legal-immunity category apply. Digital Equipment's importance and category discipline remains; Johnson supplies no numerical ceiling and retains its factual boundary.

##### An authorized appeal uses assumed facts and excludes factual sufficiency

**Holding:** Within an otherwise authorized qualified-immunity appeal, the appellate court may decide whether conduct on the District Court's properly assumed or sufficiently supported facts violates clearly established law, but may not choose which factual account is true or reweigh whether evidence supports it. If the relevant assumptions are unstated, the court may identify the likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant in the manner Johnson permits, without deciding truth or evidentiary sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, unanimous. Stevens and Breyer condition agreement on an otherwise authorized appeal and do not find successive authorization here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson and Kimberlin preserve assumed-fact review without factual selection. Elder requires relevant conduct-date law but creates no jurisdiction or retrospective notice. Swint rejects convenience-based expansion; Doe's factual-sufficiency exclusion remains.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No immunity, constitutional violation, property interest, factual truth, evidentiary sufficiency or damages issue is decided.

##### A different surviving claim does not itself defeat a qualifying immunity appeal

**Holding:** The fact that an official must still defend another claim does not, by itself, eliminate an otherwise authorized immediate appeal of a separable legal immunity denial on a different claim. That claim-specific principle neither supplies missing appellate authorization nor makes the other claim reviewable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela does not join; Stevens and Breyer continue to oppose successive appeal as of right.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mitchell protects against defending the claim to which immunity applies; Swint prevents a proper appeal from carrying unrelated matters and leaves its necessary-connected-review reservation open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse only the categorical Ninth Circuit dismissal and remand to identify proper assumptions favorable to the nonmovant and any actually reviewable legal issue, dismissing factual-sufficiency and otherwise unavailable issues. The summary-judgment denial is not reversed, immunity is not awarded, and neither the separate United States negligence claim nor earlier substitution ruling is reopened. No stay or permission is granted.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Hodge v. Jones

#### Hodge v. Jones

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 93-1182; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** October 23, 1995.

##### Expunction and related declaratory relief after the applicable retention period expires

**Holding:** The presented requests for expunction and related declaratory relief are moot because the applicable five-year retention period expired in January 1994 and plaintiffs do not contend that required expunction then failed. That rule applied if no further incidents were reported involving the same alleged perpetrator; this disposition neither applies the later 120-day rule retroactively nor finds actual destruction from evidence outside the record.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The surviving damages dispute does not maintain the separate prospective controversy. Contingent fear of future disclosure establishes neither a newly retained record nor a mootness exception. No fee entitlement or remedy for the unpleaded inaccurate-code episode is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismiss the presented prospective requests as moot. The damages-immunity holding is separately recorded under Civil Rights, Hodge, October 23, 1995, Fourth Circuit No. 93-1182.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Ordinary live-controversy principles govern the specific relief sought; immunity is not a basis for prospective mootness.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1

#### Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** 95-266
**Decided:** June 13, 1996

##### What confidential psychotherapy communications does federal common law protect?

**Holding:** Federal common law protects confidential communications between a patient and licensed psychotherapist during diagnosis or treatment, including confidential psychotherapy by a licensed clinical social worker and the treatment conversations and notes at issue. Professional title alone does not protect administrative conversations, ordinary workplace counseling or communications outside confidential treatment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), joined on all controlling grounds by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight. Scalia dissents.

**Material application:** Beyer's license, training and actual psychotherapy place Redmond's treatment within the privilege. Rule 501's reason-and-experience delegation, treatment's reliance on predictable confidentiality, and widespread state recognition justify the defined relationship privilege despite evidentiary cost.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general physician-patient or all-social-worker privilege is created. State recognition does not establish identical definitions or exceptions; proposed Rule 504 was not enacted. Rule 501's state-law sentence continues to govern an element of a civil claim or defense supplied by state law. Choice of privilege law for evidence relevant to both state and federal claims in an actual conflict remains undecided; no general federal-preemption rule is announced.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Trammel supplies development through reason and experience with attention to function and cost. University of Pennsylvania's rejection of an unsupported peer-review privilege is distinguished by this demonstrated relationship and justification.

##### May routine evidentiary balancing defeat the privilege or valid withholding support an adverse inference?

**Holding:** A protected communication does not lose privilege merely because a judge weighs the opposing litigant's evidentiary need more heavily. Valid assertion cannot support the adverse inference and lack-of-legal-justification instruction used here.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same eight-Justice controlling opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A patient may waive protection, but no wholesale waiver is adjudicated from partial answers and three redacted pages. Waiver rules, court-ordered examinations, litigation-use exceptions, criminal constitutional overrides and serious-threat disputes remain open. The seven Associates' serious-threat illustration is nondecisional and establishes no operative exception; Stone-Zsela does not join it.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Seventh Circuit affirmed, 8–1; the new trial stands without its general balancing rationale, protecting validly withheld communications. No reinstatement of the two damages verdicts or finding that the shooting was lawful follows; the distinct deadly-force instruction issue is not reopened.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Nixon's executive privilege in criminal process supplies no universal balancing requirement. Daubert and Williamson concern admissibility and reliability rather than Rule 501 relationship protection.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona

#### Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-974.
**Decided:** March 3, 1997.

##### Whether this former employee retains a live challenge to the employment rule

**Holding:** An individual prospective employment-rule challenge becomes moot when the employee voluntarily leaves covered employment without an intention to return, realistic recurrence or a surviving claim. A general relief request cannot manufacture nominal damages against a State or official-capacity defendant that is not a damages person under §1983.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous; all nine Justices join every holding.

**Material application:** No class or replacement claimant remained, and the dismissed individual-capacity claims were not preserved on appeal. State nonpersonhood under §1983 is a statutory bar distinct from waivable Eleventh Amendment immunity; a proposed consensual payment cannot supply the missing cause of action. The Attorney General’s constitutional participation under §2403(b) did not overturn the denial of State party status.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general requirement to plead nominal damages by name is established. A legally available individual-capacity claim presents a different question. The Court does not fix the precise April 1990 date when mootness attached.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Preiser requires a controversy throughout review. Will and Hafer distinguish the unavailable State or official-capacity damages claim from genuine individual-capacity liability.

##### Whether limited corrective jurisdiction permits vacatur without deciding sponsor standing or constitutional merits

**Holding:** The Court may correct a lower federal court’s jurisdictional error without exercising constitutional merits jurisdiction. On the equities here, that authority requires vacating the appellate judgment and directing vacatur of the District Court declaration and dismissal for mootness, without resolving ultimate initiative-sponsor standing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Material application:** The prevailing plaintiff ended the covered employment during review efforts; this was not settlement or abandonment by a reviewing loser. The later appellate merits adjudication and absence of a lawful damages residue justify clearing both unreviewable constitutional judgments.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 2106 does not independently create merits jurisdiction. Intervention, an appeal notice or political commitment does not confer automatic sponsor standing. The original declaration is not declared void when entered, and the Court resolves neither every intervention ruling nor Article XXVIII’s validity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate both merits judgments and dismiss the action as moot.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bender and Corrick supply limited correction of jurisdictional error. Lujan and Brown Group retain constitutional injury and lawful representation requirements. Bancorp requires equitable rather than automatic vacatur; Munsingwear and Anderson support judgment clearing within that inquiry.

##### Whether state-law certification can revive a moot federal action

**Holding:** Certification of unsettled state law can assist a live federal controversy but cannot cure the absence of Article III jurisdiction. No certification is ordered in this moot case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stevens), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The meaning of Article XXVIII and any constitutional challenge in future live litigation remain open. No mandatory certification requirement is adopted.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.

#### Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.

**Docket or dockets:** Seventh Circuit No. 96-1136; review of 90 F.3d 1237.

**Decided:** 1997-06-02.

##### Constitutional jurisdiction before a disputed EPCRA merits question

**Holding:** A federal court cannot assume disputed Article III jurisdiction to decide the contested merits of this EPCRA reporting claim. Section 11046(c) describes enforcement and relief without making every statutory liability element jurisdictional; the holding preserves legitimate exceptional threshold sequences and does not prescribe an exhaustive order for all nonmerits issues.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five direct joins, including O'Connor and Kennedy's nonexhaustive sequencing reservation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Whether EPCRA authorizes suit over reporting violations cured before filing remains undecided. Norton and Avrech retain their distinct settings involving already-controlled merits.

##### Requested relief cannot redress this completed informational deprivation

**Holding:** Assuming past informational injury and traceability, Article III requires requested relief likely to repair that injury or prevent a supported continuing or imminent personal injury. When reports were current before suit and no continuing or imminent violation is alleged, a declaration of past lateness, Treasury penalties, monitoring, future report copies and litigation expenses supply no such redress.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer; six direct joins. Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg support dismissal on statutory grounds without joining this constitutional rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes rather than rejects informational injury. Standing is absent at commencement, rather than lost by later cessation. Deterrent penalties may redress a supported threatened injury; annual reporting duties alone do not allege one. Section 11046(f) permits appropriate prevailing-or-substantially-prevailing-party costs on a final order, not independent compensation. A properly presented replacement-information loss claim, monitoring in a live action, recoverable prefiling expenses, associational requirements and statutory temporal reach remain open. Section 11022(f) gives the fire department no transferable automatic inspection entitlement for this plaintiff.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand with directions to dismiss, unanimously as to judgment. No merits relief, standing discovery, amendment entitlement or newly inferred recurrence inquiry is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Bell v. Hood:** Applied to distinguish a substantial federal claim from its ultimate success; a contested statutory element is not automatically jurisdictional.
- **Young v. Northern Illinois Conference:** Applied at that same distinction; its constitutional-defense holding is not expanded into permission to assume Article III.
- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife:** Its controlling standing elements and stage-appropriate support remain governing. Its three-Justice absent-agency application is neither enlarged nor needed.
- **Church of Scientology v. United States:** Meaningful partial relief remains enough; no effectual relief of that kind is available on these allegations.
- **United Food & Commercial Workers v. Brown Group:** Congress's representative-enforcement choice does not eliminate personal injury and likely redress.
- **Bennett v. Spear:** Supported pleading-stage causal and remedial links remain sufficient; its ongoing constraint differs from the completed reporting deprivation here.
- **Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona:** Applied for the live-controversy requirement and limited corrective authority to vacate without reaching merits.
- **Norton v. Mathews; Secretary of the Navy v. Avrech:** Their already-controlled merits and distinct jurisdictional settings remain intact; neither decides this unresolved EPCRA dispute.
- **Gwaltney of Smithfield v. Chesapeake Bay Foundation:** Its different statutory wording and enforcement scheme do not settle the merits question the Court leaves open.
- **Meghrig v. KFC Western:** Its RCRA cause-and-remedy limits remain statute-specific; it supplies no automatic EPCRA current-violation rule.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District

#### Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 94-60631; 88 F.3d 274 identifies the judgment below.
**Decided:** March 19, 1997.

For the official-school-worship grounds and limits on the interim injunction, see *Ingebretsen*, March 19, 1997, No. 94-60631 (First Amendment — Official school worship and private student expression).

##### Whether imminent personal school-event exposure supports prospective standing

**Holding:** Students concretely and imminently facing an official school-event mechanism may challenge that threatened personal exposure before it occurs, where the injury is causally connected to the mechanism and prospectively redressable. Standing does not establish that the threatened conduct is unlawful.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part I, unanimous.

**Material application:** The enrolled students face impending events under a mandatory permission mechanism administered by officials, supported by the hearing record. Lack of a completed local policy does not make that prospect remote; parents may represent their children's interests through the proper representative route.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general taxpayer standing or standing for an abstract objector elsewhere is recognized.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Lujan* supplies concrete injury, causation, and redressability; standing is not derived from abstract opposition to a statute.

##### Whether the student applicants established inadequate representation under Rule 24(a)(2)

**Holding:** A timely applicant with a protectable interest that litigation may impair is entitled to intervene when existing representation may be inadequate. Shared desired results and the State's official identity do not conclusively prove adequacy, but the Attorney General actually represented these applicants' private-expression interests and no relevant omitted interest was identified; denial stands without reaching unnecessary alternative elements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part V-A, joined by all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela dissents from this component.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Amicus participation cannot substitute for an otherwise established intervention right. Materially changed interests may support a proper later application, without an advance entitlement or a required new adequacy hearing.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Trbovich* supplies the minimal burden of possible inadequacy and recognizes potentially different governmental and private interests; the inquiry remains individualized.

##### Whether a common question compels permissive intervention

**Holding:** Rule 24(b)'s common-question condition permits intervention but does not require it. The court retains discretion considering undue delay or prejudice, and its duplication-and-delay assessment justifies denial in this narrow statutory proceeding.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part V-B, joined by all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees with the disposition only.

**Material application:** Available amicus submissions support the conclusion that excluding duplicative party participation caused no demonstrated prejudice; they do not defeat intervention as of right.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court creates no novelty-of-argument requirement, unreviewable discretion, or universal plain-error prerequisite.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton

#### Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.
**Decided:** June 23, 1997.

**Material judgment:** Both judgments vacated and remanded, 5–4, for tailored prospective Rule 60(b)(5) relief; Lemon modified as the governing test; Aguilar's categorical rule overruled and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale displaced at the stated scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** All four holdings are delivered by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela in the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Each has five direct joins at its stated level of generality, including the modification of Lemon in Holding I. No aggregation of separate opinions supplies controlling authority.

##### III. The Court's present change supplies the legal change required for prospective relief

**Holding:** When this Court expressly removes the constitutional premise supporting a continuing adjudicated injunction, that present change is a significant change in law under Rule 60(b)(5), and continued categorical enforcement on the displaced premise is inequitable. The Court may apply its ruling in the pending review of denied prospective relief without requiring a second motion merely to invoke the rule it has just announced; lower courts remain bound by directly controlling precedent until this Court changes it.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the Court's opinion; the same five Justices directly join the present-change rule and its distinction between this continuing decree and a completed judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** Denial of Rule 60(b)(5) relief is reviewed for abuse of discretion, with its governing legal premises reviewed as questions of law. Rufo requires a significant change making continued enforcement inequitable and a modification fitted to that change. The legal change here is the Court's ruling today. It is not the expectation that the Court might reconsider Aguilar, an increase in the expense of compliance, or a supposed earlier overruling. Kiryas Joel expressly preserved Aguilar and Ball. Zobrest decided antecedent statutory and regulatory questions without deciding establishment. Rosenberger's actual printing benefit and Pinette and Chabad's private-expression holdings did not authorize this public teaching program. Rodriguez de Quijas required the lower courts to apply the directly governing rule.

The objection that Rule 60 review cannot become an untimely rehearing of the original judgment does not bar this relief. The motion addresses the decree's continuing prospective operation, and the preserved question presents the constitutional rule supporting that operation. The Court changes that rule expressly; it does not hold that the district court should have anticipated the change. Requiring a new motion solely to invoke today's decision would preserve a now-rejected prohibition without a remaining legal reason.

Rufo's limits remain. Dissatisfaction, a cheaper lawful alternative and anticipated doctrinal change are insufficient. Actual anticipation and acceptance of a risk can impose a heavy burden, but mere foreseeability is not an absolute bar and an unforeseeable catastrophe is unnecessary. A clarification of constitutional permission does not automatically erase a voluntarily accepted stricter consent obligation. This injunction enforces an adjudicated constitutional prohibition; the record establishes no negotiated promise independently requiring that prohibition. The Court neither reopens completed claims nor finds an independently sufficient factual change in costs.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail is applied to the present legal change and retained in its significant-change, accepted-risk and tailored-relief requirements. Rodriguez de Quijas v. Shearson/American Express, Inc. is applied to preserve lower-court obedience to direct precedent. Browder v. Director, Department of Corrections does not foreclose adjustment of continuing relief on the basis of the legal change actually adopted here; its limit on using a Rule 60 appeal to reopen the underlying completed judgment remains.

##### IV. Relief must remove the rejected prohibition while preserving independently lawful obligations

**Holding:** On remand the district court must remove the injunction's prohibition of the described secular Title I service insofar as that prohibition rests on religious location, presumed inculcation from location or ordinary supervision of public personnel; if no independently supported legal basis sustains the onsite prohibition, it must be dissolved. Any remaining obligation must have an identified lawful basis, and any further proceedings must concern a specified material issue under the surviving law rather than require proof that future misconduct is impossible.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of the Court's opinion; the same five Justices directly join this tailored relief and its public-control limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Rufo connects modification to the proven change while preserving the lawful purposes of continuing relief. The change today eliminates the categorical site-and-monitoring ground. The district court therefore cannot simply reinstate that same ground, presume that public service and religious instruction are inseparable, or treat ordinary secular supervision as itself forbidden. It must remove the prohibition to that extent. Supported questions about actual religious instruction, diversion, religious control of public employees or another independent legal prohibition remain subject to ordinary procedure and the governing law; hypothetical misconduct does not justify preserving Aguilar's rejected inference.

No additional negotiated obligation is presumed. If an independent statutory, contractual or decree obligation is established, constitutional permission does not by itself extinguish it. Existing lawful services may continue while the mandate is implemented promptly. Children currently receiving those services should continue to receive them during implementation, with existing statutory duties preserved and no new service entitlement created. Any implementation period must address identified tasks and circumstances; the Court sets no number of days, and administrative delay cannot indefinitely preserve the rejected categorical prohibition. The district court modifies the decree under the mandate. Until it acts, the City must obey the operative injunction.

The holding permits the described program; it does not command onsite delivery, create an unconditional funding entitlement, approve a replacement program, award damages or repayment, cancel contracts, or excuse past disobedience. It makes no finding that every present implementation satisfies the required secular controls.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Rufo's suitable-tailoring requirement controls implementation and preserves any independently lawful obligation. Its consent-decree rule is not converted into a presumption that this adjudicated injunction contains a voluntary stricter undertaking.

For the Lemon test and the onsite secular-service rule in Holdings I–II, see First Amendment, “Public instruction on religious-school premises,” Agostini, June 23, 1997, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Lindh v. Murphy

#### Lindh v. Murphy

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-6298.

**Decided:** 1997-06-23.

##### The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application

**Holding:** AEDPA's chapter 153 amendments, including amended §2254(d), do not govern Lindh's noncapital federal habeas application filed before April 24, 1996. The application-date construction preserves separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case rule and the successive-petition provisions governed by Felker; it does not exempt every pending proceeding from every AEDPA provision.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer. Six Justices directly adopt the statutory rule, its limits and reversal with remand.

**Material application:** The express pending-case direction for chapter 154 in §107(c), contrasted with its omission for chapter 153 enacted alongside it, supplies the temporal construction. Lindh filed his noncapital application on July 9, 1992.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary statutory construction may establish temporal direction without a prescribed formula. No universal substantive/procedural classification, permanent constitutional entitlement to filing-date judicial rules, chapter 154 State-qualification ruling, or decision on confrontation, bias, harmlessness, exhaustion or entitlement to a writ is made.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Seventh Circuit is reversed and must consider the appeal under pre-AEDPA habeas law; no writ is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Landgraf supplies the statutory-direction inquiry and Rivers its provision-specific operation. Felker is distinguished because its authorization-review proceedings began after enactment and its other Title I timing questions were reserved; its successive-petition and retained-original-authority holdings remain. Hughes Aircraft requires attention to the actual statutory change and does not displace the chapter comparison.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Akins v. FEC

#### Akins v. FEC

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit No. 94-5088; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

**Decided:** 1997-06-25.

For political-committee coverage and the separate membership and transaction provisions, see Akins v. FEC, D.C. Circuit No. 94-5088, decided 1997-06-25, Election Law — Political-committee coverage and membership communications.

##### I. The continuing denial of particular electoral information is a concrete injury

**Holding:** Voters denied particular campaign-financing information allegedly required by FECA, which they seek for their own electoral participation, have a concrete informational injury even when many voters share it. Here the Commission's legal ground blocks a statutory process capable of supplying information still sought; the possibility of a later lawful discretionary choice does not defeat traceability or redressability.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Souter's Court opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven direct votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Statutory complainant status, opposition to an organization's policies or a generalized wish for legal compliance alone is insufficient. The plaintiffs must support their own injury at the litigation stage; standing determines neither entitlement to every requested item nor ultimate regulatory classification.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Public Citizen supports a concrete widely shared informational injury. Lujan retains personal injury and stage-appropriate proof. Bennett requires examination of the real remedial chain despite lawful discretion. Steel Co.'s completed deprivation and already-current reports are distinguished from information still withheld.

##### II. FECA permits review of this dismissal's legal premise

**Holding:** Section 437g(a)(8) permits an aggrieved complainant to obtain judicial review of a complaint dismissal within sixty days and permits correction of a legally erroneous coverage premise. A court may declare the dismissal contrary to law and may direct conformity within thirty days; if the Commission fails to conform, the complainant may bring an action to remedy the violation involved in the original complaint.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II has the same seven-Justice coalition as Part I.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The separate 120-day inaction route is not invoked. Judicial correction does not compel prosecution, disclosure or penalties, remove lawful discretion, or permit substitution of the agency's separate corporate-prohibition membership rationale for the actual political-committee dismissal ground. Genuine ambiguity and remaining discretion remain for lawful agency consideration.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Standing and legal-error review are affirmed, 7–2; the case returns through the enacted review channel. The separate corporate-prohibition nonenforcement choice is not disturbed merely by correcting this dismissal.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Brown Group separates statutory enforcement authority from Article III. Bennett requires an actual authorized channel, and Legi-Tech preserves lawful agency reconsideration and statutory prerequisites. The agency's interpretation of constitutional decisions is not conclusive because it administers FECA.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-courts.md — Raines v. Byrd

#### Raines v. Byrd

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1671.

**Decided:** June 26, 1997.

##### Whether individual Members may challenge abstract institutional dilution after fully effective votes

**Holding:** Individual Members of Congress whose votes were counted and given full effect lack Article III standing to challenge the Line Item Veto Act on the asserted abstract dilution of Congress's institutional power and alteration of future votes. Express statutory permission to sue removes the applicable prudential obstacle but cannot supply the missing concrete personal injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct rationale votes. Souter supplies the seventh judgment vote on a separate ground.

**Material application:** The Members lost votes that were counted and identify no personal injury from a cancellation; neither House authorized them to represent Congress.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No categorical bar to legislator or official injury is established. Personal exclusion, lost salary, nullified decisive votes, an authorized institutional claim satisfying Article III and concrete cancellation injury remain distinct. Legislative remedies create no exhaustion requirement; no independent traceability holding is added.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lujan supplies concrete personal injury, without enlargement of its noncontrolling redressability analysis. Akins's concrete shared informational injury is distinguished. Powell preserves personal exclusion claims; Coleman preserves complete nullification of decisive votes. Bender and Allen support the personal-stake and unauthorized-representation boundaries.

##### What disposition follows from the Members' lack of standing

**Holding:** Because these plaintiffs lack Article III standing, the District Court's constitutional declaration must be vacated and the complaint dismissed for lack of jurisdiction. The Court does not decide the Act's constitutionality, nondelegation, Presentment or the independent ripeness question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg; six direct rationale votes. Souter supplies the seventh judgment vote on a separate ground.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the District Court's constitutional declaration and remand for jurisdictional dismissal, 7–2. Vacatur neither sustains the Act nor orders a merits remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Steel Co. requires resolution of disputed Article III power before disputed merits in this setting; statutory expedition and direct review do not supply constitutional injury.

## Source: state/holdings/federal-taxation.md — United States v. Carlton, 512 U.S. 26

#### United States v. Carlton, 512 U.S. 26

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1941.

**Decided:** 1994-06-13.

##### The enacted amendment governs the earlier transaction

**Holding:** Section 10411(b) expressly makes the 1987 amendment to §2057 effective as if included in the original 1986 enactment, so its decedent-ownership requirement reaches the estate's December 1986 transaction, subject to constitutional review. The original statute contained no such condition; neither later legislative explanation nor an agency announcement supplies a preexisting ownership requirement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg. These six Justices adopt the statutory holding; the judgment concurrences accept the express temporal direction without joining the opinion.

**Material application:** The Government stipulated that the estate qualified under the original text. The express 1987 effective-date provision, rather than an earlier agency notice or later account of original purpose, supplies the new ownership condition's retrospective operation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, April 26, 1994: applies its priority for express temporal direction, subject to constitutional limits.
- Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., April 26, 1994: distinguishes enacted change from judicial construction; corrective purpose alone does not provide temporal reach.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, June 18, 1993: preserves its rule for judicial decisions in open direct review; that rule does not displace Congress's express amendment.

##### The limited retroactive ownership amendment satisfies due process

**Holding:** Retroactive economic legislation satisfies due process when its retroactive operation rationally serves a legitimate legislative purpose. Applying this prompt correction of the unexpectedly broad estate-tax deduction to the short interval since its enactment meets that standard despite the executor's established reliance, lack of advance notice and actual loss; this decision creates neither a fixed one-year limit, an automatic reliance exemption nor an unlimited revenue-purpose safe harbor.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg supply six direct joins in this constitutional rationale; O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas agree only with the judgment on their distinct grounds.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides neither the abandoned Contract Clause and Takings theories nor a new tax-loss compensation claim. No allocation failure, agency power to amend the original statute, fixed constitutional lookback limit or new dollar assessment is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Pension Benefit Guaranty Corp. v. R. A. Gray & Co., 467 U.S. 717: applies rational-purpose review to the retroactive operation itself and explains the harsh-and-oppressive formulation.
- General Motors Corp. v. Romein, March 9, 1992: applies its rational-justification rule despite actual reliance, without treating all retroactivity as valid.
- Usery v. Turner Elkhorn Mining Co., 428 U.S. 1: preserves legislative power to adjust economic obligations retrospectively for a rational legitimate purpose.
- Welch v. Henry, 305 U.S. 134, and United States v. Hemme, 476 U.S. 558: preserve context-sensitive tax review; their harsh-and-oppressive language creates no automatic reliance veto.
- United States v. Darusmont, 449 U.S. 292, and Milliken v. United States, 283 U.S. 15: preserve permissible tax retroactivity in their settings, without a universal advance-notice condition.
- Untermyer v. Anderson, 276 U.S. 440: distinguishes its wholly new gift tax from this amendment to a deduction within an existing estate-tax regime.
- United States v. Irvine, April 20, 1994: distinguishes its later taxable transfer; the present amendment is genuinely retroactive and receives constitutional review.
- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994, permits the bounded congressional reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals under an expressly retrospective statutory rule preserving independent judicial adjudication. The present case concerns a substantive amendment to the estate-tax law and its application to an earlier transaction, not the reinstatement of a completed federal judicial judgment. Neither decision establishes unlimited constitutional permission for retrospective legislation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for judgment applying the amended ownership requirement. No refund follows from the rejected due-process theory and no compensation is awarded for the transaction loss. The Court fixes no new assessment, penalty, valuation, allocation failure or refund amount.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

#### Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1014.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

For the live-controversy holding in *Lee v. Weisman*, June 24, 1992, No. 90-1014, see Federal Courts — Prospective equitable relief.

##### Whether officials may organize clergy-led worship at public secondary-school graduation

**Holding:** Public secondary-school officials violate the Establishment Clause when they select clergy, request invocations and benedictions, guide the prayers' religious content and incorporate that worship into the official graduation program. Neither nonsectarian language nor formally optional attendance cures that sponsorship; no separate showing of punishment or quantified social pressure is required to invalidate this practice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor and Souter; five Justices adopt this independently sufficient sponsorship ground. Kennedy does not join Part II.

**Current force:** Remains controlling for the identified school-organized graduation worship, independently of a separate coercion showing.

**Material application:** The principal selected the rabbi, requested prayer, supplied civic-prayer guidance and placed the exercise in the program. A desire for solemnity does not authorize the State to undertake religious presentation; the record does not establish that officials wrote or edited a complete prayer script.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding does not prohibit genuinely private prayer or private forum speech, replace the general Establishment Clause framework, or resolve adult legislative prayer, funding, denominational aid or universal accommodation questions. Lemon's purpose and entanglement prongs are not separately decided; broader views in Souter's concurrence are not additional Court holdings.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the First Circuit and preserve the prospective injunction against officials' authorizing or encouraging the challenged clergy-led graduation prayer practice. The injunction reaches the official program, not private prayer or genuinely private forum speech.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203, are applied: nonsectarian language, voluntary attendance and permission to abstain do not cure official school worship. Lemon v. Kurtzman, 403 U.S. 602, is applied through its prohibition on a primary effect advancing religion, without a replacement test. Marsh v. Chambers, 463 U.S. 783, is distinguished as legislative prayer in a different setting; Board of Education of Westside Community Schools v. Mergens, 496 U.S. 226, protects private religious equal-access activity rather than official sponsorship.

**Independent alternative holding:** School-directed clergy prayer at a public secondary-school graduation independently violates the Establishment Clause when school control and the ceremony's importance place objecting students under substantial pressure to participate, appear to participate or forgo the occasion. Formally optional attendance and the opportunity to stand or remain silent do not remove that pressure here; mere offense alone is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Kennedy's concurrence in part and in the judgment, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor and Souter; five Justices expressly adopt this independently sufficient ground. It controls through direct majority support, not Marks. Stone does not join this writing.

**Current force of alternative holding:** Remains independently controlling for the stated school-directed secondary-school graduation practice and substantial participation pressure.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** The absence of a disciplinary penalty does not eliminate the injury; a student need not protest publicly or miss the singular educational ceremony to avoid apparent participation. The ground does not equate disagreement or ordinary courtesy with coercion and leaves mature adult audiences and other accommodations open. It independently sustains the same limited prospective injunction. Engel and Schempp apply to this indirect religious pressure; Marsh is distinguished by the adult legislative setting and different freedom to leave. Lynch v. Donnelly, 465 U.S. 668, supplies the minimum prohibition on coerced religious support or participation without extending its display holding. County of Allegheny v. ACLU, 492 U.S. 573, is not reopened as to displays or every accommodation dispute.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Waters v. Churchill, 511 U.S. 661

#### Waters v. Churchill, 511 U.S. 661

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1450.

**Decided:** 1994-05-31.

##### A genuine, reasonable account of disciplinable speech supplies a limited safe harbor

**Authority of the judgment:** The Seventh Circuit's categorical actual-words rule is vacated and the case remanded, 7–2; defendants' summary judgment remains set aside. Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg support vacatur. No complete controlling rationale exists under Marks; the express majority propositions below have only their stated scope.

**Independently majority-supported proposition:** A public employer does not violate the employee's substantive speech right by disciplining her because of a genuinely held, objectively reasonable account of speech that the Pickering–Connick balance permits it to discipline, even if that account is later shown mistaken. The protection requires actual reliance on the permissible ground; it does not protect a reasonable investigation used to conceal retaliation for different protected speech.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's plurality opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg, adopts this sufficient condition; Scalia's concurrence in the judgment, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, expressly accepts it under their broader genuine-ground rule. Seven Justices agree at this precise level; only four adopt the full objective-inquiry framework.

**Current force:** Controlling only at the precise scope of this express majority agreement; no complete rationale is added by combining the writings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Pickering v. Board of Education, 391 U.S. 563 (1968): applies the public-employee speech balance, adding this limited factual-error permission.
- Connick v. Myers, 461 U.S. 138 (1983): applies contextual public-concern analysis and reasonable prospective assessment of institutional disruption; personal embarrassment alone is insufficient.
- Givhan v. Western Line Consolidated School District, 439 U.S. 410 (1979): preserves private speech protection and the relevance of manner, time and place.
- Mt. Healthy City Board of Education v. Doyle, 429 U.S. 274 (1977): preserves actual causation; a potentially permissible ground is not proof of the ground actually used.

##### An unreasonable false account cannot defeat actually protected speech

**Independently majority-supported proposition:** Discipline because of an unreasonable and incorrect report of speech that was actually protected violates the substantive speech right when the protected speech bears the required causal relation to the action, subject to distinct causation defenses and remedial immunity. This agreement does not establish liability for every investigative imperfection, for an unreasonable report of actually unprotected speech, or without proof of the underlying protected conduct and causal action.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor, Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg accept this proposition under objective factual care; Stevens's dissent, joined by Blackmun, expressly accepts it under their broader actual-speech rule. Six Justices agree on this proposition, but the two dissenters do not join the reasonable-error safe harbor or the judgment vacating the appellate rule.

**Current force:** Controlling only at the precise scope of this express majority agreement; no complete rationale is added by combining the writings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Pickering, Connick and Givhan: preserve the substantive protection of the actual public-concern speech within the employment balance.
- Board of Regents v. Roth, 408 U.S. 564 (1972): distinguishes a property-based hearing entitlement from this substantive speech claim.
- Mt. Healthy, 429 U.S. 274 (1977): retains the distinct causation and same-decision requirements; unreasonableness alone is not an automatic recovery rule.

##### The motive dispute requires remand without restoring defendants' summary judgment

**Independently majority-supported proposition:** The Seventh Circuit's categorical actual-words rule is vacated, but defendants' summary judgment remains set aside because genuine reliance and retaliation for other protected criticism are materially disputed. On remand the employee must show protected conduct was a substantial or motivating factor; the employer may prove by a preponderance that the same decision would have occurred without it, and individual qualified immunity remains available and, if necessary, must be assessed as a distinct defense under the law relevant to the 1987 conduct.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's four-Justice plurality and Scalia's three-Justice concurrence in the judgment support this common vacatur and limited remand. Blackmun and Stevens would affirm the appellate reversal under their different actual-speech rule; their votes are not counted as support for vacatur.

**Current force:** Controlling only at the precise scope of this express majority agreement; no complete rationale is added by combining the writings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mt. Healthy, 429 U.S. 274 (1977), and Givhan, 439 U.S. 410 (1979): apply the employee's causal showing and the employer's preponderance same-decision defense.
- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800 (1982): preserves individual qualified immunity as distinct from the substantive speech rule.
- Elder v. Holloway, February 23, 1994: requires considering relevant authority on the preserved immunity issue, without expanding the claim or supplying notice from later law.
- Rankin v. McPherson, June 24, 1987, and Anderson v. Creighton, June 25, 1987: may inform later doctrine but cannot themselves establish notice for the January and February 1987 actions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No complete factual-investigation theory commands five votes. The seven-Justice reasonable-and-genuine safe harbor and six-Justice unreasonable-false-actually-protected proposition are express agreements, not a unified Marks framework; the latter includes two judgment dissenters and is not selected as a judgment-supporting narrowest rationale. No universal freestanding investigation duty or rule for unreasonable investigation of actually unprotected speech is established. Personal immunity remains tied to law giving notice at the 1987 conduct, not the 1994 decision itself.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand, 7–2, without restoring defendants' summary judgment. Determine genuine reliance, actual motivation and the protection of causally relevant other speech under ordinary summary-judgment and Mt. Healthy rules. The completed investigation's conditional objective adequacy and the reported account's conditional permissibility are not reopened as wholly unresolved issues. Personal immunity is considered if necessary; no damages, reinstatement, municipal-liability finding or revived association claim is ordered.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

#### United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1170.
**Decided:** February 22, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see Federal Courts — Scope of public-employee speech relief (The existing ban is unenforceable throughout the certified class; The injunction does not adjudicate the claims of unrepresented senior employees; Crane retains his individual relief without a grade-only remand).

##### A broad prospective honoraria ban requires justification for its actual speech burden

**Holding:** When the Government broadly restricts future compensated expression by its employees acting as citizens, Pickering requires a justification commensurate with the restriction's actual reach and burden on employees and listeners. Section 501(b)'s honoraria ban is unconstitutional as applied to the represented lower-level employees' protected expression unrelated to their employment because the record does not adequately connect that burden to actual or apparent misuse of office; narrower valid ethics restrictions remain available.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Pickering v. Board of Education, 391 U.S. 563, supplies the employment balance, applied to the breadth of a prospective restriction. Connick v. Myers, 461 U.S. 138, supplies public-concern principles without limiting protected subjects to politics or workplace criticism. Waters v. Churchill, May 31, 1994, retains its distinct particular-speech and reasonable-account rules. Simon & Schuster v. Members of the New York State Crime Victims Board, December 10, 1991, supplies the relevance of financial burdens. Turner Broadcasting System v. FCC, June 27, 1994, preserves substantial but reviewable legislative predictive judgments; it does not decide this employment case.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 501(b) prohibits receipt of an honorarium by a Member, officer or employee; §505(2) excludes special Government employees as defined in 18 U.S.C. §202. Section 505(3) covers money or anything of value for an appearance, speech or article; a series falls within the definition if its subject directly relates to official duties or payment is because of governmental status. That parenthesis does not already restrict every single honorarium. Actual and necessary travel expenses for the individual and one relative are excluded to the extent another person pays or reimburses them, and otherwise deducted to the extent they are not paid or reimbursed. Travel expenses mean transportation, lodging and meals away from residence or principal employment.

Section 501(c) treats a payment on the individual's behalf to a §170(c) charitable organization as not received, subject to a $2,000 ceiling on each payment and no financial benefit to the individual, parent, sibling, spouse, child or dependent relative. Section 504(a) permits an Attorney General civil action and a civil penalty up to the greater of $10,000 or compensation received. Under §504(b), a written opinion from an entity identified by §503 protects its recipient and another covered person whose facts are materially indistinguishable from subsection (a)'s sanction only for conduct after issuance, in good faith and in accordance with the opinion's provisions and findings. These are qualified provisions, not general exemptions. The separate §501(a) high-level outside-income ceiling is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. Nonenforcement of the existing ban throughout the certified below-GS–16 class is preserved by five votes; Crane retains individual relief by six; relief for unrepresented senior employees is removed. Congressional and judicial coverage, §501(a)'s outside-income ceiling and valid independent ethics restrictions are not invalidated. No penalty or compensation award is entered.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette

#### Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-780.
**Decided:** June 29, 1995.

For the other questions in Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, decided June 29, 1995, No. 94-780. see Federal Courts — Mootness and seasonal permits (The expired permit dispute remains reviewable).

##### Ohio’s categorical Establishment Clause defense cannot sustain this exclusion

**Holding:** Ohio may not exclude this genuinely private religious display from Capitol Square’s actual, generally available forum on the categorical ground that its religious message and proximity to the Statehouse necessarily establish religion. The judgment concerns the proposal presented, including its offered private-attribution safeguard; it neither finds the later sign adequate nor creates an unconditional right to every unattended display.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone, Parts II–III, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer: seven votes for this bounded speech and access holding. Those seven do not adopt one comprehensive Establishment Clause test.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The offered readable clarification of private sponsorship had negotiable wording; it is distinct from the later actual sign and the injunction's terms. No finding establishes approved final wording or the sign's adequacy. Government-sponsored displays, actual religious favoritism and future rules remain open. Parts II–III have seven direct joins; Part IV's four-vote contextual explanation and the three-vote private-access rationale supply no controlling comprehensive test or Marks subset. No categorical forum immunity or universal disclaimer requirement is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Widmar v. Vincent: applies protection of religious expression in a genuinely open forum, while preserving examination of an actual establishment defense.
- Lamb’s Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District: applies its controlling protection against exclusion of a religious perspective from comparable private access; its contextual reasoning is not converted into categorical forum immunity.
- Lemon v. Kurtzman and County of Allegheny v. ACLU: not overruled; the Court does not replace existing establishment law with a comprehensive test in this case.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Sixth Circuit at the original scope of the Count One merits judgment: the December 21–24, 1993 permit period remains expired. No new permit, extension, sign condition, removal order, damages or remand is entered. Unappealed rally, damages and other matters remain outside review.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method. Pinette's bounded private-expression and access holding remains; it supplies no universal forum immunity or automatic attribution from location.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia

#### Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-329.
**Decided:** June 29, 1995.

##### Religious viewpoint in the private publication program

**Holding:** The University may not exclude an otherwise eligible private student publication from its generally available printing benefit because its editorial viewpoint is religious. The rule applies to this privately edited, independently attributed publication program on its otherwise lawful eligibility, payment and administrative terms; it does not require funding every religious activity or every subject.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas; five Justices adopt the program-specific viewpoint rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Recognition, facilities, distribution and money remain distinct; not every public expenditure creates a speech forum. The publication remains independently edited and attributed under required disclaimers and approved vendor-payment terms.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Perry Education Association v. Perry Local Educators’ Association: preserves limited-forum definition while forbidding viewpoint discrimination.
- Lamb’s Chapel v. Center Moriches; Widmar v. Vincent: apply private religious-viewpoint equality without assuming that facility access decides every subsidy.
- Regan v. Taxation With Representation: distinguishes definition of a government financing program from discriminatory exclusion within this opened private-publication arrangement.

##### Permissible printing support on the actual program terms

**Holding:** Providing this neutral printing benefit to the otherwise eligible private religious publication does not itself violate the Establishment Clause. Its secular student-expression purpose, broad neutral availability, independent editorial attribution and controlled vendor payments matter together; neutrality alone is not a universal authorization of devotional financing or governmental sponsorship of worship.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas join this concrete program conclusion. Lemon remains governing law; no new universal framework is adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The actual purpose, effect and entanglement inquiry remains; neutral availability alone neither authorizes unrestricted devotional financing nor governmental worship. Compelled-fee challenges, unrestricted religious aid and particular awards are outside review.

The denial concerned religious activity, defined by the Guidelines as primarily promoting or manifesting belief in or about deity or ultimate reality, rather than an established finding of religious-organization ineligibility. The organization rule separately concerns devotion as organizational purpose. Political restrictions separately exclude principal-purpose lobbying and candidate electioneering while permitting incidental or insubstantial advocacy, nonpartisan study/research and ideological views; those permissions are not exceptions to the religious-activity rule. Permitted ability-to-perform membership limits likewise remain distinct. Abandoned free-exercise/state individual-rights claims and unchallenged equal-protection intent findings are not reopened.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Lemon v. Kurtzman: retains the existing purpose, effect and entanglement inquiry; no universal replacement is adopted.
- Witters v. Washington Department of Services for the Blind; Mueller v. Allen: apply neutral-benefit and private-direction distinctions within the actual program, not neutrality alone.
- Board of Education of Kiryas Joel v. Grumet: distinguishes denominational governmental authority and retains religious-neutrality limits.
- Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills: preserves the actual antecedent-law remand; supplies no religious-aid merits holding here.
- Lee v. Weisman: preserves limits on governmental religious participation and coercion; this private printing benefit compels no student worship.
- Church of Scientology v. Clearwater: distinguishes RFRA burdens and exemptions from constitutional financing authority.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for removal of the religious-viewpoint exclusion under otherwise lawful eligibility, payment and administrative terms. Examine any properly available independent Virginia funding defense consistently with the federal viewpoint holding, without declaring applicability, validity or revival of forfeited defenses. No full payment, damages, immunity, fee or compelled-fee refund is determined.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method. Rosenberger's actual controlled vendor-funded printing benefit remains permissible on its program terms. It concerns financing, not merely facility access; it neither decided Agostini's public-teaching case nor authorized every devotional expenditure.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21 of Wheeling Township, judgment below 980 F.2d 437

#### Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21 of Wheeling Township, judgment below 980 F.2d 437

**Docket or dockets:** Seventh Circuit No. 91-1684; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.

**Decided:** February 12, 1996.

##### This voluntary civic Pledge is distinct from an official worship program

**Holding:** A public elementary school may conduct the daily civic Pledge containing the brief acknowledgment “under God” on this record, where the exercise expresses allegiance to the flag and Republic rather than conducting prayer, devotional instruction or a required religious profession, and pupils may decline words and gestures without punishment or forced exit. This permission does not authorize school-directed worship or compulsory assent merely because it appears within a patriotic program.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Government speech is at issue. The religious reference need not become meaningless to believers. Protected abstention matters within this civic exercise but does not cure actual school worship.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Engel and Schempp's official-prayer and devotional prohibitions, and Lee's official-sponsorship and independent religious-pressure holdings, remain intact. Lynch and Allegheny inform contextual civic acknowledgment; Good News and Pinette's private-expression rules do not characterize this government-selected exercise as private.

##### The program satisfies the existing contextual establishment inquiry

**Holding:** The challenged Pledge program serves a civic educational and ceremonial purpose, has no primary effect of governmental religious advancement in its actual use, and creates no religious-institutional entanglement. Its validity rests on that actual civic function and context, not on a universal historical exemption or voluntariness alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II, joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices. Kennedy, Scalia and Thomas withhold this comprehensive methodology while joining the concrete civic ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Possible disagreement or exclusion alone does not establish primary religious advancement; absence of supported hazing proof does not establish that every child feels no pressure.
