The decision reaches no universal historical exemption, other public motto, adult legislative prayer, historical-instruction or teacher-employment question.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lemon's purpose, effect and entanglement inquiry is applied, informed by Lynch. Lee remains intact.

**Current-force modification:** Effective June 23, 1997, Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test as modified in Agostini v. Felton, Holding I. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform a single inquiry with three routes: controlling specific prohibition or distinct doctrine, established permissible relationship, and residual attribution and function. Clear cases apply that test; its former independent prongs do not remain a separate or fallback method.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553, June 23, 1997, Holdings I–II; First Amendment — Public instruction on religious-school premises.

##### Permission to conduct the program does not permit compelled affirmation

**Holding:** School officials may not require a pupil to recite the Pledge or make gestures affirming it, punish refusal, or require the pupil to leave as the price of declining participation. Because the established practice permits silence, remaining seated, declining the gesture and remaining in the room without penalty, the record supplies no compelled-affirmation ground for relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight direct opinion joins. Stone-Zsela independently accepts the compelled-personal-recitation conclusion but does not join the establishment rationale.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Barnette's protection against compelled words and gestures is applied and preserved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** School-program judgment affirmed, 8–1; no injunction or damages. The separate Attorney General dismissal remains undisturbed, unanimously, without a new holding on its grounds; the unappealed Society claim remains outside review.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727 (1996)

#### Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727 (1996)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 95-124 and 95-227.
**Decided:** June 28, 1996.

##### Whether 1992 §10(a)’s leased-access permission is facially invalid

**Authority of the judgment:** The facial challenge fails, 6–3: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Breyer sustain §10(a); Stone-Zsela, Kennedy and Ginsburg would invalidate it. No controlling sufficient rationale exists under Marks. Souter’s contextual accommodation rationale has Stevens, O’Connor and Breyer; Scalia and Thomas instead rely on the absence of a freestanding right to compel an unwilling private editor. Neither rationale is established as a logical subset of the other. The component judgment does not establish a general permission-review framework.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The retained permission allows, but does not require, prospective enforcement of a written and published policy prohibiting programming reasonably believed to depict or describe sexual/excretory activities or organs in a patently offensive manner under contemporary community standards, specified by the FCC for cable. Protected nonobscene indecency is included; this is not Miller’s full obscenity test and has no categorical serious literary, artistic, political or scientific value exemption, although context matters. No universal validation of indecency definitions, certification, actual policies or genuinely governmental facilities follows.

##### Whether §10(b)’s compulsory identification, segregation and opt-in arrangement violates the First Amendment

**Holding:** When covered leased-access programming is not voluntarily prohibited under §10(a), the government may not impose this integral identification/notification, channel-segregation, default-blocking and advance written-election system. Its cumulative burden on willing speakers and adult recipients fails independent necessity and means review despite compelling child-protection and parental-assistance interests.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part III and V-B, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** Programmers identify and notify; operators concentrate covered speech on blocked capacity. Access requires a written request and FCC-required statement of adult age, fulfilled within thirty days, not a mandatory thirty-day wait; another blocked channel may be used if capacity is insufficient. The operator-installed lockbox accommodation described for systems without premium/pay-per-view channels leaves the operator holding the key/code pending a request and retains default denial and written opt-in. It differs from a household-controlled device. Mixed unpredictable programming creates real parental difficulty but does not justify the integral design’s delay, deterrence and channel-wide burdens. Household controls remain usable after opt-in.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No finding supplies a public registry, universal affordability, perfect technical alternative, implementation of 1996 blocking laws or absence of all supporting evidence. Ordinary age checks, notices, voluntary requests, subscription mechanisms and blocking devices are not individually held unconstitutional. No additional vagueness, prior-restraint or certification ruling is necessary.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Approval reversed, 7–2; the integral compulsory scheme and corresponding rules must be set aside, without a judicial replacement design.

##### Whether §10(c) may authorize suppression of protected indecency on PEG access capacity

**Authority of the judgment:** Approval is reversed within the protected nonobscene-indecency scope, 6–3: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer support relief; O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas oppose it. Souter/Stevens/Breyer’s contextual means/attribution ground and Kennedy/Stone-Zsela/Ginsburg’s strict legislative-content ground each have three votes. Neither is a demonstrated logical subset of the other; no controlling sufficient rationale exists under Marks, and no universal PEG public-forum or state-action rule is adopted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 10(c) directs enabling rules concerning obscenity, sexually explicit conduct and unlawful-conduct solicitation/promotion; it does not compel operator censorship. The FCC interprets sexual explicitness through indecency and the unlawful-conduct category as otherwise proscribed material, permitting operator content certifications. Section (c) lacks (a)’s written/published-policy and reasonable-belief limits and (b)’s subscriber machinery. Lawful obscenity controls remain; unlawful solicitation and freestanding certification are not independently adjudicated, and protected advocacy is not automatically unlawful solicitation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The corresponding rule must be set aside to the extent it authorizes protected-indecency suppression. This is final relief implementing the component judgment, not a remand to reconsider constitutionality after a universal franchise inquiry.

##### Whether §10(a) remains operative and what the judgment reaches

**Holding:** Section 10(a) remains independently operative after §10(b)’s removal. The permission’s independent operation and Congress’s purposes permit severance; the invalid compulsory scheme is removed as an integral whole.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s V-A, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer; six Justices. The seven-Justice V-B coalition controls removal of (b). All nine accept V-D’s boundaries of the adjudicated issues without opponents thereby joining invalidity of a disputed component.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 10(d)’s obscenity exception to access-programming immunity remains and is not general liability for protected indecency. Independent 1996 §506(a) permits refusal on public-access capacity for obscenity, indecency or nudity; its public-access language does not automatically cover all educational/governmental capacity. Section 506(b) separately covers leased access. Neither commands refusal nor imports the 1992 policy/request conditions; their constitutionality and application are not decided or extinguished. Sections 504 and 505 retain their distinct subscriber-request/no-charge and sexually-oriented-channel triggers and transitions; their enactment proves no technical efficacy or actual implementation. Act §561’s three-judge facial-review route is not a new proceeding here. No unconditional carriage right, general private-editor immunity, universal forum designation, damages, new FCC hearing or automatic interim relief follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Remand to implement the three distinct component results within the challenged 1992 authorities.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **ACT/Pacifica (March 4, 1996):** The Court applies its protection of nonobscene indecency and independent necessity/means review to the compulsory (b) arrangement. Its broadcast timetable disposition is not a cable rule. The divided permission opinions preserve the governing protection while disagreeing on application.
- **FCC v. Pacifica Foundation:** Its narrow, contextual broadcast holding remains; it does not automatically sustain every cable indecency restriction or establish that the present protected speech is of lower constitutional value.
- **Sable Communications v. FCC:** The (b) majority applies the distinction between compelling protection of children and adequately justified restrictions on adults. Scalia's separate willing-outlet analysis concerns the permissions and is not the Court's sufficient (b) ground.
- **Turner Broadcasting:** Cable editorial interests retain ordinary speech protection. The prior content-neutral full-power must-carry classification and intermediate standard do not govern an expressly content-selected indecency scheme. Neither the earlier limited shared predictive-review principle nor its fractured record-insufficiency discussion requires remand in every cable case.
- **Hurley:** The protection of an actual private expressive presentation remains intact. The component opinions disagree about the legal consequences of access duties; none establishes that ownership alone makes every independent access program the operator's message.
- **Perry and Cornelius:** Their attention to the actual forum and government's arrangement remains. The Court creates no universal cable-access forum; Kennedy's conditional forum ground does not become such a holding.
- **Rosenberger, Pinette and Chabad:** Their program-specific viewpoint, attribution and contextual rules are not converted into a presumption that every regulated channel is a public forum or governmental speaker. The divided opinions' use of those limits creates no new general forum or attribution test.
- **Blum, Jackson and Lugar; Reitman and Crawford:** The distinction between a private act and governmental alteration or encouragement remains relevant. No categorical operator immunity or rule making every private refusal government action is adopted.
- **Alaska Airlines:** The Court applies independent operation and congressional intent in retaining (a) after invalidating (b).

No earlier decision is overruled. The separate opinions' broader grounds do not independently alter controlling law.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Settle v. Dickson County School Board

#### Settle v. Dickson County School Board

**Docket or dockets:** Sixth Circuit No. 93-6207; no separate Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** June 25, 1996.

##### What genuine curricular grounds may limit a religious research topic

**Holding:** A teacher may enforce genuine topic-approval, research, source and academic-method requirements reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns, and a student's religious convictions create no exemption from them. But curricular discretion does not make categorical exclusion of an otherwise permissible religious perspective, or a mistaken blanket legal prohibition on academic religious subjects, a legitimate ground merely because it is described as educational judgment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part I, unanimous.

**Material application:** The teacher-only submission is not presumed publicly attributable to the school or an open forum. Objective inquiry may be distinguished from devotion or personal testimony. Genuine factual or evaluative mistakes can remain pedagogical judgments, while a legally impermissible categorical religious ban does not become valid as an honest academic mistake. The full policy permits objective, respectful, balanced academic religious treatment without promoting tenets or demeaning belief and accommodates student-initiated belief or nonbelief; neither provision guarantees approval.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal curricular viewpoint neutrality, unrestricted topic choice, religion-based exemption, perfect-accuracy duty or independent Free Exercise, Establishment or equal-protection holding.

##### Whether the summary-judgment record establishes lawful cause or the same-decision defense

**Holding:** Summary judgment cannot rest on a merely possible pedagogical justification when supported evidence permits a material inference that a student's work was rejected for an impermissible religious reason rather than legitimate application of assignment requirements. Under Mt. Healthy, the student must establish protected conduct and its substantial or motivating role; defendants may then prove by a preponderance that the same adverse decision would have occurred without that conduct.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Part II, unanimous.

**Material application:** Failure to secure permission was supported but its independent causal effect was disputed by the next-day no-penalty alternative, mixed reasons and original-choice answer, which also covered Buddha and Muhammed. Faith alone does not prove inability to research. Familiarity does not prove absence of new inquiry. Source requirements must reflect permitted secondary and outside sources, not a new Bible-only or school-library-only premise. Comparator descriptions are relevant without proving identical circumstances.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No pretext, hostility, final causation, liability or automatic defeat of a proved same-decision defense is found. Waters supplies its shared genuine-ground distinction, not an imported employment investigation code. Graduation proves no universal mootness; actual capacities, municipal attribution, immunity and remedies remain open. Conduct-date immunity notice concerns March–April 1991, not later decisions.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacated and remanded, 9–0, for ordinary proceedings on the free-speech claim. No compulsory approval, reading, grading, credit, removal of the zero, damages, new discovery procedure or trial; another supported lawful disposition remains possible.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260:** Applies legitimate pedagogical control to this graded research assignment, preserving genuine method and quality decisions and reasonable academic error. Its public-attribution rationale is not automatically transferred to a teacher-only submission.
- **Tinker v. Des Moines Independent Community School District, 393 U.S. 503:** Preserves student expression without creating unrestricted topic choice or displacing genuine assignment requirements.
- **Epperson v. Arkansas, 393 U.S. 97:** Applies the distinction between curricular authority and constitutional immunity, and between objective academic study of religion and governmental religious instruction. No new religion-clause claim is adjudicated.
- **Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District, June 7, 1993; Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, June 29, 1995; and Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, December 11, 1995:** Preserve the distinction between genuine program boundaries and religious-viewpoint exclusion in their actual settings. They do not classify this assignment as a forum or establish a right to a particular grade.
- **Lee v. Weisman, June 24, 1992, and Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, February 12, 1996:** Preserve their limits on official worship and coercive religious participation without creating a blanket prohibition on academic religious subjects.
- **Waters v. Churchill, May 31, 1994:** Applies the genuine-reliance and causation distinction at its shared scope. Its separate investigation approaches and public-employment balance are not adopted as a classroom test.
- **Mt. Healthy City School District Board of Education v. Doyle, 429 U.S. 274:** Applies the claimant's protected-conduct and substantial-or-motivating-factor burden and defendants' separate preponderance same-decision defense. A possible lawful reason is not sufficient proof, and partial improper motivation does not automatically establish entitlement to relief.

No precedent is overruled.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District

#### Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 94-60631; 88 F.3d 274 identifies the judgment below.
**Decided:** March 19, 1997.

For personal standing and both intervention rulings, see *Ingebretsen*, March 19, 1997, No. 94-60631 (Federal Courts — Standing and intervention in school-religion litigation).

##### Whether student delivery or a disclaimer insulates officially organized school worship

**Holding:** The Establishment Clause forbids a school's organization, adoption, direction, or religious supervision of worship within its own event. Student initiation or delivery and a statutory disclaimer do not alone make that activity private, and official sponsorship can violate the Clause without a separate quantified-pressure showing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, and Breyer; six Justices directly adopt the sponsorship ground.

**Material application:** Section 1(2)'s permission for student-initiated, nonsectarian, nonproselytizing prayer at compulsory and noncompulsory events cannot be administered through official devotional organization or religious supervision. Section 1(4)'s disclaimer cannot erase actual official conduct. Section 2's prohibition on prescribing prayer's form or content is a legal command, not a finding that officials always obey or violate it.

**Independent alternative holding:** School direction of religious exercise also violates the Clause when compulsory attendance or comparable practical pressures create actual pressure to participate. Calling participation voluntary or using a student speaker does not eliminate that inquiry; offense at another student's private viewpoint is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Part III, joined by Ginsburg, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer; six direct votes independently support this ground, without a *Marks* synthesis.

**Limits, remedy, transition, or precedent treatment:** *Lee* supplies the separate sponsorship and school-pressure rules. Pressure requires no quantified measurement or legal penalty, but is not presumed from hearing private religious speech or attending any voluntary gathering. The hearing-supported threat justifies bounded interim protection, not final invalidation of every statutory application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Pinette* and *Chabad of Southern Ohio* require contextual attribution; a disclaimer or forum label is not conclusive. *Rosenberger* preserves neutral aid to independently edited private speech. *Lamb's Chapel*, *Good News Club*, and *Brown v. Polk County* preserve independent expression without making every school event a private forum or importing employment burdens.

##### Whether the preliminary injunction may exempt graduation categorically or suppress independently lawful expression

**Holding:** Interim relief must address the supported unlawful official mechanism while preserving genuinely private religious expression and independently lawful provisions. Graduation has no automatic student-initiation or student-delivery exception to the official-attribution and pressure inquiries.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV's private-expression and lawful-provision limit has all nine joins. Continued restraint, removal of the graduation exemption, and remand are adopted by Ginsburg, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer; Scalia and Thomas would also vacate the retained restraint.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the supported restraint, vacate the graduation safe harbor and overbroad reach, and remand for a severed, provision- and event-sensitive interim order. Give effect separately to §1(3)'s other voluntary expression, §2's voluntary-prayer provision and prohibition on prescribing content, and §1(5)'s severability direction.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new forum or wider access entitlement is created by judicial deletion of the religious filters. State-law construction and precise decree terms remain below; no final damages claim or universal statutory invalidity is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Lee* forecloses the lower-court *Jones II* criteria as a categorical graduation safe harbor while leaving genuine private graduation speech protected.

## Source: state/holdings/first-amendment.md — Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton

#### Agostini v. Felton; Chancellor, Board of Education of the City of New York v. Felton

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.
**Decided:** June 23, 1997.

**Material judgment:** Both judgments vacated and remanded, 5–4, for tailored prospective Rule 60(b)(5) relief; Lemon modified as the governing test; Aguilar's categorical rule overruled and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale displaced at the stated scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** All four holdings are delivered by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela in the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Each has five direct joins at its stated level of generality, including the modification of Lemon in Holding I. No aggregation of separate opinions supplies controlling authority.

##### I. Lemon governs through one inquiry into governmental religious authority and function

**Holding:** Lemon remains the governing Establishment Clause test, modified so that purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence within one inquiry: a specific prohibition or distinct doctrine controls when it resolves the conduct; an established permissible relationship defeats the claim on that ground; otherwise a legally sufficient unresolved allegation is decided through attribution and function under ordinary procedure. These are three routes within the updated Lemon test, not freestanding prongs, exemptions from review or an alternative test for uncertain cases.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join this modification in full, supplying five direct joins at this level of generality.

**Operative qualifications:** The court identifies the governmental act—an official message, organized event, exercise of supervisory power or expenditure—and the function it performs. For financing, the question is what government purchases or performs; all religious conduct of the recipient need not become government speech. Private-access authorization, religious identity and physical location are relevant in context but are not individually decisive.

Government may not organize worship as its own activity, prescribe religious profession or observance, adopt doctrine as governmental truth, solicit religious adherence through public authority, govern theology or confer civil authority on a religious basis. Denominational classifications retain their distinct strict-scrutiny rule. Independently identifiable secular work in a religious setting may be purchased with enforceable controls against devotional use and diversion. Restoration of private exercise restricted by custody or comparable governmental control remains subject to the applicable accommodation inquiry, not an automatic financing prohibition. Those distinct doctrines retain their own sources and burdens.

**Controlling explanation:** Lemon addresses governmental sponsorship of religion, financing of religious functions and the improper union of civil and religious authority. Its method must identify those acts rather than condemn an independently secular service because officials supervise it to remain secular. Aguilar exposes that defect: location and repeated contact become substitutes for examining what the public program actually does. The Court therefore modifies Lemon's operation while retaining its constitutional concerns.

Purpose and effects help identify the governmental function; institutional relationships show who directs it. Once prohibited conduct is established, actual conversion, religious success or a completed penalty is unnecessary. A secular objective cannot justify prohibited religious means. Conversely, a provider's religious motivation does not make its secular work devotional. Ordinary monitoring of secular performance is not itself establishment, even when repeated; civil control of doctrine and religious control of public authority remain forbidden.

This method requires real operational separation and reliable accountability for funded work, but not invariably separate institutional governance or wholly distinct personnel. Shared structures cannot excuse devotional financing, and they do not automatically defeat a genuinely severable public service. Here public selection, staffing, materials and supervision identify the remedial service to be examined. The school's entire religious mission is not the governmental act.

The dissent's institutional-support objection warrants scrutiny of the service, not a presumption that its secular and religious functions are inseparable. Existing specific holdings continue to govern their established relationships: Kiryas Joel restricts denominational allocation of civil power; Rosenberger permits its controlled publication benefit; Pinette and Chabad distinguish private expression and governmental attribution. The Court does not decide absent voucher, monument, legislative-prayer or accommodation programs, or declare every future application compatible with every preserved precedent.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no tuition-voucher or provider-redemption scheme, monument, legislative-prayer program, general accommodation rule or all forms of direct aid. Parental selection does not itself answer what a particular expenditure purchases; no conclusion about an absent program displaces Witters or Mueller. Religious editorial viewpoint in Rosenberger is not automatically purchased devotional instruction. Private speech, free exercise, institutional religious autonomy, denominational scrutiny and accommodation retain their distinct sources and burdens. No controlling rationale is drawn from separate opinions under Marks.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lemon v. Kurtzman remains governing, with its independent purpose, primary-effect and excessive-entanglement prongs modified into the three routes of this single inquiry. The actual holdings of Kiryas Joel, Rosenberger, Pinette, Chabad, Witters and Mueller remain in force; Zobrest supplies no Establishment Clause merits holding. Kiryas Joel's denominational-allocation and accommodation holdings remain intact; its preservation of Aguilar and Ball established the rule entering this decision, not a bar to the Court's express overruling. Zobrest's antecedent-law remand remains; it supplied no Establishment Clause merits holding and did not already undermine Aguilar by such a holding. Rosenberger's actual controlled vendor-funded printing benefit remains permissible on its program terms; it concerns financing, not merely facility access, and neither decided this public-teaching case nor authorized every devotional expenditure. Pinette's bounded private-expression and access holding remains; it supplies no universal forum immunity or automatic attribution from location. Chabad's contextual-attribution and access holding remains; location alone does not establish governmental sponsorship. Witters's actual benefit holding remains in force; no absent tuition-voucher or provider-redemption scheme is decided. Mueller's actual tax-benefit holding remains in force; the Court does not decide an absent funding program or silently enlarge or displace that holding.

##### II. Separately administered secular instruction is not forbidden because of its religious-school location

**Holding:** The Establishment Clause permits the described supplementary Title I service for children independently selected under neutral public eligibility criteria, delivered by public employees under public supervision with public secular materials, without religious instruction, team teaching or other cooperative instructional activity with religious-school teachers, or participation in worship, and with effective controls against diversion. Religious-school location, presumed inculcation arising from that location, incidental benefit to the school, and ordinary monitoring of that separately administered public service do not by themselves establish unconstitutional sponsorship, devotional financing or institutional control; Aguilar's categorical prohibition and Ball's corresponding Shared Time rationale are displaced at that scope.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the Court's opinion; the five-Justice coalition stated above directly joins the rule, its secular-control qualifications and its precise treatment of precedent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the constitutional inquiry must distinguish the public remedial service from the religious school's own instruction. Lemon, as updated in Part I, identifies the governmental act and its religious or secular function, using purpose, effects and institutional relationships as evidence within that inquiry. Here the remedial purpose, independent selection of eligible children, public staffing and supervision, secular materials and restrictions on religious participation identify a supplementary public service. Those safeguards establish this program's severable secular operation; operational separation and reliable accountability do not invariably require wholly distinct personnel or separate institutional governance. Ordinary consultation about a child's secular educational needs is distinct from joint teaching. Neutral eligibility or public employment alone would not excuse devotional instruction or diversion.

Aguilar treated the supervision needed to preserve secular instruction as a reason to prohibit the instruction on religious premises. That categorical inference is unsound: supervising public teachers' secular work is not, without more, governing the school's religious affairs. The absence of a finding of indoctrination does not prove every possible defect absent, but location cannot substitute for the connection between the challenged public service and an actual constitutional objection. Nor does an incidental institutional benefit alone turn this separately controlled service into religious teaching.

These safeguards existed when Aguilar was decided. The legal defect is the categorical inference that confuses secular supervision with religious sponsorship and control, not a newly discovered factual transformation. Continuing expense and impaired access make the consequence substantial; they are not the constitutional rule. Property, contractual, completed-transaction and educational-service interests receive their actual weight. No identified concrete property or completed-transaction entitlement requires retaining the categorical ban; established obligations and service continuity remain protected in implementation. Ball's Shared Time program implicated the corresponding inference about supplementary public teaching. Its distinct Community Education program predominantly used religious-school teachers and principals, and today's holding neither approves that arrangement nor reopens its judgment. The onsite rule applies Lemon as modified in Part I; the Court does not approve every program considered in Ball or reopen its completed judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Independent prohibitions on governmental worship, devotional financing and improper institutional control remain; public employment and neutral eligibility do not immunize supported devotional conduct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Aguilar v. Felton is overruled as authority for categorically excluding this safeguarded public Title I service from religious-school premises; no reopening of completed claims or authorization of religious instruction follows. School District of Grand Rapids v. Ball loses its contrary Shared Time rationale at the same scope, insofar as religious location, presumed inculcation, incidental school benefit or ordinary secular monitoring categorically condemns the separately administered public service; its Community Education judgment and unaddressed grounds remain undisturbed. Lemon v. Kurtzman governs through the updated inquiry stated in Holding I.

For the significant-change inquiry and injunction remedy in Holdings III–IV, see Federal Courts, “Modification of continuing injunctions,” Agostini, June 23, 1997, Nos. 96-552 and 96-553.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc., 510 U.S. 17

#### Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc., 510 U.S. 17

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1168.

**Decided:** 1993-11-09.

##### Hostile employment conditions do not require psychological injury

**Holding:** Sex-based harassment violates Title VII when, considered in all the circumstances, it is sufficiently severe or pervasive to alter employment conditions and create an environment a reasonable person would find hostile or abusive and that the employee actually perceives as abusive; serious psychological injury, treatment, diagnosis, and lost productivity are not prerequisites. Relevant circumstances include frequency, severity, physical threat or humiliation rather than a merely offensive utterance, and unreasonable interference with work, but no listed consideration is indispensable; psychological effects may inform subjective abuse, while offensive conduct that does not meet the objective and subjective requirements remains insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; O'Connor and those eight Justices supply nine votes for the complete rule and reversal with remand. Direct unanimous authority; no fractured-opinion inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Meritor Savings Bank, FSB v. Vinson*, 477 U.S. 57 (1986): applies and clarifies its severe-or-pervasive alteration-of-conditions rule; its extreme examples do not impose a psychological-injury element. Its employer-responsibility questions are not enlarged here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Mere offense remains insufficient. Meritor's employer-responsibility issues and Hicks's persuasion rules are not altered; proposed EEOC regulations and a sex-specific reasonable-person formulation are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Sixth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for application of the objective and subjective standard without an injury prerequisite; liability, constructive discharge and damages are not decided.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc.

#### Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 95-30510; 83 F.3d 118 identifies the judgment below.
**Decided:** March 19, 1997.

##### Whether Title VII excludes harassment by a person of the employee's own sex

**Holding:** Title VII's protection against discriminatory employment conditions includes same-sex harassment. The employee must still plead and ultimately prove discrimination because of sex and every other element of the asserted claim; common sex supplies neither immunity nor liability.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Meritor* supplies coverage of discriminatory abusive conditions, extended here to same-sex actors without eliminating any statutory element.

##### Whether sexual desire or an actual opposite-sex comparator is necessary to establish discrimination

**Holding:** Competent direct or circumstantial evidence can establish discrimination because of sex without proof of desire, a particular sexual orientation, or an actual opposite-sex coworker. Sexual content, contact, or the gravity of conduct does not alone conclusively establish that causal element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** Sex-directed hostility, selective treatment, discriminatory statements, threats, and surrounding circumstances may support the inference. These examples create neither exclusive proof channels nor a compulsory checklist.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court finds no participant's sexual orientation or motive and decides no independent sexual-orientation coverage question. Personal hostility unrelated to sex is outside this cause of action.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Hicks* preserves ultimate persuasion and permissible circumstantial inference; it does not impose a compulsory desire showing or comparator. *Brown v. Polk County* and *Nabozny* retain their distinct statutory and constitutional scopes.

##### Whether a single-sex workplace changes the hostile-environment standard

**Holding:** Sex-based conduct must be severe or pervasive enough to produce an objectively hostile or abusive workplace in context, and the employee must actually perceive it as abusive. Workforce composition informs context but creates no exemption; psychiatric injury and measurable productivity loss are unnecessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** Workplace custom cannot automatically recast forced physical humiliation or rape threats as harmless camaraderie. The allegations remain unproved, and their gravity does not establish unwelcome treatment, sex causation, or attribution.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No single circumstance, diagnosis, treatment, medical proof, or universal reasonable-woman formulation is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Harris* supplies the contextual severe-or-pervasive inquiry and its separate objective and subjective requirements, without an economic or psychiatric injury prerequisite.

##### Which attribution and remedial issues survive removal of the categorical bar

**Holding:** Employer attribution, if reached, requires application of *Andrade*: actual delegated supervisory authority must materially aid creation or maintenance of the unlawful environment. A supervisory title alone does not establish that connection, and lack of higher-management notice alone does not defeat this agency route; negligence and a preserved quid-pro-quo theory remain distinct.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The agency route does not invariably require a completed employment action or economic loss. A quid-pro-quo claim separately requires a supported employment benefit or detriment conditioned on sexual participation. No strict employer liability, new affirmative defense, individual-coworker rule, or finding about Lyons's statutory status is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the categorical ground as to affected Sundowner and Lyons claims and remand under ordinary procedure. Pippen and Johnson's separate not-an-employer dismissal remains outside review, without endorsement of a new immunity rule. Liability, factual sufficiency, constructive discharge, discovery, trial entitlement, and available relief for the 1991 conduct remain undecided; properly presented independent defenses survive.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Andrade* supplies the authority-aided agency rule and preserves negligence as another route. The lower-court *Garcia* same-sex exclusion cannot govern after this decision.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86

#### John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1074.

**Decided:** 1993-12-13.

For the other questions in John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 1993-12-13, No. 92-1074, see Administrative Law — An agency interpretation cannot erase the qualified exclusion.

##### The guaranteed-benefit exclusion operates by component

**Holding:** Under ERISA §401(b)(2), a guaranteed-benefit policy itself is a plan asset, while the insurer's underlying assets are not deemed plan assets solely because it issued the policy to the extent the policy genuinely guarantees the amount of benefits. The disputed nonguaranteed free-funds component is not excluded: its plan-asset character subjects Hancock to fiduciary obligations to the extent it exercises authority or control over management or disposition of those assets under §3(21)(A), without deciding breach, causation, loss, or remedy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, and Souter. Six Justices adopt the component-specific construction and its application to the disputed free funds.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- SEC v. United Benefit Life Insurance Co.: used for the distinction between a genuine guarantee and retained investment risk; the ERISA text supplies the governing exclusion.
- Mertens v. Hewitt Associates: kept separate; its treatment of available equitable categories does not decide whether these funds are plan assets or whether Hancock committed a breach.

##### Insurance regulation does not displace ERISA's express direction

**Holding:** McCarran-Ferguson does not bar application of ERISA's express insurance-related fiduciary and guaranteed-benefit provisions to this general-account contract. ERISA's insurance-saving clause preserves compatible state insurance regulation, but does not make a conflicting state obligation controlling over ERISA itself; a concrete conflict must be assessed as such rather than treated as a categorical exemption for all insurer assets.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, and Souter. This six-Justice ground rejects the asserted state-insurance obstacle to affirmance.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Massachusetts: applied to the coexistence of ERISA and state insurance regulation; no blanket withdrawal of federal obligations follows.
- FMC Corp. v. Holliday: applied to the coexistence of ERISA and state insurance regulation; no blanket withdrawal of federal obligations follows.
- Silkwood v. Kerr-McGee Corp.: applied for the distinction between compatible state regulation and a conflicting obstacle to federal law.
- United States Department of Treasury v. Fabe: distinguished because its federal-priority and policyholder-protection issue is different; its holding remains intact.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The contract contains guarantees and a residual floor; those do not guarantee the disputed free-funds benefits. A guaranteed reasonable return and fixed conversion protection are indicators, not an exhaustive mandatory formula. No breach, loss or remedy follows from status alone, and no categorical anti-deference rule for inconsistency or litigation positions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Second Circuit's relevant judgment is affirmed, 6–3. Asset status and applicable fiduciary functions remain distinct from breach, causation, loss and relief, which must be addressed in further proceedings; no monetary or equitable award is made.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — North Star Steel Co. v. Thomas / Crown Cork & Seal Co., Inc. v. United Steelworkers of America, AFL-CIO-CLC

#### North Star Steel Co. v. Thomas / Crown Cork & Seal Co., Inc. v. United Steelworkers of America, AFL-CIO-CLC

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 94-834; 94-835.
**Decided:** May 30, 1995.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** North Star Steel Co. v. Thomas / Crown Cork & Seal Co., Inc. v. United Steelworkers of America, AFL-CIO-CLC. The dockets share the holdings at their stated component scopes.

##### Source and application of the WARN limitations period

**Holding:** For these WARN claims, courts must borrow an appropriate analogous state limitations period because NLRA § 10(b) is neither a clearly closer analogy nor shown significantly more suitable through federal policy and litigation practicalities. Both claims are timely under the state candidates presented; the Court need not select an exact statute or decide the WARN merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices directly support the rule and application. Scalia joins only the judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Federal borrowing requires both a clearly closer federal analogy and significantly greater suitability from federal policy and litigation practicalities. Section 1658 supplies no period for this 1988 cause. Arbitration, exhaustion, statutory deferral and associated tolling are outside review; the earlier union action does not dispose of the separate employees' claims.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- DelCostello v. Teamsters, 462 U.S. 151: preserves the ordinary state-borrowing rule and confines its federal exception to a genuinely closer analogy and significantly greater federal suitability; its hybrid labor-settlement application is distinguished.
- Reed v. United Transportation Union, 488 U.S. 319: applies its restrictive account of the exception; a labor setting alone remains insufficient.
- Fort Halifax Packing Co. v. Coyne, 482 U.S. 1: applies its separation of independent substantive employment protections from bargaining mechanisms; no arbitration question is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm both Third Circuit judgments: in No. 94-834, preserve reversal of the employees' limitations dismissal; in No. 94-835, preserve rejection of the six-month defense. Both claims are timely under the state candidates presented; no exact period, liability, damages, accrual, tolling or WARN merits exception is decided.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers' Association

#### DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers' Association

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit No. 93-8700.

**Decided:** January 6, 1997.

##### The credited course of publication does not meet Title VII's objective threshold

**Holding:** Sex-based harassment violates Title VII when the complete circumstances establish conduct sufficiently severe or pervasive to alter employment conditions, objectively hostile or abusive and actually perceived as abusive; no physical advance, superior-subordinate relationship, psychological injury or measurable work loss is indispensable. Crediting this verdict record and favorable reasonable inferences, the supported course of newsletter commentary falls below that objective threshold, so the Association's hostile-environment judgment is affirmed.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; six direct joins. The seven-Justice hostile-environment judgment includes Stone-Zsela, who does not join the statutory ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court disapproves requirements of destroyed equal opportunity or palpable performance deterioration. General commentary, workplace distribution and favorable verdict inferences count; contextual evidence of good ratings and support is not an indispensable defense. The First Amendment defense is not reached and no newsletter immunity is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Harris is applied without adding a prerequisite; Meritor's alteration-of-conditions boundary remains controlling. No constitutional speech precedent is extended by this holding.

##### The complaint-related publications establish no actionable retaliatory consequence here

**Holding:** Protected activity and hostile commentary do not alone establish Title VII retaliation without a cognizable adverse consequence. The mocking EEOC reference, routine minutes reporting the complaint and announced intention to sue if the action proved groundless establish no denial of an employment or Association benefit, concrete imposed penalty or comparably supported material disadvantage on this record; judgment for the Association is therefore affirmed.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision does not confine retaliation to ultimate employment decisions or categorically exclude threats, harassment or public shaming on stronger facts. No comprehensive retaliation standard is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Harris's distinction between offensive treatment and legally sufficient discrimination is preserved at its hostile-environment scope; its injury rule is not converted into a comprehensive retaliation test.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm judgment for the Association: hostile environment 7-2, retaliation unanimously. No liability or damages are restored.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Andrade v. Mayfair Management, Inc.

#### Andrade v. Mayfair Management, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 95-2476.
**Decided:** February 26, 1997.

##### Whether delegated authority can support employer liability without higher-management notice

**Holding:** Title VII permits attribution of a supervisor-created hostile environment to the employer when authority actually delegated to the supervisor materially assists the harassment. Rank and workplace proximity alone do not suffice, but lack of actual or constructive notice to higher management does not defeat this agency route; negligent failure to correct known or reasonably knowable harassment remains a separate basis.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), unanimous; all nine Justices join both holdings.

**Material application:** Hiring, firing, discipline, threatened personnel action and daily control of working conditions can supply the necessary connection. An express threat, completed personnel action or economic loss is not always required. The administrator’s hiring and firing powers therefore required examination despite his reporting to a corporate officer.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision adopts neither automatic liability for every supervisor’s private misconduct nor a new affirmative-defense regime. Whether an actionable environment exists remains distinct from whether the employer is responsible for it.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Meritor Savings Bank v. Vinson requires an actual agency inquiry rather than either automatic imputation or an exclusive notice test. Harris v. Forklift Systems and DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers’ Association govern the separate severity-or-pervasiveness inquiry.

##### How the erroneous notice-only premise affects the employer’s Rule 50 judgment

**Holding:** A Rule 50 judgment that forecloses supervisory-agency attribution solely because higher management lacked notice must be reconsidered under the correct attribution rule, drawing permissible evidentiary inferences for the verdict winner. That legal error does not itself establish attribution or resolve preserved alternative grounds.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Evidentiary sufficiency, instructions and preservation remain for proper consideration below; constructive discharge is outside the grant.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The reviewed employer-attribution judgment is vacated and remanded. The $25,000 verdict is not automatically restored, no new trial is automatically required, and the constructive-discharge disposition is undisturbed.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Faragher v. City of Boca Raton

#### Faragher v. City of Boca Raton

**Docket or dockets:** Eleventh Circuit No. 94-4878; no separate Supreme Court docket number is supplied.

**Decided:** September 8, 1997.

##### Whether actual delegated authority establishes attribution without higher-management notice

**Holding:** Actual delegated supervisory authority materially aiding creation or maintenance of an actionable hostile environment supplies an independent ground for employer responsibility under Title VII; the plaintiff must establish that connection. Supervisory rank or workplace proximity alone is insufficient; higher-management notice, an express threat, a completed personnel decision and tangible economic loss are not invariably required.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary employment scope, apparent authority and negligence remain distinct alternatives and are not decided. An actionable environment and discriminatory causation remain separate predicates. Personal purpose and violation of an employer prohibition do not alone negate actual aid.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Meritor requires agency analysis and rejects both automatic supervisory liability and notice-only immunity. Andrade supplies the plaintiff-proved actual-power connection; Oncale preserves distinct causation, environment and attribution inquiries. Harris and DeAngelis govern hostile-environment severity, not attribution or constructive notice.

##### Whether the supported operational findings establish material aid in this work unit

**Holding:** The established allocation and exercise of assignments, discipline, daily duties and supervision in this work unit materially aided maintenance of its accepted hostile conditions. These supported bench findings establish the actual-authority ground without a new threat, fear or employment-penalty finding, and do not require a further attribution proceeding.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–IV, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight votes. Stone-Zsela joins Part I only and opposes this conclusive application and restoration mandate.

**Material application:** Actual control over daily work, assignments and discipline in the geographically separate unit supported the connection to maintaining its hostile conditions. Historical findings receive clear-error review; legal significance receives independent review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new subjective fear, discharge threat, order to submit, lost wage or employment-penalty finding is added. Remoteness and failed policy communication are relevant circumstances, not universal prerequisites or substitutes for actual aid.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Meritor's unresolved findings and Andrade's Rule 50 questions warranted their own remands; Oncale's allegation-stage posture likewise does not require renewed factfinding on these adequate bench findings.

##### Whether the ineffective policy and eventual response defeat this proved attribution

**Holding:** In this case, the combination of a policy not communicated to the relevant workforce, failure to complain to distant higher management and correction after another employee's eventual report does not defeat the proved authority-aided liability. The Court leaves open whether genuinely effective protection and unreasonable employee nonuse could establish a complete defense, including its elements, burden, relationship to tangible employment action and any distinct limitation on particular relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–IV, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight votes. Stone-Zsela joins Part I only and opposes this conclusive application and restoration mandate.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general complete defense, categorical rejection of every defense, burden-shifting formula, tangible-action exception, universal complaint duty or freestanding dissemination duty is adopted. Effective measures, negligence, apparent authority and avoidable-loss issues retain significance when their predicates exist; dissemination failure alone is not negligence per se.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Meritor does not make paper policy and noncomplaint conclusive. Andrade's reserved defense question remains open; later correction does not retroactively erase earlier attributable misconduct.

##### What relief the supported Title VII findings require

**Holding:** The appellate judgment is reversed only insofar as it reversed Faragher's Title VII judgment against the City, and the case is remanded for reinstatement of the existing one-dollar judgment without a new merits trial or further agency findings. No individual or state-law award, remaining appellate disposition, fee entitlement or additional remedy is adjudicated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Parts II–IV, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight votes. Stone-Zsela joins Part I only and opposes this conclusive application and restoration mandate.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reinstate the existing one-dollar City judgment without another trial or attribution findings. Individual §1983 and battery awards, other state claims and remaining appellate dispositions are undisturbed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Landgraf prevents new §102 compensatory and punitive liability for the 1985–1990 conduct and forecloses its dependent new jury right. Farrar distinguishes nominal relief from reasonable fees under §1988, supplying only an analogy; Title VII fees retain their own requirements.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Harrison v. Eddy Potash, Inc.

#### Harrison v. Eddy Potash, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** Tenth Circuit Nos. 96-2045 and 96-2065; no separate Supreme Court docket number is supplied.

**Decided:** September 15, 1997.

##### Whether material aid requires alter-ego or hire-and-fire authority

**Holding:** An employer may be responsible under Title VII when actual delegated supervisory power materially aids creation or maintenance of an actionable hostile environment; the plaintiff must prove that connection by a preponderance of the evidence. Hiring or firing authority, alter-ego identity, higher-management notice, an express threat, a completed personnel action and tangible economic loss are not invariably required, while rank, employment or workplace proximity alone is insufficient.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–V, unanimous.

**Material application:** Evidence that the foreman used daily work orders to isolate Harrison supported this route without proving every alleged incident. Personal purpose, a harassment prohibition and rejection of status-alone liability do not remove the requirement to instruct an adequately supported theory.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Harris's objective-and-subjective severe-or-pervasive standard and Oncale's sex-causation requirement remain distinct; psychological injury, treatment or lost productivity is not mandatory and grave misconduct alone proves neither causation nor attribution.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Meritor supplies agency analysis; Andrade, Oncale and Faragher require actual delegated power materially aiding the wrong without categorical management-notice or personnel-action barriers. Harris and DeAngelis retain the contextual environment standard.

##### Whether the charge prejudicially excluded the supported actual-authority route

**Holding:** Considered as a whole against this record, the preserved charge was prejudicially incomplete because it made apparent authority and reasonable reliance cumulative requirements, imposed an alter-ego barrier and omitted the supported actual-authority/material-aid route. If a distinct apparent-authority theory is properly submitted, it requires reasonable belief traceable to the employer's words or conduct and reasonable reliance on that appearance, not merely the supervisor's claimed power; that alternative is not a condition of actual-authority attribution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–V, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The whole charge receives independent legal review with prejudice required; the choice of particular proposed wording remains a different inquiry. No special verdict establishes an independent correct ground for the employer verdict. The separate tort judgment against Brown proves no complete statutory case against Potash. Apparent-authority factual sufficiency is not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The actual-authority omission independently sustains the instructional reversal; no second appellate prejudice determination is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Restatement §27 supplies employer-attributable manifestations and reasonable reliance for a distinct apparent-authority theory. Faragher did not decide that alternative; its actual-authority rule is not extended to every assertion of power.

##### Whether policy and response cure the omitted route or establish a complete defense

**Holding:** A prohibition of harassment and correction after notice do not themselves negate earlier material aid from actual delegated power or cure the prejudicially incomplete charge. The Court preserves Faragher's reservation concerning a general complete defense based on genuinely effective prevention and unreasonable employee nonuse, including its elements, burden, relation to tangible employment action and any distinct limitation on relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–V, unanimous.

**Material application:** The posted policy discovered after some incidents, its omission from new-hire materials, and the later investigation, discipline, crew change and contact restrictions may matter to their proper inquiries; they do not themselves remove earlier actual aid.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No complete defense is supplied to Potash. Universal policy ignorance, unavailable reporting routes, unreasonable nonuse and inadequate response are not found. No policy-publication duty, general reporting duty or new burden-shifting formula follows; dissemination failure is not negligence per se.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Faragher's general-defense reservation remains unchanged. Meritor distinguishes unwelcome conduct from apparently voluntary participation; the investigator's account is no judicial finding that the conduct was welcome.

##### Whether the asserted contractual grievance requirement bars this independent Title VII action

**Holding:** A contractual grievance assertion does not remove federal subject-matter jurisdiction over an independent Title VII claim. Potash cannot obtain dismissal for nonexhaustion on this appellate record, which includes neither its CBA nor the district court's exhaustion ruling and identifies no agreement covering arbitration of statutory claims or delegation of the antecedent question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–V, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The appellate omissions do not establish that no contract existed or that the employer waived every claim. The Court declines a blanket CBA/FAA rule, reserves the general employment-contract exclusion and validity of every explicit collective statutory-arbitration agreement, and reaches no conditional post-delegation review question.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm rejection of the employer's cross-appeal in No. 96-2065; no new contract-development or exhaustion proceeding is ordered to cure the unsupported assertion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Gardner-Denver preserves independent Title VII rights despite contractual grievance arbitration. Gilmer enforces an actual statutory-arbitration agreement without supplying missing terms. First Options requires independent judicial determination of assent and clear, unmistakable delegation. Livadas and Hawaiian Airlines protect rights whose adjudication does not require disputed contract interpretation without creating immunity from every arbitration agreement.

##### What mandate and remedial limits govern further Title VII proceedings

**Holding:** The Tenth Circuit's reversal of Potash's Title VII verdict and remand in No. 96-2045, and its rejection of Potash's cross-appeal in No. 96-2065, are affirmed. The remand contemplates a new Title VII trial under corrected instructions, subject to lawful resolution of genuinely preserved independent grounds; if the surviving claim is submitted to a jury, the supported actual-authority route must be accurately instructed, with the prejudicial error already resolved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–V, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The 1993 conduct is outside Landgraf's pre-enactment bar but establishes no damages entitlement. Section 102 compensation excludes backpay, backpay interest and other §706(g) relief. Punitive damages require malice or reckless indifference to federally protected rights. If available §102 compensatory or punitive relief is sought, either party may demand a jury, which must not hear the caps. No amount, cap calculation or punitive predicate is found; duplicate compensation remains prohibited.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm both appellate results. The employer claim proceeds toward retrial under corrected instructions, subject to lawful genuinely preserved independent grounds. Brown's $102,500 compensatory and $40,000 punitive tort awards remain undisturbed; no employer award, fee, reinstatement or new termination claim is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Faragher's nominal restoration on accepted bench findings supplies no jury-case findings. Landgraf retains its separate compensation, punitive and jury conditions without limiting this later conduct to nominal relief.

## Source: state/holdings/labor-and-employment.md — Robinson v. Shell Oil Co.

#### Robinson v. Shell Oil Co.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1376.
**Decided:** February 18, 1997.

##### Former employees fall within this retaliation protection

**Holding:** Title VII §704(a)'s protection of employees includes former employees alleging postemployment retaliation by their former employer because they engaged in Title VII-protected activity. Former-employee status therefore cannot alone support dismissal of Robinson's claim; inclusion within the protected category does not establish retaliatory conduct, motive, causation, actionable harm or a remedy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Souter), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No actionable-harm standard, new employment-nexus element, causation rule, universal postemployment tort or deference rule is adopted. The reference, motive and job consequences remain unproved allegations.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The former-employee coverage dismissal is reversed and the retaliation claim returns for unresolved elements, defenses and relief. Discriminatory-discharge merits are not reopened, and no trial, hiring, reinstatement or recovery is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Walters's §701(b) daily-count rule still excludes separated workers on later days; it does not control §704(a)'s different temporal setting. Darden and Town & Country retain their distinct relationship and statutory-context inquiries.

## Source: state/holdings/property-and-economic-rights.md — Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, 508 U.S. 602 (1993)

#### Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, 508 U.S. 602 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-904.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, June 14, 1993, No. 91-904, see Civil Rights — Pension withdrawal liability and fair adjudication.

##### The asserted impact does not establish a taking

**Holding:** This withdrawal liability is not a taking when its economic impact, reasonable investment-backed expectations and the character of the governmental action are considered together. The substantial monetary burden alone does not establish appropriation or a constitutional percentage ceiling; broader reliance on the former 30%-of-net-worth limit is not independently decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The asserted impact does not establish a taking portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No constitutional percentage ceiling, independent entitlement to the former thirty-percent-of-net-worth cap or adjudication of employers entering plans before ERISA is established. Contractual limits do not alone defeat federal legislation, and the regulatory setting supplies no universal immunity for retrospective pension laws.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm enforcement of this award under independent arbitration and the stated statutory construction; no second arbitration is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Connolly, 475 U.S. 211 — as-applied pension takings analysis followed.
- Penn Central, 438 U.S. 104 — economic impact, reasonable expectations and governmental character considered together.
- Village of Euclid, 272 U.S. 365 — diminution alone insufficient; no numerical safe harbor derived.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Present legal change and prospective relief from an adjudicated injunction

### Present legal change and prospective relief from an adjudicated injunction

**Governing question:** Rule 60(b)(5) relief when this Court expressly removes a continuing adjudicated injunction's constitutional premise.

**Current rule:** When this Court expressly removes the constitutional premise supporting a continuing adjudicated injunction, that present change is a significant change in law under Rule 60(b)(5), and continued categorical enforcement on the displaced premise is inequitable. The Court may apply its ruling in the pending review of denied prospective relief without requiring a second motion merely to invoke the rule it has just announced; lower courts remain bound by directly controlling precedent until this Court changes it.

**Authority by component:**

- Present legal change, pending review and lower-court obedience to direct precedent: Agostini v. Felton — III. The Court's present change supplies the legal change required for prospective relief, 1997-06-23. All four holdings are delivered by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela in the Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Part III of the Court's opinion; the same five Justices directly join the present-change rule and its distinction between this continuing decree and a completed judgment.
- Tailored prospective relief and public-control limits: Agostini v. Felton — IV. Relief must remove the rejected prohibition while preserving independently lawful obligations, 1997-06-23. Part IV of the Court's opinion; the same five Justices directly join this tailored relief and its public-control limits.

**Present operation effective:** Agostini v. Felton, June 23, 1997.

**Recurring remedy:** On remand the district court must remove the injunction's prohibition of the described secular Title I service insofar as that prohibition rests on religious location, presumed inculcation from location or ordinary supervision of public personnel; if no independently supported legal basis sustains the onsite prohibition, it must be dissolved. Any remaining obligation must have an identified lawful basis, and any further proceedings must concern a specified material issue under the surviving law rather than require proof that future misconduct is impossible.

**Standard of review:** Denial of Rule 60(b)(5) relief is reviewed for abuse of discretion, with its governing legal premises reviewed as questions of law.

**Exceptions and limits:** Rufo's limits remain. Dissatisfaction, a cheaper lawful alternative and anticipated doctrinal change are insufficient. Actual anticipation and acceptance of a risk can impose a heavy burden, but mere foreseeability is not an absolute bar and an unforeseeable catastrophe is unnecessary. A clarification of constitutional permission does not automatically erase a voluntarily accepted stricter consent obligation. Browder v. Director, Department of Corrections does not foreclose adjustment of continuing relief on the basis of the legal change actually adopted; its limit on using a Rule 60 appeal to reopen the underlying completed judgment remains. Applying the Court's express change does not hold that the lower court should have anticipated it, and completed claims are not reopened.

**Implementation limits:** No additional negotiated obligation is presumed. If an independent statutory, contractual or decree obligation is established, constitutional permission does not by itself extinguish it. Existing lawful services may continue while the mandate is implemented promptly. Children currently receiving those services should continue to receive them during implementation, with existing statutory duties preserved and no new service entitlement created. Any implementation period must address identified tasks and circumstances; the Court sets no number of days, and administrative delay cannot indefinitely preserve the rejected categorical prohibition. The district court modifies the decree under the mandate. Until it acts, the City must obey the operative injunction.

The holding permits the described program; it does not command onsite delivery, create an unconditional funding entitlement, approve a replacement program, award damages or repayment, cancel contracts, or excuse past disobedience. It makes no finding that every present implementation satisfies the required secular controls.

**Related current rules:** Modification of institutional consent decrees.

**Holdings navigation:** Agostini v. Felton (1997-06-23) — III. The Court's present change supplies the legal change required for prospective relief; IV. Relief must remove the rejected prohibition while preserving independently lawful obligations.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Concrete personal interests and procedural citizen-suit standing

### Concrete personal interests and procedural citizen-suit standing

**Governing question:** Article III standing for a citizen-suit plaintiff alleging a deprivation of procedure, including ESA §11(g) enforcement.

**Current rule:** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury; a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant's challenged conduct rather than to independent third parties' own decisions; and a likelihood, not mere speculation, that the requested relief will redress the injury. Authorization for any person to sue does not supply standing for only the public's undifferentiated interest in lawful administration. A procedural right must protect the plaintiff's own threatened concrete interest; when that connection exists, the plaintiff need not prove that the required procedure will certainly alter the ultimate agency decision. Aesthetic, recreational or professional wildlife interests may qualify, but past visits and indefinite return intentions without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure do not establish threatened interference with personal use. At summary judgment the plaintiff must support challenged standing elements with specific facts by affidavit or other evidence, with favorable reasonable inferences available; allegations cannot replace missing evidence.

**Authority by component:**
- Stage-specific evidentiary requirement and existing injury, traceability and redressability elements: Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, “1. Article III elements must be supported at the litigation stage”, June 12, 1992; Scalia Part II, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas (five).
- Prospective personal-use boundary: Lujan, “2. Indefinite return intentions and general species interests do not establish threatened personal injury here,” June 12, 1992; Scalia Part III-A, same five-Justice opinion; Stone separately supports the record-specific conclusion.
- Citizen-suit and procedural-right boundary: Lujan, “3. Citizen-suit authorization does not dispense with a threatened concrete interest,” June 12, 1992; Scalia Part IV, same five Justices.

**Present operation effective:** Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, June 12, 1992, for the identified citizen-suit and procedural-right clarification; ordinary Article III elements and evidentiary burdens are retained.

**Exceptions and limits:** No purchased-ticket or particular-document requirement is adopted.
