The redressability application to absent agencies, binding force of the challenged regulation upon those agencies, effect of particular funding changes and ESA §7(a)(2)'s foreign reach are not controlling holdings.

**Holdings navigation:** Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992) — Standing and personal injury.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Federal rules applied to parties govern still-open direct review

### Federal rules applied to parties govern still-open direct review

**Governing question:** What instructions govern federal rules applied to parties govern still-open direct review?

**Current rule:** When this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs all still-open direct-review cases concerning events within its substantive scope, whether before or after announcement. Selective prospectivity cannot withhold the rule from another such case. Chevron Oil is displaced to that extent. Later judicial doctrine alone does not reopen final judgments; genuine remedial rules remain distinct, and every conceivable prospective disposition is not decided.

**Authority by component:**

- An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review: Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation — An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review, 1993-06-18. Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Present operation effective:** 1993-06-18; Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, for the components identified above.

**Holdings navigation:** Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993).

**Related current rules:** Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments states Morgan Stanley's and Plaut's distinct rule for an express statutory entitlement to reinstatement under §27A(b); Harper's judicial-retroactivity rule neither creates that entitlement nor defeats it.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Presented questions, subsidiary arguments and exceptional Supreme Court review

### Presented questions, subsidiary arguments and exceptional Supreme Court review

**Governing question:** When an issue outside the stated certiorari question may be considered.

**Current rule:** Rule 14.1(a) creates a strong prudential presumption against deciding questions neither stated in the petition nor fairly included, subject to exceptional circumstances. A distinct intervention denial is not fairly included merely because reversing it would permit reaching a settlement-vacatur question; discussion in the petition’s body and merits briefing does not alone suffice. A consistently asserted Teague defense may be subsidiary where it limits application of the very constitutional rule and habeas relief challenged by the petition. Geographic tailoring is fairly included in a challenge to exclusion from designated speech zones. An exceptional extension requires its own supported basis; adversarial briefing, preservation, logical priority or usefulness alone does not enlarge the questions. The duty to notice jurisdictional defects remains separate. A respondent may defend the existing favorable judgment on a record-supported alternative rejected below when doing so neither enlarges its own rights nor reduces the adversary’s rights under that judgment. The Court may also elect to consider a deliberately disavowed legal argument supporting the same preserved claim when it falls within the question presented and can be resolved on the established record without a new factual theory or additional findings. That discretion imposes no duty to consider abandoned grounds and does not erase limits on distinct claims, relief, factual stipulations or missing facts.

**Authority by component:**

- Strong prudential presumption and independent-question boundary: Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp — The intervention question was not fairly included and no exception is warranted, 1993-11-30. Per curiam opinion of the Court supported by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg, seven Justices; Blackmun and Stevens dissent. The reasoned dismissal establishes this question-presentation ruling, not the merits of the judgment below.
- Confined subsidiary Teague defense: Caspari v. Bohlen — The preserved nonretroactivity defense is fairly included here, 1994-02-23. O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; eight Justices. Stevens disagrees with fair inclusion and would decline the defense under Rule 14.1(a).
- Geographic inclusion and independently justified exceptional review: Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc — The limited scope of geographic and paragraph (4) review; The independent conduct-language claims remain undecided, 1994-06-30. O'Connor's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight. Stevens declines these distinct merits questions. O'Connor's Part IV-A, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: eight on the Rule 14 ground. Stone-Zsela agrees only in the nonreach disposition for these independently disputed terms and applications and adds no ground.

- The two renewed alternatives may defend the existing favorable judgment: The two renewed alternatives may defend the existing favorable judgment, 1994-11-29. Part II of Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer, supplies six direct joins to this reach ruling. Stone-Zsela joins only the dispositions on the two alternatives and adds no reach ground. Scalia and Thomas accept the legal availability of such defenses but do not decide either alternative because their independent ground would sustain the appellate judgment.
- The Court may consider this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal: The Court may consider this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal, 1995-02-21. Justice Scalia's reachability portion, joined by Justices Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Seven Justices adopt the stated discretionary-review rationale; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela reaches the question but does not join this portion, and Justice O'Connor declines the argument as a prudential matter.

**Present operation effective:** Lebron, February 21, 1995, for the bounded deliberate-disavowal component; X-Citement Video, November 29, 1994, for the favorable-judgment defense; the earlier authorities retain their separate components.

**Exceptions and limits:** Madsen’s separate noise-and-images review rested together on a continuing categorical restraint, direct connection to the clinic remedy, existing medical record, adversarial presentation and severable legal correction. It declined further independent conduct-language questions despite briefing. Those circumstances are not a universal mandatory checklist or permission to review every related clause.

**Holdings navigation:** The intervention question was not fairly included and no exception is warranted; The preserved nonretroactivity defense is fairly included here; The limited scope of geographic and paragraph (4) review; The independent conduct-language claims remain undecided; The two renewed alternatives may defend the existing favorable judgment; The Court may consider this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Statutory temporal reach and new consequences for completed conduct

### Statutory temporal reach and new consequences for completed conduct

**Governing question:** How courts decide whether new legislation governs events preceding enactment.

**Current rule:** First determine whether Congress prescribed the statute’s temporal reach. If it did, that direction governs subject to independent constitutional limits. Otherwise ask whether applying the provision would attach new legal consequences to completed events by impairing existing rights, increasing liability for past conduct or imposing new duties concerning completed transactions. Such operation requires clear congressional intent. Pending litigation alone does not answer the question. Procedural, jurisdictional and prospective-relief labels are not substitutes for examining the provision’s actual operation. Legislative correction of a judicial construction, or a purpose to restore an earlier understanding, does not by itself establish retrospective reach; this legislative inquiry differs from the temporal operation of judicial decisions.

**Authority by component:**

- Sequential temporal inquiry: Landgraf v. USI Film Products — Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s general-framework portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this organizing rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur on a distinct relevant-activity approach, not as additional joins at this level of generality.
- Corrective purpose and distinction from judicial interpretation: Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc — Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes at this explanatory level. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas accept the judicial/legislative distinction through their separate textual method, not through a presumed full join.

**Present operation effective:** Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc, 1994-04-26.

**Holdings navigation:** Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences; Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach.

**Exceptions and limits:** An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. Landgraf concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Related current rules:** Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments supplies Morgan Stanley's and Plaut's separate Article III rule for §27A(b).

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after dismissal

### Jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after dismissal

**Governing question:** When a federal court may enforce a settlement after dismissing the original action.

**Current rule:** Enforcement of the settlement is a separate contract controversy requiring an independent jurisdictional basis unless the dismissal order lawfully incorporates the settlement obligation or expressly retains jurisdiction to enforce it. Mere awareness, approval or party consent does not confer jurisdiction. Under Rule 41(a)(2), the court may make compliance or retention a condition of its discretionary dismissal. For a stipulated Rule 41(a)(1)(ii) dismissal, the parties must agree to the retained enforcement authority and the court must timely embody it in its order; a private agreement alone is insufficient.

**Authority by component:**

- Independent basis or operative dismissal order: Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America — Postdismissal contract enforcement requires a jurisdictional basis; A lawful dismissal order may make settlement compliance enforceable, 1994-05-16. Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt the rule, application, and reversal with remand. Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this qualification defining the boundary of the jurisdictional holding.

**Present operation effective:** Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, 1994-05-16.

**Open question:** Possible reopening under Rule 60(b), as distinct from exercising jurisdiction over a separate contract-enforcement action, is not decided.

**Holdings navigation:** Postdismissal contract enforcement requires a jurisdictional basis; A lawful dismissal order may make settlement compliance enforceable.

**Related current rules:** Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments concerns a distinct statutory source for reinstatement under §27A(b). Morgan Stanley and Plaut separately sustain its constitutionality; the existence of a statutory source alone does not validate every reopening command.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments

### Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments

**Governing question:** Whether and on what conditions §27A(b) requires reinstatement of a completed, unappealed private securities limitations dismissal.

**Current rule:** Under Morgan Stanley, Article III permits Congress to require reinstatement under §27A(b) of a qualifying private §10(b) action even though its limitations dismissal became final without appeal before the provision's December 19, 1991 enactment: the action must have commenced on or before June 19, 1991, been dismissed as time barred after that date, and been timely under the jurisdiction's law, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991, with a reinstatement motion filed within sixty days after enactment. The court independently determines those cumulative conditions and, when they are satisfied, must reinstate without discretionary balancing, retaining independent responsibility for all claims, defenses, and remedies; this expressly retrospective entitlement does not authorize legislative findings, dictated liability, or unrestricted reopening of completed merits judgments. A narrowly defined, exclusively retrospective class does not by itself turn this procedure into a legislative judgment: the statute covers every action meeting its legal criteria and selects no parties for legislatively determined liability.

**Authority by component:**

- Defined statutory entitlement, cumulative conditions, mandatory reinstatement, independent judicial adjudication and constitutional limits: Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co. — Bounded statutory reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals, May 23, 1994. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Blackmun, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg.
- Application to a final unappealed dismissal and the boundary for narrow, exclusively retrospective class coverage: Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc. — Section 27A(b) permits mandatory reinstatement of this completed limitations dismissal, April 18, 1995. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

**Present operation effective:** Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994.

**Exceptions and limits:** Congress may not command findings contrary to the record, impose liability on identified defendants by legislative declaration, or disguise an individualized award as a procedural reopening law. The Court decides no general power to set aside completed judgments adjudicating the underlying merits, no constitutionality of every retrospective statute, and no independent due-process question; applicable due-process protections and other constitutional restrictions remain. Section 27A(a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, federal waiver, continuing prospective relief, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The decision neither adjudicates other parties' completed cases nor creates criminal or innocence remedies. Fraud, reliance, causation, liability, damages, and ordinary merits defenses remain for the courts; reinstatement establishes none of them.

**Related current rules:** Federal rules applied to parties govern still-open direct review concerns judicial retroactivity alone, which supplies no reopening entitlement. Statutory temporal reach and new consequences for completed conduct separates express temporal direction from constitutional authority; the ordinary presumption against retroactivity does not defeat §27A(b)'s express reach. Robertson's pending-litigation distinction between changing governing law and prescribing adjudication remains intact; Morgan Stanley supplies the completed-dismissal rule Robertson left open.

**Holdings navigation:** Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co. — Bounded statutory reinstatement of completed securities limitations dismissals; Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc. — Section 27A(b) permits mandatory reinstatement of this completed limitations dismissal.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Qualified-immunity legal appeals and factual sufficiency

### Qualified-immunity legal appeals and factual sufficiency

**Governing question:** The boundary of immediate review under §1291 when qualified immunity is denied.

**Current rule:** Immediate review may address the separable legal question whether conduct on the relevant assumed facts violates clearly established law, including the legality of a categorical direct-evidence condition. It may not determine only whether pretrial evidence creates a genuine dispute about the official’s participation, or choose between disputed motive, causation or factual accounts. A legal appeal uses the district court’s assumed facts; if not stated, the appellate court may identify likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant without deciding truth or reweighing sufficiency. An abstract legal contention does not bring the factual dispute within immediate review. An earlier pleading-stage appeal does not by itself exhaust review of a later summary-judgment order: a separable legal immunity question must independently satisfy the collateral-order conditions. This permits neither repetition of an unchanged ruling nor relitigation of matters resolved by the first mandate. Having to defend a different surviving claim does not itself defeat otherwise authorized claim-specific review, but neither supplies missing authorization nor makes that other claim reviewable.

**Authority by component:**

- Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal: Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal, 1995-06-12. Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this bounded rule.
- Separable legal review of the categorical evidence rule: Separable legal review of the categorical evidence rule, 1995-06-12. Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer; all nine support this legal-review proposition.

- Successive-stage review and preserved mandate limits: Behrens — Holding 1, February 21, 1996; Ginsburg Part I for Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and herself (seven).
- Conditional assumed-fact rule: Behrens — Holding 2, February 21, 1996; unanimous Part II. Claim-specific effect of another surviving claim: Holding 3, Part III for all eight Associates; Stone-Zsela does not join. Stevens and Breyer join these conditional rules while opposing authorization of the successive appeal.

**Present operation effective:** Behrens, February 21, 1996; Johnson and Kimberlin retain their original component authority.

**Open question:** Genuinely necessary connected review and a categorical rule for every mixed order remain unresolved. Johnson and Kimberlin use their common entering baseline; neither is treated as earlier same-day law for the other.

**Holdings navigation:** Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal; Separable legal review of the categorical evidence rule; Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Three-judge review of facial challenges to Telecommunications Act Title V

### Three-judge review of facial challenges to Telecommunications Act Title V

**Governing question:** The judicial channel for a civil facial challenge to Title V of Public Law 104-104 or an amendment or provision of that title.

**Current rule:** A three-judge district court convened under 28 U.S.C. §2284 must hear a covered facial challenge. An interlocutory or final judgment, decree or order of that court holding a covered provision unconstitutional is reviewable as of right by direct Supreme Court appeal filed within twenty days after entry. This route does not extend to every communications dispute, every as-applied challenge, a judgment upholding the provision, or litigation confined to the separate 1992 provisions.

**Authority by component:**

- Challenge and appeal triggers and deadline: Public Law 104-104, Act §561, effective 1996-02-08; admitted noncase text, preserved as distinct from the 1992 review in Denver, statutory review boundary, 1996-06-28.

**Present operation effective:** Public Law 104-104, Act §561, 1996-02-08.

**Holdings navigation:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727 (1996).

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — One-year state-custody habeas filing period

### One-year state-custody habeas filing period

**Governing question:** The limitation period under amended §2244(d).

**Current rule:** A person in custody pursuant to a state-court judgment has one year to apply for habeas relief, running from the latest of: finality by completion of direct review or expiration of time to seek it; removal of a State-created filing impediment violating federal law that actually prevented filing; initial Supreme Court recognition of the asserted constitutional right, if newly recognized and made retroactively applicable on collateral review; or when due diligence could have discovered the claim’s factual predicate. Time during which a properly filed application for state postconviction or other collateral review of the pertinent judgment or claim is pending is excluded.

**Authority by component:**

- Four alternative latest-event triggers and pending-state-review exclusion: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §101, 28 U.S.C. §2244(d), enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

**Present operation effective:** AEDPA enactment, 1996-04-24.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Habeas and §2255 appeals and appellate certificates

### Habeas and §2255 appeals and appellate certificates

**Governing question:** Appeal from district habeas and §2255 orders and the certificate procedures in amended §2253 and Rule 22.

**Current rule:** District final habeas and §2255 orders are reviewable in the appropriate circuit, except that §2253(b) supplies no right of appeal from a final order testing a federal criminal removal warrant or detention pending removal. An applicant’s appeal from a final state-process habeas order or §2255 order requires a certificate of appealability from a circuit justice or judge, issued only upon a substantial showing of denial of a constitutional right and identifying the qualifying issues. Rule 22(a) directs original applications to the appropriate district court; a circuit judge must transfer an application there, and after district denial the applicant may pursue an authorized appeal rather than renew before a circuit judge. For state-process habeas appeals, Rule 22(b) permits a district or circuit judge’s certificate; the district judge must issue it or explain refusal and send the certificate/reasons with the notice and file to the appellate court. After district refusal, the applicant may request a circuit judge’s certificate; a request addressed to the court is addressed to its judges, and a notice of appeal without an express request serves as that request. No certificate is required for an appeal by a State or its representative.

**Authority by component:**

- Appeal, certificate, district/circuit application and transfer procedures: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §§102–103, 28 U.S.C. §2253 and Fed. R. App. P. 22, enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

**Present operation effective:** AEDPA enactment, 1996-04-24.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Exceptions and limits:** Rule 22’s circuit-judge transfer language does not repeal the Supreme Court’s distinct original habeas authority; see “Supreme Court original habeas after an authorization-review bar.”

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Exhaustion and merits denial under amended §2254

### Exhaustion and merits denial under amended §2254

**Governing question:** State-remedy exhaustion and express waiver in §2254(b).

**Current rule:** Relief for a person in custody under a state judgment may not be granted unless state remedies have been exhausted, no available state corrective process exists, or circumstances render that process ineffective to protect the applicant’s rights. The court may deny on the merits despite nonexhaustion; it is not required to do so. A State is neither deemed to waive exhaustion nor estopped from relying on it unless its counsel expressly waives it.

**Authority by component:**

- Exhaustion alternatives, permissive merits denial and express counsel waiver: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §104(1), 28 U.S.C. §2254(b), enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

**Present operation effective:** AEDPA enactment, April 24, 1996; Lindh, June 23, 1997, for the chapter 153 amendments at issue in preenactment noncapital §2254 applications. The separate §2255 and capital questions are not thereby decided.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — State-court merits decisions and factual development under amended §2254

### State-court merits decisions and factual development under amended §2254

**Governing question:** Amended §2254(d)–(e) restrictions on state-custody habeas relief, factual presumptions and federal hearings.

**Current rule:** For a claim adjudicated on its merits in state proceedings, §2254(d) forbids granting relief unless the adjudication resulted in a decision contrary to, or involving unreasonable application of, clearly established federal law as determined by the Supreme Court, or rested on an unreasonable factual determination in light of the evidence presented in the state proceeding. Under §2254(e)(1), a state-court factual determination is presumed correct, with clear-and-convincing rebuttal borne by the applicant. If the applicant failed to develop a claim’s factual basis in state proceedings, §2254(e)(2) forbids a federal evidentiary hearing unless the claim relies either on a previously unavailable new constitutional rule made retroactive on collateral review by the Supreme Court or on a factual predicate not previously discoverable through due diligence, and the underlying facts would establish clearly and convincingly that but for constitutional error no reasonable factfinder would have found guilt of the underlying offense.

**Authority by component:**

- Merits-relief restriction, factual presumption/rebuttal and cumulative hearing conditions: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §104(2)–(4), 28 U.S.C. §2254(d)–(e), enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

**Present operation effective:** AEDPA enactment, April 24, 1996; Lindh, June 23, 1997, for the chapter 153 amendments at issue in preenactment noncapital §2254 applications. The separate §2255 and capital questions are not thereby decided.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Unresolved interaction:** No controlling construction of “contrary to,” “unreasonable application” or “unreasonable determination,” or general interaction of these restrictions, is established. Thompson’s former-statute custody classification and Keeney’s received hearing rule do not erase a genuinely applicable amendment. Gray requires resolving actual applicable, previously unadjudicated conditions before entering an operative writ without reopening settled issues or inventing barriers.

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Collateral counsel under amended §2254 and §2255

### Collateral counsel under amended §2254 and §2255

**Governing question:** Authority to appoint counsel and collateral-counsel ineffectiveness under Title I.

**Current rule:** In §2254 proceedings and ensuing review, the court may appoint counsel under §3006A for an applicant who is or becomes financially unable to afford counsel, subject to the separate Controlled Substances Act §408 provision and any Supreme Court rule prescribed under statutory authority. Section 2255 likewise permits counsel under §3006A subject to those stated statutory/rule qualifications. Under §2254(i), ineffectiveness or incompetence of counsel during state or federal collateral postconviction proceedings is not a ground for relief under §2254. This prohibition does not itself expand, contract or replace the separate capital-counsel appointment mechanism.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Federal-prisoner limitation and successive-motion certification

### Federal-prisoner limitation and successive-motion certification

**Governing question:** The new §2255 limitation period and certification for a second or successive motion.

**Current rule:** A §2255 motion has a one-year limitation running from the latest of conviction finality; removal of an unlawful governmental filing impediment that actually prevented the motion; initial Supreme Court recognition of the asserted newly recognized right made retroactively applicable on collateral review; or when due diligence could discover the supporting facts. A second or successive motion requires certification under §2244 by an appropriate appellate panel of either newly discovered evidence which, if proved and considered with all evidence, would establish clearly and convincingly that no reasonable factfinder would have found guilt, or a previously unavailable new constitutional rule made retroactive on collateral review by the Supreme Court. The evidence route has its own text and is not silently replaced with §2244(b)(2)(B)’s distinct state-prisoner constitutional-error/diligence formulation.

**Authority by component:**

- Latest-event period and alternative certification predicates: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §105(2), amended 28 U.S.C. §2255 limitation and successive-motion paragraphs, enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.

**Present operation effective:** AEDPA enactment, 1996-04-24.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Exceptions and limits:** The amendment supplies no conclusion that an earlier dismissal makes a later motion successive, that a particular deadline has expired, or that collateral relief is guaranteed.

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Second or successive state-custody habeas applications

### Second or successive state-custody habeas applications

**Governing question:** Repeated/new claims, appellate authorization and district verification under amended §2244(b).

**Current rule:** A claim in a second or successive §2254 application already presented in a prior application must be dismissed. A new claim must also be dismissed unless it relies on a previously unavailable new constitutional rule made retroactive on collateral review by the Supreme Court, or both its factual predicate was not previously discoverable through due diligence and its facts, if proved and viewed with all evidence, would establish clearly and convincingly that but for constitutional error no reasonable factfinder would have found the applicant guilty of the underlying offense. Before district filing, the applicant must seek appropriate appellate authorization; a three-judge panel may authorize only on a prima facie showing of these requirements and must act within thirty days. A grant or denial is not appealable and cannot be the subject of rehearing or certiorari. Authorization is not final entitlement: the district court must dismiss unless the applicant establishes the statutory conditions.

**Authority by component:**

- Separate repeated/new claim restrictions and two-stage authorization/verification: Source, authority and operative text, Public Law 104-132, Title I, §106(b), 28 U.S.C. §2244(b)(1)–(4), enacted 1996-04-24; the Record’s retained complete enacted text supplies these components.
- Post-enactment authorization-review bar and distinction from original filing: The withdrawn review route and retained original jurisdiction; Temporal operation of the channel rule; Successive restrictions and original-writ discretion, 1996-06-28. Felker Part I-A and II, all nine; the channel temporal result has all nine, its Part I-B explanation Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six).

**Present operation effective:** Statutory requirements enacted 1996-04-24; Felker’s specified review-channel construction, 1996-06-28.

**Effective transition:** Enacted April 24, 1996. “Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments” states Lindh's noncapital chapter 153 application-date construction, effective 1997-06-23. Separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case clause and Felker's successive-petition treatment remain distinct; Lindh does not settle every other Title I provision's application to pending proceedings. Authority: Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg's six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Exceptions and limits:** A request to announce a new rule is not reliance on an existing Supreme Court rule made retroactive. Schlup’s gateway and §2244(b)(2)(B)’s cumulative conditions are not interchangeable. The district authorization mechanism does not govern Supreme Court original filing; direct mandatory force of paragraphs (1)–(2) there remains reserved.

**Holdings navigation:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651 (1996). Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Continuing individual controversy and legally unavailable damages

### Continuing individual controversy and legally unavailable damages

**Governing question:** Whether an individual former employee's challenge to an employment rule remains live after departure.

**Current rule:** A prospective individual challenge becomes moot after voluntary departure from covered employment when no intent to return, realistic recurrence or surviving claim is established. A general prayer cannot preserve the controversy by producing nominal damages against a State or an official-capacity defendant whom §1983 does not make a damages person. Consent to payment cannot create the missing cause of action, and certification of an unsettled state-law question cannot supply missing Article III jurisdiction.

**Authority by component:**

- Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona — A continuing personal controversy is absent, 1997-03-03; Stevens for all nine supplies the continuing-controversy and unavailable-damages limits
- Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona — Certification cannot restore the controversy, 1997-03-03; Stevens for all nine supplies the certification boundary

**Present operation effective:** Arizonans for Official English, 1997-03-03.

**Exceptions and limits:** This is no categorical express-pleading requirement for nominal damages and does not decide a legally available personal-capacity damages claim. Limited authority to correct lower-court jurisdictional error is distinct from merits authority; judgment vacatur remains equitable under the existing rule. No automatic initiative-sponsor standing or mandatory certification rule is established.

**Holdings navigation:** Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona (1997-03-03) — Federal Courts / Mootness, corrective jurisdiction and equitable vacatur.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Article III power before disputed statutory merits

### Article III power before disputed statutory merits

**Governing question:** Whether a court may assume disputed constitutional jurisdiction to resolve contested federal statutory liability.

**Current rule:** A federal court may not assume disputed Article III power merely to decide a contested statutory merits question. Statutory enforcement-and-penalty jurisdictional wording does not automatically make every liability element jurisdictional; substantial federal claims remain distinct from success on their merits.

**Authority by component:**

- Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co. — 1. Constitutional power before this disputed statutory merits question, 1997-06-02; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; five, with O'Connor and Kennedy reserving other legitimate exceptional sequences

**Present operation effective:** Steel Co., 1997-06-02.

**Exceptions and limits:** This rule does not prescribe an exhaustive order among all nonmerits thresholds. Norton and Avrech retain their already-controlled-merits and distinct jurisdictional settings. EPCRA citizen-suit coverage of fully corrected reporting defaults remains undecided.

**Holdings navigation:** Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co. (1997-06-02) — Constitutional jurisdiction before a disputed EPCRA merits question.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments

### Temporal reach of ordinary AEDPA habeas amendments

**Governing question:** Which law governs a noncapital federal habeas application filed before April 24, 1996.

**Current rule:** AEDPA's chapter 153 amendments at issue, including amended §2254(d), do not govern a noncapital federal habeas application filed before April 24, 1996. The governing pre-AEDPA law remains applicable to that application. This construction preserves separately directed provisions, chapter 154's express pending-case reach and Felker's treatment of the successive-petition provisions; it is not a blanket exemption of all pending proceedings from every AEDPA amendment.

**Authority by component:**
- Application-date construction and preserved exceptions: Lindh, Lindh v. Murphy — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application, Ginsburg with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer (six), 1997-06-23.

**Present operation effective:** Lindh v. Murphy, 1997-06-23.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no constitutional entitlement to preserve every filing-date judicial doctrine and decides no capital State qualification, exhaustion, merits or ultimate entitlement to relief.

**Holdings navigation:** Lindh v. Murphy (1997-06-23) — The chapter 153 amendments at issue do not govern this pre-enactment noncapital application.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Concrete electoral-information injury

### Concrete electoral-information injury

**Governing question:** Whether voters seeking particular campaign-finance information still withheld have Article III standing.

**Current rule:** Denial of particular information allegedly required by FECA sought for a voter's own electoral participation is a concrete injury even if widely shared. When an erroneous legal premise blocks a disclosure-capable process, possible later lawful agency discretion does not defeat traceability or redressability. Complainant status or a general desire for legal enforcement alone does not suffice. Ordinary standing elements still require stage-appropriate proof, and standing does not establish that the information is legally owed.

**Authority by component:**
- Continuing information deprivation, traceability and redressability: Akins, Akins v. FEC — I. The continuing denial of particular electoral information is a concrete injury, Souter with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer (seven), 1997-06-25.

**Present operation effective:** Akins, 1997-06-25.

**Holdings navigation:** Akins v. FEC (1997-06-25) — I. The continuing denial of particular electoral information is a concrete injury.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Individual legislator standing for institutional dilution

### Individual legislator standing for institutional dilution

**Governing question:** Whether individual legislators may challenge abstract dilution of institutional legislative power after fully effective votes.

**Current rule:** Members whose votes were counted and given full legal effect lack Article III injury on a claim of abstract institutional-power dilution or altered force of future votes. Express statutory permission to sue may remove prudential restrictions but cannot supply personal concrete injury. Losing a fully effective vote differs from complete nullification of votes sufficient to determine an outcome or a personal deprivation of office or pay.

**Authority by component:**
- Personal/institutional boundary and statutory authorization: Raines, Raines v. Byrd — I. These individual legislators lack a concrete personal injury, Scalia with Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg (six), 1997-06-26.

**Present operation effective:** Raines, 1997-06-26.

**Exceptions and limits:** No universal ban on official or legislator litigation, legislative-exhaustion requirement or categorical rule against an authorized institutional suit is adopted. Concrete cancellation injury and complete vote nullification retain their distinct Article III questions.

**Holdings navigation:** Raines v. Byrd (1997-06-26) — Federal Courts / Legislator standing.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Review of FEC complaint dismissals

### Review of FEC complaint dismissals

**Governing question:** The FECA route for an aggrieved complainant challenging a dismissal's legal premise.

**Current rule:** Section 437g(a)(8) permits an aggrieved complainant to obtain review of a dismissal within sixty days. A court may declare it contrary to law and may direct conformity within thirty days; if the Commission does not conform, the complainant may sue to remedy the violation in the original complaint. Review may correct a legally erroneous coverage premise, but does not compel prosecution, disclosure or a penalty. Lawful enforcement priorities cannot insulate that premise, and an unexercised alternative rationale cannot replace the legal ground actually used.

**Authority by component:**
- Review sequence and legal-premise distinction: Akins, Akins v. FEC — II. FECA permits review of this dismissal's legal premise, Souter with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer (seven), 1997-06-25.

**Present operation effective:** Akins, 1997-06-25.

**Exceptions and limits:** Article III remains an independent requirement. Genuine statutory ambiguity and lawful discretion remain for appropriate agency consideration; the Commission's reading of constitutional decisions is not conclusive. The separate 120-day inaction route is not decided.

**Holdings navigation:** Akins v. FEC (1997-06-25) — II. FECA permits review of this dismissal's legal premise.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Municipal responsibility and the underlying federal deprivation

### Municipal responsibility and the underlying federal deprivation

**Governing question:** The distinction between an underlying federal deprivation and municipal responsibility, including final policymaking for the challenged function.

**Current rule:** A municipal employee who proves deprivation of a federal right under color of state law and the required municipal responsibility need not prove an additional, independent abuse-of-governmental-power element. The federal deprivation and its attribution to municipal policy or custom remain separate required inquiries. Employment alone establishes neither municipal liability nor a substantive right; *Canton*'s training analysis supplies attribution and causation, not the underlying constitutional guarantee. For a final-policymaker theory, the court identifies through state and local law, including custom or usage with legal force, who speaks finally for the particular entity on the challenged subject. Final operational discretion does not alone answer that question; labels, election, territory, financing or lack of commission control is individually insufficient. An Alabama sheriff's independent local office supplies County final law-enforcement authority in the criminal-investigation function addressed in McMillian, even without commission delegation or review. An attributable policy or final decision must still cause the proved federal injury.

**Authority by component:**

- Rejection of the additional element and separation of deprivation from attribution: Collins v. City of Harker Heights — No extra governmental-abuse element under §1983, February 26, 1992; Part A of Stevens's opinion, joined by all nine Justices.

- McMillian v. Monroe County — I. Final authority and the government represented are distinct inquiries, 1997-06-02; Ginsburg, all nine
- McMillian v. Monroe County — II. The sheriff's independent local office supplies County law-enforcement authority, 1997-06-02; Ginsburg with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer; five
- McMillian v. Monroe County — III. The County claim returns for the remaining liability elements, 1997-06-02; the same five

**Present operation effective:** McMillian, 1997-06-02, for final-policymaker identity and the specified Alabama function; Collins retains the separate underlying-deprivation rule.

**Holdings navigation:** Collins v. City of Harker Heights, 503 U.S. 115 (1992) — Municipal liability and public employment. McMillian v. Monroe County (1997-06-02) — Civil Rights / Municipal liability and final policymaking.



**Exceptions and limits:** Other sheriff functions, other States' arrangements, individual liability and State liability or immunity require separate analysis. Institutional identity does not prove the truth of misconduct or make every official act municipal policy. Bryan County's pending reargument supplies no hiring, fault or causation change.

Rule 11, discovery control and Rule 56 remain distinct. No individual-officer pleading standard is decided.

Stone's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; unanimous controlling authority.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements

### Prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements

**Governing question:** What instructions govern prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements?

**Current rule:** Ordinary investigative work to identify a perpetrator before probable cause has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for alleged fabrication; later courtroom use does not retrospectively transform it into advocacy. Genuine evaluation and preparation in an advocate's role remain protected; neither all pre-indictment nor all post-indictment acts share one classification. Statements to the press announcing an indictment also lack absolute prosecutorial immunity merely because they concern prosecution or cause injury through subsequent proceedings. Qualified immunity, an actionable constitutional deprivation and other liability predicates remain separate.

Preparing and filing charging papers or a warrant motion, evaluating evidence for those submissions and presenting the State's legal case receive absolute immunity when they are advocacy functions. Bad purpose or alleged false evidence does not remove that protection; the Court adopts no general dishonesty exception. A prosecutor's distinct personal oath supplying facts to establish probable cause for an arrest warrant is instead a complaining-witness evidentiary function without absolute prosecutorial immunity. That classification does not require proof of falsity and leaves ordinary courtroom testimony immunity intact. Denial of this immunity does not establish liability: constitutional deprivation, culpability, materiality, causation and compensable injury and available personal defenses remain separate. A judge's warrant does not categorically sever causation where material deception procured it, while a truthful corrected account may still establish probable cause.

**Authority by component:**

- Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity: Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity, 1993-06-24. Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.
- A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity: Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity, 1993-06-24. Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.
- Protected advocacy and personal warrant certification: Fletcher v. Kalina, Fletcher v. Kalina — I. Protected advocacy and a distinct evidentiary act (eight Associates for the complete advocacy rule), Fletcher v. Kalina — II. Personal factual certification supporting an arrest warrant (all nine), and Fletcher v. Kalina — III. The immunity ruling permits further proceedings, not a liability judgment (eight Associates for the source-specific causation and ordinary qualified-immunity qualifications), 1997-06-16.

**Present operation effective:** Fletcher v. Kalina, 1997-06-16; Buckley's investigation and press components retain their original operation.

**Holdings navigation:** Buckley v. Fitzsimmons, 509 U.S. 259 (1993). Fletcher v. Kalina (1997-06-16) — Protected prosecutorial advocacy remains distinct from personal evidentiary acts; Fletcher v. Kalina (1997-06-16) — Civil Rights / Prosecutorial investigation and press statements.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Relevant precedent in appellate qualified-immunity review

### Relevant precedent in appellate qualified-immunity review

**Governing question:** What legal sources an appellate court must consider on a preserved clearly-established-law issue.

**Current rule:** Whether a federal right was clearly established when officials acted is reviewed independently using all relevant precedent, including cases neither cited to nor discovered by the district court. This does not admit a new claim or factual record, make each considered precedent controlling or permit later decisions to supply notice retrospectively. The notice inquiry stays fixed to the conduct date. Ordinary claim and evidence preservation, particularized objective immunity and proper summary-judgment treatment remain distinct; no mandatory merits-first sequence follows.

**Authority by component:**

- Independent legal review, contemporaneous notice and preservation limits: Elder v. Holloway — Appellate review must consider all relevant law on the preserved immunity issue, 1994-02-23. Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. All nine adopt the legal-authority rule and the limited reversal and remand.

**Present operation effective:** Elder v. Holloway, 1994-02-23.

**Holdings navigation:** Appellate review must consider all relevant law on the preserved immunity issue.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — The 1991 Civil Rights Act’s new liabilities and jury election

### The 1991 Civil Rights Act’s new liabilities and jury election

**Governing question:** Whether the 1991 Act’s new damages and contractual coverage reach preenactment conduct.

**Current rule:** Section 102’s new compensatory and punitive damages do not apply to conduct before November 21, 1991. Its jury election exists only when statutory damages under that section are available and requested; either party may then demand a jury, which must not be told the statutory caps. Section 102 damages supplement rather than duplicate §706(g) relief and exclude backpay, interest on backpay and other relief authorized there. Punitive damages require malice or reckless indifference to federally protected rights and are unavailable against governments, government agencies and political subdivisions. Section 101’s expansion of §1981 to contractual performance, modification, termination and enjoyment of benefits likewise does not govern preenactment conduct. Corrective purpose alone supplies no contrary temporal command; a defendant need not prove actual reliance on the former interpretation.

**Authority by component:**

- New damages, their limits and dependent jury election: Landgraf v. USI Film Products — Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct; The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s §102 application, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas independently reach the same unavailability of new damages through their concurrence. Blackmun disagrees about compensation and reserves punitive damages. Stevens’s jury portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, supplies the controlling five-Justice explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reach the same conditional conclusion in their concurrence; Blackmun would permit a jury election because he would make compensation available.
- Expanded contractual coverage and temporal reach: Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc — Section 101’s new contractual coverage does not govern the earlier conduct; Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s opinion, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; these five adopt the complete explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reach the same temporal result through their separate relevant-activity and textual-command method. Stevens’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes at this explanatory level. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas accept the judicial/legislative distinction through their separate textual method, not through a presumed full join.

**Present operation effective:** Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc, 1994-04-26.

**Exceptions and limits:** Claims already available under the earlier law remain available on their own terms. Neither section supplies a new remedy for every earlier Title VII or §1981 wrong.

**Holdings navigation:** Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct; The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim; Section 101’s new contractual coverage does not govern the earlier conduct; Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity

### Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity

**Governing question:** Proof, discovery and causation when unconstitutional motive is an element of a claim and qualified immunity is asserted.

**Current rule:** Qualified immunity imposes no categorical direct-evidence requirement for unconstitutional motive. The plaintiff must establish the actual constitutional elements, personal responsibility and causation through legally competent direct or circumstantial evidence under ordinary procedural burdens. Specific nonconclusory support, lawful discovery limits and the clearly-established-law defense remain available; the rule abolishes neither all heightened specificity requirements nor a burden supplied by the underlying claim. Ordinary preponderance governs unconstitutional motive in the §1983 retaliation claim; immunity and litigation burden cannot justify a special clear-and-convincing quantum. Courts may decide dispositive legal defenses first and require specific nonconclusory support, but cannot categorically withhold discovery needed to obtain proof until the rejected heightened showing is made. Rule-authorized clarification, replies, sequencing, protective orders, relevant proportional discovery and ordinary summary-judgment duties remain available. Under Mt. Healthy the claimant must establish protected conduct and its substantial or motivating causal role; the defendant may then prove by a preponderance that the same action would have occurred without it. A merely possible lawful explanation does not establish that defense or exclude competent motive evidence.

**Authority by component:**

- Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims: Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims, 1995-06-12. Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; nine votes support the rule and its operative limits.

- Crawford-El v. Britton — Proof of unconstitutional motive, 1997-06-16; Stone-Zsela, all nine
- Crawford-El v. Britton — Discovery and stage-specific screening, 1997-06-16; all nine
- Crawford-El v. Britton — Actual causation and independent defenses, 1997-06-16; all nine

**Present operation effective:** Crawford-El, 1997-06-16, rejecting a special proof quantum and derivative categorical discovery barrier while preserving the carried direct/circumstantial-evidence rule.

**Holdings navigation:** Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims. Crawford-El v. Britton (1997-06-16) — Civil Rights / Qualified immunity and motive evidence.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Knowing supervisory acquiescence in school-authority sexual abuse

### Knowing supervisory acquiescence in school-authority sexual abuse

**Governing question:** When a school supervisor's own inaction violates a pupil's bodily integrity.

**Current rule:** A supervisor is personally responsible when the supervisor actually knows of sexual abuse through school authority or a substantial risk of it, deliberately and unreasonably disregards that known danger despite authority and a reasonable opportunity to protect the pupil, and that culpable inaction causes the injury or its continuation. A reasonable response is not actionable merely because injury continues. Circumstantial evidence may establish awareness, but warning signs do not compel that finding and what the official merely should have known is insufficient. Supervisory status, negligence, failure to discover misconduct, or breach of a state reporting duty alone does not establish the federal wrong.

**Authority by component:**

- Personal culpability and causation: Knowing, causally effective acquiescence in a teacher’s sexual abuse is personal wrongdoing, 1995-10-16; Ginsburg Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Breyer (five).

**Present operation effective:** Doe v. Taylor ISD, 1995-10-16.

**Exceptions and limits:** Individual qualified immunity requires its separate conduct-time notice inquiry; recognition of the current rule supplies no retroactive notice. No general monitoring duty, vicarious liability, or finding of ultimate liability follows.

**Holdings navigation:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Initial employment suspension and continuing procedural protection

### Initial employment suspension and continuing procedural protection

**Governing question:** Procedural due process for provisional unpaid suspension of a police officer from sensitive duties.

**Current rule:** Assuming a protected property interest, an independent felony arrest and formal charges can support a brief initial unpaid suspension under Mathews without an invariably required advance hearing, continued pay or separate fiscal emergency. Continuing deprivation requires its own justification and a prompt informed opportunity to contest the employment consequence, considering continuing loss, delay reasons and reliability of the remaining grounds. Once charges are dismissed, the employer must rely on actual employment grounds rather than indefinitely on the original arrest.

**Authority by component:**

- Gilbert v. Homar — Initial unpaid suspension after independent felony arrest, 1997-06-09; eight Associates
- Gilbert v. Homar — Prompt, effective process during a continuing deprivation, 1997-06-09; all nine

**Present operation effective:** Gilbert, 1997-06-09.

**Exceptions and limits:** No general property-interest ruling, fixed deadline or automatic reinstatement is adopted. Backpay bears on relief, not retroactive provision of process; ultimate liability, damages and immunity remain separate.

**Holdings navigation:** Gilbert v. Homar (1997-06-09) — Civil Rights / Public-employment suspension and continuing process.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Qualified-immunity eligibility of commercial prison employees

### Qualified-immunity eligibility of commercial prison employees

**Governing question:** Whether employees of an independently managed commercial prison receive Harlow immunity for delegated custody.

**Current rule:** Full-time guards of a private for-profit firm independently undertaking substantial continuing prison management, under limited direct governmental supervision and potential market competition, do not receive Harlow qualified immunity merely because they perform governmental custody. State attribution and immunity eligibility are separate; eligibility requires its own historical and functional justification before the content of qualified immunity is applied.

**Authority by component:**
- Institutional eligibility exclusion: Richardson v. McKnight, Richardson v. McKnight — I. These commercial prison employees are not eligible for Harlow immunity, Stone-Zsela with Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (six), 1997-06-23.
- Unresolved merits and private defense: Richardson, Richardson v. McKnight — II. Denial of immunity does not establish a constitutional violation or personal liability, same six-Justice opinion, 1997-06-23.

**Present operation effective:** Richardson v. McKnight, 1997-06-23.

**Open question:** Brief private assistance, adjunct service, close official supervision and governmental integration remain open. So do the availability, elements and burdens of a separate private good-faith defense. The rule establishes neither state action, constitutional injury, causation, damages nor corporate liability, and leaves eligible public officials' Harlow protection intact.

**Holdings navigation:** Richardson v. McKnight (1997-06-23) — I. These commercial prison employees are not eligible for Harlow immunity; Richardson v. McKnight (1997-06-23) — Civil Rights / Section 1983 immunity.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Physical injury in a nontrivial malicious custodial beating

### Physical injury in a nontrivial malicious custodial beating

**Governing question:** Whether significant physical injury is an additional necessary element where a restrained convicted prisoner suffers a nontrivial malicious beating satisfying *Whitley*.

**Current rule:** A nontrivial beating inflicted maliciously and without disciplinary justification on a restrained convicted prisoner violates the Eighth Amendment despite limited resulting physical injury when the established conduct satisfies *Whitley*'s demanding malicious-and-sadistic inquiry. Injury remains relevant evidence and bears on compensation; accidental harm or merely technical battery is insufficient. This rule identifies a sufficient constitutional violation and does not impose a universal mental-state standard for all prison-force claims.

**Authority by component:**

- Rejection of a significant-injury floor at the adopted narrow scope: Hudson v. McMillian — Limited injury does not defeat this established nontrivial malicious beating, February 25, 1992; Part A of O'Connor's opinion joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Kennedy, and Souter. The proposed universal *Whitley* extension has only four votes and is noncontrolling.

**Present operation effective:** *Hudson*, February 25, 1992.

**Open question:** A comprehensive mental-state rule for other force settings, psychological injury alone, and a general definition of punishment covering isolated unauthorized assaults remain unresolved. Medical-care, confinement-condition, personal-defense, and compensation rules remain distinct.

**Holdings navigation:** Hudson v. McMillian, 503 U.S. 1 (1992) — Prisoners’ rights.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Successive prosecutions, evidentiary overlap and criminal contempt

### Successive prosecutions, evidentiary overlap and criminal contempt

**Governing question:** When evidentiary overlap, conspiracy or completed criminal contempt bars a successive prosecution.

**Current rule:** Evidentiary use of an uncharged crime does not itself place the defendant in jeopardy for that crime. A conspiracy and its substantive objects remain distinct offenses; prosecuting the substantive crime does not bar prosecution of the agreement merely because acts help prove both. Grady's essential-conduct rule remains operative and does not displace the established agreement/substantive-offense distinction or become a same-evidence or same-transaction rule. Ordinary evidentiary restrictions retain their separate force. Beyond Blockburger, Grady bars a later prosecution when establishing an essential element requires proof of conduct constituting an offense already prosecuted; evidence reuse or a common episode alone does not suffice. Completed nonsummary criminal contempt places the accused in criminal jeopardy despite private initiation or a protective purpose. When contempt necessarily prosecutes a substantive offense forbidden by the order, the Government cannot prosecute that same offense again. Coercive civil sanctions, summary courtroom contempt and separate sovereigns remain outside Dixon. Foster II-V may proceed under its case-specific judgment, but no controlling rationale governs those counts: the Blockburger-only/overruling and special judicial-order rationales are incompatible, not logical subsets. Grady survives without an invented general contempt exception.

**Authority by component:**
- Evidentiary use is not prior prosecution: United States v. Felix, March 25, 1992, holding 1, White's unanimous Part I.
- Grady's relation to the distinct-agreement rule: the same record, holding 2, “Agreement remains a distinct offense,” White's Part II for Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. The two narrower judgment votes do not limit the seven-Justice rationale.

- Nonsummary punitive contempt triggers double-jeopardy protection: United States v. Dixon — Nonsummary punitive contempt triggers double-jeopardy protection, June 28, 1993. White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.
- Previously prosecuted necessary offenses cannot be tried again: United States v. Dixon — Previously prosecuted necessary offenses cannot be tried again, June 28, 1993. White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Possessory seizures and the claimant's own Fourth Amendment interest

### Possessory seizures and the claimant's own Fourth Amendment interest

**Governing question:** What instructions govern possessory seizures and the claimant's own Fourth Amendment interest?

**Current rule:** Meaningful government-attributable interference with possession of a home is a Fourth Amendment seizure without a search, privacy or liberty invasion, or criminal investigative purpose. A competing due-process characterization does not displace the specific seizure claim; coverage does not establish unreasonableness or §1983 liability. A suppression claimant must show interference with his or her own protected property interest in a seizure or reasonable expectation of privacy in a search. Conspiracy membership neither creates an exception to that personal-interest requirement nor destroys an actual protected interest. Attribution, immunity and the remaining merits must be separately established.

**Authority by component:**

- A seizure of a home protects possession independently of privacy: Soldal v. Cook County — A seizure of a home protects possession independently of privacy, 1992-12-08. White’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; nine Justices adopt the same rule.
- A due-process characterization does not displace the specific seizure claim: Soldal v. Cook County — A due-process characterization does not displace the specific seizure claim, 1992-12-08. White’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; unanimous holding.
- Suppression depends on the defendant’s own protected interest: United States v. Padilla — Suppression depends on the defendant’s own protected interest, 1993-05-03. Per curiam Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Present operation effective:** 1993-05-03; Soldal v. Cook County; United States v. Padilla, for the components identified above.

**Holdings navigation:** Soldal v. Cook County, 506 U.S. 56 (1992); United States v. Padilla, 508 U.S. 77 (1993).

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Serious risks in prison conditions and deliberate indifference

### Serious risks in prison conditions and deliberate indifference

**Governing question:** What objective danger, official culpability and response govern preventive prison-conditions claims and failure-to-protect liability.

**Current rule:** A prisoner may establish an Eighth Amendment violation through deliberate indifference to involuntary exposure posing an unreasonable risk of serious future harm without waiting for existing disease. For future-health exposure, the objective inquiry includes the seriousness and likelihood of harm and contemporary society’s unwillingness to tolerate the risk. For a substantial danger to inmate safety, an official must actually know facts indicating the risk, actually draw the inference and disregard it unreasonably. Constructive knowledge or negligence is insufficient. Reasonable protective action defeats deliberate-indifference liability even if harm occurs. Actual awareness may be proved circumstantially, including from an obvious risk, but obviousness permits rather than compels the inference. Deliberate avoidance of confirmation may support actual awareness; a specific prisoner warning, identification of the exact assailant or prediction of the precise time is unnecessary. Knowledge cannot be imputed collectively without proof of the individual official’s awareness. Prospective relief considers current danger and awareness through the litigation and whether unlawful disregard is likely to continue; an adequate showing of nonrecurrence may defeat that relief. Later notice can support prospective protection but does not retroactively establish awareness for damages.

**Authority by component:**

- Preventive protection does not require existing disease: Helling v. McKinney — Preventive protection does not require existing disease, 1993-06-18. White’s Opinion of the Court, Preventive protection does not require existing disease portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

- Actual-awareness standard, circumstantial proof, reasonable response and preventive relief: Farmer v. Brennan — Actual awareness and unreasonable disregard of substantial danger; Circumstantial proof and reconsideration of summary judgment; Current danger governs preventive relief, 1994-06-06. Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Eight Justices adopt this rule; Thomas joins only the judgment. The proof-and-remand portion of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Eight adopt its reasoning; all nine support vacatur and remand. The prospective-relief portion of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg; eight Justices adopt this rule and the bounded remand.

**Present operation effective:** Farmer v. Brennan, 1994-06-06, for subjective culpability, proof and preventive operation; Helling’s objective future-health inquiry remains effective from 1993-06-18.

**Holdings navigation:** Helling v. McKinney, 509 U.S. 25 (1993); Actual awareness and unreasonable disregard of substantial danger; Circumstantial proof and reconsideration of summary judgment; Current danger governs preventive relief.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Scientific expert evidence under original Rules 104(a) and 702

### Scientific expert evidence under original Rules 104(a) and 702

**Governing question:** What instructions govern scientific expert evidence under original Rules 104(a) and 702?

**Current rule:** Frye's mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite does not govern scientific expert evidence under the federal Rules. Under original Rules 104(a) and 702, the proponent must establish by a preponderance a preliminary foundation of scientifically reliable knowledge fitting the disputed factual question and assisting the factfinder. The judge examines principles, methodology and valid application flexibly. Relevant nonexclusive considerations include testability and testing, peer review and publication, meaningful error information and operational standards, and acceptance; none is an invariant prerequisite. Acceptance alone does not establish admissibility, and its absence alone does not require exclusion. Admissibility does not determine causation or sufficiency. No later Rule text, universal technical-expertise rule or appellate-review standard is adopted.

**Authority by component:**

- The enacted Rules displace mandatory general acceptance: Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals — The enacted Rules displace mandatory general acceptance, 1993-06-28.
