Article 106a’s later express espionage provisions do not withdraw the murder authority.

**Authority by component:**

- Statutory limiting power and clear-authorization question: I. Congress authorized these limitations on its capital murder punishment; II. No additional capital-specific wording is required for this authorization; constitutional boundaries: III. This military implementation assignment has adequate constitutional boundaries, 1996-06-03. Kennedy with Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (eight).

**Present operation effective:** Loving, 1996-06-03.

**Open question:** Article 36 alone, independent Article II power, general criminal-delegation specificity and a capital service-connection requirement remain undecided. No universal transfer of civilian capital doctrine or exemption of military institutions from structural review follows. Article 71 finality and presidential-approval requirements remain separate.

**Holdings navigation:** Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Judicial enforcement of federally authorized interbank liability

### Judicial enforcement of federally authorized interbank liability

**Governing question:** Enforcement of loss and liability allocated among banks in the check-payment system.

**Current rule:** A depository institution may enforce Federal Reserve interbank liability established within 12 U.S.C. §4010(f)’s delegation in federal district court or another competent court under §4010(d), within one year after the violation. Subsection (a)’s exclusion of another depository institution withholds its customer remedy, including its added damages and fees; it does not exclude the distinct subsection (f) action from the section-wide forum. The ordinary subsection (f) ceiling is the check’s amount; other damages require bad faith and loss proximately caused by the act or omission. Jurisdiction does not establish regulatory liability, damages or an automatic entitlement under every Board regulation.

**Authority by component:**

- Bank One — Judicial enforcement and preserved statutory limits, 1996-01-17; Thomas for all nine Justices.

- Bank One — subsection (f) liability limits and subsections (c)–(e) conditions, 1996-01-17; Thomas for all nine.

**Present operation effective:** Bank One, January 17, 1996.

**Exceptions and limits:** Subsection (c) protects an institution proving by a preponderance an unintentional violation caused by bona fide error despite reasonably adapted preventive procedures; an error of legal judgment is excluded. Subsection (e) separately protects good-faith conformity with a Board rule, regulation or interpretation despite later amendment, rescission or invalidation. The residual administrative-enforcement provision does not make Board damages adjudication exclusive; the statutory forum and liability delegation supply the action, not the absence of an administrative remedy.

**Holdings navigation:** Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264.

## Source: state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md — Saved seed under the preserved former Plant Variety Protection Act

### Saved seed under the preserved former Plant Variety Protection Act

**Governing question:** Reproductive sales protected by former §2543 where the former Act remains applicable.

**Current rule:** A qualifying farmer may sell for reproductive purposes only seed genuinely saved for replanting the farmer’s own acreage, including qualifying later disposition when planting plans change. Intentional multiplication for reproductive marketing is not exempt merely because the farmer primarily grows food or feed. Seed must be produced by the person from seed obtained, or descended from seed obtained, with the owner’s authority for seeding. Seller and purchaser must each primarily farm crops for nonreproductive sale, and the sale must comply with applicable state seed-sale law. Occupation concerns the whole business, not a variety-specific percentage; there is no fixed fifty-percent or prior-acreage ceiling.

**Authority by component:**

- Saved seed means seed retained for the farmer's own planting: Saved seed means seed retained for the farmer's own planting, 1995-01-18. Justice Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Seven Justices adopt the construction, explanation, and application that these sales fall outside the exemption. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela independently accepts the stated construction but does not join the Court's final application; his separate remand position is not counted as an additional application vote.
- Marketing does not require an advertising or distribution apparatus: Marketing does not require an advertising or distribution apparatus, 1995-01-18. Justice Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Seven Justices adopt these related constructions; Justice Stevens rejects the marketing premise. The Chief's separate opinion rejects an advertising prerequisite and a judicial fifty-percent quota without joining the final application.
- The former Act governs these transactions: The former Act governs these transactions, 1995-01-18. Justice Scalia's temporal-text portion, joined by Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct opinion votes. The Chief and Justice Stevens also apply former law in their separate writings, without joining every broader temporal rationale of one another or of Landgraf.

**Present operation effective:** Asgrow, January 18, 1995, for the former-law construction; the separately admitted April 4 transition determines which varieties retain it; the component authorities identified above.

**Controlling construction:** Marketing under former §2541(3) includes holding protected seed forth for sale and preparatory selling activities without extensive advertising, intermediaries or merchandising. Former §2541(4)’s separate restriction on using the variety to produce rather than develop a hybrid or different variety remains. Bona fide nonreproductive sales through customary nonreproductive channels retain independent protection; a buyer diverting such seed to planting is deemed to have §2567 notice of infringement.

**Exceptions and limits:** Failure of the saved-seed defense does not alone establish §2541(6)’s separate protected-notice infringement elements or damages. Whether an actually authorized §2543 sale must also comply with §2541(6) remains undecided. Under former §2567, owner-authorized distribution without marking bars damages absent actual awareness that propagation is prohibited or the variety protected; recovery then reaches only postnotice infringement. Courts may be lenient as to damages and injunction disposal for material acquired in good faith through prenotice acts; leniency is permission, not obligation.

**Effective transition:** The April 4, 1995 amendments delete the farmer-sales proviso only within their reach. Selecting plant-variety regimes and amended-protection notices states the preservation and refiling rules; former-law mechanics remain current for qualifying older varieties.

**Holdings navigation:** Saved seed means seed retained for the farmer's own planting; Marketing does not require an advertising or distribution apparatus; The former Act governs these transactions.

## Source: state/STANDING_STATE.md — 3. Standing Practices

## 3. Standing Practices

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991; signed explanations and certification, effective October 2, 1995.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). From October 2, 1995, referral does not prevent the Circuit Justice, or the substitute the term-effective Rules designate, from taking a necessary temporary action in his or her own name while the Court considers the application. Each action is explained in short signed writings, including the Circuit Justice's own explanation at final disposition, whether prevailing or dissenting, and a statement by each participating Justice in each Court action deciding relief, and is certified for issuance by the Chief Justice or the Chief's substitute, as Engine §10 provides. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice. Source: the OT1991 opening practice carried in the opening Standing State and the OT1993 Term Continuity Note. Source of the October 2, 1995 revision: user instruction.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/almendarez_torres_v_united_states_merits_1998-03-24.md

## Event

Almendarez-Torres v. United States, No. 96-6839. Argued October 14, 1997; decided March 24, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, reviewing its affirmance of an 85-month sentence. The question is whether the prior aggravated-felony conviction in 8 U.S.C. §1326(b)(2) is an element of aggravated unlawful reentry or a sentencing factor that need not appear in the indictment.

The September 1995 indictment charged unlawful reentry under §1326 without alleging the prior aggravated-felony convictions. At his December 1995 plea, Almendarez-Torres admitted the prior convictions and deportation; he preserved his objection that the indictment authorized no more than §1326(a)'s two-year maximum. The District Court imposed the enhanced sentence in March 1996.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the Fifth Circuit's judgment and remands for resentencing under the charged offense.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Souter and Ginsburg.
**Opposing:** O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer.

The enhanced sentence is vacated. On the existing indictment and plea, the sentence may not exceed §1326(a)'s two-year maximum. The guilty plea is not withdrawn and no acquittal is entered. Whether another source of law permits a further proceeding on a properly charged aggravated offense is not decided.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope and relationship to judgment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg | Statutory element classification, charging consequence and resentencing remedy |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part II | Scalia | Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg | Constitutional-avoidance explanation; no definitive constitutional classification rule |
| Concurrence in Part I and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Definitive constitutional ground for the element classification; does not join Part II's reservation of that question |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer | Penalty construction and bounded constitutional permission for this recidivist enhancement; would affirm |

## Holdings

### Section 1326(b)(2) requires an aggravated-offense allegation before its higher maximum may apply

**Holding and operative rule:** The prior aggravated-felony conviction specified in §1326(b)(2) is an element of the aggravated unlawful-reentry offense. It must be alleged in the federal indictment and, unless validly admitted as an element of the charged offense, proved to the jury beyond a reasonable doubt; an admission to the fact of a prior conviction does not cure its omission from this indictment, so the existing conviction permits no sentence above §1326(a)'s two-year maximum.

**Authority:** Part I of Scalia's opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. These five expressly adopt the statutory rule, its ordinary element consequences and the remedy. Four explain the construction through constitutional avoidance; Stone-Zsela gives a definitive constitutional ground separately. No majority adopts a general constitutional rule governing all facts that increase a statutory maximum.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that subsection (a)'s complete unlawful-reentry offense and two-year maximum must be distinguished from subsection (b)(2)'s aggravated offense carrying a twenty-year maximum. The prior conviction authorizes a different punishment ceiling; it does more than inform selection of punishment within the ordinary range. The provisions' cross-references permit that element construction. Hamling requires the indictment to state the offense's elements, and Gaudin requires proof of an actual element to the jury beyond a reasonable doubt when it has not validly been admitted. Almendarez-Torres admitted the prior convictions, but the indictment did not charge that aggravating element. His factual admission therefore does not answer the preserved objection to the punishment legally authorized by this charge.

The traditional use of recidivism at sentencing does not decide this statute's classification. McMillan addressed a minimum within an unchanged maximum, and Watts permits ordinary sentencing findings within conviction-based authority while reserving element evasion and other constitutional limits. Custis addresses collateral attacks on existing convictions, not the indictment's content. Old Chief confirms that prior-conviction status can be proved without unnecessary prejudicial detail; that evidentiary concern does not require treating status as a sentencing factor. The decision does not determine a subsidiary standard-of-proof claim, invalidate the admitted prior convictions, select a replacement sentence, or decide facts that do not increase the statutory maximum.

**Precedent treatment:** Hamling and Gaudin govern the consequences of the statutory classification; McMillan, Watts, Custis and Old Chief retain the distinct boundaries stated below.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Hamling v. United States:** Applied to require an indictment alleging each element of the aggravated offense.
- **United States v. Gaudin:** Applied after statutory classification; its jury-and-proof rule for actual elements remains controlling without deciding all sentencing classifications.
- **McMillan v. Pennsylvania:** Distinguished because its mandatory minimum left the statutory maximum unchanged; its limited holding remains intact.
- **United States v. Watts / United States v. Putra:** Preserved for ordinary sentencing within punishment authorized by the conviction; it does not resolve this higher-maximum classification.
- **Custis v. United States:** Distinguished as a collateral-review decision; the existing limits on attacking prior convictions remain intact.
- **Old Chief v. United States:** Applied to the distinction between element status and the form of adequate prior-conviction proof; no broader compulsory-stipulation rule is created.

## Law After Decision

Section 1326(b)(2)'s higher range requires the aggravated-offense element and the corresponding charging and proof protections. This statutory rule binds lower courts. The Court establishes no general constitutional prior-conviction exception and no general constitutional rule that every maximum-increasing fact is an element. Ordinary consideration of prior convictions within an independently authorized range remains available, subject to governing law.

## Separate Writings

**Scalia, joined by Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg, Part II.** These Justices regard the tenfold increase in maximum punishment as presenting serious doubt about Congress's ability to assign the aggravating fact solely to a sentencing judge. The element reading is fairly available, so they apply constitutional avoidance and decline to decide that ultimate question. McMillan's unchanged ceiling and Watts's ordinary sentencing setting do not eliminate the doubt. The risk of prejudice from prior-conviction evidence can be addressed through adequate admissions or other proper proof rather than removal of an element from the charge.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in Part I and in the judgment.** The constitutional allocation turns on the fact's legal effect. A fact necessary to increase the authorized maximum from two years to twenty years performs the work of an element and must be charged and either validly admitted as an element of that offense or proved to a jury beyond a reasonable doubt. Legislative placement cannot transfer that work entirely to the sentencing judge. Gaudin protects element determination; Watts permits ordinary judicial sentencing findings only within conviction-based authority. Custis concerns collateral attack, and Old Chief demonstrates how adequate status proof can avoid unnecessary prejudice. The admitted facts here do not cure an indictment that never charged the aggravated offense. This position permits consideration of prior convictions within a separately authorized range, preserves Custis's collateral-review limits, requires no prejudicial details when adequate admission or stipulation establishes the element, and decides no sentencing fact that leaves the statutory maximum unchanged.

**O'Connor, joined by Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer, dissenting.** Subsection (b)(2)'s recidivist subject, reciprocal cross-references and punishment structure make it a penalty provision for the alien already described in subsection (a). Existing conviction status differs from facts about commission of the present crime, and Congress may treat that prior adjudication as a sentencing factor in this setting. The admission supports the application but is not a substitute for their statutory classification. These Justices acknowledge that McMillan and Watts do not alone resolve an increased ceiling; the distinct recidivism ground does that work. They would affirm without adopting unlimited legislative power to remove offense elements or resolving an unpresented subsidiary proof-burden question.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Fifth Circuit for resentencing. The enhanced sentence cannot remain in effect under the existing indictment and plea; the sentencing court must act within §1326(a)'s maximum. No new aggravated-offense proceeding or particular replacement term is ordered.

## Source Notes

The applicable provision is the [1994 text of 8 U.S.C. §1326](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title8/pdf/USCODE-1994-title8.pdf), which increased the earlier fifteen-year aggravated-felony maximum to twenty years. The published account of the indictment, plea, preserved objection and sentence appears in [523 U.S. 227–229](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-224.pdf). That account does not establish the exact charged found-in date or an intentional waiver of the indictment objection. No temporal challenge or broader waiver is decided.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/aponte_matos_v_toledo_davila_merits_1998-02-26.md

## Event

Aponte Matos v. Toledo Dávila, First Circuit No. 97-1645, 135 F.3d 182. Plenary merits decision on submission, February 26, 1998. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the First Circuit’s February 3, 1998 judgment in the plaintiffs’ appeal from final summary judgments. The questions concern a warrant affiant’s alleged material fabrication, other defendants’ responsibility, search execution and the separately preserved appellate components. The action seeks damages under 42 U.S.C. §1983 against Puerto Rico officials and under Bivens and §1331 against federal officials. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is assigned. The lower-court argument on December 3, 1997 is not an argument before this Court.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

## Public Action

The reviewed appellate disposition is affirmed, preserving reversal of summary judgment on the material-fabrication claim against Laboy and remand of that claim.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Remedy and basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Laboy’s material-affidavit-fabrication claim | Affirm the First Circuit’s reversal of summary judgment, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Preserve the ordinary factual remand; no liability finding |
| Other officers’ participation; timely scope, announcement and force claims; Puerto Rico supervision | Affirm the distinct defense dispositions, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Lack of supported personal involvement or the particular conduct-date immunity grounds described below |
| Plichta computer-access and federal supervisor Ilario components | Sustain appellate nonreview, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Untimely appeals from separately final judgments; no merits endorsement |
| Counsel access and requested compulsory supplemental jurisdiction | Preserve waiver and reject compulsory retention, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | No counsel-right holding; a proper discretionary state-claim request remains possible |

Stone-Zsela agrees with the dispositions without joining the complete retained-immunity reasoning. His agreement concerning appellate nonreview of the Plichta and Ilario judgments, counsel-access waiver and the request to compel supplemental jurisdiction supplies no additional ground.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer | Affirms the reviewed component dispositions | All eight Associate Justices join all holdings, with the distinct procedural and immunity grounds preserved |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela | Agrees with every disposition | Joins the deliberate-falsehood application of Holding 1 and Holding 2’s personal-responsibility ground; not the complete knowing-or-reckless rule, retained-immunity explanation or procedural rationales |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | — | Agrees with every disposition | Preserves his distinct defense position without an additional ground for the identified procedural components |

## Holdings

### A material fabrication dispute cannot be resolved by relying on the warrant it produced

**Holding and operative rule:** An officer’s personally responsible knowing or reckless falsehood material to probable cause cannot obtain qualified immunity merely from the warrant that deception procured. Competent conflicting sworn accounts of the essential observation require ordinary factual resolution; negligence, a failed search or ulterior purpose alone does not establish this claim.

**Authority:** The Court opinion, joined by all eight Associate Justices, directly adopts the complete rule and this summary-judgment application. Stone-Zsela joins the deliberate-falsehood application, without joining the complete knowing-or-reckless and retained-immunity rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Franks requires culpable material falsehood; Malley prevents judicial issuance from automatically insulating the responsible officer. Clanton preserves both propositions and the corrected-affidavit inquiry. Laboy asserted that he saw two men exchange a firearm and enter the house. The resident swears that no man entered then, and her husband supports her account that he was elsewhere. The other officer reports what Laboy told him rather than an independent observation. This evidence presents a credibility dispute about the asserted observation necessary to probable cause; it is not merely an inference from an empty search. The Court assumes the competent plaintiff-favorable account for summary judgment without finding a lie. Crawford-El retains ordinary proof rather than an enhanced motive threshold. The alleged fabrication must still be proved, together with materiality, responsibility, causation and injury. No liability, damages, mandatory trial or automatic discovery entitlement follows.

### Each defendant must be connected to the asserted constitutional wrong

**Holding and operative rule:** Participation in a later search or supervisory rank alone does not establish responsibility for an affiant’s fabrication. Summary judgment stands where the supported record supplies no knowledge, assistance, agreement or affirmative causal link connecting the other officers or Puerto Rico supervisors to the alleged falsehood.

**Authority:** All nine Justices join this severable personal-responsibility ground and its record application.

**Controlling explanation:** The other officers’ presence supplies no evidence that they knew of, helped create or agreed to the alleged invention. The monthly warrant expectation and remote judicial criticism of Laboy do not establish a supervisor’s encouragement, acquiescence or deliberate indifference to this alleged deception. Fletcher separates actual evidentiary conduct from official role; Crawford-El preserves personal responsibility and causation. These principles reject collective liability without immunizing knowing assistance or a supported supervisory wrong in a different record. The disposition concerning federal supervisor Ilario rests instead on the untimely appeal, not these merits grounds.

### The timely execution claims fail on their own record and conduct-date grounds

**Holding and operative rule:** Qualified immunity on the timely search-scope, announcement and force components is sustained under the law and information relevant to December 6, 1993, without deciding that every challenged act was constitutional. Neither a Supreme-Court-only notice rule nor a physical-injury prerequisite governs.

**Authority:** All eight Associates join these distinct applications of the retained Harlow/Anderson defense.

**Controlling explanation:** Garrison and Ross confine the weapons search to places capable of containing the authorized objects; no evidence identifies a search beyond that scope. Uncompelled questions about money do not establish the claimed clearly unlawful expansion and authorize no unrestricted money search. On announcement, the supported preconduct legal setting left reasonable uncertainty concerning this entry’s constitutional requirement. Elder requires all relevant pre-existing law, and Lanier permits obvious coverage without a factual twin; later Wilson and Richards do not themselves provide 1993 notice. The ultimate reasonableness of the unannounced entry remains open. Graham governs force from the contemporary security perspective. The ax entry, officers, dogs and blockade formed an active weapons search; the reported conduct inside was orderly. Assuming that Colón pointed his gun and threatened the returning daughter who insisted on passing the blockade, this record does not establish conduct plainly outside the latitude allowed by 1993 law. Smith concerned considerable force against a wholly unresisting arrestee and does not turn injury into a necessary element. The security context, not the absence of touching alone, supports this immunity result. No universal permission to threaten a person follows.

### Appellate nonreview, waiver and supplemental jurisdiction retain distinct consequences

**Holding and operative rule:** The untimely appeals from the separately final Plichta and Ilario judgments do not permit review of their merits, and the undeveloped counsel-access claim remains waived. Survival of the federal fabrication claim does not compel the District Court to exercise supplemental jurisdiction over state claims; a proper discretionary request remains available.

**Authority:** The eight-Justice Court opinion adopts these grounds. Stone-Zsela agrees only with these dispositions and adds no rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The reported Rule 54(b) judgments were entered May 29 and July 22, 1996; the May 5, 1997 notice came after the applicable 60-day period. The computer component therefore supplies no holding that an unsuccessful attempt to access files cannot be a search, and the federal-supervisor component supplies no supervisory-merits ruling. Counsel access was not specifically developed on appeal. Section 1367 and City of Chicago keep supplemental power separate from discretionary retention: remand of one federal claim does not determine state claims against every defendant. This is the plaintiffs’ appeal from final judgments, so Johnson and Behrens’ limitation on officers’ interlocutory factual appeals does not bar ordinary final-judgment evidentiary review. The Court neither compels retention nor directs dismissal of every state claim.

## Precedent Treatment

- Franks and Malley are applied to personally responsible, culpable material affidavit falsehoods; judicial issuance is not categorical protection.
- Clanton supplies the same bounded material-deception rule and preserves corrected truthful probable cause and all liability predicates.
- Harlow and Anderson retain particularized, objective conduct-date immunity. Elder requires all relevant existing law; Lanier explains obvious coverage without supplying retrospective notice.
- Graham governs force; Smith is distinguished on the whole security record, preserving its rejection of a factually identical precedent requirement.
- Garrison and Ross retain object-based limits on a premises search.
- Crawford-El preserves ordinary proof, and Fletcher preserves responsibility for personal evidentiary acts; neither establishes liability here.
- Johnson and Behrens concern interlocutory review and do not remove this final-judgment appellate jurisdiction.
- City of Chicago preserves the distinction between supplemental power and discretionary retention. Wilson and Richards supply no retroactive notice for the 1993 conduct.

## Law After Decision

The surviving fabrication claim returns for ordinary proceedings under Franks, Malley, Clanton and the retained immunity framework. No contextual-defense replacement, heightened motive proof, collective responsibility, universal physical-injury rule or Supreme-Court-only notice rule is adopted. The separate nonreviewed and waived matters remain outside the merits decision.

## Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Stone-Zsela joins the Court’s deliberate-falsehood application and independent personal-responsibility ground. Intentional creation of facts material to a warrant’s justification cannot be reasonable reliance on that warrant, but the alleged lie and its materiality must be proved: an empty search is not proof of fabrication. Other officers and supervisors require evidence of their own knowledge, assistance or independent violation, and scope or execution objections require a supported invasion. He preserves his objection to treating a deliberate violation as a reasonable mistake under a duplicative official excuse, without converting his proposed contextual defense into the Court’s governing rule or joining its complete immunity rationale. Failure of a defense does not establish a violation, causation, damages, supervisory responsibility or entitlement to trial. He adds no reason for the procedural dispositions identified in the judgment.

## Procedure After Action

The First Circuit’s disposition stands: only the surviving Laboy material-fabrication claim returns for ordinary proceedings on the present federal claim map. The separate final judgments, appellate waiver and distinct defense rulings remain intact. A proper request concerning supplemental state claims may be considered under the applicable discretion; no particular result, hearing, discovery schedule, trial or damages is ordered. This Supreme Court review is complete.

## Source Notes

The complete lower opinion, [135 F.3d 182](https://static.case.law/f3d/135/html/0182-01.html), supplies the sworn-account recitations, individual claim map, December 1993 conduct and separately final judgments; [Public.Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/135/135.F3d.182.97-1645.html) provides a cross-check. Original declarations, the full appendix, Rule 54(b) orders and appeal notices are not separately reproduced. Their published recitations support the bounded dispositions; no additional testimony, default or concession is inferred. No independent warrant based on Plichta’s separate information is adjudicated.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/arkansas_educational_television_commission_v_forbes_merits_1998-05-18.md

## Event

Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, No. 96-779. Argued October 8, 1997; decided May 18, 1998. On review of the Eighth Circuit's judgment, 93 F.3d 497, reversing judgment for the Commission on Forbes's First Amendment challenge to exclusion from a televised candidate debate.

The state broadcaster planned a selective, moderated debate program. Its spring 1992 selection category covered major-party candidates and other candidates with strong popular support. Forbes later qualified for the ballot, requested inclusion and was denied. Staff cited lack of campaign organization, appreciable voter support and press recognition as a serious candidate. The jury found that political pressure and disagreement with his views did not cause the exclusion. The question is whether this actual program created a forum requiring his admission and whether the exclusion was constitutionally permissible.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses and directs entry of judgment for the Commission on the reviewed forum claim.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg.

The case returns for implementation of judgment for the Commission on this claim. No prospective publication requirement, fixed admission formula or compelled future broadcast is imposed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and the specified judgment | Entire opinion. |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg | Would sustain the appellate judgment against the exclusion | Insufficiently definite government candidate-selection standards, without universal ballot access. |

## Holdings

### 1. This selectively composed candidate debate is a nonpublic forum

**Holding and operative rule:** A public broadcaster's candidate debate is a nonpublic forum when its actual program retains editorial selection and has not been opened generally to every qualified candidate. Exclusions must be reasonable in light of the program's journalistic purpose and viewpoint neutral; neither public ownership nor the label journalism supplies immunity from those requirements.

**Authority:** O'Connor's opinion is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer. That six-Justice coalition controls both holdings.

**Controlling explanation:** The candidates express their own political views in this debate, so the broadcaster cannot treat their speech simply as the State's message. Yet a selective invitation program is not a general opening of airtime. Cornelius and Perry Education distinguish selective access from a designated public forum and preserve reasonable, viewpoint-neutral management.

The Commission planned a limited, moderated exchange and selected individual participants. Ballot eligibility did not itself open the program to every candidate. Rosenberger protects viewpoints within the opportunity actually created; it does not enlarge every opportunity into general access. Bronx likewise protects comparable private expression in a broad community forum while preserving genuine program boundaries. Its rental setting does not remove the broadcaster's supported selective function here. Denver Area supplies no categorical broadcasting classification or government editorial immunity.

The holding concerns this program, not every public broadcast or every candidate debate. A debate genuinely opened to all qualifying candidates would present a different access question. The State may neither disguise viewpoint exclusion as program definition nor rely on its own classification without examination of actual operation.

**Precedent treatment:** Cornelius and Perry Education supply the selective-access rule; Rosenberger and Bronx preserve viewpoint equality within genuine boundaries; Denver Area is not expanded beyond its distinct components.

### 2. The supported exclusion is reasonable and viewpoint neutral

**Holding and operative rule:** A genuine assessment of public support and journalistic significance may justify exclusion from this selective debate when it reasonably serves the program's purpose and is not a pretext for viewpoint discrimination. The established criteria and application satisfy that rule here; the Constitution imposes no additional prerequisite that future criteria be published.

**Controlling explanation:** Limited discussion time supports selecting a manageable group that will provide a useful exchange. Reasonableness does not require the best or only conceivable debate design. The advance selection category and staff's supported assessment of organization, voter support and press recognition bear on the program's purpose. The jury's express rejection of political pressure and viewpoint disagreement confirms the absence of those asserted improper causes; it does not by itself substitute for examining the criteria's reasonable relationship to the program.

The contrary standards argument rightly identifies the danger of officials controlling political access through vague judgments of seriousness. It does not establish an additional constitutional requirement of a numerical formula, universal written policy or prospective publication for this otherwise reasonable, neutral selection. Courts retain authority to test genuine viewpoint neutrality and pretext.

Ballot qualification alone neither establishes strong public support nor defeats a legitimate selective format. This holding does not authorize incumbent favoritism, sham criteria, selective administration, exclusion because a viewpoint is unpopular or denial from a program actually opened on terms the candidate satisfies. It decides no damages amount, official immunity, election remedy or future broadcast obligation.

**Precedent treatment:** The nonpublic-forum reasonableness and viewpoint rules are applied to the established record without adding a new publication or formula requirement.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Cornelius and Perry Education:** Applied to actual selective access and reasonable, viewpoint-neutral restrictions.
- **Rosenberger:** Preserves private viewpoints within the actual program rather than compelling universal access.
- **Good News/Good Sports and Bronx Household:** Preserve actual comparators and genuine forum boundaries; the planned selective debate differs from broad rental access.
- **Denver Area:** No universal public-broadcast classification or government editorial immunity is drawn from its separate components or incompatible rationales.

## Law After Decision

This selective debate may use reasonable, genuinely viewpoint-neutral judgments about public support and journalistic significance. Forbes's exclusion satisfies that standard on the established record. Judicial review for pretext and discrimination remains; no universal candidate-access, published-criteria or numerical-threshold rule is created.

## Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Souter and Ginsburg, would sustain the appellate reversal on a narrower ground than an automatic right of every ballot-qualified candidate to participate. The State has created a platform for candidates' competing private political speech, and sufficiently definite, reviewable selection standards are necessary to constrain its discretion. Cornelius's access principles, Shuttlesworth's objection to uncontrolled permit discretion and Rosenberger's neutral-allocation requirement inform that conclusion. The spring category existed, but major-party status and strong popular support leave too much room for officials' judgments about seriousness. The jury's rejection of improper motive does not answer this distinct objection to the governing standards. The dissent permits reasonable selection, prescribes no particular polling number, finds no viewpoint bias contrary to the verdict, and would order no automatic damages or future broadcast.

## Procedure After Action

The lower courts must implement judgment for the Commission on Forbes's reviewed First Amendment forum claim. No additional criterion-publication requirement applies. The abandoned statutory-access claim is not revived, and no separate damages, immunity, costs or future-election determination is made.

## Source Notes

The reported record at 523 U.S. 666 and the underlying Eighth Circuit judgment identify the advance selection category, invitations, ballot qualification, staff testimony and jury findings. The original trial papers are not reproduced. The verdict rejects improper motive; it is not a finding about every conceivable access standard. No broader past selection practice or later election fact is supplied.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/brogan_v_united_states_merits_1998-01-26.md

## Event

Brogan v. United States, No. 96-1579. Plenary merits decision, January 26, 1998; argued December 2, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the Second Circuit, United States v. Wiener, 96 F.3d 35 (1996). The question is whether the version of 18 U.S.C. §1001 governing Brogan's October 4, 1993 statement excludes a bare false denial of wrongdoing during federal investigative questioning.

Department of Labor and Internal Revenue Service agents made an unannounced visit to Brogan's home. He agreed to answer and denied receiving cash or gifts from JRD Management. The agents already had payment records; only afterward did they reveal those records and warn that lying to investigators was criminal. He did not change his answer, and no further questions were asked. A jury convicted him of the false statement and unlawful employer payments. The latter conviction is outside this review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The judgment on the reviewed §1001 conviction is affirmed. The statute supplies no categorical exculpatory-no exception for an otherwise covered false statement merely because it is a simple denial of wrongdoing.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirm the reviewed §1001 conviction, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg.
**Opposing:** Stevens and Breyer.

The conviction remains in force. No special remand, new trial, hearing, release, resentencing or collateral relief is ordered. The separate employer-payment conviction and other issues resolved in the appellate summary order remain outside this decision.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter | Supports affirmance | Parts I–II, III-A and IV: coverage, privilege boundary, contextual interpretation, limits and mandate |
| Same opinion | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance | Part III-B(1): potential abuse does not supply the categorical statutory exception; actual abuse remains undecided |
| Noncontrolling portion of principal opinion | Thomas | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance on an additional explanatory ground | Part III-B(2): the proposed doctrine may not prevent eliciting an elaborated fabrication; four votes only |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Souter | Supports affirmance | Separate statutory-coverage agreement, crime-generation concern, fault reservations and call for congressional attention |
| Statement concurring in part and in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Joins the principal opinion except all of III-B and joins Ginsburg's concurrence |
| Dissent | Stevens | Breyer | Would reverse as to the §1001 conviction | Bounded statutory exception based on intended coverage, purpose and established practice |

Ginsburg agrees with the narrow no-exception proposition through her separate opinion; she does not join the principal opinion. Stone-Zsela joins the limited abuse response in III-B(1), but not III-B(2). Souter's exclusion concerns the discrete abuse response, not the rest of the statutory or constitutional analysis.

## Holdings

### A false negative answer has no categorical exemption

**Holding and operative rule:** The offense-date §1001 does not categorically exclude an otherwise covered, knowingly and willfully false material denial merely because it is a bare response to investigative questioning about the speaker's wrongdoing. Every actual statutory predicate remains necessary; this decision adopts no universal materiality rule for every §1001 clause and decides no immaterial-statement application.

**Authority:** Thomas's Parts I–II, III-A and IV are joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, providing six direct votes for the principal statutory framework. Ginsburg independently agrees with the narrow no-exception proposition in her judgment concurrence. Part III-B(1)'s limited response to the abuse argument has Thomas, Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy; III-B(2)'s further response has only four votes and is not a holding.

**Controlling explanation:** A negative answer communicates a factual proposition. The statute's reference to any false statement is not limited to volunteered narratives, documents, benefit claims or statements under oath. The separate concealment clause's trick, scheme or device requirement does not become an element of every false-statement charge. The Labor and IRS investigation concerns included executive agencies, unlike the court-entity defect in Hubbard.

Gilliland describes protection of authorized governmental functions; it does not add a requirement that a lie successfully deceive investigators or independently pervert their work. Their prior knowledge therefore does not create the claimed categorical exemption, without deciding every distinct materiality question.

Text must be read in context. Ratzlaf preserves contextual fault and residual lenity, and Wells, Bates and Salinas distinguish enacted predicates from unsupported additions. The statute's claims and regulatory-reporting origins, longstanding lower-court doctrine and executive restraint make the proposed limitation substantial, but do not adequately support excluding this category from the enacted false-statements language. Generally applicable interpretive principles differ from a particular exemption lacking a sufficient statutory basis. Circuit disagreement alone supplies neither an amendment nor residual ambiguity requiring this exemption. Internal charging policy and unenacted proposals likewise create no statutory defense, and congressional silence does not ratify the doctrine.

The five Justices joining III-B(1) further conclude that potential investigative or charging abuse does not authorize this categorical exclusion. Actual materiality and culpability requirements, lawful charging discretion and Congress's responsibility remain distinct responses; discretion cannot replace proof or cure ambiguity. This conclusion neither approves every investigative tactic nor decides a claim of actual charging abuse. The Court affirms the reviewed construction without importing later statutory language, supplying unreported trial findings or treating the postanswer warning as proof of earlier knowledge.

### The privilege does not authorize a deliberately false answer

**Holding and operative rule:** The privilege against compelled self-incrimination provides no general right to answer falsely instead of invoking lawful protection against answering. It therefore supplies no constitutional-avoidance basis for the categorical exculpatory-no exception on this record; actual coercion, a valid privilege claim and unlawful demands for waiver remain distinct questions.

**Authority:** Thomas's principal analysis outside III-B has Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, providing six direct votes. Ginsburg's judgment concurrence is not counted as a join in this entire explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** Bryson rejects deliberate falsehood as a privileged substitute for lawful resistance to an official inquiry. Its benefit-affidavit facts do not themselves resolve every oral interview, but the privilege's function does not become a general license to lie. Brogan could remain silent; the difficulty of choosing among admission, silence and denial does not establish the claimed entitlement to false speech.

LaChance preserves the same distinction between a meaningful response and proved knowing falsity in employment proceedings, while expressly leaving this criminal statute to its own construction. Its administrative burden and service requirements do not migrate here. Garrity and Gardner retain their actual compelled-testimony and waiver protections. The Court creates no confession duty, universal warning requirement or rule approving every use of silence, and finds no custodial compulsion or waiver of a valid privilege in this interview.

### Affirmance leaves unpresented element and trial questions unresolved

**Holding and operative rule:** Rejection of the presented statutory exception leaves the reviewed conviction in force without reopening the separate payment conviction or ordering a proceeding on hypothetical trial errors. The meaning of willfulness concerning knowledge of illegality, actual surprise or lack of reflection, materiality, jury allocation and conduct-time notice remain subject to their own properly presented claims and supported records.

**Authority:** Part IV has the same six direct principal-opinion joins; Ginsburg independently concurs in affirmance and these limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Brogan's reported concession that the answer was false and knowingly and willfully given permits resolution of the categorical exception; it does not define culpability for every future case. The Second Circuit's reservation about knowledge of illegality and its discussion of possible surprise are not findings of error in this prosecution. Gaudin requires a jury determination beyond reasonable doubt when materiality is an actual element, but supplies no finding that this jury lost such an issue. Wells decides materiality under §1014, not every §1001 clause. Lanier protects conduct-time warning; a later decision cannot supply missing notice in 1993, but no new notice violation is adjudicated here. Literal truth, ambiguous questions, immaterial statements, coercion and actual charging abuse are not decided. The original denial preceded the agents' warning; failure to correct is not treated as an additional charged statement.

## Precedent Treatment

- Hubbard retains its offense-date institution requirement, its rejection of Bramblett's incompatible all-branch construction and its question-versus-argument boundary. This included executive-agency investigation presents no excluded-court nexus or new litigation exception.
- Gilliland's agency-function rationale is applied without adding successful deception or an independent perversion-of-functions element. It does not exempt simple investigative denials.
- Bryson supplies the privilege boundary against deliberate falsity as a substitute for lawful resistance. Its affidavit and benefit setting is distinguished from the present interview; it does not decide every coercion or oral-statement question.
- Ratzlaf's contextual interpretation and independent residual-lenity principle remain intact. They do not establish automatic ambiguity from circuit disagreement or a universal knowledge-of-illegality requirement.
- Wells, Bates and Salinas retain the actual statutory conduct, institution, transaction and fault predicates of their respective offenses. They inform the distinction between enacted requirements and unsupported additions without transferring their elements to §1001.
- Gaudin's jury-allocation rule and Lanier's conduct-time warning rule remain operative at their stated scopes. Neither establishes a trial error or new remand here.
- LaChance preserves actual employment proof and process while rejecting categorical knowing-denial immunity; it creates no criminal §1001 rule. Garrity and Gardner retain their separate compelled-testimony and waiver protections.

## Law After Decision

A bare exculpatory denial is not a categorical exception to the offense-date false-statements provision. This adds a criminal statutory coverage holding distinct from LaChance's employment rule. The actual included-agency matter, conduct, culpability, applicable materiality and criminal proof requirements remain necessary; negative phrasing does not establish immunity or automatically prove those predicates. The Court imposes no universal duty to answer, warning code, enforceable charging-policy defense or complete definition of willfulness.

The narrow statutory proposition has seven supporters, while the principal framework has the more limited joins shown above. The further abuse response in III-B(2), the Ginsburg–Souter concern analysis and the Stevens–Breyer exception are noncontrolling. No combined rationale is inferred from common concerns. The 1996 revision is not applied to the 1993 statement, and the later LaChance decision supplies no retrospective notice. Literal truth, ambiguity, immateriality, genuine compulsion and actual abuse retain their own legal predicates and procedural scope.

## Separate Writings

**Thomas's Part III-B(2), joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy.** These four Justices add that a simple-denial exception would not reliably prevent the feared escalation: an interrogator could press for a more detailed false account outside the proposed exemption. This policy response has no majority and establishes no rule approving investigative tactics or rejecting an actual abuse claim. Stone-Zsela does not join this additional argument; Souter joins neither part of III-B.

**Ginsburg, joined by Souter, concurring in the judgment.** The unqualified false-statements language forecloses the judicial exemption, but permits a serious problem requiring congressional attention. Officials who already know the answer can elicit a predictable denial and generate a new felony when the underlying conduct cannot be proved, limitations have run, or the conduct is innocent. This is different from merely imposing an additional punishment for an established underlying crime. The reported interview's surprise, absent prior warning and existing records illustrate the concern without proving that this prosecution was unlawful or that Brogan's underlying conduct was innocent.

The statute began with fraudulent claims in 1863, expanded in 1918 under a financial-fraud limitation and broadened in 1934 to reach regulatory falsehoods. Its present reach into informal criminal questioning extends well beyond those original problems. That history supports legislative attention, though it does not overcome the enacted language. In Nunley, the Court vacated a conviction at the Government's request because prosecution lacked normally withheld internal approval; that disposition established no statutory exculpatory-no defense. The charging-era manual said the statute did not apply to exculpatory denials; the 1996 and September 1997 versions stated restrictive charging policies. They evidence restraint, not judicially enforceable immunity. The sentencing policy excluding ordinary nonperjurious denials from an obstruction enhancement likewise does not amend the substantive offense.

Affirmance does not require conviction whenever a suspect denies wrongdoing. Wiener left open whether willfulness requires knowledge that the answer is unlawful and recognized that surprise or lack of reflection can bear on actual intent. Those reservations supply no finding of error or new proceeding here. Materiality language added in 1996 governs its own temporal scope and cannot be substituted for the 1993 statute. Proposed oral-denial protections and warnings were never enacted; they illustrate possible legislative choices, not present defenses. Congressional silence does not ratify the lower-court exception. The concern about creating a criminal offense also differs from adverse employment consequences for a proved knowing false denial under LaChance; accepting that employment rule does not eliminate this concern.

**Souter, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He joins the principal opinion except its discrete response to prosecutorial-abuse concerns in III-B, and joins Ginsburg's concurrence calling for congressional attention. His statement identifies that limited withheld assent; it does not withdraw his support from the remaining statutory, privilege or interpretive analysis.

**Stevens, joined by Breyer, dissenting.** They would reverse only insofar as the judgment sustains the §1001 conviction. A bounded statutory exception should cover a bare reactive oral denial of the questioned person's own suspected wrongdoing during an unsolicited criminal inquiry. Volunteered deception, affirmative misdirection, benefit submissions, regulatory reports and embellished accounts remain outside that exception. The Second Circuit accepted that Brogan's answer fell within the doctrine's core, so resolving its disputed margins is unnecessary here.

The dissent relies on intended statutory coverage, purpose, ordinary criminal-law background and sustained concrete judicial and executive practice. Williams illustrates that a claimed false instrument must actually make the statutory factual assertion; Sorrells and Palmer illustrate generally applicable limits concerning entrapment and statutory territorial reach. Their different holdings do not themselves create this defense, but demonstrate that broad language does not exhaust interpretation. The history of claims and regulatory reporting, together with longstanding treatment of ordinary defensive denials, supports the limited construction here. The dissent does not make executive policy binding law, infer congressional ratification from silence, adopt an unenacted bill, or announce a constitutional right to lie. Its substantial agreement with Ginsburg's concern leads to a different statutory judgment. The proposed reversal would not disturb the separate payment conviction, command release or prescribe an unverified sentence.

## Procedure After Action

The reviewed §1001 conviction remains in force. This Supreme Court review is complete, with no additional remand instruction, new trial, evidentiary hearing, resentencing or retained jurisdiction. The separate employer-payment conviction and independently unpresented questions receive no new disposition.

## Source Notes

United States v. Wiener, 96 F.3d 35, supplies the lower judgment, treatment of the claimed exception and reserved fault questions. The October 4, 1993 answer is governed by the 1988-edition §1001, including its then-existing department-or-agency coverage and penalty language; later amendments do not replace that text. The charged answer preceded the agents' warning. The reported concession of falsity and knowing/willful conduct does not establish unreported jury instructions, a universal materiality rule, illicit charging motive or custodial compulsion. Nunley, 434 U.S. 962 (1977), is procedural history concerning internal prosecution approval, not a merits holding adopting the statutory defense. The cited executive manuals, sentencing policy and unenacted proposals have the limited roles stated in the separate writing.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/bronx_household_of_faith_v_community_school_district_no_10_merits_1998-05-11.md

## Event

Bronx Household of Faith v. Community School District No. 10, Second Circuit No. 96-9633, 127 F.3d 207. Decided May 11, 1998, on review of the judgment sustaining the school district's worship and religious-instruction exclusions. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

The church sought to rent the M.S. 206B gymnasium each Sunday for services including hymns, communion, Bible reading, preaching and teaching. The school serves grades 5–7. The district opened facilities for educational, civic, social, recreational and community-welfare activities, including secular ethical and character instruction; it had rented to the church for sports and a banquet. Its policy barred religious services and instruction but permitted religious discussion and viewpoints. The stipulated use record covers one school year and establishes consistent exclusion of worship services. The questions concern viewpoint discrimination and the proposed establishment justification; the separate free-exercise, equality and RFRA rulings are outside review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate on submission and at decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the challenged worship and religious-instruction dispositions and remands for nondiscriminatory consideration and appropriate relief under the rules stated below.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Exclusion of the requested private Sunday worship | Reverse, 5–4 | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Remand for consideration on lawful neutral terms and appropriate relief. |
| Exclusion of religious instruction on subjects admitted for comparable secular instruction | Reverse, 8–1 | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela | Remand within the admitted-subject boundary. Stone-Zsela accepts the viewpoint rule but would affirm this worship application without a separate instruction judgment. |

No final rental permit, damages award or fixed injunction text issues from this Court. Agreement that establishment does not categorically compel exclusion is not a separate vote for worship access.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Rejects categorical establishment justification | Entire establishment analysis. |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg; Stone-Zsela joins only the general viewpoint rule | Supports instruction reversal | Eight join the application and remedy; all nine agree that admitted instruction cannot exclude an otherwise eligible religious perspective. |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts III and IV on worship and its remedy | Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports worship reversal and remand | Entire applied worship-access rule and its limits. The instruction portion of Part IV has the eight-Justice Part II coalition. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg; Stone-Zsela joins only the worship-activity analysis | Supports instruction reversal; opposes worship reversal | The integrated-service distinction; no establishment requirement to exclude. |
| Dissent from the disposition, with specified joins in the Court's reasoning | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would affirm narrowly without remand | Worship as a distinct excluded use; instruction rule as a limit on affirmance, not a separate judgment on this request. |

## Holdings

### 1. Private worship is not government worship merely because it uses school space

**Holding and operative rule:** Permitting this outside congregation to use school space for its own Sunday worship does not by itself establish religion. The district cannot justify this exclusion as constitutionally required, or rely on an identification of the school with the church, without facts establishing genuine governmental adoption rather than private access.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part I is joined by all eight other Justices. It answers the establishment question without determining the separate scope of the speech forum.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini retains Lemon as a single inquiry in which purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence: a specific prohibition or distinct doctrine controls when it resolves the conduct; an established permissible relationship defeats a claim on that ground; an otherwise legally sufficient unresolved allegation receives an attribution-and-function inquiry. The earlier access cases reserved standalone worship, so their results do not automatically settle this application.

Here the governmental act would be administering rental access. The pastor and congregation would arrange the services. No official selection, control, adoption, preference or obscured sponsorship is established. Tanford and Chaudhuri address the different act of incorporating devotional prayer into an official university program. Chabad and Pinette reject location alone as a sufficient attribution rule; Good News shows that the students' ages do not themselves turn independent expression into the school's speech.

The Second Circuit did not require reliance on the Establishment Clause, but treated avoidance of identification with a particular church as supporting the exclusion. That rationale cannot make religious identity and physical location decisive without actual governmental adoption. This conclusion neither immunizes every religious use nor opens every public building. Genuine future adoption or preferential operation would require its own inquiry, and government organization of worship as its own activity remains forbidden.

**Precedent treatment:** Agostini's modified Lemon inquiry is applied to the reserved worship setting; Tanford and Chaudhuri are distinguished as official programs; the contextual limits of Chabad, Pinette and Good News remain intact.

### 2. An admitted instructional subject cannot exclude its religious perspective

**Holding and operative rule:** Once this forum admits instruction on a subject, the district may not exclude an otherwise eligible speaker because the instruction presents a religious perspective. Permitting religious discussion does not cure a ban on religious instruction where comparable secular ethical and character teaching is admitted.

**Authority:** All nine Justices support the general rule in Part II. Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer also join its application and the instruction reversal; Stone-Zsela does not join that disposition.

**Controlling explanation:** Lamb's Chapel protects a religious perspective on an admitted subject even assuming a nonpublic forum. Good News and Church on the Rock apply that principle to teaching and related devotional expression within admitted programs; Rosenberger protects viewpoint equality within the actual program. The district admitted broad education and secular ethical and character instruction. It cannot require the comparable religious speaker to abandon instruction and confine itself to a different permitted format of discussion.

The Court does not convert this limited forum into an unrestricted curriculum or require access for every subject. It reverses the judgment sustaining the challenged instruction exclusion only insofar as it reaches subjects actually admitted for comparable instruction. The church's integrated worship request presents a separate access question, decided in Holding 3; the instructional rule does not make every worship service an admitted use by definition.

**Precedent treatment:** Lamb's Chapel, Good News, Church on the Rock and Rosenberger are applied to the actual instructional comparator, preserving their forum and program limits.

### 3. This broad private community forum cannot exclude the requested use solely for its devotional form

**Holding and operative rule:** In this actual after-hours educational and community forum, the district may not disqualify the church's otherwise comparable private Sunday meeting solely because its expression takes the form of worship. The district retains reasonable, viewpoint-neutral operational and genuine activity limits, but it cannot define comparable admitted uses simply by removing their religious expression.

**Authority:** Breyer's Parts III and IV are joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, a five-Justice controlling majority. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg oppose this application; no narrower rationale is assembled from their votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Perry Education and Cornelius permit a limited forum and genuine reasonable, viewpoint-neutral access restrictions. The actual forum, however, broadly admits community meetings and education, including moral and character development. A private meeting does not cease to serve those admitted functions merely because participants express their convictions devotionally. Widmar distinguishes religious exercise in a forum from government religious organization; Lamb's Chapel and Rosenberger prohibit subtracting the religious perspective from otherwise admitted expression.

The district's consistent exclusion proves that it maintained the challenged rule. It does not establish the rule's neutrality. The opposing approach treats the integrated service, particularly communion, as a categorically different activity. The Court rejects the premise that a religious meeting must have an exact secular sacramental counterpart before its educational and community expression is comparable. The denial rests on its worship character, not an established neutral scheduling, capacity, safety or supervision problem.

This is an applied holding on the described broad forum. It creates no right to every public building, continuous rental, preferential control, public financing of devotion or a permanently open forum. A genuinely different neutral activity boundary remains permissible. No compulsory disclaimer or universal sign rule is imposed. The absence of a categorical establishment bar is a distinct conclusion and is not itself the source of the access right.

**Precedent treatment:** The viewpoint principles of Lamb's Chapel and Rosenberger extend to this otherwise comparable worship use; the earlier standalone-worship reservations are resolved only at this scope. Perry Education and Cornelius retain genuine forum limits; Widmar supports the private/government distinction.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Agostini:** The governing modified Lemon inquiry is applied without reinstating independent former prongs or replacing it with a coercion-only rule.
- **Lamb's Chapel; Good News/Good Sports; Church on the Rock:** Their instruction and viewpoint holdings apply; their standalone-worship reservations are answered only for the present comparable use.
- **Rosenberger:** Viewpoint neutrality within the actual program supplies the speech principle; its printing-benefit setting is not converted into a general funding obligation.
- **Pinette and Chabad:** Preserve bounded private access and contextual attribution; location and religious identity alone are insufficient.
- **Tanford and Chaudhuri:** Official organization of devotional prayer remains prohibited and distinct from independent rental use.
- **Perry Education and Cornelius:** Preserve reasonable, viewpoint-neutral genuine subject and activity limits; consistency alone does not validate an exclusion.
- **Widmar:** Supports the distinction between private religious exercise in a forum and a governmental religious program.
- **Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department:** Supplies no traditional-public-forum classification or transplanted tailoring standard for this school.

## Law After Decision

Private Sunday worship may not be excluded from this broad forum solely for its devotional form, and comparable religious instruction receives the same access as admitted secular instruction. Establishment does not categorically require the exclusion, while actual governmental adoption remains legally distinct. Neutral management and genuine forum boundaries survive; no unrestricted facility entitlement or funding obligation arises.

## Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Souter and Ginsburg, agrees that the instruction exclusion impermissibly disadvantages a religious perspective on admitted subjects. He disagrees with the worship holding: an integrated recurring church service, expressly including communion, is a genuinely distinct activity rather than merely instruction from a religious perspective. Perry Education and Cornelius permit limiting the forum to the instructional, civic, social and recreational uses actually opened. Consistent exclusion is relevant to identifying that forum, although it is not sufficient by itself to establish constitutionality. The partial dissent would affirm the worship exclusion while reversing instruction. It does not claim that establishment requires exclusion or that incidental prayer in an admitted program may automatically be banned. Stone-Zsela joins this worship-activity reasoning only.

Stone-Zsela would affirm the entire judgment narrowly and order no remand. The application sought regular Sunday worship, and the stipulated record supplies no separate request for permitted discussion or instruction; he therefore treats the agreed religious-instruction rule as a limit on affirmance, not a separate judgment. He finds no comparable worship activity in the actual one-year use record and accepts a reasonable, viewpoint-neutral activity boundary. If comparable activity were established, he would reverse. His affirmance rejects the identification-with-a-church rationale and does not approve excluding a religious perspective on an admitted subject. Under Agostini, school location and religious identity do not establish governmental worship; actual official adoption could change that answer. Neither his forum position nor the Court's ruling creates a right to every building, a duty to keep the forum open or public financing of devotion.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Second Circuit for nondiscriminatory consideration and appropriate declaratory or injunctive relief within the two reviewed questions. The lower courts must apply the component holdings and otherwise lawful neutral conditions. The Court issues no permanent permit, damages award, fixed disclaimer, monopoly rental, required new evidentiary hearing or rewritten school policy. The ungranted free-exercise, equality and RFRA issues receive no new holding, and no automatic Supreme Court return is directed.

## Source Notes

The complete Second Circuit opinion, 127 F.3d 207, including Cabranes's separate instruction analysis, supports the request, stipulated use record, policy and actual comparators. The record covers one school year and supplies no finding that the service would be closed to the public or that an admission fee was proposed. No separate Supreme Court argument date or docket is supplied. The questions are analytical formulations rather than purported quotations from an unavailable petition.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/chaudhuri_v_tennessee_merits_1998-04-27.md

## Event

Chaudhuri v. Tennessee, Sixth Circuit No. 96-5538. Decided April 27, 1998, on review of the Sixth Circuit’s judgment at 130 F.3d 232. The questions concern officially arranged, nonsectarian invocations and benedictions at public-university functions, including a claim of practical employment pressure, and officially designated moments of silence adopted in their place.

Tennessee State University formerly invited clergy to deliver prayers at commencement and other university functions, requested nonsectarian wording, and incorporated the prayers into its programs. It replaced spoken prayers with silence in 1993. Chaudhuri, a tenured professor, sought damages and declaratory and injunctive relief. The lower court decided the former-prayer merits because the damages claim remained live; it upheld both practices. The live damages controversy permits review without deciding whether any particular defendant is ultimately liable.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the judgment concerning genuine moments of silence, reverses it concerning officially arranged spoken prayers, and remands within those questions.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Genuine designated moments of silence | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | The rejection of this challenge stands |
| Former officially arranged invocations and benedictions | Reversed and remanded, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Apply the official-worship prohibition at each function with established official arrangement and program placement; consider remaining liability, defenses and relief |

The 1993 private audience recitations and other matters outside the questions reviewed remain undisturbed. No damages, immunity disposition or prospective injunction is entered. Any prospective relief requires a continuing controversy and must reach the unlawful official practice while preserving genuine silence and private expression.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
