| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg; Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join Part I only | Supports partial affirmance, reversal and remand | Part I: review scope, live damages controversy and genuine silence; Part II: official-prayer rule and prayer remand |
| Dissent in part, Part I | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would affirm the former-prayer ruling; joins the Court’s Part I | Voluntary adult ceremonial relationship |
| Dissent in part, Part II | Scalia | Thomas | Same disposition | Distinct compulsion account |
| Separate dissent in part | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would affirm the former-prayer ruling; joins the Court’s Part I and Part I of Scalia’s dissent | Practical-pressure analysis; does not make formal punishment indispensable |

## Holdings

### Genuine silence leaves religious thought to the participant

**Holding and operative rule:** A public university may designate a genuine moment of silence at an official function, including as a replacement for prayer, when it neither prescribes nor directs prayer, religious profession or other devotional exercise. A period called silence loses that justification if its actual administration directs worship; genuinely private religious expression does not become governmental merely because it occurs during the silence.

**Authority:** Part I of Breyer’s opinion is joined by all eight other Justices and controls unanimously.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the governmental act must be identified by what it does. Agostini makes purpose, effect and institutional connection evidence within the updated Lemon inquiry. Sherman distinguishes a civic exercise from official worship; its permission depends on actual function, not voluntariness alone. Here the University prescribed silence and left each person free to reflect, pray or do neither. Its president stated a solemnizing purpose, and the record supplies no official direction to pray.

The earlier counsel letter favoring silence as an alternative to prayer is relevant, but neither that advice nor the replacement’s origin converts the implemented exercise into worship. The speaker’s clerical identity at the August ceremony likewise does not supply a direction absent from his request for silence. Chaudhuri conceded no evidence of university complicity in the audience recitations. Those private acts lie outside the questions reviewed and receive no new merits judgment. Officially prompted recitation or university complicity would present a different case; private prayer need not be suppressed to preserve genuine silence.

**Precedent treatment:** Agostini governs the inquiry; Sherman supplies the civic/worship distinction; Tanford’s reservation of silence is resolved only for this nondevotional practice.

### A university may not present devotional prayer as its own program

**Holding and operative rule:** A public university violates the Establishment Clause when officials select or invite a speaker to deliver an invocation, benediction or other spoken prayer as part of its official function. Nonsectarian wording, an adult audience, voluntary attendance, freedom to abstain and a secular ceremonial purpose do not cure that official worship; no separate finding of employment pressure is required.

**Authority:** Part II is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, including the author, and controls by six direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the University’s selection and presentation of prayer makes the devotional exercise its own. Agostini preserves the specific prohibition on government-organized worship. Tanford applies that prohibition to university commencement despite maturity, nonsectarian wording and genuine abstention. Lee and Ingebretsen preserve the distinction between official worship and private expression.

The University invited clergy, requested generic religious language and placed actual invocations and benedictions in its functions. Its failure to review every word did not make the prayers private. The same governmental act occurs at the other supported university functions, so the prohibition is not confined to graduation. A purpose of solemnizing an event cannot justify the forbidden means. Marsh’s legislative-prayer relationship does not establish permission for these university prayers, and Sherman’s civic Pledge is distinct from actual devotion.

The Court need not resolve the separate employment-pressure theory. Chaudhuri’s service-evaluation allegation and the defendants’ nonmonitoring/no-penalty account, including the dean’s affidavit, do not become a new finding of coercion or its absence. Nor does the decision require formal punishment for every pressure claim. The holding leaves private forum speech, spontaneous remarks, nondevotional acknowledgment and lawful silence distinct. Correcting the prayer-merits ruling does not determine conduct-time immunity, damages or prospective mootness.

**Precedent treatment:** Tanford’s official-prayer rule is applied to the same practice at other university functions; Agostini, Lee and Ingebretsen retain their independent limits; Marsh and Sherman remain distinct.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Agostini v. Felton:** Applied. Purpose, effects and entanglement inform one inquiry; no former independent Lemon prong returns.
- **Tanford v. Brand:** Applied to officially arranged devotional prayer at the supported university functions beyond commencement; its private-expression and remedial limits remain.
- **Lee v. Weisman:** Applied for the independent prohibition on official worship. Its practical-pressure ground remains intact and is not decided here.
- **Ingebretsen v. Jackson Public School District:** Applied for official attribution and relief preserving private expression; student or private delivery alone cannot privatize official worship.
- **Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21:** Applied to distinguish actual civic function from worship, without extending permission to devotional prayer.
- **Marsh v. Chambers:** Distinguished as legislative prayer; no general exemption for adult ceremonies follows.
- **Arizonans for Official English v. Arizona:** Preserved. A damages request against the State or an official-capacity defendant who is not a §1983 damages person cannot alone sustain jurisdiction; this live controversy does not decide any defendant’s ultimate liability.
- **Will v. Michigan Department of State Police; Hafer v. Melo:** Their distinct state/official-capacity and personal-capacity rules remain; no capacity allegation is adjudicated here.
- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald; Anderson v. Creighton:** Personal immunity, if applicable, remains tied to the challenged conduct’s date, not the announcement of this decision.

## Law After Decision

The official-worship prohibition governs the University’s arranged prayers at each function supported by the record. Genuine designated silence is permissible on the stated terms. The law does not equate private audience prayer with governmental worship, create an adult-ceremony exemption, or impose liability merely from a present constitutional conclusion. Unresolved prospective relief and conduct-time defenses retain their independent requirements.

## Separate Writings

**Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas in Part I, and by Thomas in Part II, dissenting in part.** These Justices accept the actual silence holding but would also uphold the former prayers. Part I preserves their Tanford view that a voluntary adult university ceremony is a permissible ceremonial relationship under the updated Lemon inquiry, informed by Marsh’s accommodation of ceremonial prayer. They acknowledge official attribution but reject treating this adult setting as prohibited worship merely on that basis. Part II emphasizes historical ceremonial accommodation and requires governmental compulsion to engage in religious observance, rather than official presentation of prayer alone. The University’s nonmonitoring and no-penalty account, supported by the dean’s affidavit concerning evaluations, does not establish required religious participation or penalized nonattendance. On that record Scalia and Thomas find no compulsion under their account. Kennedy does not join that methodology; he retains the distinct practical-pressure inquiry. Neither part supplies a rule binding lower courts contrary to the Court’s holding.

**Kennedy, separately dissenting in part.** Kennedy joins the adult-ceremony ground but retains Lee’s practical-pressure inquiry. Formal penalties are not indispensable to religious coercion. Here the uncontradicted nonmonitoring and no-penalty account, together with the dean’s affidavit concerning Chaudhuri’s evaluations, fails to establish the alleged practical pressure. He would affirm the former-prayer judgment on that record while leaving a materially different employment-pressure case open.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Sixth Circuit for further proceedings within the two reviewed questions. Remaining defendant-specific liability, statutory remedy, immunity and prospective-controversy issues must be decided under their own rules. The Court orders no new evidentiary hearing, document production or automatic injunction and reopens no unrelated claim.

## Source Notes

The factual and procedural account is supported by [Chaudhuri v. Tennessee, 130 F.3d 232](https://openjurist.org/130/f3d/232), including the lower court’s live-damages account and the distinction between official practices and private audience recitations. That opinion does not adjudicate defendants’ capacities, the statutory basis of damages or immunity; no defendant-specific liability finding follows.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/city_of_florence_v_chipman_merits_1998-06-01.md

## Event

City of Florence v. Chipman / Stemler v. City of Florence; Sixth Circuit Nos. 96-5993 and 96-5996, 126 F.3d 856. Plenary merits decision, June 1, 1998, on submission. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) concerns the Sixth Circuit's October 8, 1997 reinstatement of Black's individual substantive-due-process claim and Stemler's individual equal-protection claim; rehearing was denied November 13, 1997. The plaintiffs appealed final individual dismissals and municipal summary judgments. This is not an interlocutory officer appeal. The questions concern compelled dangerous placement, purposeful discriminatory selection despite probable cause, and qualified immunity for conduct on February 18–19, 1994.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The two individual-claim reinstatements are affirmed for further proceedings under the limits stated below.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0 on each reinstated individual claim.** **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Black's substantive-due-process claim and Stemler's equal-protection claim may proceed. The decision determines legal sufficiency on the proper favorable account, not final wrongdoing, damages or every defendant's ultimate immunity. It orders no automatic trial, discovery program or new hearing. Municipal and official-capacity judgments and the other excluded claims are undisturbed.

## Opinion Topology

O'Connor delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine adopt the narrow restraint, arbitrary-selection and conduct-time immunity holdings with their individual-proof limits. Scalia files a concurrence, joined by Thomas, preserving their direct-restraint basis and rejecting any implication that they adopt a broader conscience framework. Their concurrence withholds assent from no proposition actually adopted by the Court.

## Holdings

### 1. Knowing, involuntary transfer into danger states a bounded due-process claim

**Holding and operative rule:** An officer who actually restrains a person's ability to protect herself and personally directs or carries out her involuntary transfer into a serious danger the officer actually appreciates may not deliberately and unreasonably disregard that danger when the officer has authority and a real opportunity to respond. The claim requires injury attributable to that defendant's conduct; negligence, constructive knowledge, another officer's knowledge, assistance in a genuinely voluntary departure or a reasonable protective response that fails does not suffice.

**Authority:** O'Connor's opinion for all nine Justices controls this direct-restraint application. It adopts no broader free-standing conscience framework or general prison-to-roadside rule.

**Controlling explanation:** DeShaney distinguishes failure to protect from the State's affirmative restraint of a person's ability to protect herself. Youngberg supplies the custody-related protection principle, Daniels excludes negligence, and Collins distinguishes actual compulsion from voluntary exposure. Pinder's noncoercive assurances did not create custody and do not foreclose this alleged compelled transfer. Farmer, Doe and Lewis preserve actual awareness, individual responsibility, reasonable response and genuine opportunity; their distinct settings do not supply a mechanical roadside checklist.

The favorable account alleges specific warnings of Kritis's violence and intoxication, a threat of arrest if Black did not leave with him, and physical removal and placement in his truck. Those successive actions differ from Lewis's rapidly unfolding pursuit. Neither the earlier private chase nor the later crash turns the roadside transfer into a police-pursuit case. No fixed number of seconds establishes opportunity or culpability.

Dusing allegedly received warnings and directed the threat; Dolan and Alsip allegedly removed Black, and Alsip placed her in the truck. Reuthe's alcohol report is not itself culpable placement. Alsip's limited information and the competing voluntary-departure account remain material. Knowledge and responsibility cannot be pooled. Obvious danger may support an inference of actual awareness but does not compel it. The fatal crash permits the causal theory, not a finding against every officer; the renewed fight, uncertain steering cause and legally proper scope of the state causal ruling remain relevant. The Court creates no general duty to investigate every warning, arrest Kritis or prevent private violence, and does not reach noncustodial state-created danger.

### 2. The favorable knowing-coercion account cannot be dismissed categorically on qualified-immunity grounds

**Holding and operative rule:** Qualified immunity does not categorically bar Black's claim on the favorable allegation that an officer knowingly used actual police restraint to compel her transfer into an appreciated serious danger. The relevant clarity is that of February 18–19, 1994, assessed for each officer's own information, authority and acts; the ruling leaves supported factual and legal defenses available at their proper stage.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion controls this separate conduct-time immunity holding; it neither finds every defendant liable nor substitutes a new defense for Harlow and Anderson.

**Controlling explanation:** Harlow and Anderson ask whether existing law made the actual conduct's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent. DeShaney, Youngberg, Daniels and Collins supplied the restraint/direct-deprivation distinction before this encounter. Meador and Heflin applied custodial deliberate indifference. Walton's absence of police custody distinguishes its immunity result. Nishiyama's intentional unreasonable exposure to a known danger supplies relevant warning, but its gross-negligence language and contested noncustodial reach are neither adopted nor indispensable.

An exact roadside factual twin is unnecessary, while a general assertion of bodily security would be insufficient by itself. The alleged knowing use of police control supplies the particular connection. Neither uncertainty about noncustodial protection nor disagreement about every officer's information justifies dismissing that favorable account categorically.

Elder's February 23, 1994 decision, Farmer and later decisions including Pinder, Doe, Lanier, Lewis and DeCarlo may clarify present analysis but cannot supply missing notice retroactively. DeCarlo reinforces personal role and stage-specific adjudication; its different administrative setting does not compel immunity here. There is no mandatory order between difficult constitutional merits and immunity. The Court resolves this properly presented narrow claim without determining disputed knowledge, actual opportunity, causation or the outcome of a later supported motion.

### 3. Probable cause does not defeat the pleaded arbitrary-selection claim

**Holding and operative rule:** Probable cause to arrest does not defeat an equal-protection claim that officers deliberately selected an otherwise chargeable driver solely to impose arbitrary group hostility while deliberately leaving an actually comparable driver unenforced on that basis. The claimant must establish discriminatory purpose and effect, relevant comparison, each defendant's participation and attributable injury; a supported legitimate enforcement distinction defeats the asserted arbitrary comparison.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion controls this narrow selective-enforcement rule, without an orientation-based scrutiny tier.

**Controlling explanation:** Yick Wo prohibits arbitrary discriminatory enforcement; Oyler preserves enforcement discretion while rejecting selection on an unjustifiable arbitrary basis; Wayte requires purpose and effect. Moreno excludes bare group hostility as a legitimate governmental end. Whren preserves an independent equality claim despite objective Fourth Amendment authority, and Romer applies ordinary review's prohibition on bare hostility without creating a new tier.

Stemler alleges that officers deliberately arrested her while leaving the visibly intoxicated Kritis unenforced because they perceived her as a lesbian and sought to impose arbitrary harm. Her denial of that orientation does not erase the alleged perceived classification. Wince arrested her; Wince, Dusing and Dolan are her individual defendants. Other defendants on Black's claim are not automatically defendants on hers.

Probable cause, a hostile epithet, knowledge of Kritis's characterization, differential arrest or later acquittal alone proves neither purpose nor the necessary comparison. The favorable account permits the claim, but legitimate differences and each defendant's actual responsibility remain for proof. Bowers's particular conduct holding does not authorize hostile use of unrelated DUI laws. The Court imposes no duty to arrest every offender, imports no Armstrong prosecutor-discovery threshold and prescribes no universal new burden sequence from Crawford-El. It resolves neither every kind of personal animosity nor every orientation classification.

### 4. The narrow arbitrary-selection prohibition predates this encounter

**Holding and operative rule:** The existing prohibition against purposeful arbitrary group-based enforcement permits Stemler's narrow claim to proceed despite qualified immunity, subject to proof of the actual comparator, arbitrary purpose, effect and each officer's conduct. The Court relies on law existing before February 18–19, 1994, not later recognition of a new protected classification or retroactive notice from Romer or Whren.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion controls this distinct immunity application and its proof limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Yick Wo, Oyler, Wayte and Moreno already prohibited the particular arbitrary selection alleged here. Oyler did not make every selective enforcement choice unconstitutional; its rejection of mere selectivity and insufficient statistics remains. The claim rests on deliberate selection for bare group harm, supported by an actual comparator, rather than a demand for heightened scrutiny or an inference from nonarrest alone.

The competing contention that no established orientation tier existed fails to answer that narrower prohibition. Bowers did not exempt unrelated enforcement from equal protection. Romer and Whren clarify present doctrine but supply no February 1994 notice. Harlow and Anderson still require each defendant's particular conduct to fall within the established prohibition. The decision permits ordinary proof and defenses; it does not adjudicate motive, comparative intoxication or responsibility as established facts.

## Precedent Treatment

- **DeShaney v. Winnebago County:** Applied to actual restraint rather than a general failure to protect; noncustodial state-created danger remains unaddressed.
- **Youngberg v. Romeo:** Applied for the protection principle arising from actual custody, without mechanically transferring every institutional standard.
- **Daniels v. Williams:** Applied to exclude negligence; the separate actual-awareness requirement is preserved by the custody authorities and this direct-restraint application.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, February 26, 1992:** Its distinction between voluntary exposure and actual compulsion is applied; its separate municipal-attribution inquiry remains intact.
- **Pinder v. Johnson, May 21, 1996:** Distinguished because assurances and a noncoercive undertaking did not restrain the claimant there; its immunity result and reservation of distinct affirmative deprivation remain.
- **Farmer v. Brennan, June 6, 1994:** Its actual-awareness, permissible-inference and reasonable-response limits inform the present analysis; it supplies no earlier notice or wholesale roadside Eighth Amendment framework.
- **Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, October 16, 1995:** Its personal knowledge, authority, opportunity and causal-role limits inform the analysis without becoming a mechanically transplanted checklist.
- **County of Sacramento v. Lewis, May 26, 1998:** Distinguished from alleged knowing roadside restraint and transfer; its rapid-pursuit harmful-purpose requirement is not extended to every vehicle-related injury.
- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald:** Applied to preserve personal qualified immunity and the conduct-time inquiry.
- **Anderson v. Creighton:** Applied to require particularized apparent unlawfulness without an identical factual precedent.
- **Elder v. Holloway, February 23, 1994:** Its all-relevant-law method is preserved; its date postdates the encounter and supplies no earlier notice.
- **United States v. Lanier, March 31, 1997:** Its rejection of a factual-twin prerequisite informs present review without retrospective notice or substitution of an unpresented Fourth Amendment claim.
- **DeCarlo v. Fry, May 28, 1998:** Applied to preserve personal role, actual information and the proper procedural stage; its administrative immunity result does not decide this coercive-transfer claim.
- **Meador v. Cabinet for Human Resources:** Its pre-event custodial deliberate-indifference application supports notice at the bounded restraint level.
- **Heflin v. Stewart County:** Its pre-event custodial protection rule supports the same particularized notice inquiry.
- **Walton v. Southfield:** Distinguished because the absence of police custody mattered to its immunity result.
- **Nishiyama v. Dickson County:** Considered for intentional unreasonable exposure to known danger; its gross-negligence language and disputed noncustodial reach are not adopted.
- **Yick Wo v. Hopkins:** Applied to arbitrary discriminatory enforcement despite formally lawful authority.
- **Oyler v. Boles:** Applied while preserving discretion, the insufficiency of mere selectivity and the need for an unjustifiable discriminatory basis.
- **Wayte v. United States:** Applied to require discriminatory purpose and effect.
- **United States Department of Agriculture v. Moreno:** Applied to exclude bare group hostility as a legitimate enforcement purpose.
- **Bowers v. Hardwick:** Distinguished as addressing a particular substantive conduct question, not authorization for arbitrary unrelated DUI enforcement.
- **Whren v. United States, June 10, 1996:** Applied to preserve independent equality despite objective probable cause; it supplies no 1994 notice.
- **Romer v. Evans, May 20, 1996:** Applied at ordinary review's bare-hostility boundary, without a new scrutiny tier or retrospective notice.
- **United States v. Armstrong, May 13, 1996:** Its prosecutor-discovery threshold is not extended to this police-enforcement merits claim.
- **Crawford-El v. Britton, June 16, 1997:** Ordinary proof and lawful case management remain; no heightened evidentiary hurdle or universal burden sequence is imposed.

## Law After Decision

The decision permits the two individual claims on knowing coercive restraint and purposeful arbitrary selection, while retaining actual awareness, genuine opportunity, comparison, personal responsibility, causation and particularized immunity. It establishes no general police-protection duty, universal conscience framework, orientation scrutiny tier or collective-knowledge rule. A lawful basis to arrest answers neither every equality objection nor the separate dangerous-transfer claim. Current constitutional law and conduct-time notice remain different inquiries. No municipal liability, final constitutional tort or damages award follows from reinstatement.

## Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs fully in the narrow opinion. DeShaney's direct-restraint principle and Collins's distinction between voluntary exposure and compulsion support this alleged affirmative deprivation. They do not adopt a free-standing judicial-shock inquiry or a general duty to rescue persons from private danger. Lewis's distinct reckless-pursuit claim therefore does not compel the same merits result here. Thomas's disagreement with Farmer's Eighth Amendment punishment predicate remains; today's direct-deprivation holding neither converts that judgment concurrence into a full Farmer join nor transplants every prison rule to roadside custody.

They also join the narrow equality and immunity holdings. Moreno's bare-harm prohibition, preserved in their Romer position, does not depend on a suspect classification. Bowers does not license deliberate hostile selection under unrelated traffic laws. Actual comparison, purpose, effect, personal conduct and the old prohibition must do the work. Their concurrence supports no broader framework than the Court adopts and no finding that the disputed allegations have been proved.

## Procedure After Action

The two individual claims return for ordinary proceedings at their proper stages. Supported motions, personal defenses and ordinary proof remain available; no trial or discovery step is automatically required. The existing state probable-cause judgment remains effective. The lower court's possibility of reconsideration if that judgment is actually reversed remains conditional, with no present reversal or automatic revival. The state direct-cause determination receives its legally proper preclusive scope without deciding every federal causal issue. Municipal and official-capacity judgments remain favorable to defendants. False arrest, ordinary malicious prosecution, voluntarily dismissed excessive force, unpleaded fabrication and a separate sex-classification theory are not reinstated.

## Source Notes

[Stemler v. City of Florence, 126 F.3d 856](https://static.case.law/f3d/126/html/0856-01.html) and the [complete opinion, concurrence and rehearing addition](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/126/126.F3d.856.96-5996.96-5993.html) supply the final-judgment posture, distinct claimants, individual roles and source limits. The allegation of involuntary departure conflicts with Dolan's account; Alsip's information is separately limited. The crash's steering cause remains uncertain. Wellford's rehearing explanation requires actual awareness and drawing the risk inference; the majority's occasional constructive-knowledge shorthand does not replace that requirement. No later Kentucky reversal or new factual finding is established.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/clanton_v_cooper_merits_1997-12-29.md

## Event

Clanton v. Cooper, review of the Tenth Circuit's interlocutory qualified-immunity judgment in No. 96-7082, 129 F.3d 1147. Decided December 29, 1997, on the surviving federal personal-damages claims against Cooper. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated on submission and at decision; no Justice was absent.

## Public Action

The judgment is affirmed in part and vacated in part, and the case is remanded to the Tenth Circuit with instructions to dismiss the factual-sufficiency portions of the interlocutory appeal and proceed on the reviewable legal questions within the limits below.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Legal immunity rejection on the assumed deliberate material warrant deception and later NCIC detention deception | Affirm, without liability findings | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer — nine | None |
| Factual-sufficiency and participation challenges in the interlocutory appeal | Require dismissal for lack of appellate authority | All nine Justices | None |
| Distinct coerced-third-party-confession branch | Vacate the appellate disposition and remand for proper legal review on assumed historical facts | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer — eight | Stone-Zsela would affirm the surviving immunity denial on this branch |
| Rejected subsidiary warrant theories, disposed defendants and state claims | Leave undisturbed | All nine Justices | None |

The existing District Court denial remains operative subject to lawful claim-specific appellate adjudication. The Court awards no immunity, liability, damages, mandatory trial, discovery plan or new hearing on the remanded confession theory.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: legal/factual appellate boundary | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-A: warrant deception | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer in full; Stone-Zsela joins the deliberate-falsity application and subsidiary-theory limits |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II-B: later NCIC detention deception | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III: distinct confession branch and remand | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | None |

## Holdings

**1. Immediate review separates legal immunity from historical facts.** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve a separable legal defense on the proper facts assumed in the nonmovant's favor, but may not decide factual sufficiency, participation, credibility or which disputed account is true. Ultimate constitutional voluntariness is not wholly historical merely because its test considers circumstances. Part I is unanimous.

Johnson and Behrens protect the assumed historical account without foreclosing genuine legal review. Section 1254 permits this Court to review the circuit case but does not enlarge the circuit's §1291 authority. Miller distinguishes the ultimate voluntariness question from historical facts; Ornelas preserves task-specific allocation rather than a universal rule for mixed questions. A court therefore cannot avoid a legal immunity question by labeling the entire coercion inquiry factual, or obtain factual review by attaching a legal label. The factual portions of Cooper's appeal must be dismissed; the distinct reviewable questions remain.

**2. The assumed material warrant deception defeats the asserted legal immunity defense.** On the proper assumed facts, an officer cannot obtain immunity for knowing or reckless factual falsehoods material to probable cause merely because a judge issued the warrant. Franks and Malley supplied the relevant pre-August-1993 rule. Part II-A's complete rule has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela additionally joins its application to the assumed deliberate material falsehoods and its subsidiary-theory limits.

The rule requires actual culpable falsity, personal responsibility and materiality. A truthful corrected account may still support probable cause, and a later recantation does not itself prove that the affiant knowingly fabricated evidence. Judicial issuance does not categorically sever the responsibility of an officer whose material deception procured the warrant. Cooper may contest the facts at the proper stage, but cannot secure interlocutory reversal by substituting his account for Clanton's. The Court does not find what the affidavit actually contained or place Bobby's derivative statement into it as independent probable cause.

The three subsidiary theories remain rejected: sufficiently reliable hearsay may support probable cause; the Fourth Amendment does not universally require filed criminal charges before a warrant; and the federal grand-jury indictment requirement does not govern state prosecutions. These propositions establish neither universal reliability of accomplice statements nor federal liability from a state-law warrant defect.

**3. Deliberate material detention deception does not require a prior case using the same communication system.** The asserted categorical immunity defense fails on the assumed personal communication of deliberate material homicide and no-bond falsehoods to procure otherwise unjustified additional unreasonable physical detention actually caused by that message. The established Franks/Malley prohibition on material official deception has an obvious application to this particular seizure; electronic transmission and a different custodian do not require a factually identical earlier NCIC case. Part II-B is unanimous.

The arrest under the original warrant and the later message's alleged extension of custody are distinct events. The Oklahoma warrant's $5,000 bond provision does not itself establish a federal release entitlement, and Texas's authority to set bond does not prove that the same detention would independently have occurred. The claim requires falsehood, culpability, materiality, attributable causation and an actual unreasonable physical seizure. An inaccurate message, bad motive, state-law breach or passage of one to three days alone is insufficient. If the same lawful detention would have occurred without the message, or Cooper had no material causal role, the pertinent defense remains available. Ordinary factual resolution must answer those questions; the Court decides no general duration of Fourth Amendment coverage through every stage of a prosecution.

**4. The independent coerced-confession theory requires proper legal review and conduct-date notice analysis.** The appellate disposition of the distinct third-party-confession branch is vacated because treating ultimate constitutional coercion as wholly outside interlocutory review fails to separate the historical assumptions from the legal defense. On remand the court must identify Clanton's own protected interest, the particular use and legal voluntariness of the statement, and whether the claimed violation was clearly established at the relevant 1993 conduct date. Part III has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela dissents from this disposition.

The Tenth Circuit did discuss Clanton's own due-process interest and notice. The defect is its overbroad treatment of the ultimate coercion conclusion, not total omission of those subjects. A claim involving an involuntary but not knowingly false statement is distinct from the preserved knowing-deception claims. Michael's personal self-incrimination privilege cannot be borrowed, but that does not categorically eliminate an independent injury to Clanton's liberty. Nor does Albright establish a universal Fourth Amendment displacement rule or a general state-remedy bar: its incompatible rationales supplied no controlling synthesis.

Jackson, Blackburn, Spano, Haynes and Fulminante supply the received voluntariness background. Frazier does not make an evidence lie conclusive, and Hutto does not make every promised benefit coercion. The alleged threats, promised leniency, suggested narrative and asserted physical evidence must be assessed together under the appropriate totality rule. The earlier third-party trial-use cases identified below, including LaFrance, Merkt, Chiavola and Bradford, do not automatically establish a particular pretrial-detention damages right in August 1993; their actual holdings and relevant circuit law must be evaluated. An identical factual precedent is unnecessary for obvious coverage, but an abstract liberty principle or bad intent cannot resolve a genuinely unsettled extension. No ultimate coercion, independent due-process right, clearly established violation or immunity is decided here.

**5. Existing proof and immunity rules remain distinct from ultimate liability.** Harlow and Anderson preserve objective, particularized conduct-date immunity. Elder requires consideration of all relevant pre-existing law on the preserved issue; later adjudications cannot retroactively provide notice, and no mandatory merits-first sequence prevents use of a genuinely dispositive notice defense. The eight-Associate opinion adopts these limits throughout; Stone-Zsela joins their application to the preserved deliberate-deception claims while proposing the separate change described below.

Crawford-El and Jeffries preserve competent direct and circumstantial proof, actual constitutional elements, personal responsibility and causation without an invented heightened motive requirement. Their retaliation-specific burden allocation does not become the burden for every fabrication claim. Fletcher distinguishes evidentiary acts from advocacy and preserves the remaining liability requirements; it does not supply Cooper with prosecutorial immunity or itself provide 1993 notice. Failure of a legal immunity defense establishes no damages, supervisory liability, automatic discovery entitlement or right to trial.

## Precedent Treatment

Johnson and Behrens are applied to legal review on assumed facts and exclusion of factual appeals. Miller supplies the particular legal-voluntariness distinction; Ornelas retains task-specific review. Franks and Malley remain the pre-conduct material-deception authorities, applied to the warrant and the particular later seizure communication. Harlow and Anderson remain governing immunity law; Elder preserves all-relevant-law review fixed to the conduct date. Lanier supplies the current clarity analogy without merging civil and criminal elements or furnishing retrospective notice. Albright remains a judgment without a controlling rationale on its presented claim; no new synthesis is imposed. Crawford-El, Fletcher and Jeffries retain ordinary proof, function, causal and remedial boundaries. No precedent is overruled and no contextual replacement defense is adopted.

## Law After Decision

Material official warrant deception and the particular deliberate message-caused additional unreasonable detention are not insulated by judicial issuance, electronic transmission or the absence of an identical earlier communication case. Each claim still requires its actual source, culpability, materiality, personal cause and injury. The distinct involuntary-third-party-statement claim remains open for proper legal and 1993-notice analysis. Current qualified immunity remains Harlow/Anderson; the decision creates no general fabrication right, derivative Fifth Amendment privilege, per se threat-or-promise rule, universal pretrial due-process right, or factual appellate jurisdiction.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the legal/factual boundary and the preserved deliberate warrant- and detention-deception applications, but would affirm denial of immunity on the third surviving theory as well. On the plaintiff-favorable account, use of a coerced manufactured confession to deprive Clanton of her liberty concerns her own due process, not a vicarious claim to Michael's interrogation rights. Cooper remains free to contest falsity, coercion, materiality and causation at the proper stage; no appellate finding of fabrication or damages follows. Hearsay, absence of an indictment and timing of charges supply no independent liability ground.

Stone-Zsela also favors a contextual defense placing the justificatory burden on the official and rejecting a duplicative excuse for deliberate violations. That is a proposed doctrinal change, not the Court's law: Crawford-El did not adopt it, and Harlow/Anderson remain controlling. His position creates no majority burden shift or new defense procedure and does not equate denial of immunity with proof of the constitutional claim or entitlement to trial.

## Procedure After Action

The Tenth Circuit must dismiss the unavailable factual-sufficiency portions, preserve the bounded legal immunity rejections for the warrant and later detention-deception claims, and reconsider the distinct confession branch under the proper legal/factual and conduct-date boundaries. The existing summary-judgment denial remains subject to that lawful claim-specific review. Disposed defendants, state claims and rejected subsidiary warrant theories remain undisturbed. No trial, new hearing, production order, discovery schedule, damages award or automatic Supreme Court return is ordered.

## Source Notes

The complete Tenth Circuit opinion and four footnotes, 129 F.3d 1147, provide the plaintiff-favorable account, quoted claims and rulings, dated authorities and review posture. The interrogation occurred August 26, 1993; the affidavit and warrant August 31; initial NCIC dissemination September 2; arrest and the later custodial message approximately September 13. Original affidavit, pleadings, complete interrogation transcript, depositions, message records and the later state voluntariness ruling are not reproduced. The opinion's inconsistent release-year reference and unresolved one-versus-three-day duration do not supply an exact date or damages figure. Michael's later plea and voluntariness ruling are not automatically binding on Clanton without established preclusion predicates. Missing source material creates no litigant default or finding that evidence was absent.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/county_of_sacramento_v_lewis_merits_1998-05-26.md

## Event

County of Sacramento v. Lewis, No. 96-1337. Plenary merits decision, May 26, 1998. Argued December 9, 1997. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 98 F.3d 434. The case concerns the constitutional culpability required when a rapid police pursuit ends in an unintended fatal collision. After final summary judgment, the court of appeals reversed Deputy Smith's individual qualified-immunity judgment using a deliberate-indifference or reckless-disregard standard. Its affirmance of the municipal judgments and the unappealed state-law dispositions are outside this Court's review. No Fourth or Eighth Amendment claim is presented.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The reviewed judgment against Deputy Smith is reversed.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed, 9–0.** **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The case returns for proceedings consistent with termination of the reviewed federal individual claim, leaving the district court's favorable individual judgment in effect. Six Justices adopt the Court's contextual constitutional ground; Scalia and Thomas rely on a distinct constitutional ground, and Stevens relies only on qualified immunity. No damages, trial, new evidentiary hearing, municipal retrial or state-law judgment is directed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete rapid-pursuit culpability rule, application, qualifications and remedy |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Individual immunity because May 22, 1990 law did not make this conduct's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent; reserves constitutional merits |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal | Distinct constitutional ground; no join in the Court's conscience-based purpose-to-harm standard |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Fully joins the Court and supports reversal | Preserves flexibility to decide an appropriate case on immunity before difficult constitutional merits |

## Holdings

**Holding and operative rule:** In the rapidly evolving high-speed pursuit presented here, where officers must make instant choices amid competing enforcement and safety obligations, reckless or deliberately indifferent risk-taking alone does not establish a substantive-due-process violation. A purpose to cause harm unrelated to a legitimate law-enforcement objective is required; because this plaintiff-favorable record does not establish that purpose, the reviewed federal individual claim fails.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies six direct votes for the complete rule and application. Neither the two distinct constitutional votes nor Stevens's immunity vote supplies additional support for that rule. No combination of separate rationales is needed.

**Controlling explanation:** Rochin identifies the constitutional boundary against arbitrary executive abuse; its conscience-shocking standard must be applied through precedent, constitutional tradition and the actual setting, not a judge's personal reaction. Daniels excludes negligence but left intermediate culpability unresolved. Whitley explains why competing obligations and an emergency matter to fault, while reserving the civilian due-process question. Farmer's actual-awareness standard concerns prison safety with a materially different opportunity to respond; Doe likewise requires knowledge, authority and a reasonable opportunity for protection against deliberate abuse. Those holdings do not mechanically determine culpability in a rapid pursuit.

The officer chose to pursue, and the favorable account includes dangerous speed, close following distance, danger to others, inadequate communication and alleged policy violations. The lack of a serious predicate offense and the ability to abandon pursuit are substantial criticisms. They do not, in this rapidly developing encounter, establish the improper harmful purpose required for constitutional liability. The collision was unintended, and the supported record supplies no purpose to injure unrelated to enforcement. The Court finds neither wise police judgment nor an adjudicated benevolent motive.

Actual opportunity for deliberation remains important. This rule is not a universal intent requirement, an automatic deliberate-indifference rule whenever an official could desist, or permission to manufacture an emergency. Purposeful collisions, deliberate terrorizing conduct, contrived encounters and materially different opportunities for considered action require their own facts and analysis. The decision creates no seconds-based cutoff, broad noncustodial immunity or mandatory order between constitutional merits and qualified immunity. It resolves neither the excluded municipal and state claims nor a procedural-due-process challenge to state remedies.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Rochin v. California, 342 U.S. 165:** Applied as the executive-abuse boundary, with the required culpability identified through the actual emergency setting.
- **Daniels v. Williams, 474 U.S. 327:** Its negligence exclusion remains; the Court resolves the previously open intermediate-culpability question only for this rapid pursuit.
- **Whitley v. Albers, 475 U.S. 312:** Its competing-obligation analysis informs the rule, but its prison-security holding is not treated as already deciding civilian pursuit liability.
- **Farmer v. Brennan, June 6, 1994:** Distinguished by the custodial duty and actual opportunity for response. Its awareness, reasonable-response and permissible-inference rules remain intact.
- **Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, October 16, 1995:** Distinguished because knowing supervisory acquiescence in deliberate abuse requires authority, opportunity and causal participation not supplied merely by continuing this pursuit.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, February 26, 1992:** Its bounded workplace-omission holdings do not automatically resolve affirmative police conduct or merge the underlying right with municipal attribution.
- **Pinder v. Johnson, May 21, 1996:** Its distinction between nonprotection and affirmative deprivation remains; it did not decide this culpability question.
- **Uhlrig v. Harder, February 18, 1997:** Its conduct-time immunity disposition is preserved without importing the lower court's unadopted merits test or a mandatory merits-first sequence.
- **United States v. Lanier, March 31, 1997:** Its rejection of categorical immunity for noncustodial official wrongdoing remains. A governing specific constitutional provision must be used where it covers a claim; that principle does not itself dispose of every due-process claim.
- **Albright v. Oliver, January 24, 1994:** Its fractured judgment supplies no controlling general displacement rationale for this case.
- **Washington v. Glucksberg, June 26, 1997:** Its assistance-in-suicide holding remains limited; no exclusive most-specific-history methodology governing all liberties is established or displaced here.

## Law After Decision

The decision establishes a purpose-to-harm requirement for the supported rapid-pursuit setting and rejects the circuit's use of reckless disregard or deliberate indifference alone there. Existing deliberate-indifference rules retain their distinct duties, opportunities for response and proof requirements. Constitutional wrongdoing remains separate from qualified immunity, municipal attribution and state-law liability. A purposeful or materially more deliberative encounter is not resolved merely by calling it a pursuit.

## Separate Writings

Stevens concurs only in the judgment. He would resolve the individual claim on qualified immunity without deciding the difficult constitutional standard. All relevant law existing on May 22, 1990 must be considered under the particularized-notice approach. Garner and Brower concern intentional force or intentionally applied stopping means; Checki concerns intentional misuse of police power to terrorize; Wood II concerns affirmative interference with protective circumstances. The reported pursuit decisions did not establish this claim's constitutional boundary merely by recognizing a possible egregious case, and Daniels reserved intermediate culpability. Neither the absence of an identical case nor a tally of judicial disagreement decides immunity. On the complete favorable account, the existing law did not make this pursuit's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent. Later decisions cannot supply retrospective notice. He makes no finding that the conduct was constitutional.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in the judgment on a different constitutional ground. The carefully described asserted right against this reckless pursuit and unintended collision lacks sufficient textual, historical or precedential support. A general protection of life or bodily security does not by itself define the constitutional tort. They reject the Court's reliance on an open-ended conscience standard and do not join its affirmative purpose-to-harm definition, although they agree that these facts establish no constitutional violation. Glucksberg does not impose a universally exclusive history method; their objection rests on the absence of adequate support for this particular asserted right and on the limits of judicially defined constitutional tort liability. Their position does not categorically exclude direct intentional assaults outside custody.

Breyer fully joins the Court. He separately preserves flexibility to decide a suitable case on qualified immunity before resolving a difficult or poorly presented constitutional question. That approach is consistent with Uhlrig and the distinction between actual wrongdoing and conduct-time notice. Today's choice to resolve the pursuit standard is not a compulsory merits-first sequence for every later case.

## Procedure After Action

The Ninth Circuit must give effect to reversal of its ruling against Smith and termination of the reviewed federal individual claim. The district court's favorable individual judgment remains in effect. The municipal judgments and unappealed state-law dispositions are not reopened, and no additional evidentiary proceeding or relief is ordered.

## Source Notes

[Lewis v. Sacramento County, 98 F.3d 434](https://static.case.law/f3d/98/html/0434-01.html) supplies the final-judgment posture, favorable pursuit facts and the undisputed absence of an intended collision. Alleged departmental violations remain distinct from federal constitutional culpability. The lower account does not establish that the riders were dangerous felons or that Smith had a purpose to injure. The immunity notice date is May 22, 1990.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/danahy_v_buscaglia_merits_1998-02-12.md

## Event

**Danahy v. Buscaglia**, Second Circuit No. 97-7264, 134 F.3d 1185. Plenary review decided February 12, 1998; October Term 1997. The Court reviews the January 27, 1998 judgment vacating denial of qualified-immunity summary judgment and remanding on seven employees' First Amendment individual-damages claims. The question is whether the individual defendants receive qualified immunity on the facts proper for this interlocutory appeal. No separate oral-argument date is established.

## Participation

All nine Justices participated at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the Court of Appeals' judgment on the reviewed First Amendment personal-damages claims, without deciding whether each dismissal was constitutionally permissible.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The judgment affirms personal damages immunity for Vacco, Buscaglia and Page on the appealed claims of Danahy, Burke, Bissram, Mungo, Matthews, Angiletta and Henesey, preserving the Court of Appeals' vacatur of the summary-judgment denial and its corresponding remand. The same unanimous coalition supports each employee's disposition.

The judgment does not dismiss the whole action, decide Weiss, Stark or Mahl's claims, or adjudicate other claims outside the appeal. It creates no prospective or official-capacity claim and decides none of their separate requirements. No final constitutional classification, personal-causation or same-decision finding, damages, reinstatement, backpay, new trial, hearing, discovery or description-production order follows.

## Opinion Topology

Stevens delivers the opinion of the Court, Parts I–II, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Both the legal-review boundary and the particular notice ground have nine direct joins. There are no separate writings.

## Holdings

### A legal immunity appeal accepts the proper factual assumptions

**Holding and operative rule:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may resolve whether the properly assumed conduct violated clearly established law, but may not choose among disputed accounts of duties, knowledge, participation or motive. Section 1254 permits review of the circuit judgment without enlarging the circuit's §1291 jurisdiction; the particular notice question here is separable, and no additional factual-appeal component requiring dismissal has been identified.

**Authority:** Parts I–II of Stevens's opinion are joined in full by all eight other participating Justices. Each holding and its qualifications have nine direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Johnson separates legal immunity from factual sufficiency, and Behrens permits the legal question only on the proper assumed facts. Clanton and Smith preserve that distinction when reviewing a circuit judgment. The present defense accepts alleged patronage motive and nonpolicymaker status and uses the reported direct prosecutorial work and defendants' knowledge on their assumed-fact footing. It does not depend on finding a different motive or deciding that each employee personally selected targets or set policy. Calling ultimate classification a legal issue would not authorize resolving a genuine historical dispute. Conversely, the existence of unresolved constitutional or factual issues elsewhere in the action does not eliminate this separable legal question. The Court neither resolves those issues nor brings them within the appeal.

### The particular November 1995 uncertainty supports personal damages immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** On the proper assumptions about these seven employees' actual direct and confidential prosecutorial work, the law in November 1995 left reasonable uncertainty whether political affiliation could be appropriate to their functions. Vacco, Buscaglia and Page therefore receive personal damages immunity on the reviewed First Amendment claims, without a final constitutional classification of any position or an office-wide confidentiality, at-will or civil-service exception.

**Authority:** The unanimous Parts I–II authority stated above applies to this holding, each employee's application and the claim-limited mandate.

**Controlling explanation:** Branti asks whether affiliation is appropriate for effective performance of the particular work; ordinary trust and professional secrecy are insufficient. Its reservation concerned a prosecutor's deputy, not every support employee. Harlow and Anderson nevertheless protect reasonable conduct-time legal mistakes independently of ultimate constitutional permission. Elder requires all relevant pre-existing law; obvious coverage needs no factual twin, but an abstract affiliation right does not resolve every uncertain application.

The affirmative connection here is direct work with prosecutors developing confidential investigations, audits and cases. Danahy supervised investigators working with confidential informants; Burke performed undercover work; Bissram undertook surveillance and undercover work. Mungo's audit-investigator work had the reported direct prosecutor relationship, without invented statutory powers. Matthews and Angiletta shared that relationship without borrowing others' individual admissions. Henesey combined billing analysis, extensive recipient/provider interviews and evidence custody in it; his clerk title does not reduce that account to mere filing.

Those functions, evaluated under Branti and the preconduct functional authorities described in the appellate opinion, permitted reasonable uncertainty about their relationship to prosecutorial discretion. The Court does not find that each employee exercised charging authority or that partisan agreement actually improved performance. The strongest alternative is a partial remand for Henesey, Matthews and Angiletta. Their thinner individual descriptions do not erase the affirmative common relationship, while missing descriptions permit no invented power. The immunity question is reasonable legal error on the supported functions, not final proof of partisan necessity.

Civil-service exemption, general institutional importance and confidentiality alone would not suffice. Patronage motive remains assumed; subjective innocence and a hypothetical same-decision reason supply no alternative defense. Later description refinements and later decisions cannot establish November 1995 notice or require a trial solely to obtain later-demanded descriptions. The result preserves constitutional elements, ordinary proof and other claims while resolving only these personal damages defenses.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Elrod, Branti and Rutan:** Apply protection against impermissible political-affiliation conditions and the actual-function inquiry. Nonreappointment and ordinary confidentiality supply no categorical exception; Branti's reservation concerns a prosecutor's deputy, not all prosecutor-office staff.
- **Harlow and Anderson v. Creighton:** Apply the retained objective, particularized conduct-time defense, without a replacement burden or mandatory merits-first sequence.
- **Elder v. Holloway:** Apply all relevant preconduct law on the preserved issue; no new claim, evidence or retrospective notice follows.
- **Johnson, Behrens, Clanton and Smith:** Preserve legal immunity review on proper assumptions and exclude factual selection or unrelated claims. Smith's obvious-force application does not decide this employment boundary.
- **United States v. Lanier:** Preserve obvious coverage without a factual twin; the clarity analogy transfers no criminal element and supplies no retrospective notice.
- **Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy:** Preserve ordinary competent proof, actual personal responsibility and causation, and the independent actual same-decision defense. No such defense is found here, and a dispositive notice ground remains available.
- **Umbehr, O'Hare, Jeffries and Valot:** Preserve their distinct affiliation, speech, causation and remedy requirements; later holdings do not supply November 1995 notice.
- **Fletcher v. Kalina:** Preserve immunity by actual function, without absolute advocacy immunity for personnel decisions by prosecutorial title.
- **McCardle v. Haddad:** Preserve claim-specific procedural requirements; its different Rule 50 omission establishes no omission in this interlocutory appeal.

## Law After Decision

The decision applies existing doctrine to the particular November 1995 legal uncertainty associated with these seven employees' reported direct prosecutorial functions. It creates no general patronage exception for confidential, technical, at-will or exempt employees and does not definitively classify any position. The substantive affiliation rule, ordinary proof requirements, legal/factual appellate boundary and separate personal defenses remain in force. The personal-damages disposition does not establish the legality of the dismissals or decide other claims and remedies.

## Separate Writings

None.

## Procedure After Action

The Court of Appeals' claim-limited vacatur and remand remain operative. Further lower-court proceedings must respect personal immunity on the seven reviewed First Amendment damages claims and the existing posture of other matters. The Court orders no additional hearing, evidence production, trial or return to this Court.

## Source Notes

The published record is *Danahy v. Buscaglia*, 134 F.3d 1185, 1187–1193 (2d Cir. 1998), including its six numbered footnotes. The relevant functions and knowledge are used on the reported assumed-fact footing, not as newly resolved historical disputes. Branti, 445 U.S. 507, 517–520 and footnotes 6 and 12–14, supplies the functional distinction and the precise deputy reservation. No complete description, unquoted delegation or independent prosecution authority is inferred for an individual employee. The reported description gaps establish neither litigant default nor a duty to obtain every description, and later authorities supply no retrospective notice.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/decarlo_v_fry_merits_1998-05-28.md

## Event

DeCarlo v. Fry; Second Circuit Nos. 97-7702, 97-7704 and 97-7754, 141 F.3d 56. Plenary merits decision, May 28, 1998, on submission. No separate Supreme Court argument date is established. On review of the Second Circuit's April 6, 1998 decision after trial, summary judgments and cross-appeals. The questions concern Astle and Blank's personal qualified immunity and the distinct official- and personal-capacity proceedings involving DuRose. This is not an interlocutory appeal from denial of immunity. Turner's favorable personal jury verdict and the unappealed Perales official declaration are outside review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The reviewed appellate judgment is affirmed. The district court's official-capacity judgment remains vacated under the appellate mandate, and the distinct DuRose proceedings return for the stated claim-specific treatment.

## Judgment & Remedy

All nine Justices support each reviewed disposition: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

| Component | Supreme Court disposition | Operative effect below |
|---|---|---|
| Astle and Blank personally | Affirm the immunity disposition, 9–0 | Their qualified-immunity summary judgments remain. |
| DuRose officially | Affirm the appellate vacatur and municipal remand, 9–0 | The district's $300,000 entity judgment and dependent $70,324.47 fee/cost award cannot stand on the mistaken premise; the actual trial record must receive proper Rule 50 and municipal-liability analysis. |
| DuRose personally, post-expungement | Affirm the clarification and adjudication remand, 9–0 | Identify and explain any actual disposition, or decide the pending request under its proper procedural standard. |
| Pre-expungement amendment permission | Preserve existing leave to amend, 9–0 | The affected claims against DuRose, Hendricks and Kraus retain that opportunity; no amended claim's merits are established. |

The Supreme Court affirms the appellate decision; it does not reinstate the vacated district judgment. No recertification, liability finding, new monetary amount, automatic trial or specified discovery follows.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer throughout | Supports every reviewed disposition | I: personal immunity; II: official-capacity judgment and Rule 50 remand; III: later personal claim; IV: existing amendment permission |
| Same opinion, partial join | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela | Supports every reviewed disposition | Part I only insofar as superior approval supplies no automatic defense; Part II's entity-capacity, separate-deprivation/policy/causation and no-personal-immunity propositions; disposition only on the additional Rule 50 ground, Part III and Part IV |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Supports the immunity result and the bounded remands | Proposed contextual reasonable-mistake defense; preserves the distinct county and personal claims |

The eight Associates adopt the Court's full rationale. Stone-Zsela's additional limited joins do not adopt the Harlow/Anderson immunity rationale or the further procedural grounds identified above.

## Holdings

### 1. The two subordinates receive personal immunity on the actual notice and role record

**Holding and operative rule:** Astle and Blank receive qualified immunity because, during the 1988–1989 processing at issue, the law did not make their own conduct's federal unlawfulness sufficiently apparent in the supported administrative circumstances. Consultation with a superior is relevant to those circumstances but is not an automatic defense, and a state regulatory deadline violation does not alone establish a clearly established federal violation.

**Authority:** Part I of Ginsburg's opinion for all eight Associate Justices controls the complete ground. Stone-Zsela also joins the no-automatic-defense proposition but agrees with the immunity judgment on a different ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Harlow and Anderson require objective, particularized conduct-time notice. Elder requires consideration of all relevant authority; Lanier permits an obviously covered application without an identical prior licensing case. Neither those later decisions nor Lewis can supply notice retrospectively. Davis v. Scherer separates a state-law breach from the required federal obligation.

The earlier revocation was judicially sustained, while later administrative proceedings expunged the underlying indications. Expungement did not itself adjudicate automatic reinstatement. Astle sought Blank's advice about that relationship; Blank consulted Turner, and the consultation reached state regional official Klump. Klump's direction to decide went to Turner. The record does not establish that Astle or Blank personally received and defied that instruction or possessed final authority to resolve the conflict.

Prolonged inaction may deny the practical benefit of a hearing available only after denial, and asking a supervisor cannot excuse knowingly refusing a plainly required act within one's control. Those concerns do not establish these two defendants' personal liability on this supported role and notice record. The state rules required investigation within 75 days of a valid application and notification within 30 days after completion, with a hearing after denial; those commands do not themselves decide the federal process claim or entitlement to certification. The Court resolves immunity without deciding the ultimate deprivation or excusing every administrative delay.

### 2. The official-capacity judgment requires an actual Rule 50 and municipal-liability determination

**Holding and operative rule:** Denial of a defendant's summary-judgment motion is not a judgment establishing the plaintiff's liability case and cannot, merely as law of the case, justify a directed liability judgment at trial. DuRose's official-capacity judgment must instead be evaluated on the actual trial evidence under Rule 50 and the requirements of an attributable county policy or authorized final decision causing an established federal deprivation; personal qualified immunity supplies no county defense.

**Authority:** Part II has all eight Associate Justices for the complete Rule 50 correction and bounded remand. Stone-Zsela additionally joins the entity-capacity, separate-deprivation/policy/causation and no-personal-immunity propositions, but not the additional Rule 50 rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Kentucky v. Graham identifies the official-capacity judgment as the entity's judgment. Monell excludes automatic employer liability, and Pembaur requires an actual authorized final policy decision when that is the asserted basis. McMillian directs attention to the entity and the challenged function under applicable law and legally effective usage. A title, operational discretion, employment or absence of immediate supervision alone does not establish final policymaking; its Alabama holding identifies no Oneida policymaker. Collins keeps the underlying federal deprivation distinct from its municipal attribution.

The trial court treated an earlier denial of DuRose's summary-judgment request as though liability already had been established. That premise bypassed the required trial-record determination. The familiar district court must now evaluate that record and explain any supported Rule 50 ruling. Judgment for plaintiffs, judgment for the county or factual issues for a jury remain possible; the Court selects none in advance and orders no automatic new trial. The complete trial record is not reproduced here, and that limitation supplies no adverse factual finding. Neither the $300,000 judgment nor its dependent fee/cost award can remain on the erroneous premise, but the Court fixes no proper amount or independent fee entitlement.

### 3. The distinct later personal claim requires its own identified disposition

**Holding and operative rule:** An order addressing the original pre-expungement pleading cannot dispose of a distinct post-expungement personal claim added later merely by an informal assertion that all personal claims were dismissed. The district court must identify any actual ruling and its basis, or adjudicate the pending request under the standard governing its actual procedural stage.

**Authority:** Part III, joined by all eight Associate Justices, controls this procedural ground. Stone-Zsela supports the remand disposition without joining the additional rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The original complaint concerned earlier acts, while the amended pleading added the later processing claim. Treating an earlier order as a disposition of a not-yet-pleaded claim fails to identify what was decided. An informal letter does not fill that gap. If a ruling occurred, its actual basis must be explained; otherwise the pending request requires decision. Pleading review cannot demand trial proof, and summary judgment must use its own record and standard. Crawford-El preserves ordinary procedural burdens and case management rather than a special evidentiary barrier. Nothing in this remand finds DuRose personally aware of the consultation chain, responsible for every subordinate act or liable for a federal violation. The Article 78 adequate-process contention remains undecided for appropriate consideration below.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald:** Applied to the two subordinates' objective conduct-time immunity; its defense remains governing law.
- **Anderson v. Creighton:** Applied to particularized apparent unlawfulness without demanding a factual twin.
- **Davis v. Scherer:** Applied to distinguish violation of state procedural duties from violation of a clearly established federal obligation.
- **Elder v. Holloway, February 23, 1994:** Applied to consideration of all relevant law while keeping notice fixed to the actual conduct date.
- **United States v. Lanier, March 31, 1997:** Applied to reject a Supreme-Court-only or identical-facts prerequisite; it creates no retrospective notice.
- **Kentucky v. Graham:** Applied to the entity character of the official-capacity judgment and the separation from personal defenses.
- **Monell v. Department of Social Services:** Applied to policy and causation requirements without automatic liability for an employee's act.
- **Pembaur v. Cincinnati:** Applied to the need for actual final authority when a single decision is asserted to be municipal policy.
- **McMillian v. Monroe County, June 2, 1997:** Applied to function-specific identification of the responsible government and final policymaker; the Alabama result is not transferred to Oneida County.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, February 26, 1992:** Applied to the independent inquiries into a federal deprivation and municipal responsibility.
- **Crawford-El v. Britton, June 16, 1997:** Preserved at ordinary proof, lawful case management and independent defenses; it adopted no replacement for Harlow/Anderson and makes motive no element of every process claim.
- **County of Sacramento v. Lewis, May 26, 1998:** Distinguished as a rapid-pursuit substantive-due-process decision; its harmful-purpose rule does not govern licensing procedure, and its later date supplies no earlier notice.

## Law After Decision

The decision applies personal qualified immunity to these two officials without a superior-approval safe harbor or a merits ruling on every licensing delay. County liability remains independent of that personal defense. A denial of summary judgment cannot establish trial liability for the opposing party, and distinct later claims require an actual stage-appropriate disposition. Existing amendment permission creates no merits entitlement. No automatic reinstatement follows from expungement, and no general rule requiring or excusing Article 78 recourse is adopted.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela agrees with the judgment but would use a contextual reasonable-mistake defense borne by the official, preserving his proposed change to the existing immunity framework. Accurate consultation about genuinely conflicting administrative obligations, followed by a legally plausible resolution, can support that defense. Superior approval alone is insufficient and cannot provide a duplicative excuse for deliberate violations or indefinite inaction. He supports the limited protection of Astle and Blank on their actual circumstances without treating expungement as automatic reinstatement.

He also emphasizes that DuRose's official-capacity judgment concerns the county. A personal reasonable-mistake defense cannot excuse an attributable county policy. The actual federal deprivation, policymaker's role, municipal responsibility and trial judgment require their own analysis. His proposed personal defense is not adopted by the Court, and his concurrence supplies no new liability or damages finding on either DuRose claim.

## Procedure After Action

Astle and Blank's personal immunity judgments remain. The district official-capacity judgment and its dependent fee/cost award remain vacated for proper Rule 50 and municipal analysis. The later personal claim receives clarification and adjudication at its actual stage; existing leave to amend the earlier claims remains. Both spouses' pre-expungement claims remain distinct from Juanita's later process claims. The excluded Turner verdict and Perales declaration are undisturbed. No new trial, hearing, discovery plan, recertification or monetary amount is ordered.

## Source Notes

[DeCarlo v. Fry, 141 F.3d 56](https://static.case.law/f3d/141/html/0056-01.html) supplies the appellate disposition, consultation chain, separate pleading stages and mistaken Rule 50 premise. The direction to act was reported as given to Turner, not as an instruction personally received and defied by Astle or Blank. Administrative expungement and judicial affirmance of revocation are distinct. The published account does not establish DuRose's knowledge, Oneida's final policymaker or the outcome compelled by the full trial record.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/duffy_v_wolle_merits_1998-06-03.md

## Event
