Duffy v. Wolle; Eighth Circuit No. 96-3210, 123 F.3d 1026. Plenary merits decision, June 3, 1998, on submission. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established. Review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) concerns the Eighth Circuit's August 19, 1997 affirmance of final summary judgment for Wolle, Vietor and Longstaff. Duffy challenges the selection of McPhillips as chief probation officer and seeks further proceedings. The questions concern the received individual constitutional damages action despite an internal judicial EEO plan, absolute immunity for administrative hiring, and the summary-judgment and Rule 56(f) treatment. This is not an interlocutory officer appeal.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The reviewed final defense judgment is vacated, and the case is remanded for ordinary, claim-specific consideration of the Rule 56(f) request and the resulting summary-judgment record.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 8–1 as to the operative mandate.** **Supporting:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. **Opposing that limited mandate:** Stone-Zsela, who would reverse and require appropriately limited intent-and-pretext discovery before resolving merits, damages or qualified immunity. All nine reject the offered internal-plan bar and absolute judicial immunity and agree that the existing final judgment cannot stand; that agreement does not make the two remand instructions identical.

The Court orders genuine reconsideration under ordinary procedural rules, with no guaranteed deposition, evidentiary hearing, trial, appointment, backpay, damages or fee award. A properly supported denial or limitation of discovery and renewed dispositive judgment remain available. Each defendant's knowledge, participation, purpose and defenses require their own basis.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and the ordinary reconsideration mandate | I: received individual action and offered plan bar; II: administrative hiring and absolute immunity; III: Rule 56(f), summary judgment and bounded remedy |
| Same opinion, partial join | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Agrees that the judgment cannot stand but seeks a different mandate | Parts I and II; Part III only on the absence of special evidentiary protection from judicial status or favorable affidavits and the prematurity of the existing judgment |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would reverse with required limited discovery | Rejects the Court's permission to deny discovery on reconsideration and resolve a dispositive issue before that required inquiry |

## Holdings

### 1. The internal plan does not establish the asserted categorical bar

**Holding and operative rule:** The existence of the Judicial Conference's nonstatutory internal EEO procedure does not, on the supported remedial record, displace this received individual Fifth Amendment employment-discrimination damages action under Davis v. Passman. The decision establishes no direct action against the court as an agency, no Title VII coverage and no rule foreclosing consideration of a different congressional remedial scheme or another properly supported special factor.

**Authority:** Part I of Stevens's opinion has all nine Justices for this bounded rejection of the offered bar.

**Controlling explanation:** Davis recognizes the individual constitutional employment action. Meyer rejects a new direct federal-agency action while preserving received individual actions at their own scope; its agency holding cannot simply be transferred to these defendants. Bush requires attention to an actual congressional remedial design. The reported plan originated with the Judicial Conference, and no supported congressional direction makes it the asserted exclusive substitute.

The judicial-employment setting and absence of Title VII coverage warrant attention to the real remedial arrangement, not an inference that any internal procedure necessarily extinguishes the received action. An unquoted plan term or unsupported statutory scheme cannot supply the missing bar. The Court does not hold that only an express exclusivity sentence could ever matter or that internal procedures are always irrelevant.

ALPA likewise requires examination of the institution and the authority assigned to it; it supplies no universal consent exception or blanket rule against preliminary recourse. Proper case management remains, and a materially different exhaustion question requires its own supported record. This holding neither grants automatic equitable relief nor decides the plaintiff's ultimate discrimination case.

### 2. Selecting the chief probation officer is administrative hiring

**Holding and operative rule:** Selection of a chief probation officer is personnel administration, not adjudication, and receives no absolute judicial immunity merely because judges make the choice under statutory appointment authority or embody it in an order. Rejecting that absolute defense leaves qualified immunity, personal responsibility, the constitutional elements and the legal basis for each requested remedy distinct.

**Authority:** Part II has all nine Justices for the functional classification and rejection of absolute immunity.

**Controlling explanation:** Forrester distinguishes personnel administration from judging disputes. Antoine requires historical and functional justification for absolute immunity; importance to judicial work does not suffice. Bogan protects prospective legislative policy while expressly preserving the difference from individualized administrative personnel decisions.

The chief probation officer performs important duties assisting the courts. Neither those duties nor section 3602's placement of appointment authority in judges transforms selecting an employee into deciding a case. Judicial title, operational significance and the formal appointment instrument therefore do not answer the functional question. No legislative abolition or adjudicative act is presented.

The ruling does not declare discrimination or remove every personal defense. Harlow and Anderson govern the actual conduct-time notice and individual conduct questions. Davis and Forrester predate the 1993–1994 events; later decisions such as DeCarlo and Florence guide present adjudication but cannot create earlier notice. The Court neither grants nor conclusively rejects every defendant's qualified-immunity defense on an undeveloped record.

### 3. The supported request requires genuine procedural reconsideration

**Holding and operative rule:** Before sustaining summary judgment on this record, the lower courts must assess Duffy's specific Rule 56(f) request and the material evidence under ordinary standards, without treating the defendants' favorable affidavits or judicial status as conclusive proof of lawful motive. Specific nonconclusory support remains necessary; the court may clarify, tailor or deny an inadequately supported request, resolve an actually dispositive legal issue first, and enter renewed summary judgment when the properly assessed record warrants it.

**Authority:** Part III's complete rule and remedy have the eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela joins the rejection of special evidentiary protection and the prematurity conclusion but would require discovery before further dispositive rulings; that additional mandate is not the Court's holding.

**Controlling explanation:** Crawford-El preserves ordinary motive proof, necessary tailored inquiry, early legal defenses and protective case management; neither unavailable proof demanded in advance nor a general hope of impeachment decides every request. Hicks leaves ultimate persuasion with the plaintiff: rejection of an explanation may support an inference but neither compels liability nor requires a special additional category of evidence. DeCarlo reinforces individual responsibility and decision at the proper procedural stage.

McPhillips had substantial experience, education, favorable references and interview assessments. Those are genuine lawful explanations, not a finding of discriminatory pretext. Inclusive recruitment and a neutral vacancy notice are lawful. The law-clerk comparison concerns different work and inadequately described pools and may remain weak or immaterial.

But Ailts's account refers to interest in appointing a female, while Wolle describes recruiting a diverse qualified pool. Ailts's uncertain source and inferred position limit the inference; they do not conclusively rewrite his account as recruitment alone. No panel policy or adoption by any defendant is found. Counsel's actual request sought to test the stated selection reasons. The district court's reliance on favorable defense and screener affidavits requires a genuine assessment of that request rather than treating production as conclusive proof of motive. An all-male panel carries no special presumption against discrimination.

The Court does not order any particular deposition, a broad personnel inquiry or a trial. More precise identification of sought facts, written inquiry, sequencing and protection of unrelated judicial work remain available. Each defendant's knowledge, participation and purpose must be assessed separately. No clear-and-convincing rule was imposed below, and none is removed by fiction; the correction concerns application of ordinary rules to this record.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Davis v. Passman:** Applied to preserve the received individual constitutional employment-discrimination action at its existing scope.
- **FDIC v. Meyer, February 23, 1994:** Distinguished at its direct-agency boundary; its preservation of received individual actions governs here.
- **Bush v. Lucas:** Applied to require examination of actual congressional remedial design, without treating any internal procedure as necessarily exclusive.
- **Air Line Pilots Association v. Miller, May 26, 1998:** Its institution-specific analysis is preserved; no universal consent or no-exhaustion rule is imported.
- **Forrester v. White:** Applied to classify personnel selection as administration rather than adjudication.
- **Antoine v. Byers & Anderson, June 7, 1993:** Applied to require historical and functional justification for absolute immunity, not importance alone.
- **Bogan v. Scott-Harris, March 3, 1998:** Distinguished as prospective legislation; its exclusion of individualized administrative decisions remains operative.
- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald:** Preserved for the separate personal qualified-immunity inquiry, without a judicial-office evidentiary exemption.
- **Anderson v. Creighton:** Preserved for particularized conduct-time notice; later law supplies no retrospective clarity.
- **McDonnell Douglas Corp. v. Green:** Its production framework informs the evidentiary setting without making every constitutional claim identical to a Title VII action.
- **Texas Department of Community Affairs v. Burdine:** Its ultimate persuasion allocation remains; production of legitimate reasons is not conclusive proof of actual motive.
- **United States Postal Service Board of Governors v. Aikens:** Its focus on actual intentional discrimination informs the ultimate inquiry rather than an automatic result from intermediate presumptions.
- **St. Mary's Honor Center v. Hicks, June 25, 1993:** Applied to preserve permissible circumstantial inference while rejecting automatic liability and a mandatory extra category of direct or independent evidence.
- **Crawford-El v. Britton, June 16, 1997:** Applied to ordinary motive proof and supported, tailored discovery with legal screening and summary judgment preserved.
- **DeCarlo v. Fry, May 28, 1998:** Applied to separate personal responsibility and proper procedural stages; its licensing result creates no hiring outcome or earlier notice.
- **City of Florence v. Chipman, June 1, 1998:** Its personal-proof and ordinary-procedure limits are consistent with the remand; its police-restraint and enforcement holdings create no hiring inference or 1993–1994 notice.

## Law After Decision

The received individual action remains available against the three officials on this supported record, and administrative appointment receives no absolute judicial immunity. Ordinary proof, personal defenses and properly managed procedure govern. Legitimate qualifications remain material evidence, but neither a judge's denial of discriminatory motive nor the panel's sex supplies special evidentiary protection. The judgment requires proper reconsideration of the concrete request; it creates no categorical entitlement to discovery, no liability finding and no general new discrimination burden sequence.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I and II and in the conclusion that the existing judgment is premature, but dissents from the limited remedy. He would reverse and direct appropriately limited discovery into intent and pretext before resolving the merits, damages or qualified immunity. In his view, Duffy identified specific inquiry capable of testing explanations whose supporting information the defendants control, so ordinary discretion does not permit another merits resolution without that inquiry here. He does not require a particular deposition or authorize discovery into adjudicative work.

He relies on Davis's existing individual action, Meyer's preservation of officer actions, Forrester's administrative classification and Bush's different congressional remedial setting. He agrees that judicial status and supportive affidavits cannot decide disputed motive by themselves. His required-discovery conclusion remains distinct from the Court's permission to refine, limit or deny an insufficient request or resolve an actually dispositive legal issue first. He finds no discrimination, orders no appointment and decides neither damages nor qualified immunity on a developed record. A congressionally enacted exclusive scheme could present a different case.

## Procedure After Action

The final defense judgment is vacated and the case returns through the court of appeals for ordinary claim-specific Rule 56(f) and summary-judgment consideration. A supported renewed dispositive judgment remains possible. No deposition, discovery plan, evidentiary hearing, trial, appointment injunction, declaration of wrongdoing, backpay, damages or fee award is commanded. Any eventual relief requires the actual constitutional and remedial predicates and each defendant's own involvement.

## Source Notes

[Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026](https://static.case.law/f3d/123/html/1026-01.html) and the [reported opinion with affidavits and footnotes](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/123/123.F3d.1026.96-3210.html) establish the final-judgment posture, the distinct appointment and recruitment accounts, qualifications, Rule 56(f) request and lower reasoning. Ailts could not identify the source of the reported interest and inferred the position. No sex-specific recruitment measure or discriminatory panel policy is established. Original depositions, the complete joint appendix and the complete EEO plan are not separately available here; that limit supplies no unknown plan term or factual finding.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/gebser_v_lago_vista_independent_school_district_merits_1998-06-22.md

## Event

Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, No. 96-1866. Argued March 25, 1998; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit. The question is what showing permits private damages against a school district under Title IX for a teacher's sexual harassment of a student. Review concerns only the appealed Title IX judgment against the district.

In October 1992, parents of two other students complained to the principal about Waldrop's classroom comments. The principal met with him, received an apology and assurance against repetition, cautioned him and informed the guidance counselor, but did not transmit the complaint to the superintendent, the Title IX coordinator. Gebser did not report the relationship herself. Police discovered it in January 1993, and the district terminated Waldrop.

The evidence about grievance procedures is disputed. The superintendent's affidavit described a policy, while deposition testimony described no formal policy; she did not remember whether a handbook notifying students of procedures had been distributed. Gebser testified that she probably would have reported had she known what to do, while describing bewilderment and fear. These are evidentiary accounts, not findings of institutional fault or causation. The District Court granted summary judgment for the district, and the Fifth Circuit affirmed under an actual-supervisory-knowledge requirement.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court reverses the appealed Title IX affirmance and remands for ordinary further proceedings. It limits the exclusive notice requirement and recognizes the narrower attribution route stated in Holdings, without finding liability.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

The reversal affects only the Fifth Circuit's affirmance of the district's Title IX summary judgment. Further proceedings must apply the controlling attribution propositions and determine the unresolved elements, causal facts, damages and defenses. No compensation or compulsory trial is ordered, and no unappealed claim or defendant is restored.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Five directly reject actual-corrective-official notice as the exclusive attribution route, with the stated limits. |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Parts II–III | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Part II states the narrower attribution ground with the component-specific Marks force identified in Holdings; Part III gives his own record application. The four other reversal supporters do not join these parts. |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Joins Part I and supports reversal/remand; states independent actual-authority/material-aid and negligent-correction grounds. |
| Separate concurrence | Ginsburg | Souter, Breyer | Proposes the limited avoidable-harm defense within their broader framework; Stevens reserves it and Stone-Zsela leaves defenses unresolved. |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm under Kinman's exclusive actual-notice rule and its record-specific insufficiency application. |

## Holdings

### I. Actual notice to a corrective official is not the exclusive attribution route

**Holding and operative rule:** A funding recipient's responsibility for a teacher's intentional discrimination cannot invariably be defeated solely because no official empowered to correct the harassment had actual notice. Kinman is limited insofar as it made that particular notice route exclusive; employment, ordinary teacher access or a technical regulatory breach, standing alone, still does not establish recipient liability.

**Authority:** Part I of Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion is joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. These five directly adopt this limited negative proposition. They do not adopt a common complete damages framework.

**Controlling explanation:** Recipient responsibility remains distinct from the teacher's intentional wrong. That distinction does not make actual notice to a particular corrective official the only possible means of attribution. The categorical rule would exclude other supported grounds before their predicates and causal connection were examined. Franklin preserves compensation for proved intentional Title IX discrimination while requiring attribution and loss to be established separately. Kinman's actual-notice and deliberate-disregard route remains available, but its former exclusivity does not survive. The Court neither equates teacher employment with recipient responsibility nor treats a violation of grievance regulations as automatic discrimination or damages liability.

### II. Authority materially enabling discrimination, combined with the recipient's causal fault, supplies a narrower attribution route

**Holding and operative rule:** A recipient may be held responsible when school-conferred authority materially enables a teacher's intentional discrimination and the recipient's own actual knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure causes that discrimination to continue. This is a sufficient route for attributing the discrimination beyond the actual-corrective-official-notice route; it establishes neither the underlying discrimination nor causal loss, damages or the defeat of a supported defense.

**Authority:** Part II of Stone-Zsela's opinion has no other formal joins. Its stated attribution component supplies the narrower controlling ground under Marks v. United States and Cipollone's component-specific application. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer independently support attribution whenever actual delegated educational power materially aids intentional harassment, without Stone-Zsela's additional recipient-fault requirement. School-conferred authority must actually and materially enable the wrong; title or opportunity alone is insufficient under either ground. Every case meeting the narrower conjunction therefore meets that independently sufficient broader attribution route. Those judgment-supporting positions total five, without converting the four Justices into joins in Stone-Zsela's opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** Franklin's damages remedy does not make every employee wrong the recipient's own wrong. The actual use of school-conferred authority must materially connect the discrimination to the educational relationship, and this narrower route additionally requires the recipient's own causal fault in its continuation. It does not permit the recipient to defeat attribution merely by deliberately disabling transmission of substantial warning information. Ordinary negligence or a technical policy omission alone does not satisfy this route. The underlying discrimination, institutional fault and causal continuation must still be proved.

Marks permits a narrower judgment-supporting ground without whole-opinion joins; Cipollone applies that inquiry to a particular legal component while leaving distinct liability and remedial questions unresolved. Here the four Justices' independent actual-authority ground contains the narrower attribution category. Their additional negligent-correction route is unnecessary to that inclusion. The proposed avoidable-harm limit supported by three of them concerns the extent of recovery; it does not negate their attribution ground. This limited nesting establishes neither their defense nor a complete damages formula. No general circumstantial-awareness presumption, automatic policy-failure inference or pooled-knowledge rule follows.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The authority, recipient-fault and causal-continuation conditions are conjunctive. The alternative involving reporting failure requires willful blindness and deliberate indifference, not constructive notice or negligence alone. The Court does not adopt ordinary agency liability as a complete general rule, the four-Justice negligence route or every Title VII attribution principle. Discrimination, institutional fault, causation, compensable injury, damages and defenses remain unresolved. Official-policy cases, prospective enforcement, individual liability and state-law remedies are outside this holding. The separate regulatory duties to adopt and publish prompt, equitable grievance procedures under 34 C.F.R. §106.8(b) and give nondiscrimination notice under §106.9(a) are not converted into strict private damages liability.

### III. The appealed Title IX summary judgment is reversed for ordinary further proceedings

**Judgment and operative remedy:** The Fifth Circuit's judgment is reversed only insofar as it affirms summary judgment for the district on the appealed Title IX claim, and the case is remanded. The lower courts must determine whether school-conferred authority materially enabled the discrimination and whether the recipient's own actual knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure caused it to continue, while preserving the actual-notice route and the remaining elements, ordinary procedures and defenses.

**Authority and application limit:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer support this formal reversal and ordinary remand. Stone-Zsela alone adopts Part III's application of his recipient-fault reasoning to the disputed warning and reporting evidence; the other four support further proceedings on their separately stated grounds. Their judgment agreement is not a five-Justice finding of actual knowledge, deliberate disregard, willful blindness, negligence, material aid, causation or liability. The Court orders neither compensation nor a compulsory trial. The unappealed constitutional, personal and state-law claims are not reopened.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Kinman v. Omaha Public School District:** Limited insofar as actual notice to a corrective official was the exclusive recipient-attribution threshold. Its actual-notice, deliberate and unreasonable disregard and causal-injury route remains available, together with the other Title IX elements. Its rejection of automatic teacher-status attribution and a technical policy breach as sufficient by themselves remains. No earlier sole-Justice awareness inference becomes Court law merely through this decision.
- **Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools:** Preserved as allowing appropriate compensation for proved intentional Title IX discrimination, subject to recipient responsibility, causation and loss. The present attribution holding does not establish a damages award or unintended-effects liability.
- **Cannon v. University of Chicago:** The established private action remains intact; no new regulatory cause of action is recognized.
- **Marks v. United States:** Applied to the particular sufficient attribution ground, without treating every reason or remedy proposed by a judgment supporter as controlling.
- **Cipollone v. Liggett Group:** Its component-specific logical-subset inquiry supports the limited attribution holding despite the absence of whole-opinion joins; separate proof, defenses and damages questions remain distinct.
- **Gade v. National Solid Wastes Management Association:** Its distinction between a directly shared negative proposition and unadopted complete affirmative frameworks supports keeping the exclusivity holding separate from the limited Marks analysis.
- **Meritor Savings Bank v. Vinson, Andrade and Faragher:** Their employment-attribution principles are not transferred wholesale to Title IX. Faragher's unresolved effective-protection/nonuse defense is not resolved here.
- **Pennhurst State School and Hospital v. Halderman:** Its funding-notice principle remains; neither it nor Title IX's administrative enforcement sequence becomes a literal exhaustion requirement for this private action.

## Law After Decision

Actual notice to a corrective official is an available but no longer exclusive route to recipient responsibility. The narrower authority-plus-recipient-fault attribution route stated in Holding II controls at that component-specific scope under Marks. It supplies no comprehensive damages formula, no general awareness presumption and no rule of automatic liability from employment, nonreporting or a policy defect. The broader agency and negligent-correction theories and the three-Justice avoidable-harm defense remain noncontrolling. Kinman's distinct substantive harassment elements and the ordinary requirements of causation, injury, remedy and defense remain material. This record returns for further proceedings without any liability or compensation finding.

## Separate Writings

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion announcing the judgment, Part III.** The complaint to the principal, the internal warning and the evidence about the absence of a functioning grievance route create, in his view, a factual dispute about the recipient's own fault. He would require the lower courts to examine whether school-conferred authority materially enabled the discrimination and whether actual recipient knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure caused its continuation. A district cannot defeat that inquiry through its own failure to transmit substantial warning information. He does not treat the complaint about classroom remarks as an established report of a sexual relationship, resolve the conflicting policy evidence, or find liability. Employment, ordinary negligence and a technical regulatory violation alone remain insufficient. This record-specific application has no other joins; the attribution component of Part II has only the limited Marks force stated above.

**Justice Breyer, concurring in part and in the judgment, joined by Justices Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg.** They join Part I's rejection of exclusivity and support reversal and ordinary remand, but do not join Parts II–III. They renew the two independent attribution routes previously stated in their Kinman position. First, actual delegated educational power materially aiding intentional harassment can attribute the wrong to the recipient without a separate higher-management knowledge requirement. Second, negligent failure to correct after responsible officials knew or reasonably should have known, causing injury or continuation, supplies an independent ground. Teacher title or opportunity alone does not establish actual aid, and a policy breach alone establishes neither negligence nor causation.

Franklin permits compensation for intentional discrimination. These Justices distinguish the teacher's intentional wrong from the agency principles attributing that wrong; their negligence alternative is not compensation for every unintended discriminatory effect. Cannon supplies the established private action, while Title IX's administrative notice and correction process does not require a second intentional wrong by recipient management in every damages case. They acknowledge that Kinman had rejected their broader grounds and would reconsider that exclusivity. Title VII's agency decisions supply a limited analogy, not an automatic transfer of every employment rule.

The actual delegation, material aid, information received, correction, reporting arrangements and causal injury require ordinary further consideration. The affidavit/deposition tension over policy and uncertainty about dissemination are evidence, not conclusive findings. These Justices do not establish liability or compel a trial. Their judgment agreement does not endorse Stone-Zsela's record-specific awareness reasoning. Their broader attribution theories do not become controlling merely because they support the same judgment; only the narrower component identified in Holding II has the stated Marks force.

**Justice Ginsburg, separately concurring, joined by Justices Souter and Breyer.** Within their broader attribution framework, these three would permit a limited avoidable-consequences defense. The district would bear the burden of proving that internal remedies were adequately publicized and likely to provide redress without undue risk, effort or expense. Recovery would be barred only to the extent the plaintiff unreasonably failed to use those measures and consequently suffered avoidable harm. A paper policy, nonreporting alone, an unsafe or ineffective route or eventual correction would not establish the defense. The uncertain policy and dissemination record does not conclusively establish it here. This is a causal limit on avoidable recovery, not automatic complete immunity or an additional Court-adopted attribution condition. Stevens reserves the defense and does not join its terms; Stone-Zsela leaves defenses unresolved.

**Justice O'Connor, dissenting, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.** They would affirm under Kinman's actual-notice and deliberate-disregard rule. Franklin's availability of compensation does not answer recipient responsibility. Title IX's recipient structure, its administrative correction mechanism and Pennhurst's notice principle support attention to the recipient's own culpable nonresponse rather than ordinary agency attribution or knowledge imputed from the teacher's conduct. They would preserve Kinman's exclusivity and reject the additional attribution route.

Even assuming that the principal had corrective authority, the reported information and response do not, in their view, establish actual knowledge of the relationship or a substantial risk followed by deliberate and unreasonable disregard. The parents complained about classroom comments. The principal met with Waldrop, received an apology and assurance, cautioned him and informed the guidance counselor; termination followed police discovery. These responses are not declared perfect, and a warning does not invariably suffice. But this record does not establish the required culpable refusal to protect.

The possible absence of a functioning complaint procedure is not itself institutional actual notice. The principal's information cannot be pooled with Waldrop's knowledge, and the policy conflict cannot be resolved by assuming that every procedure was absent. Their rule requires neither a written complaint, a victim's personal report, a confession nor proof that a completed sexual relationship was already known. Actual notice of a substantial danger can suffice. Administrative and injunctive enforcement remain distinct, and the district receives no personal qualified immunity. These four oppose both reversal and the limitation of Kinman.

## Procedure After Action

The appealed Title IX judgment is reversed and the claim returns for ordinary further proceedings. The lower courts must examine the school-conferred authority, material enablement, recipient fault and causal continuation under the route identified above, as well as any actual-corrective-official-notice route and the remaining requirements and supported defenses. The district may prevail if the applicable elements fail or a proper defense defeats relief. Individual, constitutional and state-law claims, administrative enforcement and prospective relief receive no new disposition.

## Source Notes

The procedural and evidentiary record is described at [524 U.S. 274](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-524/pdf/USREPORTS-524-274.pdf) and in [the original record for No. 96-1866](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0076). App. 44a–45a, 64a–65a and 72a–73a and the identified summary-judgment deposition materials preserve the policy conflict and reporting testimony. A complaint about classroom comments is not a reported sexual relationship. Title IX, 20 U.S.C. §§1681–1682, and 34 C.F.R. §§106.8(b) and 106.9(a) address distinct substantive, administrative and regulatory duties. [Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/430/188) supplies the narrowest-ground inquiry.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/general_electric_co_v_joiner_merits_1997-12-15.md

## Event

General Electric Co. v. Joiner, No. 96-188. Argued October 14, 1997; decided December 15, 1997. Certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit, 78 F.3d 524, concerning review of scientific expert-admissibility rulings.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated at argument and decision; none was absent.

## Public Action

The judgment of the Court of Appeals is reversed and the case remanded for further proceedings within the limits below.

## Judgment & Remedy

Reversed and remanded, 8–1. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, who would vacate for appellate reconsideration under the proper standard. Kennedy, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer hold that the PCB-only exclusion was within the District Court's discretion. Stone-Zsela joins reversal without joining that particular application. The unchallenged furan/dioxin exposure ruling and remaining causation-admissibility questions survive; the complete defense summary judgment is not reinstated.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II: review and legal inferential-fit rule | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III: PCB-only application and bounded mandate | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Concurrence in Parts I–II and the reversing judgment | Stone-Zsela | None |
| Concurrence, joining the Court's opinion in full | Breyer | None |
| Concurrence in Parts I–II and dissent from Part III and the judgment | Stevens | None |

## Holdings

**1. Admission and exclusion receive the same abuse-of-discretion review.** Appellate courts review a trial court's admission or exclusion of scientific expert testimony for abuse of discretion. Legal error remains independently correctable, historical findings receive their proper protection, and permissible case-specific evidentiary judgments receive deference. The importance of an exclusion to summary judgment supplies no different or asymmetric standard. Part I is unanimous.

Daubert left appellate review open. Koon and the task-sensitive reasoning of Pierce and Cooter & Gell support distinguishing correct law from permissible application; their other subject-specific rules do not transfer wholesale to expert testimony. Ornelas's independent review of ultimate constitutional suspicion and probable cause addresses another task. An appellate court may scrutinize a consequential exclusion carefully, but the Federal Rules' allowance of expert evidence does not justify a separate, particularly stringent standard for exclusions. Rule 56 protects the treatment of disputed merits facts; it does not remove the antecedent admissibility decision from the judge.

**2. Reliability includes the inferential connection to the offered proposition.** Original Rules 104(a) and 702 permit the judge to examine whether the expert's reasoning reliably connects the scientific material to the conclusion actually offered. Credentials, accepted terminology, or an assertion of a weight-of-evidence method cannot substitute for an adequate connection; screening that connection does not itself decide ultimate causation. Part II is unanimous at this legal level.

Daubert requires a preliminary foundation, established by a preponderance, of scientifically reliable knowledge fitting the factual question and assisting the factfinder. Principles, methodology and valid application cannot be separated by forbidding any inquiry into the inference drawn. But the rule requires neither a study identical to the plaintiff's circumstances nor independent proof of the conclusion from each study. Animal work, nonsignificant associations, competing causes and individually inconclusive materials may contribute to a supported combined inference. The offered theory here is promotion or acceleration, not necessarily sole causation. No later Rule 702 amendment or categorical statistical-significance requirement is adopted.

**3. The PCB-only exclusion was permissible on this proffer.** The described differences between the experimental materials and Joiner's exposure and disease, considered with the identified limitations of the human studies, permitted the District Court to find an insufficiently supported PCB-only inference. Part III controls through Kennedy, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The holding is that exclusion fell within the permissible range, not that admission was necessarily forbidden or that PCBs cannot promote lung cancer.

The animal work involved infant mice, concentrated injections and alveologenic adenomas; Joiner was an adult with small-cell carcinoma and lower alleged occupational exposure. Two reported human studies showed nonsignificant excesses whose authors did not connect them to PCB exposure. The Norwegian study addressed particular mineral oil and did not mention PCBs; the Yusho group had several potential carcinogenic exposures without an isolated PCB effect in the described findings. These limitations bear on the connection offered, rather than establishing automatic scientific disqualifications. The experts invoked a combined method, exposure information, interviews, medical records and additional research; the Court does not infer that no bridge existed merely because a source extract is incomplete. Considered at the proper discretionary level, the described fit deficiencies support the ruling.

The Eleventh Circuit's separate conclusion that evidence supported furan/dioxin exposure was not challenged in the petition. The District Court had not expressly assessed causation admissibility on that assumed exposure. Those questions require further proceedings and prevent restoration of the full defense judgment.

## Precedent Treatment

Daubert's flexible foundation, valid-application and preponderance rules are applied and supplemented by today's appellate-review rule; its distinction between admissibility and ultimate causation remains. Koon, Pierce and Cooter & Gell inform the allocation of legal correction and discretionary application. Ornelas is distinguished by its particular ultimate constitutional inquiry, not narrowed into a universal rule about mixed questions. Frye's mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite remains displaced. Rules 403, 703 and 706 and Rule 56 keep their separate functions. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

Scientific expert admissions and exclusions receive uniform abuse-of-discretion review, including independent correction of legal mistakes. The reliability inquiry may examine the actual inferential connection while remaining distinct from ultimate causal proof and preserving legitimate cumulative methods. The present PCB-only exclusion is sustained within that discretionary range. No universal study-matching condition, categorical animal-study rule, compulsory expert-assistance procedure, technical-specialty rule, collateral reopening entitlement or determination of the alternative-exposure theory follows.

## Separate Writings

Justice Stevens concurs in Parts I–II but dissents from the application and judgment. The grant concerns review allocation, and the Eleventh Circuit should apply the correct standard to the actual cumulative method. Individually inconclusive studies do not necessarily defeat a combined inference; the described reliance on work by at least thirteen researchers and World Health Organization materials warrants attention to the whole method. He would vacate for appellate reconsideration, without commanding admission or finding causation.

Justice Breyer joins the Court's opinion in full and concurs separately. Difficult scientific questions do not excuse the judge's screening duty. Existing assistance, including a court-appointed expert under Rule 706 and lawful management of the foundation inquiry, may help assess tentative scientific knowledge and its application to an individual case. He requires no expert appointment, new hearing or mandatory panel; those suggestions are not controlling procedures.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I–II and reversal on the uniform-review and inferential-fit grounds. An expert's assertion cannot replace an adequately supported connection. He does not join the particular finding that this PCB-only exclusion was within discretion, and he preserves the unresolved exposure questions and the remainder of the tort action.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for further proceedings consistent with the review rule and sustained PCB-only exclusion. The unchallenged furan/dioxin exposure ruling remains, and causation admissibility on that theory remains open. No finding of causation, damages award, compulsory admission, automatic new trial or expert appointment is entered.

## Source Notes

The District Court opinion, 864 F.Supp. 1310, and Eleventh Circuit opinion, 78 F.3d 524, together with authenticated published record descriptions, establish the rulings, proffered promotion theory and study limitations. Full studies, complete depositions and the full record are not reproduced. The reported observations and experts' cumulative method are preserved without independent medical findings, invented numerical data, or an inference of litigation default from an unavailable source.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/hudson_v_united_states_merits_1997-12-10.md

## Event

Hudson v. United States, No. 96-976. Argued October 8, 1997; decided December 10, 1997. Certiorari to the Tenth Circuit, 92 F.3d 1026, reviewing reinstatement of a criminal prosecution after civil banking sanctions.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated at argument and decision. No Justice was recused or otherwise absent.

## Public Action

The judgment of the Court of Appeals is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

Affirmed, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: none. The criminal prosecution may proceed. The Court orders no additional accounting hearing and decides neither guilt nor the identity of the administrative predicates and charged offenses.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and III: classification framework, combined application, and disposition | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: Halper's personal-penalty application | Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg |
| Concurrence in Parts I and III and the judgment | Stone-Zsela | None |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas |

## Holdings

**1. Civil intent remains rebuttable through examination of actual punitive operation.** Congress's civil designation of these banking monetary sanctions and the regulatory character of the occupational restriction establish the intended civil framework. That designation may be overcome by the clearest proof that the sanctions' purpose or effect is criminal; their supported design and operation must be assessed together, without treating deterrence, severity, occupational burden or overlap with crime as individually conclusive. Part I controls through Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg.

Ward supplies the intent-and-effect inquiry. Ursery preserves both its demanding override requirement and the distinct field of personal civil penalties; Hendricks requires meaningful examination of actual operation. The monetary provisions expressly impose civil assessments through administrative procedures. Their good-faith and gravity considerations affect amount and do not establish a universal willfulness element. The applicable original monetary authorization cannot be replaced by the increased tiers enacted after the February 1989 notice. Mendoza-Martinez informs punitive-effect examination without requiring a new exhaustive checklist. Civil wording is relevant, but it cannot make actual punishment immune from constitutional review.

**2. These monetary assessments survive Halper's retained personal-penalty inquiry.** A personal civil penalty remains subject to Halper's requirement of a rational remedial relationship, including reasonable approximation; that rule is not overruled or replaced by a universal statutory-face inquiry. On the supported record, the actual $44,000 in total monetary assessments, compared with the District Court's finding of $72,000 in government costs pursuing these defendants, permits a remedial characterization and requires no further accounting. Part II controls through Breyer, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg.

The Government may recover legitimate pursuit costs without establishing that deterrence played no part in the regulatory response. The objective relation answers the present claim; the agency's failure initially to calculate its costs does not itself establish punishment. The initial $200,000 demand is not the obligation ultimately imposed. Nor are alleged banking losses substituted for proved government costs. This application creates no automatic monetary ratio, immunity for every penalty below an asserted cost figure, or universal compensation ceiling for other sanctions.

**3. The combined sanctions do not bar this prosecution.** The banking restriction, which permits participation with written regulatory authorization, protects positions of trust in a regulated industry. Considered with the actual financial assessments, its supported operation does not provide the clearest proof of criminal punishment; the subsequent prosecution therefore is not barred on the presented multiple-punishment theory. Part III controls through Breyer, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg.

The restriction's substantial occupational effect does not erase its protective function, and the Court considers that effect together with the monetary burden. Kurth Ranch involved an extraordinary arrest-linked assessment on confiscated contraband whose combined operation made it a second punishment; these sanctions have no comparable defining operation. Hendricks concerned prisonlike custody and the actual failure of a civil treatment structure, facts absent here. Those distinctions preserve both precedents. A different banking scheme or materially different operation could produce a different classification. Because criminal punishment is not established, the Court does not decide Blockburger or retained Grady offense identity, attachment in punitive civil proceedings, the Excessive Fines Clause, or independent due-process objections.

## Precedent Treatment

Halper remains binding in its exceptional personal-civil-penalty field and is applied to the supported monetary obligations and costs. Ursery's rebuttable civil classification and preservation of that field remain intact. Hendricks supplies cumulative actual-operation analysis without transferring its confinement outcome. Ward and Mendoza-Martinez retain their intent/effect and punitive-indicator roles. Kurth Ranch's extraordinary-tax holding is distinguished, not narrowed. Dixon, Grady, Blockburger and Felix retain their offense-identity rules; none is applied to determine the identity of these charges. Austin's distinct Excessive Fines protection remains available in an appropriate case. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

Effective December 10, 1997, the identified OCC sanctions supply no multiple-punishment bar to this prosecution. Personal monetary sanctions remain subject to Halper's reasonable-remedial-approximation inquiry, while actual combined operation remains material under the civil-intent/clearest-proof framework. Neither regulatory labeling nor a monetary comparison alone creates a categorical safe harbor. No same-offense rule, fines rule, settlement-waiver rule or confinement rule changes.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I and III and the judgment. He applies Ursery and Hendricks without alteration: the combined design and actual operation of these monetary and occupational measures do not establish the clearest proof of criminal punishment. Their accountability and protective functions differ from Kurth Ranch's extraordinary exaction and Hendricks's prisonlike application. He does not join the separate independent Halper-accounting analysis and does not reject Halper's continuing force. His ground leaves different operational facts, excessiveness and offense identity open.

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, concurs in the judgment. Their account requires successive criminal prosecutions, and the genuinely civil banking proceedings do not supply the first such prosecution. They would reject Halper's freestanding civil multiple-punishment method, while allowing inquiry into whether a nominally civil proceeding is actually criminal. That theory does not command a majority and does not overrule Halper.

## Procedure After Action

This Supreme Court review ends. The reinstated criminal proceedings continue in the ordinary course, with properly remaining defenses preserved. The prior appellate nonparticipation ruling retains its procedural scope; consent supplies no newly found waiver or admission. No accounting, conviction, dismissal of individual counts, or scheduled return to this Court is ordered.

## Source Notes

The Tenth Circuit opinions, 14 F.3d 536 and 92 F.3d 1026, supply the lower proceedings and the reported $72,000 cost finding. The published administrative record recitation supplies consent assessments of $16,500, $15,000 and $12,500, totaling $44,000, and the written-authorization qualification on banking participation. The lower opinion's opening individual amounts conflict with that recitation; its operative comparison uses the same $44,000 total. Alleged banking or FDIC losses of approximately $900,000 are not a proved government-loss finding. Sections 101 and 103 of Pub. L. 95-630 and the official amendment notes to 12 U.S.C. §§93, 504 and 1818 establish the civil design and version limits. The complete original administrative instruments, indictment particulars and underlying cost-hearing documents are not reproduced; no additional statutory predicate, waiver or count-specific identity finding is inferred.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/jeffries_v_harleston_merits_1997-11-17.md

## Event

Jeffries v. Harleston, Second Circuit No. 93-7876. Merits decision, November 17, 1997; October Term 1997, chunk 1. The Court reviews under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) the April 4, 1995 judgment, 52 F.3d 9, directing judgment for defendants after the shortening of Professor Leonard Jeffries's department-chair term. The questions concern the public-employee speech balance, the independent causal ground, and appropriate further proceedings.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at submission and decision.

## Public Action

The appellate judgment is vacated and the case remanded under the bounded mandate stated below.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Supporting | Opposing or distinct position |
|---|---|---|
| Vacatur and return for further proceedings, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas would affirm |
| Bounded mandate without compulsory retrial, five votes | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone-Zsela would require a limited new trial; O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas oppose remand |

The mandate does not reinstate the district court's two-year chair injunction or punitive awards. Sustainable individual defenses and ordinary verdict treatment remain available; judgment without trial or retrial may follow only if the actual law and procedural record support it. No final liability, immunity, damages, or present entitlement to a chair appointment is determined.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: employment balance; Part III: mandate | Souter | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: personal cause and independent Board decision | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Concurrence in vacatur and Part II; disagreement with the substantive rule and remand limits | Stone-Zsela | None |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas |

## Holdings

**The actual speech and service relationship govern the employment balance.** The appellate balance cannot sustain final defense judgment on this record and rationale. Neither retained tenure nor lack of entitlement to a longer chair term categorically defeats a retaliation claim; a reasonable forecast is relevant but does not itself decide constitutional protection. Part I, joined by Souter, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls this application.

Pickering and Connick require attention to public expression and actual institutional needs. The address concerned curriculum and racial history, occurred at an unaffiliated off-campus event, and was not assigned or controlled as an official duty. Introduction by the chair title did not establish sponsorship. Course assignments, tenure evaluations, records, meetings and budget work were real responsibilities, but the supported record identified no special policymaking, confidential or additional public-contact obligation. Recruitment, fundraising, faculty interaction and community relationships remain relevant institutional interests. The panel gave its forecast and the retained professorship more legal weight than that combination justified in resolving the whole case for defendants.

The jury's findings of no actual disruption and reasonable expected disruption both stand. Prevention need not await realized harm. Waters protects a genuinely relied-upon reasonable account only when it describes legally disciplinable speech; it creates no complete controlling investigation code. Perry, Umbehr and O'Hare preserve the difference between entitlement to a benefit and retaliation through its withdrawal. The Court adopts neither categorical protection of every part of this address, an actual-loss requirement, quantified damages to the institution, nor NTEU's entire prospective-ban burden for this individual action.

**Nine successful votes do not automatically establish an independent lawful cause.** If protected conduct supplies a substantial or motivating cause, each defendant's actual responsibility and any preponderance same-decision defense must be examined. Different punitive or animus findings do not alone establish that the same action would have occurred without protected speech. Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls that rule and rejection of the blanket causal ground.

Mt. Healthy and the actual-cause holdings of Umbehr, O'Hare and Crawford-El require a real counterfactual, not a conceivable lawful reason. The nine one-year votes are important evidence, but their lack of the six defendants' attributed animus does not itself answer that inquiry. Harleston and Reynolds did not vote; a causally effective proposal may matter, but procurement alone proves no liability. Everett, Badillo and Jacobs cast defeated removal votes; Bloom abstained. Those roles neither establish nor eliminate other supported causal contributions. Claims against Bernstein remain discontinued. A genuine independent lawful Board decision or a supported personal defense may still be dispositive. Ordinary competent direct and circumstantial proof applies without a special heightened motive burden.

**Further proceedings remain bounded.** Part III, joined by Souter, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer, leaves actual verdict harmonization and consequences, sustainable individual defenses, capacity attribution and relief to ordinary adjudication. It orders no compulsory new trial. Harlow and Anderson v. Creighton require particularized personal-damages immunity at the late-October 1991 conduct date, and a distinct date for any preserved March 1992 claim. Later decisions supply no retrospective notice. Punitive answers do not replace protection, cause or immunity; prospective relief has separate live-remedy requirements. Elapsed time alone establishes neither mootness nor continued chair eligibility.

## Precedent Treatment

- Pickering, Connick, and Waters retain the contextual balance, genuine lawful ground and permissible forecast. No complete Waters investigative framework is adopted.
- Perry, Umbehr, and O'Hare are applied to the actual employment benefit, service relationship and causal inquiry.
- Mt. Healthy, Kimberlin, and Crawford-El retain ordinary proof and the actual same-decision defense.
- NTEU's broad prospective-restraint allocation and Settle's student-curriculum holding remain confined to their settings.
- Harlow and Anderson v. Creighton retain conduct-date immunity; no later merits rule establishes earlier notice.

## Law After Decision

The decision applies the role-sensitive employment balance and defendant-specific cause rules to this record, displacing the blanket defense judgment. It establishes no categorical academic-speech exemption, reaction-based exclusion, investigation protocol, universal retrial requirement, or final personal liability. Each controlling part has a direct majority; no combined plurality rule is necessary.

## Separate Writings

Stone-Zsela would classify the address as citizen speech as a matter of law and expressly narrow the disruption rule: offense, controversy, audience hostility, institutional embarrassment, reputational pressure, and disruption consisting of reactions to the viewpoint may not themselves justify punishment of this private public-concern speech. A professor's title and subject matter do not make an unaffiliated address university speech. Assigned teaching, administration and delegated chair speech remain subject to institutional control. Independently proved failure to perform duties, threats, harassment, unlawful discrimination in official work, insubordination, misuse of authority and other regulable misconduct remain lawful grounds.

He joins Part II because the nine votes cannot be declared lawful superseding causes by assuming reaction-generated disruption sufficient. Jeffries must prove each defendant's substantial or motivating causal role, and a defendant may prove the same action would occur for an independently lawful reason. Stone-Zsela considers the conflicting verdicts and differentiated roles to require a limited new trial on those questions, immunity and relief. He would neither reinstate inconsistent verdicts nor decide particular liability, qualified immunity, compensatory or punitive damages, or precise prospective relief. This compulsory-trial remedy and categorical substantive limitation do not command the Court.

O'Connor's dissent, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would affirm on the employment balance. The independent public-concern setting and nonpolicymaking characterization deserve weight, but course assignments, tenure evaluations, internal representation and budget work create a consequential relationship. The identified recruitment, fundraising, faculty-interaction and community interests, supported by the reasonable-expectation finding, justify the chair-term action without waiting for actual harm. The dissent performs that legal balance rather than treating sincere belief, offensive content, retained tenure or the jury's forecast as automatically decisive. It neither invents institutional sponsorship or deficient performance nor adopts the nine-vote rationale or a final immunity determination as an alternative holding.

## Procedure After Action

The Second Circuit must conduct or direct further proceedings consistent with Parts I–III. Actual claim scope, verdict forms, instructions and preservation govern available dispositions. No March 1992 claim is created, no discontinued claim revived, and no current chair appointment or award is automatically restored.

## Source Notes

The reported record appears in 21 F.3d 1238 and 52 F.3d 9. The latter reporter's number 953 is a calendar number; 93-7876 is the appellate docket. Administrative performance assessments, allegations of predicted harms and appellate evaluations remain distinct from the jury's findings. No unreported verdict instruction, waiver or quantified institutional loss is supplied.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/lachance_v_erickson_mcmanus_merits_1998-01-21.md

## Event

LaChance v. Erickson, No. 96-1395, with the Rule 12.4 McManus companion. Plenary merits decision, January 21, 1998; argued December 2, 1997. On review of King v. Erickson, 89 F.3d 1575, covering Walsh, Erickson, Kye, Barrett and Roberts, and the separate judgment reported at 92 F.3d 1208 concerning McManus. The questions concern constitutional and Civil Service Reform Act immunity for knowingly false factual denials, and the dependent charge, penalty, impeachment and reporting dispositions.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

Both appellate judgments are reversed insofar as they adopt categorical denial immunity and its dependent charge, penalty and impeachment bars. The Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition is set aside only to the extent it depends on that erroneous categorical premise. The matters return for lawful case-specific proceedings; the Court selects no employee's final sanction.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Remedy or limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Constitutional denial immunity in both judgments | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Correct the rule; preserve meaningful response and actual proof |
| Independent CSRA immunity claim | Rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Retain the statutory safeguards; no individual element or sanction decided |
| Dependent penalty bar | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Otherwise lawful consideration permitted, not compulsory enhancement |
| Distinct impeachment bar, including McManus | Reversed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supported relevant falsity may receive lawful consideration; no automatic inference |
| Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition | Dependent disposition vacated and remanded, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Actual duty and defenses remain; unreviewed false-time findings stay intact |

Stone-Zsela agrees only in the dispositions of the independent statutory claim, distinct impeachment bar and reporting branch, while joining the specified constitutional, proof, sanction and valid-privilege propositions below. Both matters return for case-specific review of charges and sanctions, without automatic restoration of any original adverse action.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer in full | Supports all dispositions | Parts I–IV |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Souter | Stone-Zsela | Supports every disposition and the common case-specific remand | Part I in full; Part III's penalty-consideration proposition and actual-proof/lawful-procedure qualifications; Part IV's preservation of separate compelled-incrimination review and prohibition on compulsory waiver of a valid privilege |

## Holdings

### Due process protects a meaningful answer, not proved knowing falsity

**Holding and operative rule:** The Fifth Amendment's opportunity to answer an agency misconduct charge does not immunize a knowingly false factual denial from an otherwise lawful employment falsification charge or penalty consideration. An unsuccessful defense is not itself a knowing lie: the agency must establish the actual false statement and requisite culpability under the governing employment rules, while preserving good-faith factual contest, mistakes, uncertain recollection, legal disagreement and procedural nonadmission.

**Authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion has all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** Loudermill requires notice, an explanation of the employer's evidence and a meaningful opportunity to respond, with adequate later process; it does not presume that an inaccurate statement is knowingly false. Bryson rejects a general privilege to meet an official inquiry with deliberate falsehood, while its criminal statutory elements and proof requirements remain distinct. A denial's grammatical form does not make proved deception necessary to meaningful participation. The concern that an innocent employee will be punished twice merely for losing is answered by proof of the actual falsification charge and lawful process, not immunity after deliberate factual deception is established. A separate supported determination may use evidence also relevant to the underlying misconduct; no different-source or corroboration requirement is created. Insistence that the agency prove its case and disagreement about legal characterization are not automatically false assertions of fact. Nor does absence of an oath create the categorical immunity at issue. These are employment proceedings, with no criminal jury, criminal burden or universal materiality element added. Genuine self-incrimination questions remain separate.

### The Civil Service Reform Act supplies safeguards without denial immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Sections 7513 and 7701 do not confer categorical immunity for a proved knowingly false factual denial. Their cause, procedure, proof and review safeguards continue to govern every particular charge and sanction; rejection of immunity establishes no employee's falsity, service nexus, regulatory liability or final penalty.

**Authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion is joined by all seven other Associate Justices, providing eight direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees only in rejection of the statutory immunity claim.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 7513(a) permits covered action only for cause promoting service efficiency. Section 7513(b) ordinarily requires at least thirty days' advance written notice of specific reasons; its exception requires reasonable cause to believe the employee committed a crime punishable by imprisonment, not a finding made here. The employee receives a reasonable period of at least seven days to answer orally and in writing and furnish affidavits and documents, representation, and a written decision with specific reasons at the earliest practicable date. The agency may provide a hearing by regulation under subsection (c); that permission is distinct from Board hearing and representation rights under §7701. Subsections (d) and (e) preserve Board appeal and maintenance of the notice, response, decision, action order and supporting materials, available to the employee or Board upon request.

Ordinary misconduct requires preponderance proof under §7701(c); specified performance and senior-executive matters retain their separate substantial-evidence rule. Otherwise supported action still cannot be sustained if the employee shows harmful procedural error, a prohibited personnel practice or legal invalidity. Section 7703(c)'s record-based review for unlawfulness, procedural deficiency, arbitrariness or abuse, and lack of substantial evidence is appellate review, not the agency's proof burden; designated discrimination facts retain their distinct de novo treatment. Section 7703(d)'s OPM substantial-policy-impact and erroneous-interpretation predicates, reconsideration prerequisite when the Director did not intervene, and discretionary appellate review remain intact. None of these protections requires immunity for deliberate falsehood. The employee retains a meaningful answer and review while the agency bears its actual burden. The Court finds no notice exception, review-prerequisite failure or new disciplinary ground in these cases.

### Penalty consideration and impeachment remain conditional

**Holding and operative rule:** A proved knowing false denial may receive otherwise lawful consideration in selecting an employment penalty or assessing credibility; the categorical bars resting on denial immunity cannot stand. Such use is permitted, not compelled, and an adverse finding on the underlying misconduct alone establishes neither falsification nor an entitlement to enhance a sanction or impeach the employee.

**Authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion has all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela additionally joins its penalty-consideration proposition and its actual-proof and lawful-procedure qualifications, but agrees only in the disposition removing the distinct impeachment bar.

**Controlling explanation:** The protected chance to contest an allegation does not require an adjudicator to disregard a properly established deliberate deception. Yet relevance, proof of the actual charge, procedural fairness and a lawful penalty basis remain necessary. The statutory decisionmaker must distinguish a knowing factual falsehood from an honestly held denial, ambiguity, memory failure or legal nonadmission. The same safeguards prevent automatic escalation based merely on an employee's unsuccessful defense. The Court therefore removes the categorical penalty and impeachment bars, including the identified McManus impeachment ruling, without selecting an adverse inference or sanction and without displacing unrelated evidence rules.

### The reporting branch and self-incrimination require their own predicates

**Holding and operative rule:** The Barrett/Roberts reporting disposition is set aside only insofar as it rests on categorical immunity from providing information because it would implicate the employee; further proceedings must address the actual duty and every supported defense. Rejecting a right to lie neither compels waiver of a valid privilege nor decides a genuine compelled-incrimination claim, and it creates no general duty to confess.

**Authority:** Part IV of Souter's opinion has all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela joins only the proposition preserving separate compelled-incrimination review and prohibiting compulsory waiver of a valid privilege; he agrees only in the reporting disposition itself.

**Controlling explanation:** Garrity bars criminal use of answers compelled by a threat of public-employment loss. Gardner forbids discharge solely for refusal to waive protection against criminal use of compelled testimony and its fruits, while distinguishing questions specifically, directly and narrowly concerning official duties without a required waiver. The reproduced 5 C.F.R. §5.4 concerns information required by the designated civil-service bodies or authorized representatives and retains its exception for disclosure prohibited by law or regulation. These actual conditions cannot be replaced with either an unrestricted duty to report or automatic immunity whenever information may be self-implicating. No respondent-specific compulsion, criminal-use threat, refusal, immunity or waiver is established here. The Court selects no silence penalty or adverse inference and establishes no universal prohibition on otherwise lawful civil inferences. Barrett and Roberts's unreviewed false-time determinations remain undisturbed; the dependent reporting and investigatory-falsification issues return for lawful consideration.

## Precedent Treatment

- Loudermill is applied to preserve meaningful notice and response, adequate later process and the difference between inaccuracy and knowing deception. It supplies no immunity for a proved lie.
- Bryson supplies the bounded principle against deliberate falsehood as a response to an official inquiry; its criminal statutory elements and proof conditions are not transferred to employment discipline.
- Garrity and Gardner remain operative for compelled testimony, criminal-use protection and impermissible waiver demands, with Gardner's official-duty distinction intact. No respondent-specific privilege claim is adjudicated.
- Gaudin remains a criminal element-allocation holding, not a command for an administrative jury or criminal burden. Lanier preserves conduct-time warning in its stated setting; today's decision supplies no missing prior notice for an individual charge.
- Jeffries, Richenberg and Valot retain their actual-conduct, meaningful-rebuttal, lawful-process and bounded-remedy rules. None confers categorical denial immunity or creates a general investigation checklist.
- Hudson retains its distinct civil/criminal classification inquiry and supplies no complete due-process answer here. Lee's supported-duty and threshold-remand rulings establish neither employee falsity nor an individual reporting obligation.

## Law After Decision

A meaningful employment answer procedure and the CSRA's safeguards do not categorically protect a knowingly false factual denial. The actual charge, its culpability, proper administrative burden, service nexus, lawful procedure and sanction must still be established. Negative phrasing and absence of an oath do not establish immunity; failure to prevail, mistake, uncertainty and procedural nonadmission do not automatically establish falsity. The Court creates neither a criminal false-statement rule nor a duty to waive a valid privilege or confess.

Lawful consideration of proved falsity for penalty or credibility is permitted, with no mandatory enhancement, adverse inference or final sanction. The reporting branch depends on its actual rule and supported defenses. McManus's missing individual factual particulars are not supplied from the other employees, and Barrett/Roberts's unreviewed false-time determinations remain outside the correction.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

Both matters return to the Federal Circuit for further proceedings under the corrected legal rules. Walsh's falsification and dependent penalty questions return without automatic removal or a Court-selected replacement for the reported 90-day suspension. Erickson's corresponding issues return without automatic removal in place of the 15-day suspension. Kye's actual statements, knowledge and penalty require lawful review without converting her suspicion about her son into a proved assertion or automatically replacing the 45-day suspension with removal. Barrett and Roberts's investigatory-falsification and dependent reporting issues return, preserving the unreviewed false-time findings and selecting no replacement for their reprimands or automatic restoration of demotions and 30-day suspensions.
