McManus's distinct falsification and impeachment rulings return without a newly found underlying event or final sanction.

The reported Board sanctions retain their procedural status while the authorized adjudicators determine the lawful consequences of the corrected rule. The Court orders no blanket reopening, compulsory hearing, additional evidence production, criminal referral, privilege waiver, final knowing-falsity finding or automatic penalty enhancement. This Supreme Court review is complete; no return date or retained jurisdiction is established.

## Source Notes

The employee records and rule below appear in [King v. Erickson, 89 F.3d 1575](https://openjurist.org/89/f3d/1575). The companion and procedural recitations appear at 522 U.S. 264–265 in [the official report](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep522/usrep522262/usrep522262.pdf). The statutory provisions are the 1994 Code versions of [5 U.S.C. §7513](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap75-subchapII-sec7513.htm), [§7701](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap77-sec7701.htm) and [§7703](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title5/html/USCODE-1994-title5-partIII-subpartF-chap77-sec7703.htm). The lower opinion reproduces the relevant then-effective 5 C.F.R. §5.4 language. Allegations, AJ findings and Board dispositions remain distinct. No uniform oath status, individual compelled-incrimination predicate, waiver or final Supreme Court finding of knowing falsity is established, and McManus's underlying conduct and final sanction remain unadjudicated.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/mccardle_v_haddad_merits_1998-01-29.md

## Event

McCardle v. Haddad; Second Circuit Nos. 96-9133(L), 96-9503. Plenary merits decision, January 29, 1998. On review of 131 F.3d 43, decided November 21, 1997. The question is whether Haddad preserved qualified immunity as a ground for postverdict judgment as a matter of law on the unlawful-search claim. No separate Supreme Court docket or exact argument date is established.

The jury rejected the stop claim, found an unlawful search and awarded no compensatory damages. The court entered $1 nominal damages, refused a punitive instruction and awarded $0.33 in fees and costs. The Court of Appeals affirmed. The stop verdict was not appealed by McCardle; the distinct punitive and fee rulings are outside this review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The judgment is affirmed insofar as it rejects Haddad's request for postverdict judgment on the unpreserved search-immunity ground. The other existing components remain undisturbed without adoption of their reasoning.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed insofar as reviewed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The existing search verdict and $1 nominal judgment remain undisturbed. No ultimate immunity ruling, new trial, immunity remand, changed damages award or fee correction follows. The unappealed stop ruling and separate punitive and fee dispositions remain outside the decision.

## Opinion Topology

Ginsburg delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer. The same nine Justices adopt the preservation rule, its application, qualifications and bounded affirmance.

## Holdings

### The trial motion did not preserve search immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Rule 50 requires the trial motion to identify the judgment sought and its supporting legal and factual grounds; postverdict renewal does not ordinarily permit a new ground omitted from that motion. Functional presentation, including adequate explicit incorporation, can suffice without a particular label, but pleading qualified immunity and invoking it for the stop did not preserve the distinct search-immunity ground here; no demonstrated exceptional injustice requires reaching that ground.

**Authority:** The unanimous Opinion of the Court supplies nine direct votes for this rule, its application and all stated limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Rule 50(a)(2) gives the court and opposing party an opportunity to address the actual ground before submission. Rule 50(b) renews the legal questions raised by the trial motion. The official explanatory note permits explicit reference to earlier materials and argument; mistaken terminology alone is immaterial. The answer pleaded immunity, but a pleading is not itself the required trial motion.

Counsel expressly requested judgment on the stop on the merits or immunity, then separately argued that the search was lawful. The complete reported presentation identified no search-immunity ground or incorporation supplying it. Search legality and immunity for an objectively reasonable legal mistake are distinct, even when they may overlap. The Court adopts no requirement of separate titles or magic words and no prohibition on alternative defenses. Gomez treats immunity as an affirmative defense; Elder requires all relevant law on a preserved issue, without creating the missing motion ground.

The strongest contrary argument combines the answer and search-legality presentation as sufficient notice. The express claim-specific separation defeats that reading. No actual prejudice is independently found. Nor does immunity's importance itself establish exceptional injustice. The search record must be viewed favorably to McCardle; the adverse general stop verdict supplies no contrary universal factual findings. On that record, no probable-cause, consent, arrest, inventory or supported protective-search justification compelled judgment for Haddad. Long requires specific articulable grounds supporting danger and possible access to weapons within a protective scope; a valid stop alone is insufficient. The officer's denial of a search or subjective fear alone is not the constitutional test.

Those circumstances defeat the asserted injustice without deciding ultimate immunity or issuing a new search-merits holding. The Court leaves the outer limits of exceptional review open, adds no categorical instruction or pretrial-motion prerequisite, and infers no further default from unavailable original papers.

## Precedent Treatment

- Gomez is applied to distinguish an affirmative immunity defense from an obligation courts must supply without its proper presentation.
- Elder is applied to preserve review of all relevant law on a preserved immunity issue; it does not revive an omitted ground or admit a new factual record.
- Harlow and Anderson retain objective, particularized conduct-date immunity. The Court neither grants nor denies that defense on its merits.
- Long informs the rejection of claimed exceptional injustice at its actual protective-search scope; neither a valid stop nor the officer's subjective account alone establishes a lawful search.
- Crawford-El and Fletcher retain ordinary procedure and the distinction between constitutional liability and personal defenses. Their holdings create no immunity-specific Rule 50 exception or motive element for this search claim.
- Johnson, Behrens, Clanton and Smith retain their legal/factual and claim-specific limits. Their interlocutory settings do not restrict this final-judgment channel or enlarge the trial motion. Lanier and later immunity decisions supply no notice retrospectively for the March 1992 conduct.
- Allentown's distinction between a fairly included argument and an independent unpreserved objection remains confined to its actual question and governing statute; Rule 50 controls here.
- Farrar and Hensley remain unchanged. Their explained assessment of actual success permits zero without mechanical proportionality or a categorical nominal-relief presumption. The Court does not approve the lower fee rationale or decide a fee amount.

## Law After Decision

A pleaded immunity defense and a trial invocation concerning one claim do not automatically preserve immunity as a renewed-judgment ground concerning another. Courts must assess what the actual motion conveyed, including any adequate incorporation, rather than require a verbal formula. This application leaves lawful alternative defenses and genuinely supported exceptional-review questions intact.

Ultimate qualified immunity and an independent Supreme Court ruling on search legality remain undecided. Failure to preserve immunity does not itself establish constitutional liability, causation, compensable loss or punitive culpability. No stop, punitive-instruction or fee holding is added, and no new substantive immunity framework or defense burden replaces existing law.

## Separate Writings

## Procedure After Action

The Court of Appeals' rejection of postverdict search-immunity judgment remains operative, and this Supreme Court review is complete with the ordinary mandate. No remand, trial, hearing, discovery or production order, immunity reconsideration, new award, injunction or scheduled return is directed. The other existing judgments remain undisturbed at their unreviewed scope.

## Source Notes

The motion quotations, answer, trial evidence and separate appellate rulings appear in [McCardle v. Haddad, 131 F.3d 43](https://static.case.law/f3d/131/html/0043-01.html), corroborated by the [OpenJurist opinion text](https://openjurist.org/131/f3d/43/mccardle-v-haddad). The governing procedural text is [Rule 50 in the 1997 Code](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1997-title28/html/USCODE-1997-title28-app-federalru-dup1-rule50.htm), including its then-effective ten-day renewal period. No filing-time issue is decided. The reported motion history supports the stated omission; the full underlying transcript and motion papers are not reproduced. No additional waiver, concession, prejudice or counsel motive is inferred. January 29, 1998 is the assigned date of plenary review; no exact Supreme Court argument or submission date is established.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/national_credit_union_administration_v_first_national_bank_trust_co_merits_1998-02-25.md

## Event

National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co. and AT&T Family Federal Credit Union v. First National Bank & Trust Co., Nos. 96-843 and 96-847. Plenary merits decision, February 25, 1998; argued October 6, 1997. On writs of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the D.C. Circuit, 90 F.3d 525. The paired cases concern the banks’ right to seek APA review and whether §109 of the Federal Credit Union Act, 12 U.S.C. §1759, permits one federal credit union to add multiple unrelated occupational groups. The Court of Appeals allowed the challenge and rejected the agency’s construction after the District Court had sustained it.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

## Public Action

The D.C. Circuit’s judgment is affirmed in both cases.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirm in both dockets, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg.
**Opposing:** Stevens, O’Connor, Souter and Breyer.

The challenged approvals cannot stand under the occupational common-bond provision. The existing appellate invalidation remains operative. The dissenters would vacate and remand for dismissal of this APA challenge on the statutory zone ground. No new member-removal order, liquidation, monetary award, transition date or enlarged injunction is entered.

## Opinion Topology

Stone-Zsela delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg. Its Article III, statutory-zone and occupational common-bond conclusions have those five direct joins. O’Connor dissents, joined by Stevens, Souter and Breyer, on the statutory zone of interests. The dissent reaches no common-bond merits question. No legislative-history rationale or conditional merits opinion is published.

## Holdings

### These banks may seek review of the membership expansion

**Holding and operative rule:** Directly competing banks injured by these completed charter approvals have Article III standing and fall at least arguably within the interests implicated by §109’s particular membership boundary for APA §702 review. They need not be the statute’s specially intended beneficiaries, but competitive injury alone does not establish a right to challenge every regulatory limitation.

**Authority:** The five-Justice Opinion of the Court supplies direct authority for the provision-specific statutory-zone rule and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** The approvals permit customers who otherwise might use the respondent banks to obtain services from ATTF; injury is undisputed, is traceable to the approvals and can be redressed by setting them aside. Lujan supplies the distinct constitutional elements, and Bennett requires examination of the particular provision rather than the Act’s general purpose. Section 109 limits credit-union membership and thus the customers the institution principally serves. These competitors challenge the agency’s expansion across that precise boundary. Clarke and Data Processing permit that direct statutory connection without a special-beneficiary requirement. Air Courier separately requires a statutory interest beyond injury, but its collateral employment interests differ from this challenge by participants in the regulated customer market. The Court does not find that protecting bank profits was Congress’s purpose. Nor does it eliminate APA finality, adequate-remedy, preclusion or other applicable conditions; these completed, operative approvals present no established independent defect of that kind.

### Unrelated occupational groups cannot replace the common bond

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 109’s occupational route requires a common occupational bond connecting the membership of the occupationally defined credit union; it does not authorize aggregation of unrelated employer groups merely because each has its own internal bond. The challenged agency policy crosses that statutory boundary and receives no deference at Chevron’s first step.

**Authority:** The same five Justices adopt the common text-and-structure rationale, its application and limits.

**Controlling explanation:** The sentence limits federal credit-union membership, not merely the labels assigned to each component group. The plural groups permits multiple groups sharing a common occupational bond; it does not make a succession of unrelated bonds sufficient. The agency’s reading retains internal group and service-area conditions, but those are different from the connection Congress imposed on the membership. The parallel geographic alternative supports that reading. NCUA’s concession that the geographic groups must occupy the same defined community is an attributed argument, not a separate holding on geographic charters. MCI requires enforcement of an enacted boundary before deference. NationsBank permits reasonable financial classifications within an authorized domain and does not permit removal of its boundary. General rulemaking authority, serviceability and administrative efficiency cannot replace the required bond. A changed interpretation is not invalid merely because it changed. The decision imposes no single-employer requirement and does not determine the full breadth of a genuine occupation or association, geographic charters, or individual membership-retention rights.

### The disposition enforces the challenged boundary without rewriting membership law

**Holding and operative rule:** The statutory invalidation applies to the challenged unrelated employer-group additions; the other membership commands and permissions in §1759 remain intact. Affirmance supplies no independent enlargement of the injunction reported below.

**Authority:** The same five-Justice opinion adopts this scope of construction and relief.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 1759 permits elected persons and organizations within Board rules, subject to the statutory membership limitation, and requires each member to subscribe to at least one share and pay its initial installment. A uniform entrance fee is required only if the board of directors requires it. The distinct final sentence permits survivorship joint-tenancy shares with a member’s designated person; that person may not vote, obtain loans or hold office unless within the field of membership and a qualified member. The joint-tenancy permission is not unlimited membership or authority to add unrelated employer groups. None of those provisions is displaced. The Court resolves the challenged legal interpretation, not every approval condition or remedial detail. Future applications remain subject to the statutory limits then lawfully governing them.

## Precedent Treatment

- Lujan preserves injury, traceability and redressability as separate constitutional requirements.
- Bennett is applied to the interests arguably protected by the particular provision and preserves APA review conditions.
- Clarke and Data Processing supply the permissive, provision-connected competitor inquiry without a special-beneficiary requirement.
- Air Courier preserves the distinction between injury and the statutory zone; its derivative employment interest is distinguished.
- Chevron requires enforcement of the unambiguous statutory boundary before agency deference.
- MCI applies the prohibition on eliminating an enacted requirement through interpretation. NationsBank’s lawful-classification holding remains intact within statutory boundaries.

## Law After Decision

The banks’ APA action may proceed, and the challenged unrelated occupational-group construction is invalid. The decision neither abolishes statutory-zone screening nor requires Congress to have enacted the FCUA especially to protect banks. It supplies no general ban on multiple employers sharing a genuine common occupation, no geographic-charter ruling and no rule invalidating administrative change as such.

## Separate Writings

**O’Connor, joined by Stevens, Souter and Breyer, dissenting.** The dissent accepts the banks’ concrete economic injury and rejects any requirement that Congress expressly identify them as special beneficiaries. It nevertheless finds their asserted protection against competition only incidental to §109. The common-bond rule organizes a cohesive cooperative; it does not keep credit unions collectively out of an identifiable market, since a group may join another qualifying union or form one. Clarke’s permissive inquiry and Bennett’s provision-specific focus therefore do not reduce to the economic effect of enforcing any restriction. Air Courier preserves the need for this additional statutory relationship. The dissent would vacate the reviewed judgment and remand for dismissal of the APA action, removing relief dependent on it without approving the agency’s interpretation or denying Article III injury.

## Procedure After Action

Review of both dockets is complete. The D.C. Circuit’s invalidation stands, and the agency must act consistently with the occupational common-bond limitation. The Court enters no new remedial program and decides no individual member’s status.

## Source Notes

The administrative policies, litigation history, specific geographic concession and undisputed competitive injury are reported in [522 U.S. 479](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-522/pdf/USREPORTS-522-479.pdf). The governing membership text appears in the [1997 United States Code, 12 U.S.C. §1759](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1997-title12/pdf/USCODE-1997-title12-chap14-subchapI-sec1759.pdf). The reported nationwide injunction’s full wording is not separately available here; no omitted condition or enlarged remedy is inferred. The District Court’s statement that second-step reasonableness was not seriously disputed is not a universal waiver or an independent finding by this Court.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/ohio_forestry_association_inc_v_sierra_club_threshold_1998-05-18.md

## Event

Ohio Forestry Association, Inc. v. Sierra Club, No. 97-16. Argued February 25, 1998; decided May 18, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit, which reversed summary judgment for the Forest Service and held aspects of the Wayne National Forest plan unlawful. The question is whether the present substantive planning challenge is ripe before a site-specific logging authorization.

The complaint challenges: approval of the plan and below-cost clearcutting under NFMA, NEPA and the APA; below-cost timber sales under public-trust duties; and timber-suitability regulations' failure to identify economically unsuitable land under NFMA and the APA. It seeks declaratory and injunctive relief, costs and fees.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court vacates the Sixth Circuit judgment and remands for dismissal without prejudice as unripe.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded for dismissal without prejudice, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

All three substantive complaint theories are dismissed as unripe. The District Court's merits summary judgment is displaced as well. The Court orders no plan invalidation, logging injunction, plan revision or fee award.

## Opinion Topology

Scalia delivers the opinion of the Court. All nine Justices join it in full. No separate writing is filed.

## Holdings

### The present substantive planning challenge is premature

**Holding and operative rule:** A substantive challenge to a programmatic forest plan is unripe when the plan authorizes no immediate logging, establishes no sufficient present legal hardship, and leaves further project decisions and meaningful review before cutting. On this record that rule requires dismissal without prejudice of the plan/clearcutting, public-trust sales and timber-suitability-regulation claims, without deciding their substantive validity.

**Authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined in full by all nine Justices, supplies direct controlling authority for this rule and the limits below.

**Controlling explanation:** Abbott Laboratories directs courts to consider fitness for decision and hardship from withholding review. Texas applies that inquiry to a dispute whose operative details remain unresolved. The Wayne plan identifies logging goals, suitable areas and probable methods; it does not authorize cutting. Before logging, the Forest Service must identify a site and method, ensure consistency with the plan, provide notice and an opportunity to be heard, conduct environmental analysis and make a final project decision subject to review. Those choices can alter the anticipated effects and provide a concrete basis for adjudication. Review need not wait until trees are cut; the relevant planning premise may be challenged with the project that applies it.

The suitability regulations are completed rules, but Toilet Goods distinguishes finality from a presently fit controversy. The complaint establishes no sufficient present consequence requiring resolution of this substantive logging dispute now. Bennett shows why retained agency discretion alone is not decisive when completed action already has supported consequences. New York and Pic-A-State likewise preserve review of actual present burdens; no comparable burden is established here. Possible plan amendment is a consideration, not an automatic bar.

NEPA's inclusion identifies no particular omitted statement, denied participation or other completed procedural deprivation. The public-trust label supplies no different timing premise. The requests for an injunction, costs and fees do not establish current cutting authority or an independent ripe injury. Neither lower court's substantive forestry ruling can survive as the disposition of this unripe action.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides neither standing, APA finality, statutory or public-trust merits, nor an independent fee entitlement. It does not adopt the lower court's exceptional-circumstances formulation for even-aged management or equate an optimum-method inquiry with a resource-protection condition. A site-specific authorization, an immediately binding provision or regulation, or a properly presented completed procedural violation may receive review when its independent requirements are satisfied. Lujan's protection of a procedural right tied to a threatened concrete interest remains intact. No optional-appeal exhaustion requirement or future limitations ruling is adopted.

Assertions in later briefing concerning motorcycles, heavy machinery, road closures and hiking trails supply no established basis for resolving the reviewed substantive dispute differently. The Court does not find those activities or injuries proved, find intentional waiver, announce a general forfeiture rule, or adjudicate a distinct present-recreation claim on either ripeness or merits.

## Precedent Treatment

- Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner and Texas v. United States: applied to fitness and present hardship; neither requires an advisory answer about an insufficiently concrete implementation.
- Toilet Goods Association v. Gardner: applied to distinguish completed agency action from ripeness for judicial review.
- Bennett v. Spear: distinguished at its supported present-consequences premise; later implementation discretion alone does not defeat review.
- New York v. United States and Pic-A-State Pennsylvania v. Reno: distinguished because their challenged rules imposed established present burdens; pre-enforcement review remains available on that basis.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife: its concrete-interest procedural protection is preserved; this decision supplies no separate standing determination.

## Law After Decision

Forest plans and suitability regulations receive no categorical immunity from review. This substantive planning dispute must await a sufficiently concrete, reviewable application or another independently established present consequence. Distinct completed procedural injuries retain their own timing requirements. No NFMA, NEPA, APA or public-trust merits rule changes, and no rule requires physical injury before a challenge may proceed.

## Separate Writings

No separate writing is filed.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for dismissal without prejudice as unripe, replacing the lower merits adjudications. The plan is neither judicially approved nor invalidated. A later properly presented challenge must establish its own jurisdictional and procedural requirements; no future project, injunction, fee award or result is directed.

## Source Notes

The plan's operation, further project procedures, pleaded counts and requested relief appear in the published record account at [523 U.S. 726–739](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-726.pdf); the judgment below is 105 F.3d 248. The later recreational assertions are reported submissions, not findings of present activity or injury. No original complaint text is quoted, and the reference to NEPA is not a finding that a specified procedure was denied.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/pennsylvania_department_of_corrections_v_yeskey_merits_1998-06-15.md

## Event

Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey, No. 97-634. Argued April 28, 1998; decided June 15, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Third Circuit. The question is whether Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act covers state prisons and their programs.

Yeskey, sentenced to 18 to 36 months, was recommended for a motivational boot camp whose successful completion could permit parole after six months. He alleges that admission was refused because of his history of hypertension. The District Court dismissed on the ground that the ADA did not apply to state inmates; the Third Circuit reversed that categorical ruling.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the Third Circuit’s statutory-coverage judgment.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The categorical exclusion of state prisons cannot support dismissal. The remaining issues continue in the lower courts; the Court orders neither admission to the program nor early release.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | All join the statutory coverage rule, application to the categorical dismissal, reservations and affirmance. |

## Holdings

### Title II covers state prisons and their programs

**Holding and operative rule:** A state correctional department is a public entity under 42 U.S.C. §12131, and its prison services, programs and activities fall within §12132. Custody supplies no categorical exception: a qualified individual may not, by reason of disability, be excluded from participation, denied the benefits of those services, programs or activities, or otherwise subjected to discrimination by the public entity.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court is joined in full by all eight other Justices. The complete statutory rule and its limits command nine direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 12131 includes state and local governments and their departments, agencies, special-purpose districts and other instrumentalities. A state correctional department falls within that definition. A prison’s boot-camp opportunity is a program offering a benefit; its custodial setting does not make that description inapplicable. Section 12132 contains no exception for involuntary custody.

Even assuming Gregory’s clear-statement principle applies, the enacted definition and program language supply the required clarity. Gregory addressed an ambiguous express employee exception, not a rule that Congress must list each covered institution by name. Neither the importance of prison administration nor an assertion that Congress failed to anticipate this application authorizes adding an exception to comprehensive text.

Coverage does not establish liability. A qualified individual must meet essential eligibility requirements, with or without reasonable modifications to rules, policies or practices, removal of architectural, communication or transportation barriers, or auxiliary aids and services. The Court decides neither that Yeskey has a statutory disability nor that he meets those requirements. It requires no particular modification or fundamental alteration and displaces no legitimate security or direct-threat defense. The question whether a cause of action or particular relief is available, the constitutional scope of congressional power, abrogation, sovereign immunity and damages are not decided. Those distinct questions cannot be resolved by treating the institution as outside the statutory definition.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Gregory v. Ashcroft:** Assuming its clear-statement principle applies, the comprehensive public-entity definition satisfies it. The Court neither displaces that principle nor requires a prison-specific list.
- **City of Boerne v. Flores:** Its limits on Congress’s Fourteenth Amendment enforcement power remain intact; statutory coverage alone determines no application’s constitutional validity.
- **Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida:** Its actual statutory and remedy holdings remain distinct from the coverage question; no controlling direct-State amenability rule is added.
- **Pennsylvania v. Union Gas Co.:** Its existing force is undisturbed; the Court does not decide abrogation or immunity here.

## Law After Decision

Title II has no categorical state-prison exception. Its ordinary disability, qualification and discrimination requirements remain operative, and its application must respect otherwise governing defenses and remedial rules. The coverage holding determines no constitutional-power or sovereign-immunity question.

## Separate Writings



## Procedure After Action

The Third Circuit’s reversal of the categorical dismissal stands. The case returns to the ordinary lower-court proceedings for unresolved matters. No finding of disability, qualification, unlawful exclusion, damages liability or entitlement to early release is made.

## Source Notes

The reported record at [524 U.S. 206](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-524/pdf/USREPORTS-524-206.pdf) supplies the sentence, boot-camp recommendation, alleged hypertension exclusion and lower-court posture. Sections 12131 and 12132 supply the definitions and operative prohibition. The alleged medical exclusion is not a finding that every statutory element is satisfied.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/phillips_v_washington_legal_foundation_merits_1998-06-15.md

## Event

Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation, No. 96-1578. Argued January 13, 1998; decided June 15, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Fifth Circuit. The question is whether interest actually generated by client funds in a mandatory IOLTA account is the private property of the principal’s owner for Takings Clause purposes.

Texas requires lawyers to place client funds in IOLTA when they cannot reasonably be expected to earn interest for the client, or when expected interest would not cover the costs of establishing and maintaining the account, service charges, accounting and tax reporting. The interest goes to the Texas Equal Access to Justice Foundation to finance legal services for persons with low incomes. The District Court granted summary judgment on the ground that clients had no property interest in the proceeds. The Fifth Circuit reversed that determination and left the remaining takings questions for further proceedings.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the Fifth Circuit’s property determination and leaves its remand in place.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.
**Opposing:** Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, who would vacate and remand for integrated consideration of property, taking and compensation; if ownership is decided separately, they reject the asserted interest entitlement in the proper no-net-return program setting.

The lower courts must treat the interest as the client’s property while separately addressing taking, compensation, standing and remedy to the extent those questions remain live. The Court awards no compensation and does not determine the program’s ultimate constitutional validity.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Complete ownership rule, its qualifications and affirmance; later constitutional consequences remain reserved. |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would vacate for integrated consideration of property, taking and compensation; also joins Breyer’s alternative answer if ownership is reached alone. |
| Separate dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Joins the integrated-remand disposition and supplies the alternative objection to client ownership of the otherwise unavailable return. |

## Holdings

### Actually generated interest follows the client’s principal

**Holding and operative rule:** Interest actually generated by client funds in an IOLTA account is the private property of the principal’s owner unless a valid background property rule assigns it elsewhere. The inability to earn a positive net return outside IOLTA does not itself eliminate ownership of the interest once generated; this holding determines title, not whether a taking occurred or compensation is owed.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Kennedy and Thomas. These five directly adopt the ownership rule, its explanation, limits and affirmance.

**Controlling explanation:** Texas recognizes the clients’ ownership of their principal. Its traditional rule assigns the interest earned on that principal to the same owner. Roth directs attention to those existing rules and understandings; Webb’s forbids turning the resulting private earnings into public property by a new governmental declaration. Lucas preserves the distinction between a genuine antecedent limit on title and a newly imposed public burden. Its separate categorical land-use rule does not decide this case.

The pooled client funds generated the interest. Pooling and the public purpose of the proceeds do not sever that relationship. The absence of a profitable alternative return may matter greatly to the later taking and compensation questions, but value and ownership are not identical. An owner’s interests in possession, control and disposition are not extinguished merely because separately earning the increment would cost more than it produces.

Federal permission for NOW accounts and tax nonattribution do not independently assign title under the background property rule. Individuals are a permitted NOW category; for-profit corporations and partnerships face a distinct restriction, and the Federal Reserve’s charitable-interest interpretation addresses banking eligibility. The Court decides neither the validity of that interpretation nor banking legality by resolving ownership. The IRS’s treatment where clients control neither placement nor recipient likewise does not resolve title.

The Court does not decide whether Texas has taken the interest, whether compensation exceeds zero, public use, standing, First Amendment claims or entitlement to declaratory, injunctive or monetary relief. Nor does it prohibit reasonable charges for actual services or characterize this diversion as such a fee. No finding establishes that the particular funds could earn net client interest, individually or through feasible attorney pooling. Anticipated profits and improperly placed funds raise different questions.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Board of Regents v. Roth:** Existing state-law rules and understandings identify the property interest; the Court decides no procedural-due-process claim.
- **Webb’s Fabulous Pharmacies, Inc. v. Beckwith:** Its interest-follows-principal principle and prohibition on public conversion by assertion apply to the earnings actually generated. Its fee reservation is preserved; this decision does not resolve taking or compensation.
- **Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council:** Its distinction between antecedent title limits and new governmental restrictions is applied. Its complete-land-use deprivation rule is not extended to decide the unpresented IOLTA taking question.
- **Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City:** Its contextual takings inquiry remains available where applicable below; the Court makes no economic-impact, expectations or character-of-action determination here.

## Law After Decision

The property element cannot be rejected merely because properly placed IOLTA funds would not generate a positive net return for the client outside the program. Ownership of actually generated interest is established at the stated scope. Taking, loss, compensation, standing and available relief remain separate questions; neither an automatic gross-interest award nor a categorical prohibition on IOLTA follows.

## Separate Writings

**Justice Souter, dissenting, joined by Justices Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** The dissent would vacate the limited judgment and remand for the lower courts to consider property, taking and compensation together. The legal and economic arrangement matters to whether an isolated title declaration has any consequence under the Takings Clause. Roth’s reference to existing entitlements does not require examining one incident of ownership apart from the rules making that interest available. Penn Central directs attention to economic impact, reasonable investment-backed expectations and the character of governmental action, while compensation ordinarily concerns the owner’s loss rather than the recipient’s gain. Those matters could defeat a takings claim, but the dissent would have the lower courts decide them rather than declare no taking or zero compensation here.

The dissent accepts that property identification and valuation are distinct, but regards their isolated treatment in this case as liable to distort the full constitutional inquiry. It does not approve confiscation of the principal, which unquestionably belongs to the client. A claim concerning compulsory use of that principal remains available for lawful consideration even under the dissenters’ alternative rejection of the asserted interest entitlement. The four also join Breyer’s alternative analysis if the Court decides ownership alone.

**Justice Breyer, separately dissenting, joined by Justices Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg.** He joins Souter’s proposed integrated remand. If ownership is reached in isolation, he would reject the claimed entitlement for funds properly placed under the no-net-return program rule. The operative arrangement makes a charitable return possible where a client otherwise has no legally and economically realizable net benefit. Webb’s involved an independently productive fund; its protection against public relabeling does not settle ownership of this otherwise unavailable return. Lucas protects established entitlements without deciding how this claimed benefit must be identified.

This argument does not assume that every individual is legally barred from a NOW account. Individual eligibility, restrictions on corporate and partnership funds, administrative and reporting costs, and the distinction between gross and net yield must remain separate. The Federal Reserve interpretation is part of the banking setting, not a holding that its validity has been adjudicated. The reported Texas concession allows assessment of net client earnings through feasible attorney pooling; it establishes no positive alternative yield for these particular funds. Funds capable of earning net client interest belong outside IOLTA, and diversion of such interest or principal presents a different question. The dissent fixes no compensation amount, accepts no public-purpose exemption from the Takings Clause and does not direct final judgment against every claim.

## Procedure After Action

The Fifth Circuit’s judgment and existing remand remain in place. Further proceedings must respect client ownership of the generated interest and decide the remaining live questions under their own governing law. No injunction, damages award, particular compensation measure or ultimate constitutional disposition is ordered.

## Source Notes

The Texas program, reported account mechanics and lower-court posture appear at [524 U.S. 156](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-524/pdf/USREPORTS-524-156.pdf). The [record for No. 96-1578](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0064) supplies the original litigation materials. Texas IOLTA Rule 6, 12 U.S.C. §1832(a)(2), the Federal Reserve’s October 15, 1981 interpretation and Revenue Rulings 81-209 and 87-2 address distinct program, banking and tax conditions. The reported alternative-pooling concession is a party position, not a finding of net yield on the particular funds.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/ricci_v_village_of_arlington_heights_merits_1998-05-04.md

## Event

Ricci v. Village of Arlington Heights, No. 97-501. Argued April 21, 1998; decided May 4, 1998. On review of the Seventh Circuit’s affirmance of summary judgment, 116 F.3d 288. The question concerns the reasonableness of automatic station-house custody for operating a business without the required license, accepting probable cause and state and local arrest authority.

Officers at Ricci’s telemarketing business asked about his license, and he acknowledged having none. Under Village policy they took him to the station, locked him in an interview room for approximately an hour while preparing enforcement documents, and released him on recognizance. His wife obtained the license, and the charge was dismissed. The civil licensing ordinance carried a $5–$500 fine for each offense, with each day a separate offense; no accumulated fine is adjudicated here.

The separate premises-search claim was settled, and dismissal of the no-probable-cause claim was not appealed. A distinct warrant-clause argument first raised at appellate oral argument was held waived; Ricci conceded an arrest under a warrant would have been reasonable. The preserved question concerns the objective extent of this custody, not a revived warrant, search or probable-cause claim.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at argument and decision.

## Public Action

The Court reverses insofar as the judgment sustains summary judgment on the categorical probable-cause and local-authority premise, sets aside the adverse judgment on the preserved custody-reasonableness claim, and remands.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Reversed in the stated respect and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas.

The adverse summary judgment on the preserved custody-reasonableness claim is set aside. The case returns for further proceedings under the additional-restraint rule. Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer leave the actual custody application for the lower courts; Stone-Zsela would decide that custody unreasonable and limit remand to remaining policy causation, defenses and relief. No application conclusion commands five votes, and no present damages or immunity judgment issues. The settled search claim and unappealed probable-cause dismissal remain undisturbed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Breyer, Stone-Zsela | Targeted reversal and remand | Additional-restraint rule; rejection of categorical probable-cause/local-authority ground; setting aside adverse custody summary judgment |
| Opinion announcing the judgment, Part II | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Breyer | Same reversal and remand; leaves actual application for the lower courts | Four-Justice explanation of the unresolved enforcement-connection inquiry and remand scope |
| Concurrence in Part I and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand; would decide actual custody unreasonable | Objective mismatch on this record; remand limited to remaining liability, defenses and relief; does not join Part II’s application reservation |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy and Souter | Would affirm | This continuing violation and short enforcement processing satisfy the bounded reasonableness inquiry |

## Holdings

### Probable cause does not automatically justify additional station-house custody

**Holding and operative rule:** Probable cause and state or local authority to enforce a fine-only civil licensing ordinance do not, by themselves, establish that transporting an identified operator to a station and confining him is reasonable. The added restraint must reasonably serve the actual enforcement function; nonexclusive possible justifications include securing identification or appearance, ending an actual continuing violation, protecting persons or evidence, or necessary processing in the circumstances. The rule imposes neither least restrictive means nor a duty to issue a citation whenever one is conceivable.

**Authority:** Ginsburg’s Part I is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer. Those five, including the author, directly adopt this rule and the targeted reversal; Stone-Zsela does not join Part II’s affirmative reservation of actual reasonableness.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an established violation supplies authority to intervene, but the permissible extent of the seizure remains a constitutional question. Whren validates an ordinary initial probable-cause traffic stop while preserving independent manner, scope and duration limits. Robinette separates initial authority from justification for later restraint without requiring an indispensable release script. Ayeni likewise confines an authorized intrusion to its lawful function; its home-search setting is not automatically transferred to this business case. Wilson makes relevant history part of reasonableness without constitutionalizing every state rule, and Soldal prevents a civil label from excluding Fourth Amendment protection.

The lower court’s categorical two-predicate premise cannot settle this added custody merely because municipal policy requires it. A policy cannot supply its own constitutional justification. The lower opinion did discuss the continuing operation, potential daily fines, processing documents, one-hour duration and recognizance release; those supported circumstances must not be ignored or described as missing. The governing inquiry concerns their actual relationship to the added restraint.

Fine-only status matters to the reasonable extent of custody but creates no categorical immunity from arrest. The identified examples are possibilities, not findings that a particular justification occurred here. Robinson and Gustafson address consequences of a lawful arrest and cannot establish the disputed antecedent lawfulness by assumption. Hidden dislike of the business is not the ground; Whren’s objective approach remains. The separate warrant theory and the settled or abandoned claims stay outside review. The reversal rejects the automatic formula and sets aside its adverse custody judgment without a five-Justice holding that this custody was unconstitutional.

**Precedent treatment:** Whren, Robinette and Ayeni’s execution limits are applied to this additional licensing restraint; Wilson and Soldal retain their separate historical and coverage functions; Robinson and Gustafson retain the lawful-arrest predicate.

### No controlling conclusion resolves the actual custody’s reasonableness

**Judgment without controlling application rationale:** Five Justices support targeted reversal and remand, but only Stone-Zsela concludes the actual custody was unreasonable. Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer leave that application open; Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas find the supported short enforcement processing reasonable and would affirm.

**Authority:** Part I’s rule controls by direct majority agreement. Part II’s application reservation has four joins, Stone-Zsela’s contrary application has one, and the dissent’s favorable application has four. These positions cannot be combined into a majority holding that this custody was unlawful or lawful, and no Marks synthesis is adopted. The lower courts must proceed under the controlling rule without a Supreme Court finding of ultimate violation, liability or immunity.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Whren v. United States:** Preserved for ordinary initial probable-cause stops and applied at its independent execution limit; it does not establish unlimited custody for every civil violation.
- **Ohio v. Robinette:** Its separate later-restraint inquiry informs the new application without a mandatory citation or warning script.
- **Ayeni v. Mottola:** Its purpose-bound execution principle informs the added-restraint inquiry; the home setting and separate immunity decision retain their limits.
- **Wilson v. Arkansas:** History remains relevant to reasonableness, and reversal for a deficient framework need not establish a completed violation. No state arrest rule is automatically constitutionalized.
- **Soldal v. Cook County:** Civil enforcement remains within Fourth Amendment coverage, while reasonableness and liability stay distinct; its summary-judgment remedy does not establish those predicates automatically.
- **United States v. Robinson; Gustafson v. Florida:** Their lawful-arrest premise is preserved; search consequences cannot establish antecedent arrest lawfulness by assumption.
- **County of Riverside v. McLaughlin:** Its timing rule for post-arrest probable-cause review does not itself authorize this custody.
- **United States v. Lanier:** Conduct-time legal clarity remains separate from a present constitutional ruling; no newly stated rule retroactively defeats personal immunity.

## Law After Decision

Automatic station-house custody for this fine-only civil licensing offense requires an objective enforcement connection beyond probable cause and local permission alone. The rule permits reasonable practical enforcement judgments and supplies no universal citation requirement or categorical fine-only arrest prohibition. The adverse custody judgment is set aside, but no controlling holding determines whether this actual processing satisfies the rule. Municipal policy causation, individual responsibility, conduct-time immunity and relief retain their independent predicates.

## Separate Writings

**Ginsburg, joined by the other members of the four-Justice Part II coalition, on the unresolved application.** Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer conclude that the categorical premise foreclosed the required assessment of added restraint even though the lower court discussed continuing operation, possible daily fines, processing and duration. They would evaluate those existing facts under the actual-enforcement rule rather than decide now that custody was unlawful. A favorable judgment for defendants remains legally possible after that inquiry. No new hearing is required simply because further facts might exist, and practical processing may suffice when reasonably connected to enforcement. Wilson and Soldal permit correction of a legal framework and adverse summary judgment without a present violation finding.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in Part I and in the judgment.** Probable cause identifies the licensing violation but does not answer whether taking this identified operator from his business served a sufficient enforcement purpose. The record identifies an immediately obtainable license and a fine-only regulatory obligation, without failure to identify himself, flight, danger or another reason for removal. Stone-Zsela finds the automatic policy’s custody objectively disconnected from securing compliance or appearance and would decide it unreasonable on this record. His ground is not hidden official hostility toward the fundraising business, which Whren does not make decisive. He would remand only for municipal policy causation, preserved defenses and relief. Individual immunity must use the legal notice available in 1994; this present clarification does not retroactively defeat it. He neither creates a rule for every minor offense nor revives the settled search or abandoned probable-cause claim, and he does not join leaving the constitutional application open.

**Thomas, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, dissenting.** These Justices reject unlimited authority from local permission and probable cause but find the necessary enforcement connection established here. Ricci admitted an actual continuing violation; custody lasted about an hour while officers prepared the arrest sheet, local-ordinance complaint and bond receipt and arranged recognizance release. Those tasks concerned enforcement of the same offense, unlike an unrelated additional intrusion under Ayeni. Neither the civil label nor the availability of a possible on-site citation requires proof that citation was impossible before ordinary processing. Whren, Wilson and Robinette preserve review of atypical force, unjustified duration, unrelated later detention or a method lacking a reasonable enforcement relationship. None of those broader permissions is asserted as a categorical rule here. They would affirm this actual application without deciding individual immunity.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns through the Seventh Circuit with adverse summary judgment on the preserved custody claim set aside. The controlling additional-restraint rule governs further proceedings; the Court supplies no majority determination of actual unreasonableness, damages, municipal causation, officer participation or immunity. No particular evidentiary hearing, new licensing policy or revival of other claims is ordered.

## Source Notes

The custody, continuing-operation and preservation account appears in [Ricci v. Arlington Heights, 116 F.3d 288](https://openjurist.org/116/f3d/288/ricci-v-arlington-heights-illinois), including its four footnotes. That opinion does not adjudicate individual immunity, and its silence does not establish forfeiture. The record concerns approximately one hour of interview-room confinement and processing; hypothetical shackling, strip searches, deadly force and home entries are not facts of this case.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/ringuette_v_city_of_fall_river_merits_1998-06-11.md

## Event

Ringuette v. City of Fall River, lower No. 96-1107, judgment below 146 F.3d 1. Plenary merits decision, June 11, 1998, on submission; no separate Supreme Court docket or oral argument date is established. Review concerns the First Circuit's June 4 affirmance of personal qualified immunity for Officer Raymond Paradis and Sergeant Richard Levesque on the unlawful-detention claim, and the late objection to Paradis's unpleaded defense. The conduct occurred July 27–28, 1992. The trial court entered judgment as a matter of law after evidence, before submitting the separate medical-care and city-negligence claims to the jury. The reported finding concerning perceived willingness to remain, and the judge's procedure for resolving it, were not directly challenged on appeal.

## Participation

Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The personal seizure-immunity judgments and rejection of the late pleading objection are affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|
| Paradis: seizure immunity | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Personal immunity judgment stands |
| Levesque: seizure immunity | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Personal immunity judgment stands |
| Paradis: late pleading objection | Rejected, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Actually litigated immunity defense remains available; Stone-Zsela agrees only with this disposition and adds no ground |

The separate medical-care verdict, city-negligence judgment and other excluded claims are undisturbed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Affirms both officers and rejects pleading objection | Both holdings; eight Associate Justices |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Affirms personal immunity on distinct proposed contextual defense | Does not join the Court's immunity rationale; agrees only with disposition of pleading issue without a ground |

## Holdings

### 1. The two officers receive immunity on the preserved seizure record

**Holding and operative rule:** Paradis and Levesque receive personal qualified immunity because the law as of July 27–28, 1992 did not make the precise federal duty sufficiently clear on the preserved account of continuing apparent incapacity and perceived refusal of offered release. Breach of the State's twelve-hour protective-custody ceiling is significant but does not by itself establish a federal constitutional violation or automatically defeat federal qualified immunity.

**Authority:** Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court is joined by all eight Associate Justices. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela agrees with the immunity judgment on a distinct ground and does not join the Harlow/Anderson rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Harlow protects an official unless the conduct violated federal law clearly established when the official acted; Anderson requires particularized notice without demanding an identical factual precedent. Davis distinguishes a violation of a state rule from automatic loss of federal immunity. The twelve-hour limit was real: where suitable treatment was unavailable, confinement against the person's will could continue only until incapacity ended or twelve hours elapsed, whichever came first. A second form supplied no renewed statutory authority.

Paradis nevertheless received the earlier release account and reported asking Ringuette about leaving; he understood the response as continuing intoxication and unwillingness to leave. Levesque received that report and authorized the second form. Their acts and information must be assessed separately; a supervisor's approval is no automatic defense and the later emergency call does not legalize earlier custody. The preserved finding concerns what the officers understood, not an adjudication of actual consent. The locked custody remains a seizure.

The clear deadline and serious consequences do not erase the distinct uncertainty about the federal course when a seemingly incapacitated person appears to decline release. Later medical evidence does not establish what either officer knew during the challenged detention. Elder's all-relevant-law method and Lanier's rejection of a factual-twin requirement govern present review but supply no retrospective notice. The Court does not decide that the entire detention was constitutionally reasonable, authorize indefinite custody or hold that a judge may generally resolve disputed Rule 50 facts against a plaintiff. The separately litigated medical-care claim and city liability do not supply a substitute seizure-immunity ruling.

### 2. The actually litigated defense was not excluded by Paradis's pleading omission

**Holding and operative rule:** Paradis's failure to plead qualified immunity does not exclude the defense where it was actually presented, litigated and decided without timely objection or prejudice, and the plaintiff first raised the pleading objection on appeal. Rule 15(b) permits treatment of that litigated issue as within the pleadings; this record does not establish that every unpleaded defense is preserved.

**Authority:** The eight Associates join this distinct procedural holding. Stone-Zsela agrees only with the disposition of the pleading objection and adds no ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Levesque pleaded immunity; Paradis did not. The plaintiff nonetheless contested immunity for both officers at the dispositive motion and on reconsideration without the pleading objection. The reported proceedings affirmatively establish actual litigation of the defense, rather than its first introduction after verdict. Rule 15(b)'s actual-litigation principle answers the late objection in the absence of identified prejudice. McCardle concerned a different failure to present a particular immunity ground in the Rule 50 motion; it does not convert this fully litigated issue into that unpreserved ground. The Court neither excuses every pleading defect nor alters ordinary factual and legal preservation requirements.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald:** Applied and retained as the objective clearly-established-law defense; no replacement framework is adopted.
- **Anderson v. Creighton:** Applied to require particularized conduct-time federal notice without an identical precedent.
- **Davis v. Scherer:** Applied to distinguish violation of a state rule from automatic loss of federal immunity.
- **Elder v. Holloway, February 23, 1994:** Its all-relevant-law review method applies; it supplies no notice retrospectively in 1992.
- **United States v. Lanier, March 31, 1997:** Its clarity method does not itself supply earlier notice or erase the actual constitutional elements.
- **Crawford-El v. Britton, June 16, 1997:** Its ordinary proof and case-management rules leave Harlow/Anderson intact.
- **Johnson v. Jones; Behrens v. Pelletier:** Their interlocutory legal/factual boundaries are distinguished from this final-judgment review.
- **McCardle v. Haddad, January 29, 1998:** Distinguished because the present immunity issue was actually litigated; its different Rule 50 preservation holding remains.
- **DeCarlo v. Fry, May 28, 1998; City of Florence v. Chipman, June 1, 1998:** Their personal-role and stage-specific rules remain; their later dates supply no 1992 notice and their distinct facts do not decide this custody record.

## Law After Decision

The state custody ceiling remains binding as state law. Federal personal immunity turns separately on the conduct-time federal rule and each defendant's information and acts. This preserved account supports immunity without a constitutional declaration that all continued custody was lawful. Rule 15(b) applies to the actually litigated defense; Stone-Zsela's replacement immunity proposal is not adopted.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the immunity judgments. He would place the burden on the official under his proposed contextual defense, rather than retain a duplicative excuse for a deliberate constitutional violation. That proposal is not the Court's law. Here the explicit state deadline supplies significant warning, but apparent continuing incapacity and perceived refusal of available release create genuine uncertainty about the immediate constitutional course. An officer may reasonably misunderstand that concrete protective situation; invented legal doubt about a known violation would be different. Later medical information does not establish what either officer knew then. His concurrence neither authorizes indefinite detention nor resolves the separate medical-care or city-negligence claims. On the independent pleading-objection component he agrees only with its rejection and states no reason.

## Procedure After Action

The two personal seizure-immunity judgments remain in force. No new trial or damages proceeding is directed, and the distinct medical-care and municipal judgments remain undisturbed.

## Source Notes

The defendant-specific record, trial posture and appellate-preservation account appear in [146 F.3d 1](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/146/146.F3d.1.96-1107.html), corroborated by the [Caselaw Access Project text](https://static.case.law/f3d/146/html/0001-01.html). The reported judicial finding concerns perceived willingness; it is not a jury finding or established actual consent. June 11 is the merits date on the Court's assigned calendar.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/shahar_v_bowers_merits_1998-03-02.md

## Event

Shahar v. Bowers, Eleventh Circuit No. 93-9345, 114 F.3d 1097 (en banc). Plenary merits decision on submission, March 2, 1998. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the May 30, 1997 judgment affirming summary judgment for Bowers. The case concerns withdrawal in July 1991 of an accepted civilian staff-attorney offer because of a planned same-sex religious commitment ceremony. The preserved theories are intimate association, expressive association, free exercise and equal protection. Shahar seeks damages and injunctive employment relief. No separate Supreme Court docket or oral-argument date is supplied; substantive due process and an independent free-speech claim are outside this review.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

## Public Action

The defense judgment is vacated on the four preserved claims and the case remanded for the distinct inquiries stated in the Court’s opinion.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Claim | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Position of Stone-Zsela |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Intimate association | Vacate and remand, 5–3, with one not reaching the claim | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Does not reach this claim |
| Expressive association | Vacate and remand, 5–3, with one not reaching the claim | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Does not reach this claim |
| Free exercise | Vacate and remand, 6–2, with one not reaching the claim | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Does not reach this claim |
| Equal protection | Vacate and remand: 5; reverse and remand: 1; affirm: 3 | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer support vacatur; Stone-Zsela supports reversal | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas support affirmance | Would reverse and remand for intermediate scrutiny; the five-Justice vacatur and controlling instruction apply ordinary rationality |

Stone-Zsela’s nonreach is a limitation of his decision, not nonparticipation or a vote affirming the other claims. Each remanded claim has its own direct majority. No final liability, immunity, reinstatement, backpay, damages or compulsory trial is ordered.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer | Vacates and remands all four claims | Parts I (association), II (free exercise), III (equal protection) and IV (bounded mandate) have Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; O’Connor also joins Part II and Part IV only as applied to the free-exercise remand |
| Concurrence in setting aside the equality judgment and remanding; dissent from formal vacatur and the ordinary-rationality mandate | Stone-Zsela | — | Would reverse that claim and remand under intermediate scrutiny | Does not join the Court’s formal equality disposition or mandate, its ordinary-rationality reasoning, or reach the association/free-exercise claims |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | O’Connor | — | Joins the free-exercise remand; would sustain association and equality judgments | Particular professional employment balance and ordinary rationality |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm all four claims | Assumed-right employment balance, secular-ground free-exercise analysis and ordinary rationality |

## Holdings

### I. Private association requires its own protection and employment inquiry

**Holding and operative rule:** The actual selective, enduring intimate relationship and its religious expression cannot be denied constitutional protection merely because they lack civil-marriage recognition or include an invitation-only ceremony. A public employer’s genuine operational interests remain relevant, but generalized forecasts, presumed lawbreaking and presumed government endorsement do not conclusively justify this defense summary judgment.

**Authority:** Part I of the Court’s opinion is joined by Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, five direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** Roberts and Rotary distinguish close personal association from association for protected expression and require examination of the actual relationship and activity. On the plaintiff-favorable record, the selective, enduring relationship and its religious expression satisfy those protected predicates; their protection is not left open merely because the relationship lacks civil-marriage recognition. Shahar seeks protection against an employment penalty, not marital status or benefits. Umbehr separates entitlement to a benefit from an unconstitutional reason for withholding it. Jeffries requires attention to actual functions and institutional needs: prevention need not await disruption, but a reasonable forecast does not alone settle the legality of penalizing protected activity. Confidentiality, credible representation, assignment flexibility and professional trust remain substantial concerns. Neither a general office label nor speculation about endorsement establishes inability to perform lawful duties. No lawbreaking or refusal of duties is established, and later insurance or name-change evidence cannot supply contemporaneous knowledge. Waters protects reasonable factual reliance only when the substantive rule permits disciplining the conduct described and establishes no universal interview requirement. The remaining employment justification, causal application and relief return for adjudication; the Court orders no retention of a lawyer unable to perform necessary work.

### II. Free exercise cannot be absorbed into a general employee-speech balance

**Holding and operative rule:** A public-employment decision alleged to burden sincere private religious exercise must be examined for neutrality and general applicability under its own governing rule. If the actual restriction is nonneutral or not generally applicable, the employer must establish a compelling justification and narrow tailoring; religious impact alone does not establish that trigger.

**Authority:** Part II has Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, six direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Smith preserves neutral, generally applicable rules. Lukumi requires examination of objective targeting and comparable secular conduct; Brown applies the required justification to religion-specific employment restrictions while preserving control of work, resources, coercion and official representation. The lower court assumed religious protection and treated the claim as resolved by the same general employment balance. That does not answer whether this withdrawal imposed the prohibited religious disability. The contrary argument is substantial: Bowers may have responded to the represented relationship and perceived job consequences regardless of religious form. That is an argument requiring proper assessment, not an established hypothetical fact that disposes of the independent claim. Sincerity does not prove targeting, and the absence of theological compulsion does not defeat sincere exercise. All six joiners reserve the trigger’s ultimate application and any properly supported justification. No finding of discrimination, general religious exemption or new statutory claim follows.

### III. The actual equality disadvantage requires ordinary constitutional justification

**Holding and operative rule:** An adverse civilian employment classification does not escape ordinary equal-protection review by being described as conduct-based or as a hiring choice. The actual disadvantage must bear a rational relation to a legitimate governmental interest; no new orientation-based heightened tier is adopted.

**Authority:** Part III has Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, five direct joins. Stone-Zsela’s different proposed scrutiny standard is not controlling and is not combined with this rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Romer applies ordinary rationality to the actual disability, allowing reasonable predictions and generalizations while excluding bare harm or irrational prejudice. Valot distinguishes that inquiry from justification for penalizing protected activity and from a proved same-decision defense. The lower court’s conduct/employment characterization cannot itself resolve the claim. The office’s professional and representative responsibilities remain legitimate considerations; remand does not make every predictive judgment invalid or require proof of actual disruption. Walmer, Thomasson and Richenberg address military and conduct-related settings, not an irrebuttable civilian inference from a religious commitment ceremony. Bowers v. Hardwick concerns its specified conduct; orientation or the ceremony alone proves no violation. The Court neither decides liability nor requires intermediate scrutiny. The five-Justice ordinary-rationality instruction governs the equality remand.

### IV. Causation, conduct-date immunity and relief remain independent

**Holding and operative rule:** Remand requires claim-specific constitutional predicates, personal responsibility, causation and lawful relief. Where retaliation analysis applies, protected activity must be a substantial or motivating factor and a same-decision defense requires proof by a preponderance; ordinary rationality’s conceivable basis is not that proof.

**Authority:** Part IV is joined by the five common Court-opinion Justices; O’Connor joins its application to the free-exercise remand.

**Controlling explanation:** Umbehr and Jeffries preserve actual causal responsibility and the counterfactual defense. Bowers pleaded qualified immunity, but neither lower court decided it and he did not request appellate affirmance on that ground. The Court does not award the defense, decide waiver or automatically revive it. Any procedurally available personal-damages defense concerns notice in July 1991; later Romer, Brown, Umbehr, Jeffries, Valot and Danahy cannot supply retrospective notice. Prospective relief, any proper successor, personal damages and official-capacity relief retain separate predicates. Later evidence may have a lawful remedial relevance without becoming contemporaneous motive. Elapsed time establishes neither mootness nor a continuing job entitlement. The mandate compels no interview, discovery plan, trial, reinstatement, backpay, fees or damages award.

## Precedent Treatment

- Roberts and Rotary supply distinct intimate- and expressive-association inquiries; civil-marriage recognition is not their exclusive boundary.
- Umbehr distinguishes benefit entitlement from unconstitutional retaliation and preserves actual causation and the preponderance same-decision defense.
- Jeffries preserves preventive employment judgment while rejecting conclusive reliance on generalized forecasts. Waters protects reasonable factual reliance only within the substantive constitutional rule; neither supplies a compulsory interview code.
