- Smith retains neutral, generally applicable rules; Lukumi supplies objective targeting, comparable secular conduct and triggered strict justification. Brown requires the separate religion-specific employment inquiry.
- Romer supplies ordinary rationality for the actual disadvantage. Valot keeps that standard distinct from a protected-right penalty and a same-decision defense.
- Walmer, Thomasson and Richenberg retain their military and conduct-related scope; they do not establish civilian exclusion or a heightened orientation tier. Bowers v. Hardwick is not extended from specified conduct to orientation or a ceremony.
- Danahy supplies no office-wide merits exception and no retroactive July 1991 notice. NTEU’s broad prospective-restraint burden is not transferred wholesale to this single hiring decision.

## Law After Decision

The four preserved claims return under distinct constitutional standards. Five Justices establish the association and ordinary-equality grounds; six establish the separate free-exercise inquiry. Intermediate scrutiny is not adopted. No unified rationale is synthesized from different writings, and no military inference, generalized government-lawyer exception or actual-disruption prerequisite controls civilian employment.

## Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in setting aside the equal-protection judgment and remanding, and dissenting from formal vacatur and the ordinary-rationality mandate.** Stone-Zsela would reverse the equality judgment and require intermediate scrutiny on a developed record. He treats that standard as a proposed change, not a rule already established by Romer, Walmer or Thomasson. The actual reason for withdrawing the offer, any unsupported hostility and legitimate civilian operational grounds require examination; military conduct-related precedents do not settle this religious commitment ceremony. He leaves intimate association, expressive association and free exercise unresolved, as well as liability, immunity, damages and ultimate relief.

**O’Connor, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** O’Connor joins the separate free-exercise inquiry under Brown and Lukumi without finding targeting. On association she assumes the asserted protection but gives dispositive weight to this staff lawyer’s professional trust, representation, assignment flexibility and credible forecasts, as in her Jeffries position and Waters rationale. On equality she finds a sufficient ordinary-rationality relationship to those professional concerns without military deference or a new classification tier. She would sustain those three dispositions while allowing only the religion claim to return; she infers neither sodomy nor refusal to perform duties.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent assumes the association rights for decision and finds the particular legal office’s preventive professional judgment sufficient; it adopts no categorical immunity for government lawyers or license for bare hostility. On free exercise it reads the supported decision as responding to the represented relationship and perceived work consequences, regardless of religious form, and distinguishes Brown’s direct religion-specific commands. Smith and Lukumi forbid religious discrimination but do not convert every effect on religious exercise into prohibited targeting. The dissent also finds the particular professional concerns sufficient under ordinary rationality. It would affirm without a damages-immunity ruling or a finding that the ceremony proves unlawful sexual conduct.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for the four claim-specific inquiries and properly remaining defenses and remedies. The judgment supplies no final employment entitlement, personal liability or constitutional damages. No additional speech or substantive-due-process claim is revived. This Supreme Court review is complete.

## Source Notes

The record, competing en banc accounts and preservation limits appear in [114 F.3d 1097](https://static.case.law/f3d/114/html/1097-01.html), with a [Public.Resource cross-check](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/114/114.F3d.1097.93-9345.html). The published accounts conflict on the precise ceremony date; none of the holdings depends on selecting one or finding the ceremony occurred before withdrawal. Depositions and the application are available here only through published recitations. Later-acquired facts do not establish contemporaneous knowledge. The March 2 merits submission supplies the decisional placement; no additional historical docket or argument date is asserted.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/smith_v_mattox_merits_1998-01-12.md

## Event

Smith v. Mattox, review of the Eleventh Circuit's decision in No. 96-6648, 127 F.3d 1416. Decided January 12, 1998, on the legal qualified-immunity issue presented by the denial of summary judgment. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated on submission and at decision. No Justice was absent.

## Public Action

The judgment sustaining denial of Mattox's qualified-immunity summary judgment on the presented legal question is affirmed.

## Judgment & Remedy

Affirmed, 9–0. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: none. The case returns for ordinary lawful proceedings on the actual facts, remaining elements and defenses. No final constitutional violation, damages or liability is adjudged, and no trial, hearing, discovery plan or special interrogatory is commanded.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I–II: scope and concrete force application | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III: retained immunity framework and bounded disposition | Scalia | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; Stone-Zsela joins the disposition and ordinary-proceedings limits |
| Concurrence in Parts I–II and the judgment | Stone-Zsela | None |

## Holdings

**1. The separable legal immunity question is reviewable on the proper assumed account.** The Court may decide whether the plaintiff-favorable conduct was clearly established as unlawful without resolving whether that conduct occurred. It may not select between accounts of compliance, continued resistance or gun-reaching, decide credibility or review evidentiary sufficiency. Part I is unanimous.

Johnson and Behrens preserve this legal/factual division, and Clanton confirms that disputed evidence does not eliminate review of a genuine legal defense. Section 1254 does not enlarge the circuit's §1291 authority. No separate factual-sufficiency component requiring dismissal is established here. The Court accepts the proper assumptions solely to decide this appeal.

**2. The particular assumed force was obviously outside the lawful bounds in June 1993.** Under Graham v. Connor, force during arrest must be objectively reasonable in the actual circumstances from the officer's contemporaneous perspective, including the severity of the crime, immediate threat and active resistance or flight. The considerable additional arm force and reported blow against Smith while wholly unresisting on the assumed account were apparently unlawful under that pre-existing rule; an identical earlier handcuffing-injury decision is unnecessary. Part II is unanimous.

The earlier bat threat, initial refusal, flight and feigned renewed flight justified caution and some restraining force. Submission did not establish completed cuffing or make all further force unlawful. But the assumed account describes docility during the maneuver, an arm pull, a complaint of discomfort, and a grunt and blow followed by multiple fractures requiring surgery. Those circumstances together, rather than the fracture alone, exceed the remaining latitude for reasonable error. The competing reliance on a dangerous encounter cannot insert continued resistance, gun-reaching or an unassumed safety mistake into this account.

Harlow and Anderson require particularized notice, not merely invocation of a general right to bodily security. Graham supplied the force rule before June 11, 1993. Elder and Lanier explain the present inquiry into relevant authority and obvious coverage; neither supplies retrospective notice. No exhaustive finding about the absence of all earlier authority is necessary, and the Court adopts no exclusive factually-similar-case route. Subjective anger is neither assumed nor required. Injury alone, bad purpose and hindsight do not determine the result.

**3. Denial on these assumptions leaves the received defense and ordinary proceedings intact.** Harlow and Anderson remain the governing objective qualified-immunity framework. Mattox may establish a materially different supported account, including continuing resistance or necessary force, through ordinary proceedings and may obtain lawful fact-dependent reconsideration of immunity. The complete retained-framework holding in Part III has all eight Associate Justices; all nine support the bounded affirmance and these ordinary-proceedings limits.

Elder requires relevant preconduct law on the preserved issue without importing new claims or evidence. Crawford-El preserves ordinary competent proof and independent defenses but adds neither a motive element nor retaliation's burden sequence to Graham. The Court does not reach unpleaded warrantless-entry or probable-cause theories, convert force damages into unlawful-arrest damages, reopen the city or police chief's judgments, or create a separate Fourteenth Amendment claim. Failure of this legal defense establishes no ultimate constitutional violation, causation, supervisory liability, damages or entitlement to trial.

## Precedent Treatment

Graham v. Connor supplies the objective arrest-force rule and is applied to the complete assumed encounter. Harlow and Anderson retain particularized objective immunity while permitting an obvious application without a factual twin. Elder preserves independent review of relevant conduct-date authority. Lanier supplies the common legal-clarity approach without transferring criminal elements or later notice. Johnson, Behrens and Clanton retain the legal/factual review boundary. Crawford-El preserves ordinary proof and defenses within the actual elements. No precedent is overruled or replacement defense adopted.

## Law After Decision

The existing force and notice rules exclude categorical immunity merely because no earlier case used the identical handcuffing maneuver or injury. The particular complete-nonresistance account here meets the obvious-application standard despite the earlier danger. The holding creates no general prohibition on all force after submission, presumption from a fracture, hindsight test or liability finding. Objective Harlow/Anderson immunity remains governing law.

## Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela agrees that earlier menace does not authorize the assumed gratuitous injury after submission and that Mattox cannot obtain reversal by substituting a disputed account of resistance. The controlling application rests on the alleged absence of resistance, controlled position and additional blow after complaint, not the fracture alone. He also favors a contextual defense placing the justificatory burden on the official and rejecting a duplicative excuse for deliberate violations. That proposal is not adopted: the Court retains Harlow and Anderson, and Crawford-El did not replace them. Stone-Zsela preserves actual opportunities, reasonable mistakes, contrary supported facts and all remaining liability and remedial requirements.

## Procedure After Action

The circuit judgment is affirmed, leaving the District Court's immunity denial operative on the presented assumptions. Ordinary proceedings may resolve genuine historical disputes and any remaining elements or defenses. The disposed city/chief claims and unpleaded entry/arrest theories remain outside this decision. No compulsory trial or automatic return to this Court is ordered.

## Source Notes

The complete Eleventh Circuit opinion and twelve footnotes, 127 F.3d 1416, supply the June 11, 1993 encounter, expressly attributed competing accounts, claim history and appeal scope. Original pleadings and depositions are not independently reproduced. The opinion reports no sign of anger on the assumed account; no purpose to break the arm or completed cuffing is inferred. Later Kelly and Post formulations do not supply earlier notice. Missing originals establish no absent evidence, waiver or litigant default.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/spencer_v_kemna_mootness_1998-03-03.md

## Event

Spencer v. Kemna, No. 96-7171. Decision after plenary review, March 3, 1998; argued November 12, 1997. On writ of certiorari under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) to the Eighth Circuit, which affirmed dismissal of the §2254 petition. Spencer challenges the September 1992 parole revocation, not the original burglary and stealing convictions. He filed on April 1, 1993 while imprisoned under the revocation; the challenged sentence expired October 16, 1993. Review concerns statutory custody, continuing Article III injury, mootness exceptions and the asserted effect of possible civil-remedy limits.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate. No Justice is recused.

## Public Action

The Eighth Circuit’s judgment affirming dismissal is affirmed on mootness alone.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirm dismissal as moot, 8–1.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stevens.

The dismissal rests on absence of a continuing Article III controversy, not failure of custody when the petition was filed or a decision on the revocation’s merits. Stevens would reverse and remand the live petition for its remaining lawful adjudication. Stone-Zsela agrees with the ancillary custody-at-filing, capable-of-repetition exception, litigation-delay and civil-remedy dispositions without adding a ground.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg | Affirms mootness dismissal | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer join all parts; Stone-Zsela joins Part II’s actual-continuing-injury ground and mootness result, but agrees only with Part II’s capable-of-repetition and litigation-delay dispositions and Parts I and III’s ancillary dispositions, without those additional rationales |
| Dissent | Stevens | — | Would reverse and remand | Recognizes the particular official adverse adjudication’s continuing reputational consequence; does not decide the revocation merits or an affirmative section 1983 exception |

## Holdings

### I. Custody at filing and a continuing controversy are separate requirements

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 2254’s custody requirement is satisfied when the petitioner is held under the challenged revocation at filing; later expiration does not retroactively defeat that threshold. Article III nonetheless requires a concrete continuing injury after the challenged sentence expires.

**Authority:** Part I is joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, seven direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees with the custody disposition without an additional ground; Stevens also accepts custody at filing.

**Controlling explanation:** Spencer was imprisoned under this revocation on April 1, 1993. The District Court’s alternative suggestion that expiration removed statutory custody is incorrect. The later seven-year imprisonment arises from an unrelated conviction, rather than the unexpired consecutive aggregate addressed in Garlotte. A timely filing therefore satisfies the statutory entry condition without itself supplying an enduring constitutional controversy. Lujan and Arizonans require an actual personal interest throughout adjudication, not simply an interest that existed when suit began.

### II. The asserted collateral consequences and exceptions do not keep this petition live

**Holding and operative rule:** After this parole-revocation sentence expires, collateral injury is not presumed merely from the revocation; the petitioner must show an actual continuing consequence redressable in this action. Speculative future parole, sentencing or evidentiary uses, the possibility of recurrence and litigation delay do not supply that showing on this record.

**Authority:** Part II’s continuing-injury rule and mootness result have eight direct joins: Stone-Zsela and the seven Associates joining the Court opinion. The seven Associates also directly adopt the specific exception and delay applications.

**Controlling explanation:** An attack on a conviction is distinct from this challenge to a revocation of an otherwise valid sentence. Lane supports examining the actual surviving interest, although Spencer, unlike Lane’s respondents, challenges the adverse revocation finding itself. Future parole use depends on discretionary treatment, sentencing use on later legal developments, impeachment on future testimony and permissible use, and other-crimes evidence on additional contingencies. No present mandatory disability or demonstrated imminent adverse use is established. MacArthur’s actual statutory information interest and existing damages action do not make every retained adverse record a live habeas controversy; the Court adopts no categorical exclusion of reputational injury. The capable-of-repetition exception requires both a duration too short for full review and a reasonable expectation that this claimant will again face the action. Neither the asserted delay nor possible injury to others establishes those conditions. State extensions and delayed judicial action cannot create Article III power through fault or estoppel. Expiration occurred by the sentence’s terms, not a newly established strategic cessation. The Court decides no revocation claim, criminal guilt or exoneration.

### III. An unresolved future civil remedy does not create this habeas controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** Heck and Edwards do not establish an unavoidable favorable-invalidation bar for every former prisoner: their controlling rules expressly reserve that category and persons without a realistic lawful custody-review opportunity. The unresolved scope of a possible later civil action does not create a damages claim in this habeas case or dispense with continuing Article III injury.

**Authority:** Part III is joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, seven direct votes. Stone-Zsela agrees with the disposition of this ancillary contention without adding a ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Heck’s decided prerequisite concerns the specified in-custody, conviction-negating damages claim; Edwards preserves that limit while requiring genuinely independent injuries to be classified by their consequences. Neither decision supplies the universal former-prisoner bar assumed by Spencer’s argument. A claim whose success does not necessarily invalidate the revocation would also require its own analysis. The Court need not choose an affirmative former-prisoner exception to reject the asserted inevitable bar. No damages claim is pleaded here, and possible difficulty obtaining another remedy does not empower the Court to decide an otherwise moot habeas petition. The existing reservations remain open rather than becoming either a categorical bar or a newly adjudicated exception.

## Precedent Treatment

- Lane is applied for the continuing-interest inquiry while its unchallenged-revocation posture is distinguished; no automatic consequence is inferred for this revocation.
- Lujan and Arizonans preserve an actual personal controversy throughout review. Arizonans’ vacatur remedy does not require clearing a nonexistent intervening merits judgment here.
- Garlotte’s continuous consecutive-sentence aggregate is distinguished from Spencer’s unrelated later custody; custody at filing remains sufficient for the statutory threshold.
- MacArthur preserves supported actual personal information interests without creating a universal reputation-based habeas rule.
- Heck and Edwards retain their bounded invalidation prerequisite and express former-prisoner/realistic-opportunity reservations; no affirmative section 1983 exception or universal bar is added.

## Law After Decision

A timely custody-based habeas filing does not survive expiration of the challenged sentence without continuing Article III injury or a valid exception. This record establishes neither. The revocation’s merits remain unadjudicated, and the separate civil-remedy questions expressly reserved by Heck and Edwards remain open.

## Separate Writings

**Stevens, dissenting.** Stevens distinguishes Lane because Spencer challenges the official adverse revocation finding itself, not merely completed custody while leaving that finding untouched. He considers the particular government adjudication and the State’s continuing reliance on the incorporated serious-misconduct report a present personal reputational consequence that invalidation could meaningfully remove. He does not presume every revocation carries statutory disabilities or depend on a future crime, audience reaction or invented damages claim. The written order found condition violations and incorporated a report; it was not an express rape conviction, and the State’s characterization remains distinct. MacArthur’s requirement of a supported personal interest does not, in his view, erase this already imposed official determination. He would remand for unresolved lawful adjudication, without granting a writ, finding procedural wrongdoing or deciding the civil-remedy question.

## Procedure After Action

The habeas action remains dismissed as moot, without constitutional-merits preclusion. No writ, new revocation hearing, expungement, damages or exoneration is ordered. This Supreme Court review is complete, and no continuing return condition is created.

## Source Notes

The revocation order, incorporated report, custody dates, claimed procedural defects and later separate sentence are reported in [523 U.S. 1](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-523/pdf/USREPORTS-523-1.pdf). The woman’s allegations, Spencer’s admitted cocaine use and denial of force, the written condition-violation order and the State’s description remain distinct. The underlying reports and complete record are available here through published recitations; they establish no criminal conviction for the alleged conduct. No reputation-only submission or damages demand by Spencer is attributed to him. [Lane, 455 U.S. 624](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-455/pdf/USREPORTS-455-624.pdf), supplies the earlier posture and continuing-interest analysis.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/tanford_v_brand_merits_1997-12-22.md

## Event

Tanford v. Brand, review of the Seventh Circuit's judgment in No. 96-3053, 104 F.3d 982. Decided December 22, 1997, on the merits of the challenge to Indiana University's official commencement invocations and benedictions. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated on submission and at decision. No Justice was absent.

## Public Action

The Seventh Circuit's judgment is reversed, and the case is remanded for the lower courts to determine appropriate prospective relief against the official prayer practice, subject to the plaintiffs' continuing standing and the requirements for an injunction.

## Judgment & Remedy

Reversed and remanded, 6–3. Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, who would affirm. Any prospective relief the lower courts find warranted, subject to the plaintiffs' continuing standing and the requirements for an injunction, must be limited to the University's selection or conduct of invocation and benediction prayers as official worship and must preserve independent prayer and lawful private expression. No damages, fee ruling, automatic hearing, mandatory new forum or general ban on religious speech is ordered.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Part I: live controversy and official attribution | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Parts II–III: constitutional application and relief | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Dissent, Part I: adult-setting ground | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas |
| Dissent, Part II: historical accommodation and compulsion account | Scalia | Thomas |
| Separate dissenting statement | Kennedy | None |

## Holdings

**1. The live controversy concerns the University's own devotional program.** The continuing policy and Urbanski's concrete expectation of attending his 1999 graduation support prospective review. The University selects a religious leader and incorporates actual invocation and benediction prayers into its official ceremony; independent composition of the words does not make the exercises private equal-access speech. Part I is unanimous.

Lee's prospective-exposure principle supports the live claim without treating other plaintiffs as perpetual students or relying on ideological objection alone. The program places the invocation after the national anthem and the benediction at the close. The reproduced benediction invites prayer, addresses God, seeks divine assistance and concludes with Amen. The University need not have written a full script for the exercise to be its own: official selection and incorporation establish attribution. The limited instruction to offer an uplifting general message and the clergy's independent wording remain part of the record, not evidence of private-forum access.

**2. Official university commencement worship violates the Establishment Clause on this record.** A public university violates the Establishment Clause when it incorporates officially arranged religious prayer into its commencement exercises, as the University did by selecting a religious leader to conduct actual invocation and benediction prayers as components of its official commencement. The violation does not depend on whether attendance is compulsory, whether the prayer is sectarian, or whether the ceremony also serves secular purposes. This governmental organization of worship falls under the specific-prohibition route of Lemon as Agostini modified it; mature attendance, meaningful freedom to abstain, brevity, nonsectarian language and a sincere ceremonial purpose do not cure it. Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly adopt Parts II–III.

Agostini retains Lemon as the governing test while treating purpose, effects and entanglement as evidence within one inquiry, not independent prongs. A specific prohibition or distinct doctrine governs conduct it resolves; an established permissible relationship defeats the claim on that ground; otherwise a legally sufficient unresolved allegation receives the attribution-and-function inquiry. Lee and Ingebretsen supply the official-worship prohibition applied here. The University selects and presents actual devotional exercises as its own commencement activity, so that specific prohibition resolves this case without a separate primary-effect test or a residual inquiry. Today's decision applies that principle to the previously unresolved university setting. The audience's maturity and genuine freedom to remain seated, leave or attend another ceremony bear materially on coercion, but do not change whose worship is presented. Most attendees remained seated, ordinary movement was common, and no penalty followed nonattendance. The Court accepts those facts without finding adolescent pressure or requiring a separate coercion showing.

The practice's long history and purpose of solemnity receive their actual weight but establish no permissible relationship authorizing this governmental devotional act. Marsh protects a distinct legislative-prayer setting; it does not establish an exception for every adult ceremony. Sherman permits a particular civic pledge and expressly distinguishes actual prayer; placing worship in a civic event does not convert it into civic acknowledgment. A secular ceremonial objective cannot justify the prohibited means. The exercises' brevity, and the view below that any advancement or entanglement was de minimis, do not determine whether the worship is officially organized. The ruling requires no sham-purpose, denominational-exclusion or excessive-entanglement finding. It applies Lemon as Agostini modified it and decides no other adult ceremonial arrangement.

**3. Relief must address the supported official practice and preserve private expression.** On remand, the lower courts shall determine appropriate prospective relief against the University's official selection and inclusion of these prayers, subject to the plaintiffs' continuing standing and the ordinary requirements for an injunction. Independently chosen private religious expression, neutral access to a genuine forum and nondevotional ceremonial remarks remain distinct; the judgment requires no new forum and no suppression of otherwise lawful viewpoints.

Ingebretsen supplies the remedy's official/private boundary. Any decree must reach the established institutional practice, not every religious reference, speaker or activity at the University. Private remarks by graduates, historical acknowledgment without devotional operation and a moment of silence are not prohibited by this holding. Any decree the lower courts enter must not prescribe new ceremonial content or treat these reservations as a ruling that arrangements not before the Court are permissible.

## Precedent Treatment

Lee and Ingebretsen's official-worship prohibition is applied to the actual university commencement program; their separate substantial-pressure rules remain unchanged and need not be applied to find a violation here. Engel and Schempp retain their official-worship protections despite nonsectarian wording and permission to abstain. Lemon remains the governing test as Agostini modified it: the specific-prohibition route resolves this governmental organization of worship, and primary religious effect is evidence rather than an independent prong. Agostini's onsite secular-service holding does not authorize governmental worship. Marsh is distinguished by its legislative context, and Sherman by its civic exercise. Pinette, Rosenberger, Good News and Mergens preserve genuinely private religious expression; their neutral-access holdings do not authorize governmental worship. No precedent is overruled.

## Law After Decision

Effective December 22, 1997, a public university violates the Establishment Clause when it incorporates officially arranged religious prayer into its commencement exercises; the violation does not depend on compulsory attendance, sectarian content or the ceremony's secular purposes. The University's official selection and incorporation of these devotional prayers is unconstitutional even for the mature voluntary audience shown here. Lemon, as Agostini modified it, resolves this governmental organization of worship through its specific-prohibition route, independently of a coercion finding. The separate pressure protections, private-speech rules, legislative-prayer precedent and ordinary ceremonial acknowledgment retain their existing limits. The decision creates no general prohibition on religion at universities or on every religious feature of an adult ceremony.

## Separate Writings

Justice Scalia's dissent, joined by Justices Kennedy and Thomas in Part I, would affirm because this brief traditional adult ceremony, with genuine freedom to abstain and no demonstrated unconstitutional pressure, falls outside the established secondary-school prohibitions. All three joined Agostini's modification of Lemon. They regard this particular historical ceremonial relationship as permissible within that test, drawing on Marsh while recognizing that its holding concerns legislative prayer. They acknowledge that the exercises are official and devotional; they disagree with the Court over whether the official-worship prohibition or a permissible ceremonial relationship governs this adult setting. The Court rejects that extension of Marsh. Their position does not make every longstanding adult prayer practice permissible.

Justice Thomas also joins Part II of Justice Scalia's dissent, which emphasizes historical ceremonial accommodation and the absence of the compulsion required by their account. That position does not become a universal historical exemption or an opinion of the Court, and Kennedy does not join its coercion methodology.

Justice Kennedy separately explains that Lee protects against substantial practical pressure without requiring a legal penalty. The mature audience, optional attendance, ordinary movement, seated abstention and available exit or reentry do not establish that pressure here. Consistently with his full join in Agostini, he accepts the modified Lemon test but regards this concrete voluntary adult ceremony as a permissible relationship, declining the Court's application of the official-worship prohibition to this university setting. He does not call the prayers private or reject practical coercion as a constitutional concern.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns for the lower courts to determine appropriate prospective relief against the established official prayer program, subject to the plaintiffs' continuing standing and the requirements for an injunction; the suit sought injunctive relief. The Court decides no damages, fees or new ceremony design, and requires no automatic evidentiary hearing. The continuing policy and Urbanski's expected exposure keep the claim for prospective relief live now; no later enrollment fact is presumed. No Supreme Court jurisdiction is retained or return date set.

## Source Notes

The complete Seventh Circuit opinion and prayer footnote, 104 F.3d 982, establish the practice, devotional content, voluntary adult participation and expected future exposure. The source contains different references to the precise May 1995 ceremony date; that discrepancy does not affect the continuing-policy question. The full complaint, depositions and District Court entry are not reproduced. No detailed University-written script, denominational exclusion, attendance penalty, new coercion finding or later change in enrollment is inferred.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/united_states_v_bajakajian_merits_1998-06-22.md

## Event

United States v. Bajakajian, No. 96-1487. Argued November 4, 1997; decided June 22, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit. The question is whether forfeiture of the entire $357,144 transported out of the country without the required report violates the Excessive Fines Clause.

Bajakajian pleaded guilty to willfully failing to report currency under 31 U.S.C. §§5316 and 5322. A separate false-statement count was dismissed by agreement, not decided by acquittal. After a forfeiture trial, the District Court found a lawful source, intended payment of a lawful debt and no connection to another crime. It refused full forfeiture under 18 U.S.C. §982(a)(1), ordered $15,000 forfeited and imposed three years’ probation and a $5,000 fine. Section 5322(a) authorized up to five years and $250,000; the applicable Guidelines maxima were six months and $5,000.

The Ninth Circuit affirmed on the ground that reporting currency was not an instrumentality and thus could not be forfeited, without reaching its proportionality prong. The Government seeks the full fund. Bajakajian did not cross-appeal the $15,000 forfeiture.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court affirms the refusal to forfeit the entire $357,144 on gross-disproportionality grounds, while rejecting the categorical instrumentality rationale.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Affirmed, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy, who would reverse the refusal of full forfeiture.

The existing judgment remains in place. The Court does not decide the constitutional validity of the unappealed $15,000 forfeiture or the statutory authority to substitute that amount. The conviction, probation and ordinary fine are not reopened; no new forfeiture amount is fixed.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins | Relationship and scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer throughout; Stone-Zsela in Parts I and III; O'Connor, Scalia and Kennedy in Parts I and II | I: punitive coverage, gross disproportionality and rejection of a categorical instrumentality prerequisite. II: appellate review. III: actual excessiveness and bounded affirmance. |
| Dissent in part | Kennedy | O'Connor, Scalia | All three join Parts I–II but reject Part III’s application and would permit the full statutory forfeiture. |

## Holdings

### I. A punitive criminal forfeiture must not be grossly disproportionate to its offense

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 982(a)(1)’s forfeiture imposed on this convicted offender as punishment is a fine subject to the Excessive Fines Clause. A punitive forfeiture violates that Clause when grossly disproportionate to the gravity of the offense for which it is imposed; statutory property connection alone does not answer excessiveness, and instrumentality is not a categorical prerequisite barring every currency-reporting forfeiture.

**Authority:** Part I of Thomas’s Opinion of the Court is joined by all other Justices. Part II has the eight Associates. Part III has Thomas, Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Each proposition below has direct majority support at its stated scope.

**Controlling explanation:** Austin recognizes partly punitive forfeitures as fines and leaves excessiveness separate from statutory connection. Alexander requires that independent inquiry for criminal forfeiture. The Government’s exaction here is imposed as punishment for the reporting conviction; it is not exempt because the currency is the property involved in the offense.

Substantial respect for legislative punishment judgments and the imprecision of gravity assessments make gross disproportionality, rather than strict equivalence, the governing boundary. Solem supplies that respect and proportionality analogy without deciding a new imprisonment question. The nature of the offense, culpability, harm, property’s relation to wrongdoing and authorized sanctions inform the comparison where relevant. They are not a compulsory exhaustive checklist, fixed ratio or government-cost formula. Congress may punish a reporting violation through property forfeiture, but its statutory authorization does not foreclose the constitutional inquiry. The Court rejects the circuit’s conclusion that such currency can never be forfeited for want of an instrumentality.

### II. The legal proportionality judgment receives independent appellate review

**Holding and operative rule:** Appellate courts independently review the constitutional determination whether this punitive forfeiture is grossly disproportionate, while reviewing supported underlying factual findings for clear error.

**Authority:** Part II is joined by all eight Associates; Stone-Zsela does not join that proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The facts found and the constitutional evaluation perform different work. Deference to the trial court’s supported findings does not transfer the legal boundary of the Excessive Fines Clause to unrestricted sentencing discretion. Independent review supplies no authority to invent an additional offense or substitute adverse facts for the findings. The Court announces no universal review standard for every mixed question.

### III. Full forfeiture is grossly disproportionate on these findings

**Holding and operative rule:** Forfeiture of the full $357,144 is grossly disproportionate to the willful reporting offense established here, considering the lawful funds and purpose, absence of another-crime connection, limited reporting harm and particular sentencing assessment together. The refusal of full forfeiture is affirmed without reviewing the existing $15,000 amount or setting a constitutional dollar ceiling.

**Authority:** Part III is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Controlling explanation:** The reporting duty is serious, and willful failure remains culpable even when transporting the funds would otherwise be lawful. But the proved offense withheld information; the findings establish neither criminal proceeds nor laundering, trafficking, fraud or a separate revenue loss against which the whole fund may be measured. Dismissal of the false-statement count does not establish that no false statement occurred.

The applicable six-month and $5,000 Guidelines maxima reflect the particular offense’s assessed severity. Congress’s broader five-year and $250,000 maxima and the forfeiture authorization deserve weight but do not conclusively make this full exaction proportionate. Treating them as conclusive would deprive independent excessiveness review of work. The lawful source, lawful debt and lack of another-crime connection are relevant together; lawful property is not automatically immune from a fine.

The reporting system’s preventive function and the large unreported transaction supply the strongest contrary argument. They do not overcome the extraordinary mismatch on these supported findings. The Court substitutes no new amount. Because Bajakajian did not cross-appeal, neither $15,000’s validity nor authority to select it is decided; no rule identifies $15,001 as excessive. Wealth, income, livelihood effects, other materially different crime-connected forfeitures and a comprehensive innocent-owner or in-rem rule remain unresolved.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Austin v. United States:** Partly punitive coverage and an excessiveness inquiry beyond statutory connection are applied; its holdings are not converted into an instrumentality-only test or a universal historical forfeiture theory.
- **Alexander v. United States:** Its independent offense-related Excessive Fines inquiry for criminal forfeiture is applied and given the gross-disproportionality formulation here.
- **Browning-Ferris Industries v. Kelco Disposal:** Its distinction between governmental punishment and private awards supports fine coverage.
- **Solem v. Helm:** Its legislative-deference and proportionality principles inform this fines rule without a new imprisonment holding.
- **Department of Revenue v. Kurth Ranch, United States v. Ursery and Hudson v. United States:** Their distinct Double Jeopardy classification rules remain intact and supply no automatic Excessive Fines result or universal cost ceiling.
- **One Lot Emerald Cut Stones and One Assortment of 89 Firearms:** Their distinct customs and forfeiture classifications establish no exemption or proportionality answer for this direct criminal reporting fine.

## Law After Decision

Punitive criminal forfeiture is subject to real but deferential gross-disproportionality review. A statutory connection to the offense is not enough, but currency-reporting forfeiture is not categorically forbidden. The full exaction fails on these findings; the judgment establishes neither a ratio nor a constitutional or statutory approval of the existing partial amount.

## Separate Writings

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting in part, joined by Justices O’Connor and Scalia.** They join punitive coverage, the gross-disproportionality standard, independent legal review and rejection of the circuit’s instrumentality prerequisite. They disagree with the application and would reverse the refusal of full forfeiture.

The amount concealed measures the scale of a deliberate failure to report. Congress may punish that wrong seriously without establishing that the funds were criminal proceeds or proving a second offense. Reporting helps detect financial crime that otherwise may remain hidden; treating the absence of a proved additional crime as decisive undervalues that preventive function. The five-year and $250,000 statutory maxima and the expressly mandated forfeiture demonstrate substantial legislative seriousness. The $5,000 Guidelines fine operates alongside, rather than silently replacing, forfeiture.

In their view the full amount remains within the demanding constitutional boundary despite lawful source and intended use. Their ground adds no laundering or trafficking finding and does not treat dismissal of the false-statement count as a conviction. They reject neither fines coverage nor the need for an individualized constitutional judgment, and they do not reopen the conviction, probation or ordinary fine. The disagreement supplies no ruling on the unappealed $15,000 amount.

## Procedure After Action

The judgment refusing the Government’s demand for full forfeiture remains effective. No new calculation or further forfeiture hearing is directed. The unappealed partial forfeiture and other sentence components remain undisturbed without affirmative adjudication of their independent validity.

## Source Notes

The reported findings and appellate grounds appear at [524 U.S. 321](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-524/pdf/USREPORTS-524-321.pdf); the [original record for No. 96-1487](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385014_0060) identifies the underlying filings. Sections 5316, 5322(a) and 982(a)(1) supply reporting, statutory punishment and forfeiture provisions. The $250,000 statutory and $5,000 Guidelines maxima are distinct; the false-statement count was dismissed rather than resolved by acquittal.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/united_states_v_bestfoods_merits_1998-06-08.md

## Event

United States v. Bestfoods, No. 97-454. Plenary merits decision, June 8, 1998; argued March 24, 1998. On writ of certiorari to the Sixth Circuit in the Government's CERCLA response-cost action. The questions concern derivative liability of a corporate parent and direct liability for its own operation of the subsidiary's polluting facility. The District Court imposed liability using active participation in the subsidiary's business; the Sixth Circuit limited direct liability to operation in the subsidiary's stead or as a joint venturer and otherwise required veil piercing. CPC's personnel overlap, ordinary oversight and a parent-only environmental official's involvement require separate treatment.

## Participation

Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate.

## Public Action

The Sixth Circuit's judgment is vacated and the case remanded.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 9–0.** **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The lower courts must apply the separate corporate-attribution and direct-operation rules. The District Court's liability judgment is not reinstated; ultimate responsibility remains undecided.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Vacates and remands | Complete opinion and both liability routes |

## Holdings

### 1. Corporate ownership does not itself impose subsidiary liability

**Holding and operative rule:** CERCLA does not make a parent answer for its subsidiary's obligations merely because the parent owns the subsidiary, selects directors or exercises ordinary financial and policy oversight. Derivative liability remains available when the applicable law permits disregard of corporate separateness; the Court does not choose state rather than federal veil-piercing law or reopen the unchallenged derivative result here.

**Authority:** Thomas's Opinion of the Court is joined by all nine Justices on each holding and its stated limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 107(a)(2) identifies a person that owned or operated the facility when hazardous substances were disposed of. Ownership of shares is not ownership or operation of the subsidiary's facility. Ordinary corporate arrangements survive unless Congress displaces them, and CERCLA's remedial purpose supplies no unexpressed rule making every parent responsible for a subsidiary's debt. Key Tronic requires attention to the actual statutory function rather than a useful label. Peacock distinguishes a mechanism for imposing another entity's obligation from an independently founded statutory duty; it does not immunize direct operation. The Government's broad business-control theory would improperly treat routine investor oversight as operation. Conversely, preservation of separateness does not prevent liability when the applicable veil rule actually permits attribution. No governing choice-of-law question need be resolved to correct the distinct direct-operation error.

### 2. A parent answers for its own pollution-related facility operations

**Holding and operative rule:** A parent is directly liable as an operator when it itself manages, directs or conducts facility operations specifically related to pollution, including hazardous-waste leakage or disposal and decisions about environmental compliance. Such liability requires neither veil piercing nor the parent's complete substitution for the subsidiary or a formal joint venture; general control of the subsidiary's business and ordinary investor oversight are insufficient.

**Authority:** The same unanimous opinion directly controls this independent statutory route.

**Controlling explanation:** The statutory object is the facility, not the subsidiary's corporate business in general. A person that actually undertakes the relevant facility operation falls within the operator category even when another corporation also operates there. The Sixth Circuit's sole-operation or formal-joint-venture limitation therefore misses conduct the statute reaches, while the District Court's broader corporate-control rule reaches conduct it does not.

Dual officers and directors ordinarily act for the subsidiary when conducting its business. The Government must establish that the operational conduct it attributes to the parent was actually undertaken for the parent; overlapping offices alone do not supply that proof. Departure from ordinary corporate norms, including parent-serving conduct contrary to the subsidiary's interests, may bear on attribution. Ordinary monitoring, financing, budgets and general policies do not establish direct operation. The parent-only environmental official's reported involvement requires examination of actual function and capacity. It supports remand, not a finding of liability. Existing dual-office findings are insufficient by themselves, but ordinary procedure may permit identification of additional relevant evidence.

The Court does not decide CPC's ultimate responsibility, a new universal burden of proof, contribution, fees, settlement scope or amount. Aerojet's separate no-disposal and third-party defenses are outside review.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Key Tronic Corp. v. United States, June 6, 1994:** Applied for attention to the actual statutory function; its response-cost and fee rules are not enlarged.
- **Peacock v. Thomas, February 21, 1996:** Distinguished in its separate judgment-collection jurisdiction setting; its distinction between derivative liability and an independently founded duty informs the two routes here.

## Law After Decision

Corporate separateness and direct statutory responsibility coexist. General business control alone does not establish operator liability, and absence of veil piercing does not defeat proof that the parent itself conducted pollution-related facility operations. The state-versus-federal veil-law question and ultimate operational findings remain open.

## Separate Writings

No separate writing issues.

## Procedure After Action

The action returns for application of the correct direct-operation standard and any otherwise properly remaining proceedings. No damages amount, ultimate liability or new evidence entitlement is established.

## Source Notes

The reported facts and corporate roles are set out in [524 U.S. 51](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-524/pdf/USREPORTS-524-51.pdf). The derivative result was not challenged; the choice of veil-piercing law and Aerojet's separate defenses remain outside this decision.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/united_states_v_hinds_merits_1998-04-06.md

## Event

United States v. Hinds; Fourth Circuit No. 97-4030, district case CR-96-148. Plenary merits decision, April 6, 1998; October Term 1997, chunk 6. On review under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1) of the Fourth Circuit's unpublished October 15, 1997 judgment affirming the §844(i) conviction. The questions are whether the Pleasant Ridge United Church of Christ building was used in interstate commerce or an activity affecting commerce under that statute and, if the statute reaches it, whether that application lies within Congress's Commerce Clause authority. Hinds also presented an Establishment Clause objection below. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is established.

Hinds and an accomplice burned the church in Greensboro, North Carolina. Hinds waived a jury and stipulated the elements other than the commerce connection. After hearing evidence on that issue, the District Court denied his motion to dismiss and found him guilty. The Fourth Circuit recited the property-use element, held the evidence sufficient, and rejected the religious objection. The church sent annual funds through its national denomination, employed one full-time and two part-time workers, provided the full-time employee insurance procured from out-of-state companies, and purchased utilities, insurance, religious materials and other goods and services.

## Participation

All nine Justices participate at merits submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

## Public Action

The Court vacates the Fourth Circuit's affirmance and remands for application of §844(i)'s property-use requirement to the existing trial record.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Vacated and remanded, 8–1.**

**Supporting:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stone-Zsela.

The Fourth Circuit's affirmance is vacated. That court must apply the statutory construction to the existing trial record and determine the lawful disposition of the §844(i) conviction. No additional evidence or new trial is authorized. An actual finding of evidentiary insufficiency requires reversal for entry of acquittal; any supported adequate use basis and necessary, properly presented constitutional question must be addressed under current law. The Court does not itself direct acquittal, release, a substitute conviction or resentencing. Stone-Zsela would instead reverse and direct vacatur of the conviction and immediate entry of acquittal on this count.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Vacates the Fourth Circuit's affirmance and remands | Part I's complete property-function construction and application; Part II's existing-record mandate and constitutional nonreach |
| Same opinion, partial joins | Stevens | Stone-Zsela | Agrees that the affirmance cannot stand; opposes the Court's operative remand | Part I only as to independent work for the property-use element, insufficiency of owner relationships by themselves, and absence of a religious exemption; Part II only as to the independent Burks rule that evidentiary insufficiency requires acquittal and bars retrial to repair proof |
| Concurrence in part and dissent from the judgment and remand | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Would reverse and direct vacatur of the §844(i) conviction and entry of acquittal on that count | Active-commercial-use construction; present insufficiency and immediate acquittal; separate Agostini-based reason for rejecting the Establishment objection as a construction aid or alternative ground |

## Holdings

### I. The damaged property's use must connect it to commerce

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 844(i) requires a connection between the function performed by the damaged property and interstate or foreign commerce, or an activity affecting that commerce. The owner's national affiliation, purchases, an employee's out-of-state insurance and recurring payments do not establish the element merely by existing; their relation to the property's use must be established, without making religious or nonprofit ownership an exclusion or profit-making, rental, sales or an exclusively business purpose a universal prerequisite.

**Authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies eight direct joins to the complete construction and its application. Stone-Zsela joins only the propositions that the property-use element has independent work, that the owner's identified economic relationships by themselves do not establish it, and that religious identity creates no exemption. He does not join the complete formulation's rejection of an exclusively commercial-use requirement; his active-commercial-use construction is stated separately.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute asks what the damaged property was used to do and how that function connects with commerce. Russell sustains coverage of a rented apartment building because renting the premises placed the property itself in the rental market. Its broad account of statutory coverage does not erase the enacted use limitation, and its rental example does not establish an exclusive class of covered property. The words extending coverage to an activity affecting commerce retain both their breadth and their connection to the property's use.

The Fourth Circuit correctly recited the property-use element. Its application nevertheless treated the congregation's economic relationships as sufficient without establishing their relation to the function of the damaged premises. Annual transfers through a national denomination are actual movements of money, and thus the Government's strongest asserted connection. But the existence of those transfers does not itself explain the building's qualifying function. The same distinction applies to the employees, employee insurance, utilities, religious materials and other purchases. The Court neither doubts those reported relationships nor assumes every purchase came from outside the State.

Bailey/Robinson supplies the statutory distinction between required conduct and another related condition: satisfying one does not dispense with the other. Its firearm-use rule is not transplanted into this statute. Section 844(i)'s own use language does the work here. The Court adds no interstate-travel requirement, no profit-making condition and no requirement that this individual fire substantially affect the national economy. The panel's stated rationale cannot sustain its affirmance; the complete trial proof must be considered under the correct construction. No constitutional ruling or categorical exclusion of religious premises follows.

**Precedent treatment:** Russell remains controlling for actual rental use and is applied without turning that example into exclusive coverage. Bailey/Robinson is applied at the distinction between separately required statutory predicates, not as an independent definition of property use.

### II. The appellate judgment is vacated for review of the existing trial record

**Holding and operative rule:** The Fourth Circuit's affirmance is vacated, and that court must apply the property-use construction to the complete existing trial record and determine the lawful disposition of the §844(i) conviction; this mandate authorizes neither additional evidence nor a new trial. If the existing evidence fails to establish the required use connection, the court must reverse for entry of acquittal, because evidentiary insufficiency cannot be repaired through another trial; if existing proof supports a legally adequate use finding, the court must identify that basis and address any necessary, properly presented constitutional question under current law.

**Authority:** Part II of Stevens's opinion has the same eight Associate joins. Stone-Zsela joins the independent Burks proposition that actual evidentiary insufficiency requires acquittal and bars a second opportunity to supply missing proof, but rejects this judgment and remand: he finds the proof insufficient now and would direct acquittal immediately. No condition of the Court's further-review mandate is attributed to his position.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court corrects the legal application supporting the appellate affirmance. It does not decide that the complete trial record contains no other evidence relevant to the damaged building's function. Those are different determinations. The lower court must examine existing proof under the rule announced here; it may not treat this remand as permission to build a new prosecution case.

Burks requires acquittal after a determination that the prosecution's evidence is legally insufficient. Jacobson applies that consequence and forbids another opportunity to supply missing proof. Those authorities control if the existing record establishes only institutional relationships without the necessary property-use connection. Conversely, a legally adequate use finding cannot be assumed merely because the conviction once stood. Any such basis must actually be supported, and any necessary preserved constitutional question must then be addressed.

Bailey/Robinson illustrates the distinction between correcting an erroneous statutory premise and deciding every remaining lawful basis or remedial predicate. Its separate carrying branch creates no alternative element here. The remand determines neither acquittal nor retrial in advance, and orders no release, substitute §247 conviction, amended penalty or independent sentencing disposition. The Court reaches neither Congress's outer Commerce Clause authority nor the Establishment Clause objection.

**Precedent treatment:** Burks and Jacobson preserve the mandatory acquittal consequence of actual insufficiency. Bailey/Robinson supplies a bounded remedial analogy, without a new carrying theory, new evidence or an automatic retrial rule.

## Precedent Treatment

- **Russell v. United States, 471 U.S. 858 (1985):** Applied at its actual rental-use holding and retained statutory use limitation. Rental remains sufficient in its established setting, without becoming an exclusive class or establishing automatic church coverage.
- **Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States, December 6, 1995:** Applied at the separation of independently enacted conditions and used as a bounded remedial analogy. Its active-firearm-use and carrying rules are not imported into §844(i).
- **Burks v. United States, 437 U.S. 1 (1978), and Jacobson v. United States, April 6, 1992:** Applied to require acquittal after actual evidentiary insufficiency and prohibit another trial to repair missing proof.
- **United States v. Lopez and its current applications, including United States v. Robinson, March 23, 1998:** Preserved without a new constitutional application. Statutory jurisdictional words remain no automatic constitutional safe harbor, and each statute retains its actual elements.
- **Agostini v. Felton and Tanford v. Brand:** Unchanged. The Court does not decide the Establishment objection; Stone-Zsela's separate application of Agostini and Everson establishes no additional Court holding.

## Law After Decision

Section 844(i)'s property-use element requires the relationship between the function of the damaged premises and commerce or a commerce-affecting activity. The owner's economic relationships alone do not suffice. Religious and nonprofit premises receive neither categorical exclusion nor automatic coverage. Existing-record review must distinguish a legally adequate use basis from insufficient proof; the latter requires acquittal and cannot support another evidentiary opportunity. The Court changes no Commerce Clause or Establishment Clause standard and decides no §247, penalty-amendment or other-charge question. Stone-Zsela's exclusive active-commercial-use formulation, immediate-acquittal application and separate Establishment explanation do not bind lower courts.

## Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting from the judgment and remand.** The enacted use requirement demands active employment of the building itself in commercial activity. The congregation's purchases, utilities, insurance, staff, donations, mail and national affiliation are economic relationships; they do not transform premises devoted to worship into property actively employed in commerce. The national denomination's annual redistribution of funds to missionaries, seminaries, institutions of higher education and other programs is the Government's strongest argument, but it still describes what the congregation paid and gave in support of worship, rather than commercial use of this building. Russell's rented premises illustrate the distinction because the property itself operated in the rental market. The Fourth Circuit stated the use element but answered it through the congregation's economic connections. Stone-Zsela considers the Government's proof insufficient now. Under Burks and Jacobson he would reverse, direct vacatur of the conviction and entry of acquittal, and deny another trial to repair that proof.

Lopez supplies avoidance context, not a constitutional judgment. Its connection-before-magnitude sequence raises doubt about a reading that reaches every building through an owner's insurance, purchases, payroll or gifts and their downstream economic consequences. Avoidance is available because Congress enacted a use limitation to construe; it permits no inserted element. The existence of jurisdictional wording is not itself a constitutional safe harbor. Lopez preserves genuine economic-class analysis under Wickard and Perez and does not require a substantial effect from each individual act. Stone-Zsela leaves Hinds's particular substantial-effect contention and the outer Commerce question undecided. Hale, Rybar and Kirk's protection of a valid machinegun regime does not dispense with this statute's different property-use element. Robinson's direct interference with operating business assets likewise supplies no all-purchases rule. Robertson's commercial enterprise transactions and Allied-Bruce's broader transaction-involving-commerce language do not replace §844(i)'s property-use text.

This is no religious exemption. Stone-Zsela reserves the reach of the statute over religious property itself used for a commercial enterprise, rental activity, substantial commercial leasing or materially mixed commercial use. He decides no particular application within those categories, no authority under another federal statute, including FACE's unchallenged worship branches, and no reach of differently worded statutes such as the Hobbs Act. Ordinary state arson law and independently valid federal charges retain their own elements and limits.

He separately rejects the Establishment objection as a reason to construe the statute differently for religious property or as an alternative ground for reversal. Under Lemon as Agostini modified it, a specific prohibition or distinct doctrine resolves conduct it covers; an established permissible relationship defeats a covered claim; otherwise a legally sufficient unresolved claim proceeds through attribution and function. Purpose, effects and entanglement are evidence within that inquiry, not independent or fallback prongs. The governmental act here is prosecution of an alleged arsonist, and its function is ordinary property protection. Everson's established relationship of general public-welfare services, including ordinary police and fire protection, resolves the objection, provided the criminal statute validly applies.

The prosecution organizes no worship, prescribes no religious profession or observance, adopts no doctrine, solicits no religious adherence, governs no theology, confers no religiously allocated civil authority and finances no devotion. Examining a congregation's operations to determine an offense element is not regulation of its religious teaching or governance. Tanford involved the government's own organized commencement worship and therefore a different function. Facial neutrality and the beneficiary's identity are contextual evidence, not independently decisive grounds. Stone-Zsela reaches neither the residual route nor an accommodation, funding, legislative-prayer or ceremonial question; free exercise and religious autonomy retain their distinct sources and burdens. His answer creates no rule permitting every governmental benefit to religion, supplies neither statutory coverage nor an enumerated power, and does not ground his reversal. The second granted question remains unreached; insofar as the Establishment objection falls outside the grant, this discussion states only his reason for declining to rely on it. No other Justice joins this separate explanation.

## Procedure After Action

The case returns to the Fourth Circuit for application of the property-use construction to the complete existing trial record. Its prior affirmance no longer governs. If that evidence is insufficient under the construction, the court must reverse for entry of acquittal; if it supports a legally adequate use finding, the court must identify that basis and address any necessary, properly presented constitutional question. The mandate authorizes no new evidence or retrial and assumes no alternative basis for affirmance. No release or independently chosen sentencing order is entered. Supreme Court review of this event is complete; the bounded proceedings below remain.

## Source Notes

The [official Fourth Circuit opinion, No. 97-4030](https://www.ca4.uscourts.gov/opinions/Unpublished/974030.U.pdf), supplies the bench trial, stipulation, operations described, sufficiency analysis, Establishment objection and judgment below. Its full statement includes the property-use element; the defect identified here concerns the application of that element. The relevant enacted language appears in the [1994 United States Code, Title 18, §844](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title18/pdf/USCODE-1994-title18.pdf). No penalty-amendment question is decided.

[Russell, 471 U.S. 858](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep471/usrep471858/usrep471858.pdf), establishes the rental-use holding and discusses broad coverage, including Congress's removal of proposed business-purpose wording. The complete trial proof and detailed property-function findings are not reproduced in the published lower opinion; its account is not treated as an exhaustive finding that no other relevant proof existed. The precise offense date is not supplied and no amendment-dependent consequence is inferred. No separate Supreme Court argument date is supplied.

## Effective current-term public source: terms/OT1997/records/valot_v_southeast_local_school_district_board_of_education_merits_1998-01-05.md

## Event

Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education, review of the Sixth Circuit's final judgment in No. 95-3390, 107 F.3d 1220. Decided January 5, 1998, on the merits of the substitute drivers' federal retaliation and related claims. No separate Supreme Court docket or argument date is stated.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participated on submission and at decision. No Justice was absent.

## Public Action

The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and vacated in part, and the case is remanded to the Sixth Circuit for proceedings consistent with the component rulings below.

## Judgment & Remedy

| Component | Disposition | Supporting | Opposing |
|---|---|---|---|
| Count 1, petition retaliation | Reverse categorical denial of protection, vacate corresponding defense summary judgment and remand | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer — six | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas |
| Count 2, retaliatory administrative access | Vacate and remand the protected-invocation claim | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer — five | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas |
| Count 3, independent ordinary economic classification | Affirm rejection | All nine Justices | None |
| Count 3, penalty upon a protected constitutional right | Vacate and remand this distinct branch | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer — five | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas |
| Count 4, substantive due process | Affirm rejection | All nine Justices | None |
| Count 5, Ohio public-policy claim | Vacate nonretention based on dismissal of all federal claims; reconsider discretion | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer — five | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas |

No procedural-due-process or renewal-property claim is revived. Plaintiffs receive no immediate partial liability judgment; denial resting solely on a corrected categorical ground remains subject to remand. Individual immunity, actual responsibility, entity attribution, injury and precise relief remain unresolved. The Court orders no reinstatement, backpay, damages, repayment, hearing, trial or compulsory retention of the Ohio claim.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joins |
|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court, Parts I and IV: petition protection and count-1 remand | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Opinion of the Court, Part II: independent equality and substantive due process | Souter | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; Stone-Zsela agrees in the judgments |
| Opinion of the Court, Part III: access, dependent equality and state-claim reconsideration | Souter | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone-Zsela | None |
| Concurrence in Part II and dissent from Parts I, III and IV | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas |

## Holdings

**1. These lawful unemployment-benefit petitions are protected despite their private financial subject.** A public employer may not refuse continued employment solely because an employee lawfully and non-shamly invokes this established governmental unemployment-benefit mechanism and thereby imposes the benefit cost prescribed by law. That protection does not require public-concern content. Parts I and IV command six votes: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

Perry separates lack of an employment entitlement from punishment for protected conduct. California Motor Transport supplies the protection of access to governmental redress, and Smith v. Arkansas State Highway Employees distinguishes protection against retaliation from an affirmative obligation to accept a claimant's demands. Mt. Healthy, Umbehr and O'Hare require identification of the particular activity and its causal role. The formal external benefits proceeding here is not merely an informal workplace complaint. Connick's public-concern limit for employee speech does not defeat this particular invocation claim. The Court does not decide that every personal request or internal grievance escapes employment doctrine, and success is not a general condition of petition protection.

The Board admitted that applications and benefit receipts caused nonrenewal. Its dollar-for-dollar expense was real, but was the lawful cost of the very invocation it punished; no separate established service disruption or fraud supplies the asserted justification. The later reasonable-assurance practice cannot prove summer 1993 assurance or ineligibility. Government may contest eligibility, reject fraudulent or ineligible claims, enforce neutral filing requirements, lawfully change prospective terms and act for independent job-related reasons. The ruling neither freezes the benefit program nor treats all operational costs as irrelevant.

**2. The independent equality and substantive-due-process claims fail.** A classification based on known benefit applications has a rational relationship to possible recurring fiscal costs when considered independently of any protected-right penalty. This record also establishes no distinct fundamental entitlement to renewal or substantive-due-process deprivation. Part II has all eight Associate Justices; Stone-Zsela agrees in these judgments.

Beach permits a conceivable legitimate basis for ordinary economic distinctions; Heller and Romer require attention to the actual disadvantage and preserve limits on irrational classifications. That ordinary fiscal conclusion does not resolve the separate protected-right theory or prove a Mt. Healthy same-decision defense. Collins distinguishes voluntary employment and state-law expectations from an actual federal deprivation. Annual contracts and an expected recommendation did not guarantee renewal. Plaintiffs expressly confined their due-process claim to substance, so the Court does not revive a procedural claim. Albright's fractured judgment supplies no universal specific-source or state-remedy bar.

**3. The access and protected-right equality branches require reconsideration.** Successful receipt of benefits does not defeat a claim that employment was withheld for invoking the process. Count 2 therefore returns within the protected-invocation rule, without creation of a freestanding general access entitlement. The rights-dependent part of count 3 likewise returns because its categorical no-protected-right premise is incorrect. Part III has Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.
